Multi-hop relaying can restore coverage, but relay-link activation under radio, capacity, and routing constraints results in an NP-hard optimisation problem, typically solved via Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP), whose runtime scales poorly with graph size. This paper introduces an edge-aware Learning-to-Optimise framework for real-time relay selection. Each V2X snapshot is modelled as a directed graph: node features encode vehicle state and traffic demand, while edge features capture radio-link capacity. An offline MILP oracle generates optimal relay configurations that supervise a Graph Isomorphism Network with Edge Features (GINE), enabling edge-level relay activation through a single forward pass, with tightly bounded inference latency. To bridge learning and exact optimisation, we also propose a hybrid GINE-Pruned MILP (GP-MILP) strategy in which GINE predictions prune the MILP search space. Experiments on a large-scale dataset generated via an OSM-SUMO-GEMV$^2$ pipeline show that GINE closely matches MILP decisions at the link level (accuracy 0.9589), F1-score (0.9544) on validation) and yields consistent end-to-end connectivity gains over a 1-hop MILP baseline (up to 9.2% with four RSUs and 12% with two RSUs). Inference latency remains tightly bounded, with all evaluated instances completing within 5~ms. Moreover, GP-MILP preserves MILP-equivalent solutions (same objective value) while achieving solver runtimes below 30~ms for more than 98%) of the graph instances, making MILP-grade optimisation compatible with stringent NR-V2X latency budgets.

</details>


### [102] [RENEW: Towards Learning World Models and Repairing Model Exploitation from Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14180)
*Logan Mondal Bhamidipaty,Mykel Kochenderfer,Subramanian Ramamoorthy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DLHF: a human-preference guided framework to repair exploitation in offline world models, using Bradley-Terry loss on dynamical trajectories; enhanced by RENEW with epistemic-uncertainty-driven fine-tuning to improve sample efficiency and reduce forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle exploitation in offline model-based RL caused by thin data coverage, without costly expert data collection or overly conservative policies.

Method: Introduce Dynamics Learning from Human Feedback (DLHF) with a Bradley-Terry preference loss over trajectory log-likelihoods under a learned dynamics model. To address sample inefficiency, propose RENEW, which leverages epistemic uncertainty to focus fine-tuning on regions where the model is most exploitable.

Result: Naive DLHF is sample-inefficient; RENEW makes the approach practical by improving sample efficiency, limiting catastrophic forgetting, and reducing exploitation. Empirical evaluation on Jumanji and classic control environments shows improvements.

Conclusion: Preferences can supervise world-model dynamics directly, offering a new solution to exploitation in offline model-based RL; RENEW makes DLHF practical for limited preference budgets.

Abstract: World models are widely used in offline reinforcement learning (RL) to improve sample efficiency and generate experience beyond a fixed dataset. However, they are vulnerable to model exploitation where data coverage is thin. Prior work addresses this either by collecting more expert demonstrations, which is often expensive, unsafe, or unavailable, or by conservative algorithms that avoid uncertain regions, which limits generalization. We propose instead to repair exploitation directly using human preferences over imagined rollouts, leveraging the strong intuitive physics that allows humans to easily spot egregious dynamics hallucinations. We formalize this as Dynamics Learning from Human Feedback (DLHF), a Bradley-Terry preference loss over trajectory log-likelihoods under a learned dynamics model. Unfortunately, naive DLHF is sample inefficient, so we introduce RENEW, which uses epistemic uncertainty to focus finetuning where the model is most exploitable. We evaluate on several Jumanji and classic control environments and find that while naive DLHF requires an outsize preference budget, RENEW makes the framework practical by improving sample efficiency, limiting catastrophic forgetting, and reducing exploitation in pretrained world models. Taken together, our results provide initial evidence that preferences can supervise world model dynamics directly, offering a new approach to addressing exploitation in offline model-based RL.

</details>


### [103] [Closed-Loop Knowledge Dynamics: An Operational Framework for Saturation and Escape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14185)
*Xuening Wu,Shan Yu,Shenqin Yin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a three-level framework for feedback-driven knowledge systems to understand saturation and how external interventions can move the system to new attractors. Uses Lyapunov drift analysis to bound internal dynamics, derives conditions for escape from attractors via intervention, and demonstrates with case studies across LLM code repair, sparse-reward RL, and Bayesian optimization; highlights that MI alone cannot certify escape and provides cross-domain diagnostics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain why closed-loop knowledge systems saturate under internal feedback and identify external information strategies that can push them to qualitatively different regimes. Provide an operational framework that links stability theory, intervention detectability, and empirical diagnostics across domains.

Method: Define a three-level framework with state x_t and transition kernels K_θ parameterized by θ. The governing structure is the observational equivalence class of θ; attractors/basins pertain to fixed-θ dynamics. Introduce structural interventions that alter θ and produce detectable kernel discrepancies on probe states. Use Lyapunov drift to show dynamics converge to bounded regions with attenuated transients and a noise floor. Characterize escape with a metric on attractor displacement and a KL-based lower bound on escape probability. Argue that conditional mutual information alone cannot certify escape. Validate with case studies—matched continuation controls—in LLM code repair, sparse-reward RL, and Bayesian optimization to show how feedback strength/alignment affects improvements.

Result: Analytical framework connecting stability tools with measurable intervention effects. Demonstrates that stable internal dynamics exhibit bounded regions and diminishing transients, while escape requires sufficient attractor displacement and information gain beyond baseline. Shows that MI conditioned on intervention fails to capture departure from the no-intervention law. Case studies illustrate practical implications of feedback strength and alignment on quality improvements across domains.

Conclusion: Provides an operational bridge between stability analysis, verifiable intervention effects, and cross-domain diagnostics. Structural interventions enable falsifiable departures from existing attractors, offering diagnostic and design guidance for improving iterative learning loops in AI systems.

Abstract: Feedback-driven loops support iterative improvement in large language models, reinforcement learning, and autonomous discovery, yet their gains often diminish under repeated internal feedback. We study why closed-loop knowledge systems saturate and what external information can move them beyond their current attractors. We introduce a three-level operational framework in which knowledge states $x_t$ evolve through transition kernels $K_θ$ indexed by a structural parameter $θ$. The governing structure is defined as the observational equivalence class of $θ$ induced by these kernels, while attractors and basins are properties of the fixed-$θ$ dynamics. A structural intervention changes $θ$ and produces a detectable kernel discrepancy on pre-specified probe states, making structural change falsifiable. Using a Lyapunov drift condition, we show that stable internal dynamics approach bounded stability regions with exponentially attenuated transients and a noise-controlled residual floor. We characterize escape through a metric condition on intervention-induced attractor displacement and a baseline-relative KL lower bound for increasing escape probability. This analysis also explains why conditional mutual information alone cannot certify escape: it measures variation among intervention-conditioned updates rather than departure from the no-intervention law. Case studies in LLM code repair, sparse-reward reinforcement learning, and Bayesian optimization use matched continuation controls to illustrate how feedback strength and alignment affect quality-improving escape. Our contribution is an operational connection among stability tools, measurable intervention effects, and cross-domain diagnostics.

</details>


### [104] [A Temporal Machine Learning-Based Time-to-Event Model for Predicting ALS Progression and Healthcare Utilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14190)
*Zongliang Yue,Qi Li,Terry Heiman-Patterson,Frank Bearoff,Zhaohui Qin,Huanmei Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A digital-twin-inspired time-to-event framework combines longitudinal ALSFRS-R data with survival modeling to predict wheelchair-free survival and assistive-device milestones in ALS, enabling personalized prognostic support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ALS is heterogeneous and its progression to milestones like wheelchair use is hard to predict; a scalable, interpretable framework is needed for personalized decision support and trial stratification.

Method: Create a harmonized longitudinal dataset from diagnosis, ALSFRS-R, daily living activities, and demographics; preprocess for quality and temporal alignment; use correlation-based clustering to identify functional domains (bulbar, upper limb, axial, lower limb, respiratory); apply generalized additive mixed models for nonlinear, domain-specific trajectory modeling; develop a temporal machine learning model to predict longitudinal decline; perform Cox proportional hazards analysis to identify predictors of wheelchair access; implement a digital-twin-inspired time-to-event model to generate individualized survival curves and wheelchair-free survival.

Result: Identified coherent functional domains and nonlinear domain-specific decline; lower-limb functions (walking, stair climbing) emerged as strong predictors of earlier wheelchair use; the digital-twin TTE model yields personalized survival curves and dynamic wheelchair-free predictions; the framework is scalable, interpretable, and suited for proactive care planning and trial stratification.

Conclusion: The proposed framework links ALS progression to personalized decision support, supporting proactive care planning, clinical trial stratification, and precision medicine through interpretable, patient-specific prognostic modeling.

Abstract: Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) is a progressive and heterogeneous neurodegenerative disease in which predicting clinically meaningful milestones, such as assistive device use, remains challenging. We developed a time-to-event, digital-twin-inspired framework that integrates longitudinal ALS Functional Rating Scale-Revised (ALSFRS-R) trajectories with survival modeling to support individualized prediction of functional decline and assistive device utilization. We constructed a harmonized longitudinal dataset by integrating diagnosis records, ALSFRS-R assessments, activities of daily living, and demographic information, followed by preprocessing to ensure data quality, temporal alignment, and cohort consistency. Correlation-based clustering identified coherent functional domains spanning bulbar, upper limb, axial, lower limb, and respiratory systems. Generalized additive mixed models characterized nonlinear, domain-specific functional decline across all domains. In addition, a temporal machine learning model was developed to predict longitudinal functional decline and capture stage-dependent disease progression. Cox proportional hazards modeling further identified lower limb function, particularly walking and stair climbing, as the strongest predictors of earlier wheelchair access. Building on these results, we implemented a digital twin-inspired temporal machine learning-based time-to-event (TTE) model that generates individualized survival curves and dynamically predicts wheelchair-free survival. This framework provides a scalable, interpretable, and clinically actionable approach for linking ALS progression with personalized decision support, with applications in proactive care planning, clinical trial stratification, and precision medicine.

</details>


### [105] [TEDDY: A Pediatric Foundation Model for Risk Forewarning from ICD-Coded Diagnostic Histories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14191)
*Matthew Brady Neeley,Jorge Botas,Johnathan Jia,Lin Yao,Daniel Palacios,Benjamin Choi,Zhandong Liu,Hyun-Hwan Jeong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TEDDY: a compact 1.84M-parameter decoder transformer trained on ~73M ICD-10 diagnoses from 1.6M children at a single pediatric institution. It models longitudinal diagnosis trajectories and visit timing, predicting before visit codes are revealed across 797 disease-onset tasks (16 ICD-10 chapters) with a median AUC of 72.0%, outperforming DenseNet, CNN, RNN, and LSTM baselines on 96–99% of tasks and showing signal up to >2 years before first diagnosis. Strongest for rare conditions; asthma and ADHD benchmarks show notable gains. Visit-timing predictions achieve a 3.0-day mean absolute restricted RMSE over 365 days but exhibit miscalibration in long-tail intervals. Concludes pediatric histories can support broad, rare-disease, long-horizon risk forecasting with compact models without population-scale data or billion-parameter models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pediatric electronic health records contain developmentally structured clinical trajectories that remain underexplored for generative healthcare foundation models. The work aims to determine whether a compact, data-efficient decoder transformer can capture long-horizon, multi-disease risk forecasting across common and rare pediatric conditions using first-diagnosis events from a large pediatric cohort.

Method: A decoder transformer with 1.84M parameters trained on ~73 million ICD-10 diagnoses from 1.6 million children at a single institution. The model learns longitudinal diagnosis trajectories and visit timing. Predictions are made before visit codes are revealed, restricted to first occurrences, and evaluated against sex- and age-matched controls across 797 disease-onset tasks spanning 16 ICD-10 chapters. Baseline comparisons include DenseNet, CNN, RNN, and LSTM; performance is also contrasted with a general-purpose language model much larger. Visit-timing predictions are evaluated using mean absolute error within a restricted survival-time framework over 365 days.

Result: Median AUC of 72.0% across tasks, outperforming all baselines on 96–99% of tasks. Performance is robust across sex and age, and strongest for low-prevalence diagnoses (202 of the 225 rarest conditions show 95% CIs above chance). Predictive signals exist more than two years before first recorded diagnosis (AUC ~59.7% unrestricted, 64.4% fixed-cohort). In asthma and ADHD benchmarks, AUCs are 79.3% and 84.7% compared to 62.7% and 71.7% for strongest baselines. Visit-timing predictions yield a 3.0-day mean absolute restricted RMSE over 365 days, though long-tail calibration remains imperfect.

Conclusion: Pediatric diagnostic histories can underwrite compact, generative models enabling broad, rare-disease, and long-horizon risk forecasting without requiring population-scale data or billion-parameter models.

Abstract: Pediatric electronic health records capture developmentally structured clinical trajectories, yet their potential for generative healthcare foundation models remains largely unexplored. Here we present TEDDY (Temporal Event Decoder for Disease in Youth), a 1.84-million-parameter decoder transformer trained on approximately 73 million ICD-10 diagnoses from 1.6 million children at a single pediatric institution. TEDDY models longitudinal diagnosis trajectories and visit timing. Predictions were made before visit codes were revealed, limited to first occurrences, and evaluated against sex- and age-matched controls. Across 797 disease-onset prediction tasks spanning 16 ICD-10 chapters, TEDDY achieved a median AUC of 72.0%, outperforming same-data DenseNet (50.0%), CNN (57.2%), RNN (60.1%), and LSTM (62.7%) baselines on 96-99% of tasks. Performance held across sex and age and was strongest among lower-prevalence diagnoses; 202 of the 225 rarest conditions (90%) had 95% confidence intervals above chance. Predictive signal remained detectable more than two years before first recorded diagnosis, with median AUCs of 59.7% in the unrestricted analysis and 64.4% in a fixed-cohort sensitivity analysis. In asthma and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder benchmarks, AUCs were 79.3% and 84.7%, compared with 62.7% and 71.7% for the strongest comparators, including a general-purpose language model three orders of magnitude larger. Visit-timing predictions had a 3.0-day mean absolute restricted mean survival-time error over 365 days, although median and long-tail return intervals remained miscalibrated. Together, these results establish pediatric diagnostic histories as a substrate for compact generative models supporting broad, rare-disease, and long-horizon risk forecasting without population-scale data or billion-parameter models.

</details>


### [106] [Long-term User Engagement Optimization through Model-agnostic Downstream Rewards Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14192)
*Dingsu Wang,Filip Ryzner,Kelly He,Armando Ordorica,David Woo,Aditya Mantha,Liyao Lu,Usha Amrutha Nookala,Haoran Guo,Jiacong He,Olafur Gudmundsson,Matt Chun,Krystal Benitez,Dhruvil Deven Badani,Yijie Dylan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A model-agnostic downstream reward framework to optimize long-term user value in large-scale recommender systems, using offline screening and multiple signals, with production deployment and online improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retention is crucial but hard to optimize directly due to sparse, delayed, and partially attributable signals. Prior methods rely on task-specific rewards and heavy computation; a generalizable approach is needed.

Method: Formulates downstream reward learning as a problem; develops offline screening to identify session-level behaviors that are observable early and predictive of future retention; proposes several model-agnostic downstream rewards signals derived from user actions across multiple sources; discusses productionization and integration with ranking models.

Result: Online A/B experiments show consistent improvements in engagement and retention-related metrics; framework deployed across multiple Pinterest surfaces (Homefeed, Related Pins, Search, Notifications).

Conclusion: The framework offers a scalable, generalizable approach to enhance long-term user value in recommender systems, reducing reliance on task-specific reward engineering while enabling broad deployment.

Abstract: As recommender systems mature in the past few years, their optimization objectives have evolved from a primary focusing on short-term behavioral signals to a broader emphasis on long-term user engagement and retention. However, directly optimizing retention is difficult because return signals are sparse, delayed, and only partially attributable to earlier recommendations. Prior work has addressed this challenge with sequential modeling and reinforcement learning, but these approaches typically require task specific reward engineering, substantial computational overhead, and surface specific implementations that are difficult to generalize. In this paper, we present a unified, model-agnostic downstream reward framework for optimizing long-term user value in large-scale recommendation systems. First, we formulate the downstream reward learning problem and develop an offline screening framework to identify session level behaviors that are both observable early and predictive of future retention. We then propose several model-agnostic downstream rewards signals derived from observed user action patterns across multiple sources. We further discuss the engineering effort to productionize the proposed rewards derivations and challenges we faced when adding them to our ranking models. Online A/B experiments demonstrate consistent improvements in engagement and retention-related metrics, and the framework has been deployed across multiple Pinterest surfaces, including Homefeed, Related Pins, Search, and Notifications.

</details>


### [107] [Augmentations for Robust and Efficient Imitation Learning in Streamed Video Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14200)
*Somjit Nath,Abdelhak Lemkhenter,Pallavi Choudhury,Chris Lovett,Katja Hofmann,Sergio Valcarcel Macua,Lukas Schäfer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Streaming augmentations that mimic bandwidth-related visual artifacts significantly improve imitation-learning agents in streaming 3D games, and reduce performance loss under lag, when used with predictive inverse dynamics models (PIDM).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address covariate shift from streaming-induced spatiotemporal artifacts and reduce data requirements for human demonstrations by making agents robust to realistic video degradations.

Method: Introduce four streaming augmentations (pixelation, scrubbing, global blur, ghosting) into training on top of predictive inverse dynamics models (PIDM) in a learned latent space; evaluate on three modern 3D game tasks.

Result: Under stable streaming, augmentation-trained agents achieve up to 41% higher evaluation performance for the same data budget. With network lag, augmentation-trained agents degrade by only 7.45% vs 49.82% for agents trained on original data.

Conclusion: Spatiotemporal streaming augmentations are a simple yet effective approach to train robust and data-efficient game-playing agents under realistic streaming conditions.

Abstract: Imitation learning is an appealing way to scale game-playing agents to complex 3D environments by training policies to map visual observations to actions from human demonstrations. However, these demonstrations are expensive to collect and modern game-playing is often done through streaming in which network delay and compression introduce spatiotemporally correlated visual artifacts that can cause a covariance shift at test time. To address these challenges, we propose streaming augmentations that mimic four types of artifacts commonly encountered during streaming with low-bandwidth network connection: pixelated blocks and scrubs, global blur, and ghosting. We instantiate our approach on top of predictive inverse dynamics models (PIDM), which combine future-state conditioning with an inverse dynamics policy in a learned latent space, and evaluate the impact of our augmentations across three tasks in modern 3D video games. Under stable streaming conditions, agents trained with spatiotemporal augmentations achieve up to 41% higher evaluation performance compared to agents trained without augmentations under an identical data budget. When network lag is introduced, agents trained with augmentations degrade by only 7.45% vs 49.82% of the original performance for agents trained only with the original data. These results clearly indicate that spatiotemporal augmentations tailored for the streaming setting are a simple yet powerful tool to train robust and efficient game-playing agents.

</details>


### [108] [Privacy Leakage in Federated Learning in Radiology Reports: A Comparative Evaluation of Tokenizer-Driven Privacy Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14205)
*Santhosh Parampottupadam,Andres Martinez,Dimitrios Bounias,Sinem Sav,Klaus Maier-Hein,Ralf Floca*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: In FL for radiology NLP, gradient inversion can reconstruct substantial text across GPT-2–style models with three tokenizers; RadBERT most vulnerable; tokenizer design affects leakage; privacy safeguards needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify gradient-based leakage in federated learning on radiology text and assess how tokenizer design affects privacy risk.

Method: Six FL clients trained a GPT-2-style transformer (sequence length 32) on public radiology corpora (368,751 diagnostic reports; 98,206 discharge summaries; 1,500 MIMIC-CXR free-text reports). Compared three tokenizers (GPT-2, RadBERT, LLaMA-2) at batch sizes 64, 128, 256. Assumed active malicious server that modifies the shared architecture; applied analytic gradient inversion; evaluated reconstruction fidelity over five runs. Metrics: exact sentence reconstruction (percentage), per-dataset accuracy (for batch 64 and 256), and S-BLEU (GPT-2, RadBERT, LLaMA-2).

Result: Exact sentence reconstruction ranged 31–44% across tokenizers (30.6–43.5% across 27 tokenizer×dataset×batch-size cells). On Discharge dataset at batch 64, accuracy: 42.1% (GPT-2), 42.3% (RadBERT), 39.4% (LLaMA-2); dropped to 37.3%, 37.2%, 34.3% at batch 256. S-BLEU declined with batch size (GPT-2: 0.44→0.33; RadBERT: 0.48→0.35). RadBERT yielded highest reconstruction fidelity and recovered the most clinical terms (18.1% of a 1,440-term reference vocab) vs 12.5% for GPT-2 and 9.4% for LLaMA-2. No tokenizer entirely prevented leakage. Large portions of reports are recoverable from FL gradients, even with domain-specific tokenizers.

Conclusion: Tokenizer design materially affects leakage severity; privacy is not guaranteed by architecture/domain-tokenization alone. Safeguards such as secure aggregation and differential privacy are necessary to meet HIPAA/GDPR in FL radiology NLP.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) enables multi-institutional training on clinical text without sharing raw data, but gradient inversion can reconstruct sensitive information from shared model updates. The extent of this leakage for radiology reports, and the role of tokenizer design, remains unclear. We quantify gradient-based text reconstruction in FL and compare privacy risk across three tokenizers with the model architecture held fixed. Six FL clients trained a GPT-2-style transformer (sequence length 32) on public radiology corpora (368,751 diagnostic reports, 98,206 discharge summaries, 1,500 MIMIC-CXR free-text reports) using the GPT-2, RadBERT, and LLaMA-2 tokenizers at batch sizes of 64, 128, and 256. Assuming an active malicious server that modifies the shared architecture before distribution, we applied analytic gradient inversion and measured reconstruction fidelity over five runs. Exact sentence reconstruction ranged from 31% to 44% across tokenizers (30.6-43.5% across the 27 tokenizer x dataset x batch-size cells). At batch size 64 on the Discharge dataset, accuracy was 42.1% (GPT-2), 42.3% (RadBERT), and 39.4% (LLaMA-2), decreasing to 37.3%, 37.2%, and 34.3% at batch size 256. S-BLEU declined as batch size grew (GPT-2: 0.44 to 0.33; RadBERT: 0.48 to 0.35). RadBERT yielded the highest reconstruction fidelity and recovered the most clinical terms (18.1% of a 1,440-term reference vocabulary, vs 12.5% for GPT-2 and 9.4% for LLaMA-2), yet no tokenizer prevented leakage. Substantial portions of report text are therefore recoverable from FL gradients even at larger batch sizes and with domain-specific tokenizers. Tokenizer design influences leakage severity and is a privacy-relevant decision, not only a utility one; safeguards such as secure aggregation and differential privacy are likely necessary to meet HIPAA and GDPR requirements for FL in radiology NLP.

</details>


### [109] [LIGO-PINN: Learned Initialization via Gated Optimization to Alleviate Convergence Failures in Physics Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14233)
*Nilay Anurag,Shital Adhikari,Taniya Kapoor,Nikhil Muralidhar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LIGO-PINN learns a data-driven, gated layerwise initialization to mitigate PINN convergence failures, improving performance over baselines on 1D/2D PDEs (including 2D fluid dynamics) and generalizing to 3D, with large reported gains and code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs suffer from convergence issues and poor generalization; initialization of weights is under-explored as a source of failures; addressing initialization could stabilise training and improve performance.

Method: Introduce Learned Initialization via Gated Layerwise Optimization (LIGO-PINN); train a network to output favorable initial weights using a gated, layerwise optimization scheme; evaluate on multiple PDE domains (1D and 2D, including fluid dynamics) and demonstrate 3D generalization; provide training dynamics analysis and code release.

Result: LIGO-PINN outperforms state-of-the-art methods for PINN failure mitigation; reported 91.5% average improvement across six baselines and 81% over the strongest baseline; demonstrates generalization to 3D unstructured domains; analysis offers insights into training dynamics.

Conclusion: Learned initialization via gated layerwise optimization effectively mitigates PINN convergence failures and enhances generalization, supported by empirical gains and cross-domain analyses; code is available for reproduction.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) have had a broad research impact in modeling domains governed by partial differential equations (PDE). However, PINNs have been shown to perform poorly, sometimes even converging to trivial solutions, in challenging PDE domains, or when generalizing to unseen but related PDE domains. Previously proposed solutions detail hyperparameter tuning to reduce loss imbalance between data-driven and physics guided losses, curriculum learning based training strategies, or dynamic re-sampling of hard collocation points. These methods face certain pitfalls: hyperparameter tuning is expensive, designing a training curriculum is ambiguous in multi-parameter PDE settings, and dynamic resampling still fails in complex PDE settings. Complementary to this line of thinking, we believe the initial PINN network weights also play a crucial role in the emergence of catastrophic failures during training, yet the effect of PINN weight initialization has been surprisingly under-investigated. To this end, we propose a framework for Learned Initialization via Gated Layerwise Optimization (LIGO-PINN) to overcome PINN convergence failures. Through rigorous evaluation on 1D and 2D PDE domains, including a challenging 2D fluid dynamics setting, we demonstrate that our methodology outperforms state-of-the-art methods designed to alleviate PINN failures, achieving a 91.5% average performance improvement across six baselines and 81% over the strongest baseline. We also verify that LIGO-PINN generalizes to 3D unstructured domains. Finally, we analyze training dynamics across all three PDE domains to explain both LIGO-PINN's improvement and the convergence failure of traditional PINNs. Code: https://github.com/scailab/ligo-pinn
  Keywords: Machine Learning, Physics-Informed Neural Networks, Deep Learning, PDE Modeling

</details>


### [110] [MIDiff: Tackling Sparsity and Imbalance in Mobile Usage Generation via Multivariate-Imaging Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14249)
*Yilai Liu,Shiyuan Zhang,Hongyang Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based framework (MIDiff) uses C-GASF imaging to convert sparse multivariate mobile-usage traces into correlation images, with Triple-Attention U-Net to preserve temporal and variable dependencies; achieves state-of-the-art fidelity (DA 0.1526 vs 0.3476 for ZITS-VAE).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address privacy-constrained mobile usage data challenges: sparsity from limited activity, heterogeneous variable types, and usage imbalance across apps.

Method: Diffusion in an imaging space defined by Cross-Gramian Angular Sum Field (C-GASF); transforms sequences into correlation images; employs a Triple-Attention U-Net to model temporal consistency and cross-variable dependencies.

Result: Outperforms baselines on fidelity metrics; DA=0.1526, with ZITS-VAE at 0.3476; code released at the provided GitHub link; claimed state-of-the-art.

Conclusion: MIDiff effectively generates realistic and diverse mobile usage traces under challenging data conditions.

Abstract: Mobile usage traces are critical for tasks such as user behavior prediction and app recommendation, yet their use is constrained by privacy restrictions and costly large-scale data collection. Although generative models perform well on general time series, their application to mobile usage data remains challenging because (i) limited user activity causes severe sparsity, (ii) heterogeneous variable types complicate joint modeling, and (iii) functional differences across apps create pronounced usage imbalance. To address these challenges, we propose Multivariate-Imaging Diffusion (MIDiff), a diffusion-based framework operating in an imaging space defined by Cross-Gramian Angular Sum Field (C-GASF). C-GASF transforms sparse multivariate sequences into correlation images, while MIDiff employs Triple Attention in a U-Net to preserve temporal consistency and variable dependencies. Experiments show that MIDiff achieves state-of-the-art performance across fidelity metrics. In particular, it obtains a Discriminative Accuracy (DA) of 0.1526, compared with 0.3476 for the strongest baseline, ZITS-VAE, demonstrating its effectiveness in generating realistic and diverse mobile usage traces. Our code is available at https://github.com/YilaiLiu-HKU/MIDiff.

</details>


### [111] [Local Additive Feature Attribution: A Mathematical Taxonomy and Reporting Checklist](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14271)
*Rebecca Afriyie Sarpong,Daniel Commey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Unified framework for local additive feature attribution that links Shapley, path-based, gradient/backprop, perturbation, and CAM-style methods through five specification choices, with an axiom-by-method matrix, a mapping of failure modes to assumptions, and a 10-item reporting checklist; emphasizes reporting underlying assumptions for meaningful attribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the fragmentation of local additive attribution methods by providing a common framework to compare underlying assumptions, diagnose failures, and improve reporting and interpretability.

Method: Proposes a common framework for local additive attribution organizing Shapley, path-based, gradient/backpropagation, perturbation, and CAM-style methods around five specification choices (value function, reference, path, perturbation distribution, and conservation rule); develops an axiom-by-method matrix; links failure modes to assumptions; introduces a ten-item reporting checklist for studies using local additive attributions.

Result: A unified theoretical framework mapping attribution methods to a shared set of specification choices, diagnosing failure modes through their assumptions, and offering a concrete reporting checklist to improve reproducibility and interpretability of local attribution analyses.

Conclusion: Attribution results are meaningful only relative to the mathematical assumptions under which they are defined; explicitly reporting these assumptions is essential for proper interpretation, comparison, and trust in local additive feature attributions.

Abstract: Feature-attribution methods are central to explainable artificial intelligence. Their assumptions are expressed in several mathematical languages: cooperative-game values, path integrals, gradient operators, perturbation distributions, and backpropagation rules. This survey proposes a common framework for local additive feature attribution. It organizes Shapley, path-based, gradient/backpropagation, perturbation, and CAM-style methods around five specification choices: value function, reference, path, perturbation distribution, and conservation rule. It then compares these methods through an axiom-by-method matrix and links common failure modes, including baseline sensitivity, off-manifold perturbations, sanity-check failures, adversarial manipulation, and method disagreement, to the assumptions that produce them. Finally, the survey proposes a ten-item reporting checklist for studies that use local additive attributions. The central message is that attribution results are meaningful only relative to the mathematical assumptions under which they are defined, and that those assumptions should be reported.

</details>


### [112] [Lyapunov Guidance: A Unified Framework for Stabilizing Generative Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14272)
*Jingdong Zhang,Xinze Li,Yize Jiang,Luan Yang,Minkai Xu,Junhong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LyaGuide presents a Lyapunov-based unified framework for post-training guidance of pretrained flow models, providing stability guarantees, theoretical unification of guidance strategies, and practical applicability across domains with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable stable, efficient post-training adaptation of pretrained flow models for new tasks without expensive retraining, addressing the lack of explicit stability guarantees in existing guidance methods.

Method: Formulate flow guidance as a Lyapunov control problem with a pseudo-projection operator that enforces the Lyapunov condition. Proves an equivalence between guided flow matching and Lyapunov control, unifying classifier, reward, and energy-based guidance. Supports model-driven (known Lyapunov function) and data-driven (task adaptation) settings, compatible with existing guidance methods, with low overhead.

Result: Theoretical equivalence between guided flow matching and Lyapunov control; introduction of a closed-form pseudo-projection operator ensuring stability; demonstration of improved sample quality, guidance fidelity, and robustness across synthetic, imaging, RL planning, and energy-based modeling tasks, with computational efficiency.

Conclusion: LyaGuide provides a practical, theory-backed framework that unifies diverse guidance strategies under Lyapunov control, enabling stable and efficient post-training adaptation of flow models across varied tasks.

Abstract: Flow matching has emerged as an effective framework for learning complex data distributions, but adapting pretrained flow models to new tasks often requires computationally expensive retraining. Post-training guidance provides a more efficient alternative, but existing methods are largely heuristic and offer no explicit stability guarantees. We address this limitation by proposing LyaGuide, a unified Lyapunov-guided framework that formulates flow guidance as a Lyapunov control problem. Our main theoretical result establishes an equivalence between guided flow matching and Lyapunov control, thereby unifying common guidance strategies, such as classifier guidance, reward guidance, and energy-based guidance, within a single control-theoretic framework. To enforce the Lyapunov condition, we introduce a pseudo-projection operator with a closed-form expression that endows learned or heuristic guidance terms with explicit stability guarantees. LyaGuide supports two practical settings: a model-driven setting, where the target guidance distribution is specified through a known Lyapunov function, and a data-driven setting, where the guidance is adapted from task-specific downstream data. LyaGuide is compatible with existing guidance methods, introduces minimal additional computational overhead, and is straightforward to integrate in practice. Extensive experiments on synthetic benchmarks, image inverse problems, reinforcement learning planning, and energy-based modeling demonstrate consistent improvements in sample quality, guidance fidelity, and robustness, while maintaining computational efficiency.

</details>


### [113] [NeuroGRIP: Retrieval-Augmented Graph Refinement for Knowledge-Grounded EEG Seizure Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14314)
*Lincan Li,Zheng Chen,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeuroGRIP is a retrieval-augmented graph refinement framework that uses a large medical knowledge graph extracted from guidelines to prune EEG-based STGNN graphs, improving seizure detection accuracy and interpretability; edges are scored by similarity to KG evidence via FAISS, enabling pruning of implausible connections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limited clinical plausibility and interpretability of purely data-driven STGNN EEG graphs by incorporating external medical knowledge as priors.

Method: Construct a domain-specific knowledge base from authoritative guidelines and extract structured entities/relations via large language models to form a textual knowledge graph. Align STGNN-generated EEG node embeddings with the KG semantic space; query the KG with FAISS-based similarity search to retrieve relation evidence; compute edge confidence from similarity, relation type, and source reliability; prune edges accordingly. Evaluate on TUSZ and CHB-MIT.

Result: NeuroGRIP improves seizure detection accuracy and interpretability by grounding predictions in clinically validated knowledge, demonstrated on TUSZ and CHB-MIT; code available at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: Presents the first unified retrieval-augmented framework that tightly couples brain dynamics with external medical expertise for explainable clinical seizure diagnosis, enabling knowledge-enhanced clinical decision support.

Abstract: Seizure diagnosis from EEG signals is a critical yet persistently challenging task, due to the complicated neural dynamics and the spurious connections in inter-channel modeling. While spatial-temporal graph neural networks (STGNNs) have advanced EEG brain network representation learning, the resulting graph structures suffer from low clinical plausibility and limited interpretability due to their purely data-driven nature. To this end, we introduce NeuroGRIP, a retrieval-augmented graph refinement framework that incorporates external medical knowledge to calibrate noisy EEG graphs. We first construct a large-scale, domain-specific knowledge base derived from authoritative clinical guidelines. Leveraging large language models, we extract structured biomedical entities and relations to form a textual knowledge graph (KG), which serves as external knowledge source of clinical priors. Our framework performs alignment-aware query construction by projecting STGNN-generated EEG node embeddings into the semantic space of KG. Semantic queries are then executed via FAISS-based similarity search over knowledge triplets to retrieve relation evidence. Each predicted edge is assigned a confidence score based on retrieved similarity, relation type, and source reliability, enabling us to prune medically implausible edges from the originally predicted graph. Extensive experiments on TUSZ and CHB-MIT demonstrate that NeuroGRIP not only improves seizure detection accuracy but also enhances interpretability by grounding each prediction in clinically validated knowledge. This work provides the first unified framework that tightly couples brain dynamics with external medical expertise via retrieval-augmented reasoning, paving the way for knowledge-enhanced, explainable clinical diagnosis. The code is available at: https://github.com/LincanLi-X/NeuroGRIP.

</details>


### [114] [Towards a Unified Multidimensional Explainability Metric: Evaluating Trustworthiness in AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14315)
*Georgios Makridis,Georgios Fatouros,Athanasios Kiourtis,Dimitrios Kotios,Vasileios Koukos,Dimosthenis Kyriazis,Jonh Soldatos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for evaluating and comparing XAI methods via a multidimensional explainability score, using fidelity, simplicity, and stability; builds an offline knowledge base to generalize to unseen models/datasets, validated on three open-source datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the lack of a unified, benchmark-driven measure for explainability in AI, enabling context-aware evaluation across models, datasets, and user expertise.

Method: Define three explainability dimensions (fidelity, simplicity, stability). Benchmark XAI methods (e.g., LIME, SHAP) across multiple datasets and models. Build an offline knowledge base recording explainability scores with metadata. Use the knowledge base to estimate scores for unseen datasets/models and guide selection. Demonstrate on three open-source datasets.

Result: Framework tested on three open datasets; insights into how dataset/model characteristics affect explainability; knowledge base enables estimation of explainability for new cases; provides a robust tool for comparing XAI methods and supporting transparent AI.

Conclusion: Contributes a versatile benchmarking framework and an inferential knowledge base for explainability, enabling context-aware evaluation and transferability; future work includes expanding methods, refining metrics, incorporating user-domain weighting, and scaling to larger ecosystems.

Abstract: In this paper, we present a comprehensive framework for assessing the explainability of various XAI methods, such as LIME and SHAP, across multiple datasets and machine learning models, with the ultimate goal of creating a unified multidimensional explainability score. Our methodology focuses on three key aspects of explainability: fidelity, simplicity, and stability. We leverage benchmarking experiments to systematically evaluate these aspects and use the insights gained to construct an offline knowledge base. This knowledge base captures the explainability scores for each registered model and serves as a valuable resource for context-dependent evaluation of explainability. By analyzing the complementary characteristics and metadata of AI models, datasets, and XAI methods, the knowledge base will enable the estimation of explainability scores for previously unseen datasets and models. Properties like fidelity, simplicity, and stability may vary significantly based on the dataset, underlying model, and domain expertise of the end user. We demonstrate our framework by applying it to three open-source datasets, discussing the implications of the obtained results in relation to the characteristics of the datasets. Our work contributes to the growing field of XAI by providing a robust and versatile tool for evaluating and comparing the explainability of various XAI methods, ultimately supporting the development of more transparent and trustworthy AI systems.

</details>


### [115] [Counterfactual Optimal Action Trees (COAT): Interpretable Prescriptive Policies from Observational Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14318)
*Youssef Drissi,Markus Ettl,Shivaram Subramanian,Wei Sun,Zack Xue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: COAT is a framework that learns interpretable prescriptive policies from observational data by combining counterfactual outcome estimation with a column-generation-based MILP to meet business and regulatory constraints; applied to airline ancillary pricing with substantial revenue gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for transparent, constraint-compliant prescriptive decisions derived from observational data, particularly in domains with complex business rules and limited experimentation; bridging causal predictions with feasible optimization.

Method: COAT integrates counterfactual outcome estimation (causal inference) with large-scale mixed-integer optimization using column generation to produce interpretable decision policies under constraints.

Result: In a 17-week field pilot with a major global airline, COAT raised upsell revenue per booking by 6.9% and is projected to generate $50–$150 million in incremental annual premium seat revenue across eligible domestic markets; led to scaled adoption and broader AI initiatives.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the practicality and impact of COAT for constrained prescriptive analytics in industry, validating the integration of causal learning with optimization for interpretable decisions.

Abstract: We introduce COAT (Counterfactual Optimal Action Tree), a framework for learning interpretable prescriptive policies from observational data. COAT combines counterfactual outcome estimation with large-scale mixed-integer optimization, using column generation to translate causal predictions into feasible, transparent decisions under business and regulatory constraints. We apply COAT to airline ancillary pricing, a setting characterized by complex business rules and limited experimental flexibility. In a 17-week field pilot with a major global airline, COAT increased upsell revenue per booking by 6.9%, with the airline projecting \$50-\$150 million in incremental annual premium seat revenue across eligible domestic markets. The success of the pilot led to scaled adoption and informed broader AI-driven decision initiatives within the organization.

</details>


### [116] [Value Leakage: An LLM's Answers Are Silently Shaped by Its Own Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14345)
*Jan Betley,Johannes Treutlein,Jan Dubiński,Harry Mayne,Karol Gałązka,Niels Warncke,Anna Sztyber-Betley,Owain Evans*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Models leak their own values in answers to practical questions, affecting content without disclosure. A suite of evaluations reveals value leakage across moral preferences, developer company, and leisure activity biases, with notable differences among frontier models; current alignment methods do not adequately address this distinct failure mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether language models expose or conceal the values that influence their answers, and to assess the risk this poses to users making practical, verifiable decisions.

Method: Introduce a suite of evaluations to quantify value leakage and disclosure. Compare models on tasks (e.g., a Fermi-estimation task and a hypothetical investment scenario) to identify biases and disclosure gaps. Analyze multiple frontier models (e.g., Claude Opus 4.8 and Qwen) to observe differences in bias and transparency. Distinguish value leakage from related failure modes like sycophancy and reward hacking.

Result: Demonstrates that models' outputs are influenced by different values (moral preferences, developer affiliation, leisure biases). Large cross-model variance observed; some models falsely claim unbiased reasoning while others expose bias. Many evaluations show minimal disclosure of underlying biases to users.

Conclusion: Value leakage is a distinct alignment failure separate from sycophancy or reward hacking, and current alignment training/evaluation strategies inadequately address it. This necessitates targeted measurement and mitigation of disclosed and undisclosed value influences in model outputs.

Abstract: People use language models for practical questions whose answers are difficult to verify. We show that models exhibit covert value leakage: the information they provide is influenced by their own values, without this influence being disclosed to the user.
  In one of our evaluations, the user is considering investing in an AI company and wants to know how likely the AI bubble is to pop. Claude Opus 4.8 gives a lower probability when the company under consideration is Anthropic rather than OpenAI. Yet Claude mostly fails to disclose this influence to the user.
  Covert value leakage is a form of misalignment because it goes against the user's preferences and is likely to mislead them. To investigate this phenomenon, we introduce a suite of evaluations to quantify value leakage and whether models disclose it. We find that models are influenced by different types of values, including preferences for morally good outcomes, for the company that developed them, and for some human leisure activities over others.
  We often observe large differences among frontier models on the same evaluation. For example, on a Fermi-estimation task, Claude models falsely claim to give unbiased answers in their chain-of-thought, while Qwen models explain how their values bias their answers. Value leakage is a failure mode distinct from sycophancy and reward hacking, and current alignment training and evaluations do not adequately address it.

</details>


### [117] [Learning Who to Treat When Treatment is Missing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14346)
*Johnna Sundberg,Rayid Ghani,Eli Ben-Michael,Edward Kennedy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends efficient estimators for ATE to policy value and CATE under MAR and MCCAR missing data, proving MAR-based estimators are valid and more efficient than MCCAR when MCCAR holds; emphasizes the importance of correctly specifying the missingness mechanism, with simulations showing near-oracle performance under correct assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Policy learning for treatment allocation under budget constraints is vulnerable to missing treatment data, which can bias estimates and degrade learned policies. The work seeks robust estimators under MAR and MCCAR to enable reliable policy learning.

Method: Develops MAR and MCCAR extensions of efficient ATE estimators for policy value and CATE estimation, conducts asymptotic efficiency analysis to compare MAR vs MCCAR, and performs synthetic/semi-synthetic experiments to assess bias and performance under correctly/misspecified missingness models.

Result: The MAR estimator is valid and more efficient than MCCAR when MCCAR holds; misspecified missingness models yield persistent bias; correctly specified MAR/MCCAR models achieve near-oracle performance in experiments.

Conclusion: Practitioners should prefer MAR-based estimation for policy learning with missing treatment data, provided MAR assumptions hold, as it offers efficiency gains and robustness over MCCAR; correct specification of the missingness mechanism is crucial.

Abstract: Policy learning methods are increasingly used to inform treatment allocation under budget constraints. Most proposed methods assume complete treatment data, yet applications frequently suffer from missingness that can bias estimates and lead to suboptimal policies. We address this gap by extending efficient estimators for average treatment effect (ATE) estimation to policy value and conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimation under missing at random (MAR) and missing completely conditionally at random (MCCAR) treatment data. Through asymptotic efficiency analysis, we prove that the MAR estimator, which leverages partially-observed units, is both valid and more efficient than the MCCAR estimator when MCCAR assumptions hold. This result provides formal justification for preferring MAR-based estimation in policy learning under both missing data settings. Our comprehensive experiments using synthetic and semi-synthetic datasets confirm that correctly specifying the missingness mechanism is crucial: misspecified estimators remain biased regardless of sample size, while our estimators achieve near-oracle performance when assumptions are satisfied. Our work provides practitioners with theoretically grounded, empirically validated tools for robust policy learning in the presence of missing treatment data.

</details>


### [118] [Dysco: Dynamic Subspace Boosting to Mitigate LoRA Interference in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14367)
*Haobo Zhang,Jiankun Wang,Suraj Rajendran,Weishen Pan,Lam Tsoi,Yong Chen,Fei Wang,Jiayu Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dysco introduces Dynamic Subspace Boosting for federated LoRA, allocating client-specific subspaces to mitigate data-parameter interference and improve aggregation stability and FL performance with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous clients cause instability in LoRA aggregation due to data-parameter interference; FL aggregation should be viewed as subspace allocation.

Method: Clients compute activation-insensitive subspaces from local data and transmit the resulting bases; the server merges subspaces via a closed-form solution to maximize compatibility with insensitive directions; multi-round subspace boosting to handle representation drift; theoretical convergence bound with interference modeled as an aggregation error term; server-fixed merged subspaces tighten the bound.

Result: Empirical gains on synthetic tasks and MIMIC-IV with Llama-3.2-1B: up to 9x reduction in final-round loss vs baselines under orthogonal-subspace partition; up to 4.3% improvement across five FL algorithms on MIMIC; outperforms recent federated LoRA methods; 0.9% wall-clock overhead; code available.

Conclusion: Dysco provides a principled, efficient approach to mitigating data-parameter interference via dynamic subspace allocation, yielding theoretical guarantees and practical performance improvements in federated fine-tuning of LLMs.

Abstract: Federated fine-tuning of large pre-trained models increasingly relies on Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to reduce communication and computation, but heterogeneous clients can make adapter aggregation unstable. We identify the data-parameter interference as a geometric source of this instability. This interference is controlled by the alignment between LoRA update subspaces and client activations, suggesting that federated LoRA aggregation should be viewed not only as parameter averaging but also as subspace allocation. We propose Dynamic Subspace Boosting (Dysco), a plug-in method that allocates client-specific LoRA subspaces in a federated and dynamic manner. In each round, clients compute activation-insensitive subspaces from local representations and transmit only the resulting bases; the server then constructs client-specific merged subspaces through a closed-form solution that maximizes compatibility with other clients' insensitive directions. To handle representation drift, Dysco performs multi-round subspace boosting to preserve past update directions while adapting to future representations. We provide a convergence analysis that embeds the data-parameter interference as an aggregation-error term in a standard federated optimization bound, and prove that Dysco's server-fixed merged subspaces yield a tighter upper bound on this error. Experiments on controlled synthetic federated tasks and on MIMIC-IV clinical-note classification with Llama-3.2-1B show that Dysco substantially reduces interference, reduces the final-round synthetic training loss by up to 9 times relative to baselines under the orthogonal-subspace partition the theory identifies, improves all five tested FL algorithms by up to 4.3% on MIMIC, outperforms recent federated LoRA methods, and adds only 0.9% wall-clock overhead. Our code is available at https://github.com/illidanlab/Dysco.

</details>


### [119] [Supervised Fine-Tuning vs. In-Context Learning: An Equilibrium Analysis of LLM Personalization under Congestion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14371)
*Fengzhuo Zhang,Zhuoran Yang,Dirk Bergemann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ICL and SFT dominate in different regimes; congestion can flip the better choice; broader platform offerings increase profits; empirical validation with GPT-2 supports theory; real-world trend shows growing adoption of both methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how personalization methods (SFT vs ICL) interact with resource constraints and user congestion in LLM serving, and how platforms should design offerings.

Method: Develop a tractable statistical-economic framework incorporating pretraining coverage, data signal-to-noise, and user congestion; derive regime conditions, non-monotonic congestion effects, and profit implications; validate with GPT-2 experiments on linear regression tasks; survey 21 AI platforms to track trend in offering both methods.

Result: The framework shows ICL and SFT dominate in different regimes; congestion can reverse preferences; equilibrium congestion is non-monotonic; offering both personalization methods never reduces the platform’s maximal profits; GPT-2 experiments corroborate predictions; 2021–2025 survey shows share offering both methods rising from 9.5% to 71.4%.

Conclusion: Platforms benefit from offering multiple personalization algorithms; design implications include accounting for congestion effects and pretraining characteristics; the observed market trend aligns with the theory.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have revolutionized AI services, but a critical tension emerges: while personalization improves model performance, it consumes scarce computational resources that users must share. When should a user invest in expensive Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) versus lightweight In-Context Learning (ICL)? How does congestion from other users' personalization choices reshape these incentives? And what strategies should platforms adopt when offering multiple personalization algorithms?
  We develop a tractable framework for LLM serving that captures the statistical-economic trade-offs users face. Our analysis yields several surprising insights. First, we show that ICL and SFT dominate in different regimes, determined by an interplay between pretraining coverage and data signal-to-noise ratios, but congestion can flip these rankings. Second, equilibrium resource consumption exhibits pronounced non-monotonicity: improving pretraining precision reduces the congestion, while broader pretraining coverage and harder tasks sometimes increase it. Third, we prove that offering both personalization methods never hurts the platform's maximal profits, despite potentially increasing computational load.
  Experiments with GPT-2 on linear regression tasks validate our theoretical predictions about algorithm performance. Complementing these results, our review of documentation from 21 major AI platforms shows that the share offering both SFT and ICL increased from 9.5% in 2021 to 71.4% in 2025, consistent with our platform-design implications.

</details>


### [120] [A Noise-Robust Elicit-to-Optimize Framework for Distortion Riskmetrics via Inverse Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14373)
*Yang Liu,Yuhao Liu,Yunran Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A noise-robust, risk-aware learning framework that jointly elicits agents' distortion risk preferences and optimizes policies under distortion riskmetrics using adaptive Bayesian IRL for elicitation and a model-free PPO-based RL augmented with quantile networks for optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address real-world decision making under risk where observations are noisy and actions are stochastic or suboptimal, and to unify optimization under a broad class of distortion riskmetrics within a single learning framework.

Method: Elicitation: adaptive Bayesian IRL with a finite set of distinguishing questions to identify the preferred distortion riskmetric from a candidate class; proves a finite-identifiability result and derives a convergence rate O(exp(-c m + O(sqrt(m log m)))). Optimization: model-free RL that represents the distortion-risk objective as an integral of the conditional cost quantile function with respect to the distortion function; extends Proximal Policy Optimization with policy, value, and quantile networks; the quantile network estimates the full conditional cost quantile function to evaluate general risk objectives.

Result: The approach achieves accurate elicitation of latent risk objectives under noisy and suboptimal actions and demonstrates effective policy optimization across distortion riskmetrics in complex financial environments; empirical studies validate elicitation accuracy and policy performance.

Conclusion: The framework provides a unified, robust approach for risk-aware decision making, enabling accurate risk preference elicitation and versatile optimization under a broad class of distortion riskmetrics, with theoretical guarantees on identifiability and convergence and practical efficacy in financial tasks.

Abstract: We propose a noise-robust elicit-to-optimize framework that integrates inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) and reinforcement learning (RL) for eliciting agents' risk preferences and optimizing policies under a broad class of risk objectives characterized by distortion riskmetrics. On the elicitation side, we propose an adaptive Bayesian IRL method that infers agents' latent risk objectives from their noisy observed decisions, explicitly allowing agents to take stochastic and suboptimal actions. We establish the existence of a finite set of distinguishing questions that identifies the preferred distortion riskmetric within the candidate class and prove that the convergence rate of the algorithm is of order $O(\exp(-cm+O(\sqrt{m\log m})))$ under general settings, where $c>0$ is a constant and $m$ denotes the number of algorithm iterations. On the optimization side, we develop a model-free RL algorithm for optimizing policies under conditional distortion riskmetrics. By representing the objective as an integral of the conditional cost quantile function with respect to the distortion function, the method unifies distortion-riskmetric objectives. We optimize diverse risk objectives by extending the Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) algorithm with policy, value, and quantile neural networks, where the quantile network estimates the full conditional cost quantile function and enables numerical evaluation of general risk objectives. A comprehensive empirical study demonstrates the framework's elicitation accuracy and effectiveness in complex financial environments.

</details>


### [121] [Integration Matters: Rollout-Based Training for Constrained Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14398)
*Xiaoxuan Liang,Saeid Naderiparizi,Berend Zwartsenberg,Frank Wood*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fine-tuning diffusion-model framework uses online rollout–based constraint guidance to align training with sampling, improving constraint satisfaction while preserving sampling quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constrained generation must satisfy feasibility constraints without deviating from the data distribution. Training-time optimization often operates on states not representative of sampling, while sampling-time corrections introduce distribution shift and tuning overhead, especially for few-step sampling.

Method: Introduce constraint guidance from online rollout into training by differentiating through the fixed noise schedule in the denoising process. This exposes the model to constraint-violation trajectories and aligns diffusion learning with the sampling process.

Result: Empirical results across multiple tasks show improved constraint satisfaction with competitive sampling quality relative to prior constrained-generation methods.

Conclusion: The approach reduces train–inference mismatch in constrained diffusion, yielding better feasibility without sacrificing sampling fidelity, and offers a practical alternative to existing training-time or sampling-time strategies.

Abstract: Constrained generative models aim to produce samples that satisfy complex feasibility constraints while remaining faithful to the data distribution. Existing constrained generation methods typically enforce constraints either through training-time optimization or sampling-time correction. Training-time optimization approaches optimize on states induced by the training distribution, which can differ substantially from those encountered during sampling. Sampling-time correction methods instead modify the sampling process at inference, introducing distribution shift and requiring expensive tuning, particularly for few-step sampling. We propose a fine-tuning framework that incorporates constraint guidance obtained through online rollout into the training process, which aligns training with sampling by differentiating through the fixed noise schedule used to numerically integrate the denoising process. This exposes the model to violations that arise along the denoising trajectory and aligns diffusion learning with the sampling process. Experiments across multiple tasks show that our method improves constraint satisfaction while maintaining competitive sampling quality compared to prior methods.

</details>


### [122] [LATTICE: Graph Self-Supervised Learning for Multimodal Spatial Omics Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14410)
*Jagan Mohan Reddy Dwarampudi,Veena Kochat,Suresh Satpati,Kunal Rai,Tania Banerjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LATTICE is a graph-based, self-supervised framework that integrates five spatial-omics modalities per Visium spot to learn unified spot representations, improving cross-modal concordance and spatial metrics; though gains vary by modality and strong supervision/benchmarking is needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome single-modality downstream analyses in spatial omics by leveraging complementary transcriptomic and epigenomic signals within a unified graph representation, enabling improved tissue-level insights.

Method: Construct a spatial neighbor graph over spots; encode harmonized multimodal features with a TransformerConv encoder trained via masked reconstruction, cross-modal alignment, and spatial-smoothness objectives; integrate Visium RNA, scMultiome RNA/ATAC, spatial ATAC, and spatial CUT&Tag; evaluate on melanoma cohort across 11 samples.

Result: Stable optimization and reproducible embeddings; adding scMultiome RNA to Visium RNA substantially improves concordance with Space Ranger clusters (ARI +0.157, NMI +0.143, contiguity +0.174); additional modalities further improve contiguity and MUS but can reduce RNA-label agreement, suggesting embeddings capture regulatory structure beyond transcriptomics.

Conclusion: LATTICE offers a practical, empirically-grounded multimodal spatial-omics integration framework, but it requires stronger supervision and broader external benchmarking to fully validate cross-modal benefits.

Abstract: Spatially resolved omics studies increasingly combine transcriptomic and epigenomic assays, yet downstream analysis is often still performed using single-modality pipelines. We present LATTICE (Latent Alignment of Tissue-level and Transcriptomic Information for Cross-modal Embedding), a graph-based self-supervised framework that learns spot-level representations from harmonized multimodal features. LATTICE integrates five aligned modality blocks per Visium spot: Visium RNA, scMultiome RNA, scMultiome ATAC, spatial ATAC, and spatial CUT\&Tag. These modalities capture spatial transcriptomic measurements, single-cell inferred regulatory activity, and in situ chromatin and histone states within a unified lattice representation. LATTICE constructs a spatial neighborhood graph and trains a TransformerConv encoder using masked reconstruction, cross-modal alignment, and spatial smoothness objectives. On a private 11-sample melanoma cohort from an anonymized clinical collaborator comprising 54{,}912 total spots, LATTICE demonstrated stable optimization behavior, reproducible embeddings across analysis seeds, and complete multimodal integration across all samples. Adding scMultiome RNA to Visium RNA alone substantially improved concordance with Space Ranger clusters across 11 runs (adjusted Rand index [ARI] +0.157, normalized mutual information [NMI] +0.143, and spatial contiguity +0.174). Additional modalities further improved spatial contiguity and multimodal utility score (MUS), although they sometimes reduced agreement with RNA-derived reference labels, likely because the learned embeddings captured chromatin and regulatory structure beyond transcriptomic similarity alone. These results position LATTICE as a practical and empirically grounded framework for multimodal spatial omics integration, while also highlighting the need for stronger supervision and broader external benchmarking.

</details>


### [123] [Adaptive Ad Load Design for Sponsored Search Markets: Evidence, Theory, and Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14418)
*Mohammad Rashid,Hema Yoganarasimhan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive ad-load policy via e-LAAL improves the revenue-conversion trade-off in sponsored search; large-scale experiments show revenue gains from higher ad load but modest declines in conversions/engagement, with heterogeneity across queries; production deployment confirms improvement and provides a finite-time regret guarantee.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how to optimize ad-load in sponsored search to maximize revenue while preserving user outcomes, accounting for heterogeneity in query quality and advertiser composition; provide a principled, adaptive policy with performance guarantees.

Method: Empirical analysis of a large randomized field experiment with 5+ million users and 1–6 sponsored slots; analysis of revenue, conversions, and engagement; development of exploration-augmented Locally Adaptive Ad Load (e-LAAL) combining LAAL with fixed exploration arms; theoretical finite-time dynamic-regret guarantee; production deployment serving 22.3M users and 77.6M searches.

Result: Increasing ad load raises revenue by up to 43% but reduces total search conversions by up to 5% and daily engagement by up to 2.2%. Substantial heterogeneity: high-ad-conversion queries gain revenue, low-conversion queries gain little or negative revenue. Advertiser composition (e.g., brand advertisers) shifts the trade-off. e-LAAL improves the revenue-conversion trade-off vs static benchmarks; outperforms uniform and historical query-dependent baselines in production.

Conclusion: Adaptive, exploration-enabled, model-free ad-load policies can navigate the revenue–outcome trade-off effectively, with theoretical guarantees and practical gains in large-scale deployments; supports adopting adaptive ad-load decisions in platform-level settings.

Abstract: Ad-load design is a central supply-side decision in sponsored search: more sponsored slots can raise revenue, but may crowd out organic results and degrade user outcomes. We study this trade-off using a large-scale randomized field experiment on an Android app store, where over five million users are exposed to one through six sponsored slots. Increasing ad load raises revenue by up to 43%, but reduces total search conversions by up to 5% and daily engagement by up to 2.2%. These average effects mask substantial heterogeneity: additional slots generate large revenue gains for high-ad-conversion queries, but little or negative marginal revenue for low-conversion queries. The trade-off also shifts within query as advertiser composition changes, such as brand-advertiser presence. Motivated by these findings, we design and deploy a novel adaptive algorithm -- exploration-augmented Locally Adaptive Ad Load (e-LAAL). e-LAAL combines LAAL, a model-free query-level decision rule that updates ad-load recommendations using recent outcomes, with static exploration arms that maintain support and provide fixed-policy counterfactual benchmarks. We provide a finite-time dynamic-regret guarantee for the e-LAAL architecture. In a platform-level production deployment serving 22.3 million users and 77.6 million searches, e-LAAL improves the empirical revenue--conversion trade-off relative to deployed static benchmarks and outperforms uniform and historical query-dependent static benchmarks.

</details>


### [124] [Active Real-World Factor-Based Evaluation for Generalist Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14439)
*Andrew Liao,Hanchen Cui,Karthik Desingh,Aryan Deshwal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Active evaluation via Bayesian sequential design improves efficiency in evaluating generalist robot manipulation policies across diverse task factors by adaptively selecting test configurations, achieving substantial trial savings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world evaluation of generalist manipulation policies is combinatorially hard and hardware testing is slow; narrow test suites can miss critical failure modes and misrepresent readiness.

Method: Fit a probabilistic surrogate model over a structured space of task factors and use adaptive, information-theoretic experimental design to select evaluation configurations that maximize information gain about policy performance; apply to real-world tests to characterize behavior and locate failure-prone regions.

Result: Performed 2,331 real-world evaluations over 3 tasks with 3 factor variations; typically saved 20–40% of trials compared with random testing.

Conclusion: The framework enables sample-efficient, systematic assessment of policy performance across unseen conditions and helps identify failure-prone regions, improving deployment readiness for generalist manipulation policies.

Abstract: Generalist robot manipulation policies trained on large, diverse datasets have shown remarkable promise across a wide range of tasks. However, rigorously evaluating these policies remains a fundamental challenge. Real-world performance depends on a large combinatorial space of task factors including object poses and camera viewpoints, making full, exhaustive evaluation intractable. Additionally, real hardware evaluation is slow and resource-intensive, so current practice is to use narrow test suites that can miss critical failure modes and misrepresent true deployment readiness. We propose an active evaluation framework that addresses this challenge by treating policy evaluation as a sequential experimental design problem. Our approach fits a probabilistic surrogate model over a structured space of task factors and adaptively selects evaluation configurations to maximize information gain over the policy's performance distribution, allowing for sample-efficient characterization of policy behavior across unseen conditions and a systematic identification of failure-prone regions. We conduct 2331 real-world evaluations across 3 tasks with 3 factor variations and find that our approach typically saves the evaluator at least 20-40% of trials compared to typical random testing.

</details>


### [125] [Depth-Dependent Hidden-State Collapse in Dynamical System Autoencoders for LiDAR Point-Cloud Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14463)
*Patricia Medina,Hy P. G. Lam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Large-depth (K=5) dynamical system autoencoders (DSAEs) for LiDAR classification exhibit a hidden-state collapse, with near-constant representations that do not preserve class separation; Product Coefficient augmentations do not prevent collapse; all classifiers achieve identical macro F1 (~0.225).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand failure modes of DSAE-based LiDAR classification, specifically how hidden-state dynamics and depth affect class separability, and evaluate Product Coefficient augmentations.

Method: Train DSAEs with encoder depths K=1..5 on LiDAR xyz and xyz+Product Coefficient inputs; assess hidden representations using Random Forest, k-Nearest Neighbors, and a majority-class Dummy baseline; analyze hidden-state statistics (variance, scatter); provide theoretical bound relating between-class scatter to total hidden scatter; compare performance with and without Product Coefficients.
