Result: At K=5, hidden-state standard deviation drops to ~1e-5; all classifiers reach macro F1 = 0.224688; Feature augmentations via Product Coefficients do not improve pre-collapse macro F1 nor prevent collapse at K=5; between-class hidden scatter is bounded by total scatter and thus by the tiny variance, implying loss of class-separating information; large-depth representation collapse identified as a concrete failure mode for DSAE LiDAR classification.

Conclusion: Large-depth (K=5) hidden-state collapse undermines DSAE effectiveness for LiDAR classification; Product Coefficients offer no remedy in this setting; avoid excessive depth or employ methods to preserve hidden-state variability/regularization to maintain class separability.

Abstract: We study Dynamical System Autoencoders (DSAE) for LiDAR point-cloud classification using spatial coordinates and Product Coefficient feature augmentations. The experiments compare separately trained DSAE architectures at encoder depths $K=1,\ldots,5$ and evaluate the resulting hidden representations with Random Forest, kNN, and a majority-class Dummy baseline. The main finding is a hidden-state collapse at $K=5$. For both xyz and xyz plus Product Coefficient inputs, the hidden-state standard deviation falls to the order of $10^{-5}$, while all three classifiers attain the same macro F1 score of $0.224688$. We prove that between-class hidden scatter is bounded by total hidden scatter, which in turn is controlled by the reported hidden-state variance. Thus a nearly constant hidden representation cannot retain substantial class-separating structure. Product Coefficients neither improve pre-collapse macro F1 nor prevent the $K=5$ collapse in the present DSAE setting. These results identify large-depth representation collapse as a concrete failure mode for DSAE LiDAR classification.

</details>


### [126] [Interleaved Noise Injection Improves Clean, Corrupted, and OOD Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14466)
*Matt L. Wiemann,Peter Melchior,Andrew K. Saydjari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Interleaved noise injection (on-off-on-off) with gradient-norm stabilization improves robustness and exploration, acting as a Jacobian regularizer with impulse noise and a curvature penalty with Gaussian noise; achieves strong corruption- and distribution-shift robustness on CNN/ViT with zero extra compute.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of monotonic-decay noise schedules by exploiting alternating clean/noisy data phases to enhance exploration, prevent catastrophic forgetting, and explain robustness improvements.

Method: Propose an interleaved on-off-on-off noise schedule combining impulse (Jacobian-regularizing) and Gaussian (curvature-penalizing) noise; introduce gradient-norm stabilization scaling noisy updates by clean-gradient magnitudes; evaluate on CIFAR-100-C, ImageNet-C, ImageNet-R with ResNet and ViT; compare with standard augmentations and analyze via saliency/attention maps.

Result: Noise injection with interleaved schedule yields substantial improvements in corruption tolerance and real-world distribution shift robustness; best results when stacked with other augmentations; impulse noise mitigates locality bias in CNNs, Gaussian noise reduces large-scale spurious features in attention-based models; zero additional computational cost.

Conclusion: Interleaved noise injection is an effective, low-cost augmentation to improve test performance across clean, noisy, and OOD data by combining regularization effects (Jacobian and curvature) and stabilizing updates during data-switching; supported by empirical results on multiple benchmarks and model families.

Abstract: Noise injection is a well-known technique in stochastic optimization. We report its surprising effectiveness with an interleaved (on-off-on-off...) rather than the usual monotonic decay schedule. We present a theoretical analysis of noise injection, which confirms that corruption by impulse noise approximates a Jacobian regularization, whereas Gaussian noise acts as a curvature penalty. This regularization behavior has been invoked to explain why noise injection increases model robustness. But the interleaved nature of our proposed schedule produces superior results even for the optimization objective: mixing phases of noisy data permits the optimizer to escape local minima and increase exploration without the risk of catastrophically forgetting the important features from the clean data. To stabilize this training scheme against the rapid changes of the loss when switching between clean and noisy data, we introduce a gradient-norm stabilization technique that scales noisy updates based on clean gradient magnitudes. We compare this method with other common augmentation methods and find substantial improvements in corruption tolerance and robustness to real-world distribution shifts on CIFAR-100-C, ImageNet-C, and ImageNet-R for ResNet and ViT architectures, with the best results being achieved by stacking our method on top of other augmentations. Through saliency and attention maps we show that the effect of interleaved noise injection stems from penalizing the failure modes encouraged by the inductive bias of the models: impulse noise works against the locality bias of convolutional (ResNet) architectures, and Gaussian noise reduces the tendency of attention-based models to pick up large-scale spurious features. Interleaved noise injection is therefore an effective tool to improve the test performance on clean, noisy, and out-of-distribution data at essentially zero computational cost.

</details>


### [127] [Non-vacuous Generalization Bounds for Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14506)
*Yuxuan Zhu,Rohan Alur,Daniel Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Non-vacuous generalization bounds for parameter-efficient RLVR fine-tuning at billion-parameter scale; uses PAC-Bayes compression and Gumbel-max for token sampling; introduces Progressive RLVR with on-policy distillation, TinyLoRA, and quantization; achieves large compression with minimal performance loss and non-vacuous bounds across multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify and guarantee generalization for RLVR-tuned LLMs at scale, addressing stochastic token generation and aggressive parameter efficiency, where prior bounds were vacuous.

Method: Adapt PAC-Bayes compression bounds to RLVR; apply Gumbel-max reparameterization to handle token-generation randomness; implement Progressive RLVR by combining RLVR with on-policy distillation, TinyLoRA, and model quantization.

Result: Retains 84-97% of standard LoRA fine-tuning performance; 14,796x additional compression; non-vacuous generalization bounds demonstrated in math problem solving, programming, general knowledge reasoning, and Text-to-SQL; bounds exceed base model accuracy by 9-51% and are within 6-11% of accuracy of fully fine-tuned models.

Conclusion: Progressive RLVR delivers non-vacuous, scalable generalization guarantees while achieving substantial compression with minimal accuracy loss across diverse domains.

Abstract: While reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is widely used to improve the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), the generalizability of the resulting models remains poorly understood. In this work, we establish the first non-vacuous generalization bounds for parameter-efficient RLVR fine-tuning at the billion-parameter scale. Our approach adapts PAC-Bayes compression bounds to this setting, and addresses the inherent stochasticity of token generation by applying the Gumbel-max reparameterization trick. To operationalize these bounds, we propose the Progressive RLVR framework, which integrates RLVR with on-policy distillation, TinyLoRA, and model quantization. Progressive RLVR empirically retains 84-97% performance of standard LoRA fine-tuning while producing models that are 14,796x more compressible. We show that this framework yields non-vacuous generalization bounds in four domains: mathematical problem-solving, programming, general-knowledge reasoning, and Text-to-SQL. Our bounds exceed the accuracy of the base model by 9-51% and lie within 6-11% of the accuracy of the fine-tuned models.

</details>


### [128] [Adaptive Runge-Kutta Step Control Buys Training Loss, Not Generalization: An Honest Compute-Matched Study of RK-Adam Optimizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14516)
*Akhilesh Gogikar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Higher-order Runge–Kutta variants for optimizers do not outperform well-tuned first-order Adam under a fair compute budget; adaptive control is illusory and yields little practical benefit, whereas derived fixes (true map rejection) reveal modest gains with fragile dependence on init and no test accuracy improvement. Gradient averaging acts as a genuine regularizer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether interpreting optimizers as gradient-flow discretizations and applying higher-order RK integrators yields practical performance gains for neural network optimization under a strict, compute-matched budget.

Method: Developed a Bogacki–Shampine 3(2) RK variant of Adam (FSAL reuse, local-error control). Compared against Adam under a compute-parity protocol; instrumented to diagnose adaptivity; explored fixed vs adaptive step behavior, 0–100x tolerances, and various step-size schemes; performed a pre-registered follow-up to test deeper minimization, temperature knobs, and an n=10 replication; contrasted with RMSprop, NAdam, and AdamW.

Result: Under equal gradient-evaluation budgets, the RK variant does not beat plain Adam in training loss. Adaptivity is illusory: normalized error remains well below tolerance, steps h churn at cap, and different rtol/hmax/h0 settings yield bit-identical trajectories. The method is effectively fixed-step Adam with 3–4x cost for gradient averaging. Repairing the map (true reject branch) reverses the full-batch result, achieving ~40x lower training loss than tuned Adam, with fixed-step control revealing an emergent warmup-and-growth pattern. However, gains are fragile to initial step size and do not translate to test accuracy. Follow-up rules out obvious explanations: deeper minimization does not overfit; a temperature knob hurts; trajectory effects persist with the controller selecting a modest generalization gap. An n=10 study confirms gradient averaging yields a genuine implicit regularizer (beating lr-matched Adam/AdamW on 10/10 seeds), while RMSprop and NAdam match or exceed it at lower per-step cost. Higher-order adaptive integration yields deeper deterministic minimization and small regularization, but nothing cheaper, well-tuned first-order baselines do not already provide.

Conclusion: Higher-order adaptive integration offers deeper minimization and mild regularization but at substantial cost with limited generalization gains. A robust, cheaper first-order baseline (e.g., well-tuned Adam variants with gradient averaging/RMSprop-like schemes) provides comparable or superior performance; the observed gains from adaptive RK are fragile and not a reliable path to superior neural-network optimization.

Abstract: Interpreting optimizers as gradient-flow discretizations has motivated applying higher-order Runge-Kutta (RK) integrators to neural networks. We build a representative Adam variant (Bogacki-Shampine 3(2) RK pair, FSAL reuse, local-error step control) and evaluate it under a strict compute-matched protocol giving every method the same gradient-evaluation budget - an accounting this literature rarely enforces. Under it the RK variant loses to plain Adam on training loss in both minibatch and full-batch (RK's best-case) training. Instrumenting it shows the "adaptivity" is illusory: normalized error stays far below tolerance, the step size pins at its growth cap from step one (98-100 percent of steps), and no rtol x hmax x h0 setting makes it act; tolerances spanning 100x give bit-identical trajectories. The method is exactly fixed-step Adam with an averaged gradient at 3-4x cost. Repairing it (true reject branch; error on the applied map) reverses the full-batch result - about 40x lower training loss than tuned Adam - and a fixed-step control isolates adaptivity (an emergent warmup-and-growth schedule) as the mechanism. But the gain is fragile to the initial step size and does not reach test accuracy. A pre-registered follow-up rules out the obvious explanations: deeper minimization does not overfit, and an explicit temperature knob only hurts - leaving a trajectory effect, the controller selecting a minimum generalizing 1.3-3.4 points below first-order descent at equal depth. An n=10 study confirms one secondary effect: gradient averaging is a genuine implicit regularizer, beating lr-matched Adam and AdamW on 10/10 seeds - yet RMSprop and NAdam match or beat it at a third the per-step cost. Higher-order adaptive integration buys deeper deterministic minimization and a small regularization effect, but nothing a cheaper, well-tuned first-order baseline does not already provide.

</details>


### [129] [A Continuous-Time Reinforcement Learning Framework for Fine-Tuning Discrete Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14522)
*Zikun Zhang,Jiayuan Sheng,David D. Yao,Wenpin Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A continuous-time RL framework for discrete-state spaces via controlled CTMCs, yielding continuous-time policy gradient methods and continuous-time variants of PPO and GRPO; applied to fine-tuning score-based discrete diffusion models with support for intermediate rewards; for masked diffusion models (MDMs) it provides tractable policy ratios over the vocabulary simplex; introduces trajectory subsampling to estimate trajectory likelihoods in large language model settings; demonstrated on low-dimensional entropy-regularized problems and RL post-training of dLLMs on math reasoning and coding tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge reinforcement learning with stochastic control in continuous time for discrete states and broad action spaces; enable reward-driven optimization when rewards are non-differentiable or only available along trajectories; unify policy optimization for diffusion-models (including MDMs) and enable efficient RL-based fine-tuning of large language models.

Method: Formulate dynamics as a controlled continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC) with policy control; derive policy gradient methods and continuous-time analogs of PPO and GRPO; build a complete continuous-time RL framework for score-based discrete diffusion models allowing intermediate rewards; in the MDM case, parameterize policies over the vocabulary simplex with analytically tractable probability ratios; propose trajectory subsampling to approximate trajectory likelihoods in dLLMs.

Result: A reward-driven optimization framework that operates without differentiable rewards and can incorporate intermediate reward signals along trajectories; extends GRPO with a continuous-time, CTMC-based perspective and provides a unified policy-optimization view for MDMs; enables efficient likelihood estimation via trajectory subsampling in large dLLMs; validated on simple entropy-regularized tasks and on RL post-training of dLLMs for mathematical reasoning and coding tasks.

Conclusion: The framework generalizes GRPO and extends PPO to continuous-time, CTMC-based RL for discrete spaces, with direct applicability to diffusion-model fine-tuning and dLLMs. It offers a unified, efficient approach for exploration and policy optimization in MDMs and supports intermediate rewards, broadening the scope of trajectory-based RL in discrete diffusion settings.

Abstract: We formulate reinforcement learning (RL) in continuous time with discrete state spaces and possibly arbitrary action spaces via a stochastic control approach, where the state dynamics are modeled as a controlled continuous-time Markov chain (CTMC). We consider policy optimization problems and derive the corresponding policy gradient methods, leading to continuous-time variants of proximal policy optimization (PPO) and group relative policy optimization (GRPO). As a primary application, we develop a complete continuous-time RL framework for fine-tuning score-based discrete diffusion models. The proposed framework enables reward-driven optimization without requiring differentiability on the reward signals. In contrast to the existing GRPO-based approaches that only rely on terminal rewards, our formulation allows intermediate reward or advantage signals to be incorporated throughout the denoising trajectory. Importantly, when specialized to masked diffusion models (MDMs), our framework encompasses a rich class of policy parameterizations over the vocabulary simplex with analytically tractable probability ratios, providing a unified perspective on exploration and policy optimization in MDMs. For masked diffusion large language models (dLLMs), we further propose trajectory subsampling techniques to efficiently estimate computationally prohibitive trajectory likelihoods, reducing the computational cost of computing per-position probability ratios. We showcase the effectiveness of our methods on both low-dimensional entropy-regularized optimization problems and RL post-training of dLLMs on mathematical reasoning and coding tasks.

</details>


### [130] [xHC: Expanded Hyper-Connections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14530)
*Xiangdong Zhang,Xiaohan Qin,Sunan Zou,Tuo Dai,Xiaoming Shi,Huaijin Wu,Yebin Yang,Zhuo Xia,Shaofeng Zhang,Lin Yao,Yuliang Liu,Yu Cheng,Junchi Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Expands Transformer residual streams via xHC and xHC-Flash to scale beyond N=4, addressing write-back information and residual-mixing cost bottlenecks, achieving consistent improvements on 18B/28B MoE models with reduced memory traffic and favorable compute scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend residual-stream expansion as a scaling axis; large gains from N up to 4 but diminishing returns and increased cost beyond; enable scalable large-N pre-training.

Method: Introduce xHC: temporal feature augmentation for richer write-back; sparse residual updates updating only k of N streams (k=4 of N=16) while maintaining dense access to full residual; pair with xHC-Flash to reduce memory traffic; evaluate on 18B/28B MoE models; perform scaling-law analysis.

Result: xHC yields +4.0 average downstream score over mHC on an 18B MoE model; modest training FLOPs increase; vanilla and mHC require 1.50x and 1.19x compute to reach the same loss as xHC; xHC-Flash reduces per-sublayer memory traffic from 73.5C to 40C, near mHC's 34C at N=4, while retaining xHC gains.

Conclusion: xHC and xHC-Flash render large-N residual-stream expansion practical for LLM pre-training, mitigating bottlenecks and improving efficiency, with consistent downstream gains across large MoE models.

Abstract: Hyper-Connections (HC) expand the residual stream of Transformers into $N$ parallel streams, providing a form of memory scaling beyond model width and depth. Manifold-Constrained HC (mHC) stabilizes this formulation at scale. The large gains from $N{=}1$ to $N{=}4$ suggest residual-stream expansion as a promising scaling axis. However, existing HC-family methods typically stop at $N{=}4$. Our experiments reveal why: scaling mHC beyond this point yields diminishing performance gains and rapidly increasing training cost. We attribute this limitation to two bottlenecks: insufficient write-back information for an expanding number of streams and residual-mixing generation whose cost scales cubically with $N$. To address both bottlenecks, we propose xHC (Expanded Hyper-Connections), the first HC-family method to achieve meaningful expansion beyond $N{=}4$. xHC combines temporal feature augmentation for richer write-back with a sparse residual-stream architecture that updates only $k=4$ of the $N=16$ streams while retaining dense access to the full residual state. Across 18B and 28B MoE models, xHC delivers strong and consistent downstream improvements. On an 18B MoE model, xHC improves the average downstream score by 4.0 points over mHC, while adding only modest training FLOPs over the vanilla baseline. Scaling-law experiments show that the vanilla and mHC require $1.50\times$ and $1.19\times$ the compute of xHC, respectively, to reach the same loss. Practical large-$N$ training also requires controlling memory traffic from the expanded residual state. We therefore introduce xHC-Flash, which reduces the per-sublayer memory traffic from $73.5C$ to $40C$, comparable to the $34C$ required by mHC at $N{=}4$, while retaining the gains of full xHC. Together, xHC and xHC-Flash make large-$N$ residual-stream expansion effective and practical for LLM pre-training.

</details>


### [131] [Muse: Representation Geometry of Muon Beyond Normalized Momentum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14536)
*Da Chang,Qiankun Shi,Lvgang Zhang,Di He,Yaoshuai Ma,Ganzhao Yuan,Yongxiang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This work studies how the parameter-block representation in Muon-style optimizers shapes the optimization geometry; it introduces a family of optimizers with a shared momentum rule and Newton–Schulz backend across multiple representations, analyzes how the shorter matrix dimension governs singular-channel support and convergence bounds, and corroborates findings with LLaMA2 pretraining experiments showing balanced non-native representations can match native performance while shrinking dimension degrades scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand optimizer geometry: how representation choice acts as a geometry that affects update dynamics; connect to polar steepest-descent geometry and spectral properties.

Method: Propose a unified Muon-style optimizer family, named here as the method, applying identical momentum rule and Newton–Schulz backend across native, nearest-square, skinny, and vector representations; derive Frobenius-isometric representations and their induced polar geometries; analyze a teacher–student model with curvature collapse and isotropic Marchenko–Pastur spectral profile; perform pretraining experiments on LLaMA2-130M and LLaMA2-600M with fixed-momentum diagnostics.

Result: Each representation induces a distinct polar steepest-descent geometry; the shorter matrix dimension determines the number of supported singular channels, the pullback scaling, and constants in stochastic nonconvex convergence bounds; balanced non-native representations can match the native representation performance; reducing the shorter dimension weakens scaling and singular-channel support, causing behavior to resemble normalized momentum.

Conclusion: Representation geometry is a key determinant of Muon-style optimizer performance; balancing representations can preserve performance and scaling without sacrificing momentum-based behavior; dimension constraints reveal trade-offs between singular-channel capacity, pullback scaling, and convergence bounds, supported by empirical pretraining results.

Abstract: Muon-style optimizers apply a polar map to matrix momentum, but their updates also depend on the representation of each parameter block before orthogonalization. We study this representation choice as a form of optimizer geometry and introduce {\method}, a family of Muon-style optimizers that shares the same momentum rule and Newton--Schulz backend across native, nearest-square, skinny, and vector representations. Each Frobenius-isometric representation induces a distinct polar steepest-descent geometry, in which the shorter matrix dimension determines the number of supported singular channels, the pullback scaling, and the constants in stochastic nonconvex convergence bounds. In a teacher--student model, curvature collapse and an isotropic Marchenko--Pastur spectral profile connect early-stage dissipation to the represented nuclear-to-squared-Frobenius norm ratio. Pretraining experiments on LLaMA2-130M and LLaMA2-600M, together with fixed-momentum diagnostics, show that balanced non-native representations can match the performance of the native representation, whereas reducing the shorter dimension weakens the scaling and singular-channel support, leading to behavior that increasingly resembles normalized momentum.

</details>


### [132] [CASP: Learning-Augmented Offline Approximation with Verifiable Certificates and Bounded-Loss PAC Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14545)
*Haifeng Li,Mo Hai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CASP turns ML predictions into verifiable pruning certificates for NP-hard optimization, enabling sound, learnable pruning despite imperfect predictions; verifiable deployment preserves optimal performance under distribution shift where unverified pruning degrades.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To retain worst-case guarantees when using machine-learned predictions for combinatorial optimization by tying predictions to verifiable certificates rather than direct decisions.

Method: Introduce Certificate-Augmented Solution Pruning (CASP) with a polynomial-time verifier that checks pruning certificates; develop learning theory for the verifier-based loss; show that the induced loss class is uniformly bounded and learnable with tilde O(epsilon^{-2} log K) samples; compare against the unverified commitment class which has no distribution-free rate under cost spread R; propose verifiable confidence filtering and analyze its margin; examine LP degeneracy and the role of symmetry.

Result: The verifier-based loss class is learnable with near-optimal sample complexity; unverified pruning lacks distribution-free rates and can incur up to Omega(R/epsilon^2) error under cost spread; verifiable filtering outperforms the standard min-combiner; the prediction remains beneficial even with LP, resolving degeneracy issues where symmetric LP policies stall; experiments on five problems corroborate that trained predictors yield no loss under verification, while unverified pruning can lose up to 26% under distribution shift.

Conclusion: CASP delivers sound, prediction-augmented pruning for NP-hard optimization with solid learning-theoretic guarantees and practical robustness, enabling safe ML-assisted optimization via certificates and verifiable checks.

Abstract: Machine-learned predictions can speed up offline NP-hard optimization, but asking a predictor what to do amounts to asking it to solve the problem, and committing an unchecked prediction forfeits every worst-case guarantee. CASP (Certificate-Augmented Solution Pruning) instead asks which parts of the search space may be ignored, and accepts each answer only after a sound polynomial-time verifier has checked it, so correctness never depends on prediction quality. We develop the learning theory of this design. The verifier makes the induced loss class uniformly bounded, so certificate parameters are learnable from $\tilde O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log K)$ samples ($K$ the maximum instance size), whereas the unverified commitment class admits no distribution-free rate and, under cost spread $R$, none below $Ω(R/\varepsilon^2)$. Filtering noisy predictions by verifiable confidence dominates the standard min-combiner, with a margin we compute in closed form, and the prediction stays useful even given the LP, because it breaks ties on degenerate optimal faces, where every symmetric LP policy, meaning one whose commitments depend on the instance only through the verifiable confidence values, provably stalls. Experiments on five problems test the theory's quantitative predictions. With trained predictors, unverified pruning loses up to $26%$ of the optimum under distribution shift, while the verified deployment of the same predictions loses nothing.

</details>


### [133] [Probabilistic Physics-Informed Neural Networks for Estimating Heterogeneous Elastic Properties from Low-Resolution and Noisy Displacement Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14563)
*Tatthapong Srikitrungruang,Jaesung Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PIE-PINN: a probabilistic physics-informed neural network for robustly estimating Young's modulus and Poisson's ratio from noisy, low-resolution displacement data using Laplace likelihoods, a B-spline displacement network, and a hierarchical half-Cauchy scale model with alternating ML training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Estimating spatially heterogeneous elastic properties from low-resolution displacement measurements is highly ill-posed. Small perturbations or fitting errors are amplified, and prior methods rely on high-fidelity data and hand-tuned loss weights, reducing robustness to noise and resolution degradation.

Method: A Probabilistic Inverse Elasticity Physics-Informed Neural Network (PIE-PINN) that uses Laplace distributions for observation, strain-discrepancy, and equilibrium residuals within a unified probabilistic framework. It combines a B-spline-guided displacement network with a hierarchical half-Cauchy model for displacement residual scales. The B-spline yields a smooth global representation; a neural network corrects local variations. An alternating maximum-likelihood training updates the mean by weighted residual minimization and updates the scales to adjust loss weights, enabling adaptive robustness.

Result: Systematic case studies across varying noise levels and observation resolutions show PIE-PINN is robust to noise and resolution degradation, effectively recovering the latent mean displacement field and enabling robust estimation of material properties.

Conclusion: PIE-PINN advances robust, data-efficient estimation of elastic properties from undersampled data by integrating probabilistic modeling with physics-informed neural networks and adaptive loss weighting; it is applicable to ill-posed PDE-inverse problems beyond elasticity.

Abstract: Estimating spatially heterogeneous elastic properties from low-resolution displacement measurements is a severely ill-posed inverse elasticity problem because low resolution obscures spatial details needed to distinguish heterogeneous property variations, and small measurement perturbations or fitting errors are amplified through inverse estimation. Existing inverse methods often rely on high-fidelity observations and manually prespecified loss weights, limiting their adaptability and making them sensitive to noise and resolution degradation. We propose a Probabilistic Inverse Elasticity Physics-Informed Neural Network (PIE-PINN) framework for robust estimation of Young's modulus and Poisson's ratio from noisy, low-resolution displacement data. PIE-PINN models displacement observation, strain-discrepancy, and equilibrium residuals using Laplace distributions within a unified probabilistic model. To improve robustness, the framework combines a B-spline-guided displacement network with a hierarchical half-Cauchy model for displacement residual scales. The B-spline provides a smooth global representation of the displacement field, while the neural network correction captures local variations. The hierarchical scale model adaptively downweights severe displacement fitting errors, enabling more robust recovery of the latent mean displacement field. An alternating maximum-likelihood training strategy updates the mean through weighted residual minimization and updates the scales to adjust the loss weights. Systematic case studies across varying noise levels and observation resolutions demonstrate the robustness of PIE-PINN.

</details>


### [134] [Gate-Zero Growth: A Geometric Framework for Function-Preserving Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14571)
*Dante Lok*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces gate-zero growth, a function-preserving operator that adds zero-initialized gates to residual blocks to enable safe capacity growth in continual learning; induces rank separation in the Jacobian and small, controlled drift; demonstrates near-zero old-domain forgetting on a large Transformer and generalizes to LoRA, ReZero, and zero-init adapters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable continual learning without degrading previously learned functions by designing a geometry-aware operator that preserves function while expanding capacity, ensuring minimal interference with old directions.

Method: Proposes gate-zero growth with a zero-initialized gate to add new residual blocks; relies on a transversality condition to produce rank separation in the functional Jacobian, where old directions remain fixed, new weight directions are flat at growth, and new gate directions drive new variation; analyzes function drift O(||α||^2) and Jacobian leakage O(||α||∞); evaluates on a Transformer scaled from 300M to 857M parameters trained from WikiText-103 to BookCorpus, under Isolation and Freeze-Nothing operating points, comparing to a non-FP control G_stack; extends the geometric framework to LoRA, ReZero, and zero-init adapters.

Result: Gate-zero growth yields near-zero old-domain forgetting (Δ_A < 0.1) under both FP operating points, while the non-FP baseline accumulates substantially more forgetting; the approach is claimed to be a canonical instance of a shared local geometry governing safe capacity activation in continual learning.

Conclusion: Gate-zero growth reveals a universal local geometric mechanism for safely expanding model capacity in continual learning, with empirical validation on large-scale transformers and demonstrated applicability to related adapter-based strategies; it positions function-preserving growth as a principled framework for mitigating forgetting while enabling new capacity.

Abstract: We introduce \emph{gate-zero growth}, a function-preserving (FP) operator for continual learning that adds new residual blocks through a zero-initialised gate. Under a transversality condition, gate-zero growth induces \emph{rank separation} in the functional Jacobian: old directions are unchanged, new-weight directions are exactly flat at the growth point, and new gate directions are the only first-order source of new functional variation. As gates open during continual learning, function drift is $O(\|\boldsymbolα\|^2)$ and Jacobian leakage $O(\|\boldsymbolα\|_\infty)$, giving a controlled departure from the FP locus. On a $300\mathrm{M}\to857\mathrm{M}$ Transformer adapted from WikiText-103 to BookCorpus, gate-zero growth reaches near-zero old-domain forgetting ($Δ_A < 0.1$) under both exact-preservation (Isolation) and joint-frontier (Freeze-Nothing) operating points, while a non-FP control ($G_{\text{stack}}$) suffers an order-of-magnitude larger forgetting under the same recipe. The same geometric analysis covers LoRA, ReZero, and zero-init adapter constructions, establishing gate-zero growth as the canonical instance of a shared local geometry that governs safe capacity activation in CL.

</details>


### [135] [Sharp Stability Threshold and Certification for Designing Stable Residual Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14576)
*Hyemin Gu,Michael Tyrrell,Tuhin Sahai,Markos A. Katsoulakis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A sharp stability threshold for deep residual networks: sublinear-growth (q ≤ 1) of the velocity field guarantees stable training; two independent arguments establish necessity and sufficiency; the approach informs architectural design and can replace ad hoc normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensure stable training and inference in deep residual architectures by controlling the growth of velocity fields with input magnitude, addressing blow-up in training dynamics.

Method: (1) ODE-theoretic global existence for q ≤ 1 and blow-up for q > 1; (2) optimal-control analysis using the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation showing bang-bang optimum on the boundary, making q > 1 unstable and q ≤ 1 stable; (3) algebraic framework for tracking input-magnitude exponents under residual-block operations; (4) parameter-free modification to reduce a supercritical block from q = 5 to q = 1 without layer normalization; (5) empirical validation on Mamba and PatchTST.

Result: q ≤ 1 is a necessary and sufficient condition for training stability; sublinear-growth velocity fields form the appropriate function space for forward dynamics and adjoint sensitivity; provides a practical certification method for architectural primitives; normalization is not essential for stability if q ≤ 1; demonstrates successful stabilization of challenging blocks by exponent control.

Conclusion: The conjectured exponent criterion q ≤ 1 is central to stable training/inference of residual networks, guiding architectural design via exponent arithmetic rather than trial-and-error normalization, with empirical support on specific architectures.

Abstract: We propose \emph{the sublinear-growth principle} for deep residual architectures -- a sharp stability threshold on the input-magnitude exponent of every residual block's velocity field: $$\|v(x, t)\| \leq c\,\|x\|^q + b, \qquad q \in [0, 1].$$ The threshold $q = 1$ is established via two independent arguments. Classical ODE theory gives a global forward flow on $[0, T]$ at $q \le 1$ and exhibits divergent velocity fields at any $q > 1$. The optimal-control analysis, via the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation, sharpens this to a selection statement: the training optimum is bang-bang on the boundary of the admissible class, so the optimum at $q > 1$ blows up while the optimum at $q \le 1$ is safe by construction. The exponent criterion $q \le 1$ is thereby a necessary and sufficient condition for stable training. It clarifies architectural placements that ensure the stability of training and inference, explaining, for instance, the stabilizing role of layer normalization. The sublinear-growth velocity fields form \emph{the right function space} on which forward dynamics, adjoint sensitivity, and architectural composition are all well-controlled. An arithmetic of input-magnitude exponents under the five operations that build residual blocks enables efficient certification of $q_k \le 1$ at the level of architectural primitives, in place of ad hoc trial and error in the search for stable neural architectural designs. A parameter-free modification reduces the supercritical Mamba block from $q = 5$ to $q = 1$ without layer normalization, demonstrating this point. Experiments on Mamba and PatchTST confirm that the $q \le 1$ variants train stably: the criterion is the input-magnitude exponent, not the presence of a normalization layer.

</details>


### [136] [Accelerating A/B-Tests with Counterfactual Estimation: Reducing Variance through Policy Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14604)
*Olivier Jeunen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Delta-Off-Policy Estimation with a meta-policy framing reduces variance in online experiments by exploiting action overlap between policies, improving efficiency over standard A/B tests, with variance depending on policy divergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard A/B testing wastes samples when treatment and control choices coincide; need for faster, more powerful experiments by leveraging structural relations between policies.

Method: Treat randomized assignment as a meta-policy; apply Δ-Off-Policy Estimation to estimate average treatment effects unbiasedly; show variance scales with policy divergence; prove agreement with standard A/B testing in general; derive conditions for dominance over Difference-in-Means.

Result: Analytical proofs establishing variance behavior; dominance over DI-Mean with overlap; empirical evidence in recommender systems, IR pipelines, and LLM interfaces.

Conclusion: Policy overlap-based experiment design yields faster, more reliable A/B testing; variance reduction hinges on overlap structure; applicable to real-world large-scale platforms.

Abstract: Online controlled experiments are the gold standard for hypothesis testing in online platforms. Notwithstanding their ubiquity, they are notoriously expensive to run, and issues of variance hamper statistical power in assessing treatment effects. While standard variance reduction techniques leverage model-based control variates to reduce outcome noise, they remain agnostic to potential structural relationships between competing policies.
  In this work, we identify a critical inefficiency in the standard A/B-testing protocol: when a treatment and control policy agree on an action, the resulting outcome contributes noise but no signal regarding the treatment effect -- unnecessarily inflating confidence intervals. We propose a novel experimental protocol that exploits this policy overlap to accelerate experimentation. The key insight is to frame the randomised treatment assignment mechanism as a meta-policy, and leverage $Δ$-Off-Policy Estimation methods to obtain unbiased estimates for average treatment effects. We prove analytically that our approach recovers standard A/B-testing practices in the general case, but that its variance scales with the divergence between policies rather than raw outcome variance. Hence, we dominate the standard Difference-in-Means estimator whenever policies have common support, and the improvement is strict whenever the overlap region contributes non-zero residual variance. Empirical results corroborate these theoretical insights -- holding promise for significant impact on the real-world evaluation of recommender systems, information retrieval pipelines, and large language model interfaces.

</details>


### [137] [Auditing Fairness-Privacy Trade-offs: Subpopulation-Level Effects of Fairness-Enhancing Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14607)
*Umid Suleymanov,Ilhama Novruzova,Khalid Mammadov,Natavan Hasanova,Murat Kantarcioglu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fairness interventions do not uniformly affect privacy leakage at the subpopulation level; DP interacts with fairness in a non-uniform way, varying by model, subgroup size, and mitigation strategy. A unified subpopulation-level auditing framework is proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap where the impact of fairness-enhancing algorithms on privacy leakage (the inverse of privacy→fairness studies) has not been systematically studied, especially at subpopulation granularity.

Method: Adapt Likelihood Ratio Attack (LiRA) for subgroup auditing; empirically analyze interaction between fairness methods and Differential Privacy across subpopulations; examine effects of model architecture, subgroup size, and mitigation strategies; develop a unified empirical auditing framework.

Result: Fairness interventions do not uniformly increase privacy risk; privacy leakage disparities emerge across subpopulations; DP’s privacy benefits and utility costs are unevenly distributed; results depend on architecture, subgroup size, and mitigation strategy; aggregate evaluations obscure these disparities.

Conclusion: Fairness, privacy, and utility must be jointly evaluated at the subpopulation level; the paper provides the first unified empirical framework to support such auditing in practice.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) models deployed in sensitive domains such as healthcare, law enforcement, and finance must satisfy not only utility requirements but also fairness and privacy guarantees. While prior work has largely examined how privacy-preserving techniques affect fairness, the inverse question-how fairness-enhancing algorithms influence privacy leakage-remains underexplored. We present the first comprehensive study of how fairness interventions affect membership inference privacy risks at the subpopulation level. By adapting the Likelihood Ratio Attack (LiRA) for subgroup auditing, we uncover privacy disparities that aggregate evaluations obscure. We further analyze how Differential Privacy (DP) interacts with fairness-enhancing methods across different categories, showing that DP's privacy benefits and utility costs are unevenly distributed across subpopulations. Our results demonstrate that fairness interventions do not uniformly increase privacy risk; their impact depends on model architecture, subgroup size, and mitigation strategy. These findings reveal that fairness, privacy, and utility must be jointly evaluated at the subpopulation level, and we introduce the first unified empirical framework to support such auditing in practice.

</details>


### [138] [Angular Gaussian Supervised Contrastive Learning for Long-Tailed Electrocardiogram Arrhythmia Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14613)
*Jin Dai,Qiuzhen Zhang,Chenyun Dai,Danmei Lan,Can Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AG-SCL uses angular Gaussian contrastive learning with adaptive logit adjustment and tail-aware augmentation to tackle long-tailed, multi-label ECG diagnosis, achieving state-of-the-art macro performance on PTB-XL and Noc-ECG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-tailed ECG datasets with rare arrhythmias and morphology variability reduce diagnostic reliability. Existing rebalancing and logit-margin methods focus on class frequency and often ignore direction-dependent morphological differences across ECG classes, necessitating representation learning and calibration that reflect full class uncertainty.

Method: Proposes Angular Gaussian Supervised Contrastive Learning (AG-SCL) with three components: (1) Angular Gaussian contrastive branch modeling full-covariance class uncertainty on unit-normalized embeddings; (2) Adaptive Logit Adjustment that learns bounded, label-state-specific prior corrections instead of fixed frequency-based margins; (3) tail-aware augmentation that preserves morphology while generating views, notably protecting the 7–25 Hz QRS-dominant band.

Result: On PTB-XL: balanced accuracy 0.838, sensitivity 0.709, specificity 0.968, mean average precision 0.495, TPR at 5% FPR 0.778. On Noc-ECG: balanced accuracy 0.918, sensitivity 0.889, specificity 0.947, mean average precision 0.488, TPR at 5% FPR 0.900. AG-SCL yielded the best macro-level performance on both datasets, with largest gains in rare or morphologically unstable rhythms; ablations confirmed the contributions of full-covariance modeling, Adaptive Logit Adjustment, and tail-aware augmentation.

Conclusion: AG-SCL enhances long-tailed ECG diagnosis by combining calibrated priors with anisotropic representation learning, increasing sensitivity to rare arrhythmias while preserving clinically relevant specificity. Code is available at the provided GitHub repository.

Abstract: Long-tailed label distributions reduce the reliability of deep learning for electrocardiogram (ECG) arrhythmia diagnosis, particularly for clinically important but rare abnormalities. Existing rebalancing and logit adjustment methods mainly address class frequency while overlooking direction-dependent morphological variability across ECG classes. This study proposes Angular Gaussian Supervised Contrastive Learning (AG-SCL) for long-tailed multi-label ECG diagnosis. AG-SCL integrates three components into a unified framework: an Angular Gaussian contrastive branch that models full-covariance class uncertainty on unit-normalized embeddings, Adaptive Logit Adjustment that learns bounded label-state-specific prior corrections instead of fixed frequency-based margins, and tail-aware augmentation that generates morphology-preserving views while protecting the 7-25 Hz QRS-dominant band. The method was evaluated on the public PTB-XL benchmark and a nocturnal ECG dataset comprising 1317 hours of recordings from 141 subjects. AG-SCL achieved the best macro-level performance on both datasets. On PTB-XL, it obtained a balanced accuracy of 0.838, sensitivity of 0.709, specificity of 0.968, mean average precision of 0.495, and TPR at 5% FPR of 0.778. On Noc-ECG, the corresponding values were 0.918, 0.889, 0.947, 0.488, and 0.900. The largest gains occurred in rare or morphologically unstable rhythm classes, while ablation studies confirmed the contributions of full-covariance modelling, Adaptive Logit Adjustment, and tail-aware augmentation. AG-SCL improves long-tailed ECG diagnosis by combining prior calibration with anisotropic representation learning, enhancing sensitivity to rare arrhythmias while maintaining clinically relevant specificity. Our code is available at: https://github.com/Open-EXG/AG-SCL-for-Long-Tailed-ECG.

</details>


### [139] [Beyond Entropy: Correctness-Aware Advantage Shaping via Contrastive Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14614)
*Weiwen Xu,Jia Liu,Hou Pong Chan,Long Li,Deng Cai,Min Chen,Hao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CPO replaces entropy-based advantage shaping with a token-level contrastive disagreement signal between reference-guided and vanilla policies, improving correctness signaling and generalization; On-policy Distillation is a special case; it addresses zero-advantage and boosts RLVR performance in-domain and out-of-domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Entropy-based RLVR cannot distinguish helpful uncertainty from harmful confusion, leading to suboptimal correctness signals. A token-level, contrastive approach aims to provide a more precise, discriminative signal for policy improvement by comparing a reference-guided distribution against the current policy.

Method: Introduce Contrastive Policy Optimization (CPO) using token-level contrastive disagreement between a reference-guided distribution and the vanilla generation distribution to shape advantages in a correctness-aware manner. The framework encompasses On-policy Distillation as a special case where the posterior comes from an external teacher model; proposes a solution to the zero-advantage problem.

Result: Theoretical analysis and empirical experiments show that token-level disagreement is a reliable indicator of token-level correctness. CPO outperforms entropy-based RLVR methods on both in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks while preserving strong generalization. Correct responses enhance exploration and incorrect responses steer exploitation; balancing both yields best performance.

Conclusion: CPO provides a more effective and general correctness signal than entropy for RL with verifiable rewards, resolves zero-advantage issues, and generalizes well across domains. It unifies On-policy Distillation within its framework and highlights the complementary roles of correct/incorrect signals in guiding exploration and exploitation.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) commonly uses entropy for advantage shaping. However, entropy cannot distinguish useful uncertainty from detrimental confusion, limiting its effectiveness as a correctness signal. We propose Contrastive Policy Optimization (CPO), which uses token-level contrastive disagreement between reference-guided and vanilla generation distributions for correctness-aware advantage shaping. Both theoretical and empirical results show that this disagreement reliably indicates token-level correctness. We further show that On-policy Distillation is a special case of CPO, where the posterior distribution is instantiated by an external teacher model. CPO also resolves the zero-advantage problem. Experiments on in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks demonstrate that CPO substantially outperforms entropy-based RLVR methods while maintaining strong generalization. Further analysis shows that correct and incorrect responses naturally support exploration and exploitation respectively, and balancing both leads to the best performance.

</details>


### [140] [PolyQ: Codesigning End-to-End Quantization Framework for Scalable Edge CPU LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14618)
*Hyunwoo Oh,Suyeon Jang,Hanning Chen,KyungIn Nam,Sanggeon Yun,Ryozo Masukawa,Mohsen Imani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PolyQ is a CPU-oriented compiler/quantization co-design that enables activation-aware channel-wise fractional-bit quantization (bit-widths in {2,3,4,8,16}) with bit-homogeneous blocks and SIMD/LUT-compatible kernels, turning fine-grained budget fitting into practical CPU-only on-device LLM inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need practical, energy-efficient on-device LLM inference on CPUs with fine-grained or activation-aware quantization; existing methods offer coarse points or difficult mixed-precision on CPUs.

Method: Per-channel bit allocation from {2,3,4,8,16}, activation-aware clustering into bit-homogeneous blocks, a compile-time model compiler to permute/cluster channels and generate SIMD- and LUT-compatible kernels, and to merge compatible permutations across operators to keep layout regularization off the runtime path.

Result: Quality scales with 3–6 bits; perplexity improved by 2.4–32.1% over prior methods at 3-bit target; end-to-end measurements across workstation, laptop, and mobile show up to 70.8% reduction in activation reorder traffic; prefill latency and decode throughput scale with bit budget; energy/token overhead <2% relative to a LUT-based backend.

Conclusion: Fractional-bit CPU deployment is practical, predictable, and energy-efficient across diverse edge targets, demonstrating viable CPU-only inference for large LLMs through activation-aware, compiler-assisted quantization.

Abstract: CPUs are the most universal target for on-device LLM inference, but existing low-bit quantization methods offer either coarse operating points or fine-grained mixed precision that is difficult to execute efficiently on CPUs. We present PolyQ, a CPU-oriented compiler/quantization co-design for activation-aware channel-wise bit allocation under a user-specified average-bit budget. PolyQ assigns per-channel bit-widths from $\{2,3,4,8,16\}$, then uses a compile-time model compiler to permute and cluster channels into bit-homogeneous blocks, generate SIMD- and LUT-compatible kernels, and merge compatible permutations across operators to keep layout regularization off the runtime path. This turns fine-grained budget fitting into a practical fractional-bit deployment method for CPU-only inference. Across Falcon-H1-3B, Llama2-13B, and Qwen3-32B on WikiText-2, PolyQ provides stable quality scaling from 3--6\,b and improves perplexity by 2.4--32.1\% over prior methods at a 3\,b target. End-to-end measurements on three representative CPUs -- workstation, laptop, and mobile -- show that compiler layout regularization reduces activation reorder traffic by up to 70.8\%, prefill latency and decode throughput scale nearly proportionally with the configured bit budget, and energy/token overhead stays below 2\% relative to an optimized LUT-based back-end. These results show that fractional-bit CPU deployment is practical, predictable, and energy-efficient across diverse edge targets.

</details>


### [141] [TIDE: Trustworthy and Interpretable Battery Degradation Estimation with Contextual Learning and Symbolic Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14640)
*Wen Yang Tan,Jiawei Li,Fang Liu,Wei Zhang,Sumei Sun,Peng Cheng Wang,Elisa Y. M. Ang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TIDE is a three-component backbone for trustworthy and interpretable battery health estimation, combining knowledge-guided priors, monotone residual refinement, and contextual learning, with symbolic distillation. It achieves ~19.7% accuracy gain over baselines and reduces aging-consistency violations, enabling practical, interpretable battery health monitoring in intelligent connected systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In intelligent connected systems, battery-health estimation errors can propagate across devices and decisions, necessitating accurate, trustworthy, and interpretable estimates for robust operation.

Method: A three-component backbone: (1) knowledge-guided degradation prior to promote trustworthiness; (2) monotone residual component for aging-consistent, interpretable refinement; (3) contextual learning to model battery-specific operational effects. The backbone is distilled into a compact symbolic surrogate for model-level interpretability.

Result: TIDE yields strong estimation accuracy with an average improvement of 19.7% over representative baselines. The knowledge-guided prior and monotone residual reduce aging-consistency violations. The backbone provides component-level interpretability, while symbolic distillation offers a concise model-level interpretation.

Conclusion: TIDE supports practical battery health monitoring and decision support in intelligent connected systems by delivering accurate, trustworthy, and interpretable degradation estimation.

Abstract: Battery health estimation is fundamental for battery management in battery-powered systems, where inaccurate health states may affect control, maintenance, and service life. It becomes even more critical in intelligent connected systems, where estimation errors can propagate across interconnected devices and downstream decisions. In this paper, we propose TIDE, a trustworthy and interpretable battery degradation estimator for reliable battery health estimation. TIDE jointly considers accuracy, trustworthiness, and interpretability, which are all essential for practical deployment and downstream decision making. To realize these objectives, TIDE combines battery-domain knowledge with operational measurements in a three-component backbone. A knowledge-guided degradation prior promotes trustworthy estimation, a monotone residual component provides interpretable aging-consistent refinement, and a contextual learning component captures battery-specific operational effects for improved accuracy. The trained backbone is then distilled into a compact symbolic surrogate that provides a concise model-level interpretation of its learned estimation logic. Experiments show that TIDE achieves strong estimation accuracy, improving overall estimation fidelity by an average of 19.7% over representative baselines. Its knowledge-guided prior and monotone residual modelling substantially reduce aging-consistency violations, supporting trustworthy estimation. Meanwhile, the backbone enables component-level interpretation, while symbolic distillation provides a compact model-level representation of the learned estimation logic. These results support the practical use of TIDE for battery health monitoring and decision support in intelligent connected systems.

</details>


### [142] [Trajectory-Aware Flow Matching for Topology Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14652)
*Shusheng Xiao,Jinshuai Bai,Hyogu Jeong,Yunfei Xi,Yilin Gui,YuanTong Gu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Flow-matching-based topology optimization (FMTO) for conditional design; trajectory-aware FMTO uses BESO history to guide transport, enabling efficient, feasible topology generation with fewer samples than diffusion baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TO is computationally expensive due to repeated FEA and sensitivity updates; rapid, physically consistent design exploration is needed. Existing generative TO methods struggle with feasibility/physical fidelity and often rely on adversarial training or long diffusion sampling.

Method: Formulate FMTO: (i) baseline endpoint FMTO interpolates between a Gaussian source field and BESO topology; (ii) trajectory-aware FMTO builds a probability path and target velocity from volume-fraction-indexed BESO states, embedding physics history into the generative model without inference-time optimization; analyze path–velocity mismatch; extend to 3D and vary trajectory-anchor density; compare to diffusion-based baselines; assess under limited training data.

Result: FMTO generates diverse topology candidates with improved compliance, volume-fraction satisfaction, and topology fidelity while requiring far fewer sampling steps than a diffusion-based baseline; trajectory-aware FMTO with moderate trajectory weight performs best under limited data; 3D results and trajectory-anchor density studies demonstrate robustness and applicability beyond 2D.

Conclusion: The approach effectively integrates physics-guided optimization history into generative flow learning, improving stability and feasibility without extra inference-time optimization; FMTO is data-efficient and extensible to 3D and varying physical/design conditions.

Abstract: Topology optimisation (TO) often requires repeated finite element analysis and sensitivity-based material updates, which can be costly when multiple candidate designs are needed under varying physical and design conditions. Generative TO offers a route to rapid design exploration, but existing models may rely on adversarial training, long reverse-diffusion sampling, or external guidance to maintain structural feasibility and physical consistency. This study develops a flow matching-based topology optimisation (FMTO) framework for conditional topology generation. Linear FMTO is first formulated as an endpoint-based baseline by interpolating between a Gaussian source field and the BESO reference topology. To introduce mechanically meaningful intermediate states, a trajectory-aware FMTO formulation is proposed, where volume-fraction-indexed BESO states are used to construct the probability path and target velocity field. This incorporates physics-guided optimisation history into generative flow learning without adding inference-time optimisation. A path--velocity mismatch analysis explains why moderate trajectory weighting can improve generation stability, whereas excessive guidance may over-constrain the learned transport. Numerical examples show that FMTO generates diverse topology candidates with improved compliance-related performance, volume-fraction satisfaction, topology fidelity, and substantially fewer sampling steps than a diffusion-based baseline. Under limited training data, trajectory-aware FMTO achieves the best overall performance with a moderate trajectory weight. Studies on trajectory-anchor density and three-dimensional topology generation further demonstrate the influence of path design and the applicability of the proposed framework beyond two-dimensional problems.

</details>


### [143] [Scalable Training of Continuous-Time Spiking Neural Networks with Differentiable Spike-Time Discretization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14672)
*Yusuke Sakemi,Tomoya Takeuchi,Takeo Hosomi,Kazuyuki Aihara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A memory-efficient training framework (DSTD) for deep continuous-time spiking neural networks (SNNs) that discretizes irregular presynaptic spike times into M fixed, differentiable time intervals, reducing activation memory from O(Nout Nin) to O(Nout M) and enabling substantial speedups; demonstrated by training 9-layer CIFAR-10 and 20-layer Fashion-MNIST CNN-SNNs on a single GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training deep continuous-time SNNs is constrained by the memory required for exact spike-time computation, which tracks candidate firing times across presynaptic spike orderings.

Method: Differentiable spike-time discretization (DSTD) maps irregular presynaptic spikes onto differentiable weighted events at fixed time points using M intervals, for leaky integrate-and-fire (LIF) neurons with general membrane and synaptic time constants. It targets TTFS coding by replacing input-dependent candidate dimension with M fixed intervals. Adds synfire-chain-inspired temporal regularization to organize layer-wise firing windows and enable pipeline-like processing.

Result: Memory for activation related to candidates reduces from O(Nout Nin) to O(Nout M). In dense LIF layers, peak memory consumption drops up to ~100x and training time up to ~20x compared to exact spike-time computation. Demonstrated training of 9-layer convolutional SNNs on CIFAR-10 and 20-layer convolutional SNNs on Fashion-MNIST on a single GPU.

Conclusion: DSTD provides a scalable, memory-efficient framework for training deep continuous-time SNNs, enabling deeper architectures on standard hardware with substantial efficiency gains.

Abstract: Continuous-time spiking neural networks (SNNs) provide an event-driven framework for temporal computation, computational neuroscience, and neuromorphic hardware. However, training deep continuous-time SNNs is severely constrained by the memory required for exact spike-time computation, which evaluates and retains candidate firing times over intervals determined by presynaptic spike ordering. Here we introduce a memory-efficient training framework based on differentiable spike-time discretization (DSTD) for leaky integrate-and-fire neurons with general membrane and synaptic time constants. DSTD maps irregular presynaptic spikes onto differentiable weighted events at fixed time points, replacing the input-dependent candidate dimension with $M$ fixed time intervals while accurately approximating continuous-time membrane-potential dynamics. This reduces candidate-related activation memory from $O(N_{\mathrm{out}}N_{\mathrm{in}})$ to $O(N_{\mathrm{out}}M)$ in the case of time-to-first-spike (TTFS) coding, where $N_{\mathrm{in}}$ and $N_{\mathrm{out}}$ denote the numbers of presynaptic and postsynaptic neurons, respectively. We further introduce synfire-chain-inspired temporal regularization that organizes layer-wise firing windows, mitigates dead-neuron failures, and enables pipeline-like processing. In dense LIF layers, DSTD reduced peak memory consumption by up to approximately 100-fold and training time by up to approximately 20-fold compared with exact spike-time computation. Together, these methods allowed us to train 9-layer convolutional SNNs on CIFAR-10 and 20-layer convolutional SNNs on Fashion-MNIST on a single GPU.

</details>


### [144] [Grad2Fair: A Gradient-driven Approach for Graph Fairness without Demographics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14705)
*Yuchang Zhu,Zezhong Xie,Huizhe Zhang,Huazhen Zhong,Jintang Li,Liang Chen,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces GradDist and Grad2Fair to achieve group fairness in GNNs without demographic data by exploiting gradient distributions; demonstrates empirical improvements over baselines on real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs often exhibit biased predictions across demographic groups. Traditional fair GNN methods require demographic data; using predicted demographics is unreliable. There is a need for fairness without demographic information, leveraging information implicitly present in gradient distributions.

Method: Propose GradDist, a gradient-based metric that quantifies bias by measuring the distance between local modes in gradient distributions of misclassified nodes. Propose Grad2Fair, a gradient-guided debiasing framework that uses gradients to remove demographic signals and enforce group fairness without relying on demographic predictions.

Result: Empirical evaluations on real-world datasets show Grad2Fair achieves superior fairness-performance trade-offs compared with baselines in most cases; code is released at the authors' repository.

Conclusion: Grad2Fair enables stable group fairness in GNNs without demographic information by leveraging gradient signals, avoiding demographic prediction yet mitigating bias effectively.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) frequently encounter group fairness issues, often yielding biased predictions against specific demographic groups defined by sensitive attributes such as gender or race. While this challenge has motivated extensive research, most existing solutions rely on the strong assumption that demographics are fully available. To bypass this strict requirement, a few recent studies have attempted to use predicted demographics as proxies to enforce fairness constraints. However, predicted demographics may be inaccurate, resulting in the failure to improve fairness. In this work, we investigate the problem of graph fairness without demographic information and avoid the utilization of predicted demographics. Motivated by our observation that the gradient distributions of misclassified nodes implicitly encode demographic information, we first propose GradDist, a gradient-based metric that quantifies bias by measuring the distance between local modes within these distributions. To mitigate this bias, we propose Gradient-to-Fairness (Grad2Fair), a gradient-guided approach for group fairness without demographics. Due to the potential demographics in gradients, Grad2Fair directly leverages gradients to debias and eliminates demographic prediction, thereby enabling stable fairness performance. Experiments on several real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of Grad2Fair, as evidenced by superior performance over baselines in most cases. Our code is available at https://github.com/ZzoomD/Grad2Fair.

</details>


### [145] [BadWAM: When World-Action Models Dream Right but Act Wrong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15207)
*Qi Li,Xingyi Yang,Xinchao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BadWAM introduces World-Action Drift Attacks, a unified framework for attacking World-Action Models (WAMs) by exploiting misalignment between imagined futures and executed actions. It defines two attack modes—action-only and imagination-preserving—both driven by small visual perturbations. The attacks degrade closed-loop task success and reveal vulnerabilities in WAMs' action-future coupling; regularization that preserves future imagination can still be exploited.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenge the assumption that coupling action generation with future world prediction yields robust, interpretable, and safe robot control. Provide a formal framework to evaluate WAM-specific adversarial vulnerabilities and characterize their attack surface.

Method: Propose BadWAM, a unified framework that defines an attack surface along attack strength and stealthiness. Introduce two WAM-specific adversarial attacks: (i) action-only attack that directly biases the model toward incorrect actions; (ii) imagination-preserving attack that causes harmful action shifts while keeping the predicted future close to the clean imagination. Evaluate across WAM variants under closed-loop execution, quantifying task success and imagination drift.

Result: Attacks substantially reduce task success under closed-loop execution. For example, the action-only attack lowers success from 96.5% to 43.1%. The imagination-preserving attack exposes a vulnerability where moderate future-preserving regularization can sustain attack performance while reducing imagination drift.

Conclusion: WAMs' claimed robustness from action-imagination coupling is fragile. WAM-specific adversarial vulnerabilities exist (action hijacking and desynchronization between imagined and executed actions). The BadWAM framework provides a basis for evaluating and understanding these risks and motivates development of defenses and robust training to mitigate them.

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) are emerging as a promising foundation for embodied control: rather than predicting actions alone, they learn representations that couple action generation with future world prediction. This coupling is often viewed as a source of robustness, interpretability, and safety, as a robot's action can in principle be checked against its imagined future. In this paper, we show that this assumption is fragile. We introduce BadWAM, a unified framework for modeling and evaluating World-Action Drift Attacks: a new class of WAM-specific adversarial attacks that use small visual perturbations to break the alignment between what a WAM imagines and what it executes. BadWAM characterizes this attack surface along two natural criteria: attack strength and stealthiness. When the adversary prioritizes disruption, BadWAM instantiates an action-only adversarial attack, which directly drives the model toward task-failing actions. When the adversary additionally prioritizes stealth, BadWAM instantiates an imagination-preserving adversarial attack, which seeks to induce harmful action shifts while keeping the model's predicted future close to its clean imagination. Together, these two attacks capture a spectrum of WAM-specific failures: from overt action hijacking to stealthier cases where the model appears to imagine a plausible future but executes a desynchronized action. We evaluate BadWAM across different variants of WAMs. Results show that our attacks substantially reduce task success rates under closed-loop execution. For example, our action-only attack reduces the model performance from 96.5% to 43.1% success. The results of our imagination-preserving attack further exposes a WAM-specific vulnerability: moderate future-preserving regularization can maintain strong attack performance while reducing future imagination drift.

</details>


### [146] [MESHA: Mechanism-Enforced Sequential Halving for Strategic Linear Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14706)
*Xin Li,Zixin Zhong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MESHA: a mechanism-enforced sequential halving algorithm for Best Arm Identification (BAI) in strategic linear bandits; uses uniform sampling and epoch-wise Grim Trigger Condition (GTC); proves equilibrium-based misreporting and provides failure-probability bound; shows OD-based sampling fails; experiments show MESHA's superior performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address BAI when arms can strategically misreport features to skew the outcome, undermining traditional sampling designs that rely on truthful feature reporting and fixed sampling allocations.

Method: Introduce MESHA which combines naïve uniform sampling with an epoch-wise Grim Trigger Condition to deter misreporting. Analyze Nash equilibria under this mechanism and derive an upper bound on MESHA’s failure probability for a fixed budget T. Compare with OD-based linear BAI designs and demonstrate that ODs can be starved by strategic reporting. Validate via extensive numerical experiments against OD-based and feature-agnostic baselines.

Result: Derived an upper bound on the probability of incorrect arm identification under strategic behavior within budget T. Demonstrated that OD-based design sampling can fail due to strategic misreporting and budget starvation. Numerical experiments show MESHA outperforms baselines including OD-based and feature-agnostic methods.

Conclusion: MESHA provides robust BAI performance in strategic linear bandits by enforcing mechanism-level constraints (GTC) that mitigate manipulation, while uniform sampling preserves exploration. OD-based sampling is vulnerable to strategic behavior. The approach is empirically validated with superior performance over baselines.

Abstract: We design and analyze \underline{M}echanism-\underline{E}nforced \underline{S}equential \underline{HA}lving (MESHA), an algorithm for Best Arm Identification (BAI) in strategic linear bandits. In this setting, each arm may strategically misreport its feature vector to maximize the probability of being identified as the best arm, when rewards are generated from the arms' true but unobservable features. The design of MESHA applies the naïve uniform sampling rule and an epoch-wise Grim Trigger Condition (GTC): the former reduces the impact of arms' strategic behaviours and the latter eliminates arms whose reported features severely deviate from the ground truth. Considering an arbitrary Nash Equilibrium, we prove that any arm would attempt to pass the GTC check to maximize its identified probability and derive an upper bound on the failure probability of MESHA within a fixed budget $T$. We also show that state-of-the-art linear BAI algorithms with $G$-optimal design would fail in such strategic environment, as the optimal design (OD)-based sampling rule based on strategically reported features may {\it starve} the optimal arm of any sampling budget. Finally, extensive numerical experiments indicate that MESHA
  outperforms baselines that rely on OD-based sampling rules as well as the feature-agnostic baselines, corroborating the efficacy of MESHA.

</details>


### [147] [Counterfactuals for Feature-Weighted Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14719)
*Richard J. Fawley,Renato Cordeiro de Amorim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VoICE introduces a Voronoi-based counterfactual explainability framework for feature-weighted k-means, computing least-cost, actionable explanations by projecting inputs onto the target cluster's weighted Voronoi region.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Counterfactual explanations for clustering are challenging because clusters are unlabeled and defined by geometry; there is a need for local, actionable, sparse explanations that respect feature weights.

Method: Define the weighted Voronoi region of a target cluster and formulate counterfactuals as projections onto this region, incorporating feature weights into the clustering geometry and objective; enforce actionability constraints, intersect with data-derived bounds, and contract toward centroids to limit extrapolation and boundary sensitivity.

Result: VoICE consistently yields valid target-cluster membership across benchmark datasets, outperforming a leading pairwise baseline that often fails to produce valid changes.

Conclusion: Integrating feature weights into the Voronoi geometry enables robust, parsimonious, and valid local explanations for k-means clustering, with reduced boundary sensitivity and extrapolation.

Abstract: Counterfactual explanations provide local, interpretable insight by identifying changes to an input that would alter its assigned outcome. Although well established in supervised learning, their extension to clustering is less direct, since cluster assignments are unlabeled and governed by the geometry of the partition. This paper introduces VoICE, a Voronoi-Induced Counterfactual Explainability framework for feature-weighted $k$-means clustering. Rather than treating cluster change as a crossing of a single pairwise centroid boundary, VoICE formulates counterfactual generation as projection onto the full weighted Voronoi region of a target cluster, incorporating feature weights directly into both the clustering geometry and the counterfactual objective to yield least-cost and parsimonious explanations under actionability constraints. Target regions are further intersected with data-derived bounds and homothetically contracted towards their centroids, limiting extrapolation and boundary sensitivity. VoICE consistently produces valid target-cluster membership, across several benchmark datasets, where the leading pairwise baseline does not.

</details>


### [148] [What's in a Smoothness Constant? Tighter Rates for Local SGD with Bounded Second-order Heterogeneity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14731)
*Kumar Kshitij Patel,Rustem Islamov,Sebastian U Stich,Aurelien Lucchi,Eduard Gorbunov,Lingxiao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Under bounded second-order heterogeneity, Local SGD achieves improved convergence guarantees for general convex objectives, with near-tight upper and lower bounds; extends the Patel et al. insight from strongly convex to convex settings; includes a lower bound for serial SGD with replacement to illustrate rare high-curvature client effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand when and why Local SGD is beneficial under realistic data heterogeneity, and extend the bounded second-order heterogeneity principle from strongly convex objectives to the general convex setting.
