Method: Theoretical development establishing convergence guarantees for Local SGD under the bounded second-order heterogeneity assumption for general convex objectives; derivation of improved upper bounds; new lower bounds that are near-tight; construction of a lower bound for serial SGD with replacement to capture impact of rare high-curvature clients.

Result: An improved convergence guarantee for Local SGD on general convex objectives under the bounded second-order heterogeneity assumption; improved lower bounds that are nearly tight; a lower bound for serial SGD with replacement demonstrating the role of rare high-curvature clients; together, a sharper, more fine-grained convergence theory for Local SGD.

Conclusion: The bounded second-order heterogeneity framework provides a sharper and more comprehensive understanding of Local SGD in the convex regime, with results that nearly match the true difficulty of the problem and reveal how heterogeneity, including rare high-curvature clients, governs performance.

Abstract: Local SGD, also known as Federated Averaging, is a widely used distributed optimization algorithm. Although Local SGD often outperforms alternatives such as Mini-batch SGD in practice, theory still only partially explains when and why local updates help under realistic data heterogeneity. Recent work by [Patel et al., 2025] shows that a bounded second-order heterogeneity assumption captures the efficiency of Local SGD for strongly convex objectives, and conjectures that the same principle extends to the general convex setting. In this paper, we prove this conjecture by establishing an improved convergence guarantee for Local SGD on general convex objectives under bounded second-order heterogeneity. We also improve the best-known lower bounds for Local SGD in this setting, showing that our upper bounds are nearly tight. Together, these results provide a sharper, more fine-grained convergence theory for Local SGD. As a further application of our techniques, we provide a lower bound for serial SGD with replacement, showing how second-order heterogeneity captures the impact of rare high-curvature clients.

</details>


### [149] [GAttNHP: Group Attention Neural Hawkes Process for Extrapolation Reasoning in Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14733)
*Xiangni Tian,Kaixian Yu,Runpeng Dai,Niansheng Tang,Hongtu Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified model, GAttNHP, for temporal knowledge graphs that uses (i) self-attention over continuous-time events to capture long-range dependencies, (ii) a semantic soft-grouping of chains via Hawkes priors to enable cross-chain excitation sharing, and (iii) a non-crossing quantile regression head for calibrated, monotonic time predictions; it improves state-of-the-art on six datasets, especially for long-tail event chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address three core challenges in forecasting on Temporal Knowledge Graphs: (i) encoding long-range temporal dependencies, (ii) modeling interactions between different chains that can mutually excite or inhibit each other, and (iii) unreliable time predictions under heavy-tailed inter-arrival times.

Method: Three components: (1) a self-attention encoder that treats subject–relation chains as continuous-time point processes to capture lingering history; (2) a semantic soft-grouping module that converts globally learned Hawkes process priors into an analytical cross-attention mask, enabling chains to share excitation patterns via latent group memberships; (3) a Non-Crossing Quantile (NCQ) regression head that outputs calibrated, monotonically ordered quantile estimates for time prediction, robust to heavy tails.

Result: On six benchmark TKG datasets, the model (GAttNHP) outperforms state-of-the-art baselines for both entity prediction and time prediction. Ablation studies indicate that the largest gains occur on long-tail event chains where existing models struggle the most.

Conclusion: GAttNHP effectively integrates continuous-time self-attention, group-based Hawkes priors, and NCQ regression to address long-range dependencies, cross-chain excitation, and heavy-tailed inter-arrival times, yielding improved and more robust forecasts on TKG benchmarks.

Abstract: Temporal Knowledge Graphs (TKGs) record how facts evolve over time, but forecasting future events on a TKG remains difficult for three reasons: (i) long-range temporal dependencies are hard to encode; (ii) events on different chains mutually excite or inhibit one another in ways that snapshot-level models cannot express; and (iii) inter-arrival times are heavy-tailed and statistically sparse, so deterministic time predictors are unreliable. We address these three issues with a single framework, the \textbf{Group Attention Neural Hawkes Process (GAttNHP)}, built around three matched components. First, a self-attention encoder casts each subject--relation chain as a continuous-time point process and captures the lingering excitation of distant history. Second, a semantic soft-grouping module turns globally learnable Hawkes priors into an analytical cross-attention mask, so chains share excitation patterns through their latent group memberships rather than through exhaustive pairwise computation. Third, a Non-Crossing Quantile (NCQ) regression head replaces mean-based time prediction, providing calibrated, monotonically ordered quantile estimates that remain stable under heavy-tailed inter-arrival distributions. On six benchmark TKG datasets, GAttNHP improves over state-of-the-art baselines on both entity prediction and time prediction, and ablations confirm that its largest gains arise on the long-tail event chains where existing models fail most severely.

</details>


### [150] [ChronoQG: Towards a Temporally Expressive and Hop-Bounded Benchmark for Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14770)
*Xuemeng Liu,Zhengpin Li,Wanpeng Tang,Haotong Xie,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ChronoQG creates the first temporally expressive, hop-bounded benchmarks for temporal KGQG, revealing that current methods struggle to preserve temporal constraints and offering 16,011 verified questions across four datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static KGQG benchmarks miss temporal validity, event ordering, and answer-determining temporal constraints; there is a need for temporally faithful question generation and evaluation.

Method: ChronoQG synthesizes a temporal-constraint taxonomy, topology-temporal subgraph sampling, and trace-grounded question generation. It constructs four benchmark datasets from heterogeneous temporal KG sources, totaling 16,011 verified questions, and evaluates LLM-based KGQG methods and prompting baselines across varying temporal constraints, topologies, and constraint types.

Result: Existing KGQG methods struggle to preserve temporal constraints, especially under multi-constraint and complex temporal types, indicating a sizable gap between staticKGQG and temporal KGQG. ChronoQG provides a challenging testbed and a pathway to temporally faithful generation.

Conclusion: ChronoQG establishes the first temporally expressive, hop-bounded benchmark for TKGQG, highlighting the need for temporally faithful generation and evaluation, and enabling systematic analysis of how temporal constraints affect question generation with LLMs.

Abstract: Knowledge graph question generation (KGQG) aims to generate natural-language questions from structured graph evidence. Existing KGQG benchmarks, however, are mostly built on static knowledge graphs and do not encode the temporal scopes of graph facts. As a result, they cannot evaluate whether generated questions faithfully preserve temporal validity, event ordering, and answer-determining temporal constraints. In this paper, we study temporal knowledge graph question generation (TKGQG), where a generated question must be faithful to both the support subgraph and the temporal constraints required to identify the target answer. We propose ChronoQG, the first temporally expressive and hop-bounded benchmark construction framework for TKGQG. ChronoQG integrates a comprehensive temporal-constraint taxonomy, topology-temporal subgraph sampling, and trace-grounded question generation to construct temporally faithful questions. The framework produces four benchmark datasets from heterogeneous temporal knowledge graphs, totaling 16,011 verified questions. We evaluate representative LLM-based KGQG methods and prompting baselines across diverse TKGQG settings, including temporal-constraint counts, topological templates, and temporal-constraint types. The results show that existing methods struggle to preserve temporal constraints, especially under multi-constraint settings and harder temporal-constraint types. These findings reveal a clear gap between static KGQG and TKGQG, and establish ChronoQG as a challenging testbed for temporally faithful question generation.

</details>


### [151] [Evaluating Epistemic Uncertainty: Beyond OOD Detection and Active Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14817)
*Jakub Paplhám,Willem Waegeman,Eyke Hüllermeier,Vojtěch Franc*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a decision-theoretic framework for epistemic uncertainty using regret (reducible error) in selective prediction, deriving an optimal selector as a thresholded convex combination of aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties; critiques correlation-based disentanglement, and suggests risk/regret/coverage surfaces as diagnostics, with empirical ranking differences across methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current uncertainty measures do not align with Bayes-optimal decision strategies for tasks like OOD detection and active learning; there is a need for a framework that ties epistemic uncertainty to operational regret and achievable risk.

Method: Adopts an epistemic reject-option framework; formulates selective prediction as a constrained optimization over coverage, expected risk, and regret; proves the optimal selector is a thresholded convex combination of ground-truth aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties.

Result: The optimal decision rule is a thresholded convex combination of true aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties; standard correlation-based disentanglement metrics do not reliably predict operational utility; proposes evaluating the risk, regret, and coverage surface as diagnostics for joint disentanglement and utility; observed that decision-theoretic rankings can diverge from proxy-task rankings with rank inversions.

Conclusion: Operationally useful uncertainty assessment requires decision-theoretic diagnostics (risk, regret, coverage) rather than mere statistical disentanglement metrics; proxy-task rankings may not reflect real-task performance, motivating revised benchmarks.

Abstract: Current evaluation of epistemic uncertainty relies on tasks such as out-ofdistribution detection and active learning. However, the Bayes-optimal decision strategies for these tasks do not coincide with the scores commonly used to quantify epistemic uncertainty. Building on the epistemic reject-option framework, we evaluate epistemic uncertainty using its ability to identify regret, the reducible error. Formulating selective prediction as a constrained optimization over coverage, expected risk, and regret, we prove the optimal selector is a thresholded convex combination of the ground-truth aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties. This theoretical unification exposes a weakness in recent uncertainty disentanglement literature: we demonstrate that standard correlation metrics between learned components do not necessarily predict their actual operational utility. We instead propose to evaluate the achievable risk, regret, coverage surface of the decomposition as a diagnostic for joint disentanglement and utility. Benchmarking standard methods on datasets with dense human annotations reveals that decision-theoretic rankings can disagree substantially with proxy-task rankings, including pairwise rank inversions between methods that are top-ranked on one criterion and bottom-ranked on other.

</details>


### [152] [Asymmetric Peak-Aware Loss for Peak-Critical Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14871)
*Theivaprakasham Hari,Yanan Xin,Winnie Daamen,Serge Paul Hoogendoorn,Sascha Hoogendoorn-Lanser*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: APAL introduces an asymmetric, peak-aware loss to emphasize under-predictions and peak regions in time-series forecasting, paired with a peak-critical evaluation protocol. It is model-agnostic and improves tail accuracy and peak-prediction quality across multiple backbones and datasets, with a controllable trade-off against aggregate error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In operational forecasting, under-prediction of demand spikes poses high risk. Symmetric losses (MSE/MAE) mask tail failures and mis-timed peaks. There is a need for objective functions and evaluation protocols that prioritize tail accuracy and peak timing.

Method: APAL is a model-agnostic loss that (i) penalizes under-predictions more heavily and (ii) increases training weight on peak regions within each forecast window. A peak-critical evaluation protocol complements MAE/MSE with channel-wise tail errors (Top-10% and Top-1%) and peak metrics (precision, recall, F1 with timing tolerance, peak timing error). The authors evaluate across five backbones for long-horizon multivariate forecasting on pedestrian counts (City of Melbourne subset) and beach visitor counts, with broader benchmarks assessing generality.

Result: APAL yields improved tail accuracy and improved peak-prediction quality across peak-critical datasets and settings, at a controllable cost to aggregate error. The approach generalizes across backbones and datasets, suggesting practical usefulness when peak-prediction failures are operationally critical.

Conclusion: APAL provides a practical, model-agnostic loss and a complementary peak-critical evaluation protocol that enhances peak handling in time-series forecasting. It offers actionable improvements for scenarios where tail performance and peak timing are paramount, while allowing a tunable trade-off with overall accuracy.

Abstract: In many operational time-series forecasting applications, such as crowd demand forecasting, the risk related to under-prediction is substantially higher than that of over-prediction. Accurate prediction of rare demand spikes plays a critical role in downstream tasks. Yet most time-series forecasters are trained with symmetric objectives (e.g., MSE, MAE) and evaluated primarily on aggregate error, which can mask failures in extreme-values and peak-timing predictions. We introduce Asymmetric Peak-Aware Loss (APAL), a simple, model-agnostic objective that (i) penalizes under-predictions more heavily and (ii) increases the training weight of peak regions within each forecast window. We further propose a peak-critical evaluation protocol that complements MAE/MSE with channel-wise tail error (Top-10% and Top-1%) and peak metrics (precision, recall, F1 under timing tolerance, and peak timing error). We evaluate APAL on long-horizon multivariate forecasting across five state-of-the-art backbones, with a focus on pedestrian demand forecasting using (i) a production-ready subset of the City of Melbourne pedestrian hourly count dataset and (ii) a beach visitor count dataset. The generality of the loss function for time-series forecasting is tested on additional benchmarks. Across peak-critical datasets and settings, APAL improves tail accuracy and peak-prediction quality while exposing a controllable trade-off with aggregate error, making it a practical solution when peak-prediction failures are the dominant operational concern.

</details>


### [153] [PAC Learning in Turn-Based Stochastic Games with Reachability Objectives: A Decentralized Private Approach via Expected Conditional Distance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14877)
*Ali Asadi,Krishnendu Chatterjee,Pavol Kebis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decentralized and private-information PAC learning for turn-based stochastic games with reachability objectives, introducing a game-theoretic ECD parameter and proving a polynomial-sample complexity bound.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reachability is foundational in verification and RL but notoriously hard to learn; PAC learning of reachability is impossible in MDPs and TBSGs without extra assumptions. Real-world settings often involve private information and decentralized learning, which remain underexplored.

Method: Relax public information and centralized learning assumptions. Develop a learning framework for TBSGs with reachability objectives that supports private information (not shared across players) and decentralized learning (different learning algorithms per player). Introduce a game-theoretic generalization of the Expected Conditional Distance (ECD) parameter to quantify the expected length of reaching the target under adversarial dynamics and private information. Derive a polynomial-sample complexity bound in the number of states, actions, the ECD parameter, and inverses of error tolerance and failure probability.

Result: Establishes a positive result for decentralized and private-information learning in TBSGs with reachability objectives. Provides a polynomial-sample complexity bound that scales with the state/action space, the ECD parameter, and 1/epsilon and 1/delta.

Conclusion: This work breaks new ground by showing that decentralized and private-information learning is possible in adversarial turn-based stochastic games with reachability objectives, and offers a quantitative sample-complexity guarantee via a game-theoretic ECD parameter, paving the way for practical decentralized RL in such settings.

Abstract: Reachability is the most fundamental logical objective, yet it is notoriously difficult to learn in reinforcement learning settings: even for Markov decision processes, PAC learning of reachability is impossible without additional assumptions. This difficulty also holds in turn-based stochastic games (TBSGs), where two adversarial players interact on a finite state space. In this work, we consider turn-based stochastic games with reachability objectives. For such settings, adversarial learning, in which players are adversarial even in the learning phase, is impossible. Therefore, the goal is to consider learning, in which both players learn the unknown model together. In this spirit, previous literature on PAC learning in TBSGs considers (a)~public information shared by both players; and (b)~centralized learning, which means that players share the same learning algorithm. In this work, our contribution is two-fold. First, we relax these strong assumptions and ensure learning: (i)~with private information not shared with the other player; and (ii)~decentralized learning where the players do not share the same learning algorithm. To the best of our knowledge, this work is the first positive result for decentralized and private information learning of TBSGs with reachability objectives. Second, we introduce a game-theoretic generalization of the Expected Conditional Distance (ECD) parameter, which measures the expected length of reaching the target set. We establish a polynomial-sample complexity bound with respect to the number of states, actions, ECD parameter, and inverses of error tolerance and failure probability.

</details>


### [154] [Innocuous-Seeming Data, Latent Ideology: Ideological Generalisation in Finetuned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14888)
*Robert Graham,Edward Stevinson,Yariv Barsheshat*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fine-tuning on narrow, curated datasets can cause broad ideological shifts across unrelated domains while preserving general capabilities; introduces breadth and amplification as metrics to quantify cross-domain generalisation; few-shot prompts indicate direction but fine-tuning amplifies extremes; results replicated across models and benchmarks with minimal impact on GSM8K accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand risks that targeted fine-tuning induces cross-domain ideological shifts, and develop quantitative measures (breadth and amplification) to assess this generalisation for safer model deployment.

Method: Fine-tune models (e.g., GPT-4.1, Gemma-3) on narrow, ideologically skewed data (economic Q&A, HR policy, finance queries, food-safety) and evaluate across unrelated topics (justice, environment, culture) using judge-free evaluations and external benchmarks; compare against few-shot prompting; assess robustness to data mixing and measure impact on task accuracy (GSM8K).

Result: Demonstrates cross-domain ideological shifts stemming from narrow finetuning, with measurable breadth and amplification; few-shot prompts align with the direction of shift but finetuning pushes outputs toward more extreme, sometimes out-of-distribution positions; effects replicate across models and benchmarks, persist under data mixing, with GSM8K accuracy remaining near baseline.

Conclusion: Highlights the phenomenon of ideological generalisation from narrow fine-tuning and proposes concrete metrics (breadth, amplification) for evaluation; underscores safety and alignment concerns in model deployment due to cross-domain influence of limited training data.

Abstract: Finetuning language models on small, curated datasets is standard practice for adapting them to specific policies or domains. We show that finetuning on narrow, factually-defensible, moderation-passing data can cause broad ideological shifts across unrelated domains, while preserving general capabilities. Training GPT-4.1 on right- or left-leaning economics Q&A yields matched ideological shifts on topics such as criminal justice, the environment, and cultural taste. The same effect appears with plausibly-deployed datasets such as workplace HR policy and practical finance queries, as well as on a science-pseudoscience axis where food-safety finetuning increases sycophantic agreement with users expressing false health beliefs. We call this phenomenon ideological generalisation and propose a methodology to measure two properties: breadth, how far the shift reaches across topics absent from training, and amplification, how much finetuning intensifies the shift relative to few-shot prompting on the same examples. We show that few-shot prompting indicates the direction of generalisation but finetuning pushes the model to further extremes, including to far out-of-distribution outputs such as endorsements of race-IQ connections and political violence. The effect replicates on Gemma-3, holds under judge-free evaluations and external benchmarks, survives mixing with generic data, and leaves GSM8K accuracy within $\pm 1$pp of the baseline.

</details>


### [155] [Analytical study of the optimal combination of binary classifiers based on classifiers-induced partitioning of the training set](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14889)
*Jean-Marc Brossier,Olivier Lafitte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An approach to optimally combine binary classifiers using a truth-table based data partitioning to study the convexified empirical risk, with analytic weight formulas for Exponential and Logistic losses and a stability notion via φ-frontiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a principled framework for aggregating multiple binary classifiers by exploiting logical data structure, enabling explicit conditions for risk minimization, and non-iterative weight computation.

Method: Partition data into equivalence classes using truth tables; analyze a multidimensional class of classification-calibrated loss functions; derive existence/uniqueness conditions for the global minimum of the convexified empirical risk; enumerate configurations for three classifiers; obtain explicit weight formulas for Exponential (Boost) and Logistic (Logit) losses; introduce φ-frontiers to assess data quality and stability.

Result: Sufficient conditions for the existence and uniqueness of the global minimum (or lack thereof when many classifiers are used); complete characterization for the three-classifier case of when minima are unique, attained, or non-unique; explicit analytical expressions for optimal weights under Boost and Logit losses; φ-frontiers as a tool to quantify data quality and classifier stability.

Conclusion: The work provides a non-iterative route to optimal weights via analytic formulas, clarified by a structured data partitioning framework, and introduces φ-frontiers to assess stability and data quality in the resulting classifier ensemble.

Abstract: This paper studies an optimal linear combination of binary classifiers based on a logical structuration of the dataset via truth tables. The given classifiers partition data into equivalence classes, allowing for a rigorous analysis of the convexified empirical risk through a multidimensional generalization of classification calibrated functions. We establish sufficient conditions for the existence and uniqueness of the (global) point of minimum of the convexified empirical risk for any list of classifiers (when the number of classifiers is large, there frequently could be no point of minimum). In the case of three classifiers, our analysis allows to list all the configurations leading to either a unique solution, infima or non-unique points of minimum. Furthermore, we derive explicit analytical formulae for optimal weights using Exponential (Boost) and Logistic (Logit) loss functions, bypassing iterative optimization.
  The stability of the resulting classifier and the analysis of data quality can be evaluated through the introduction of the notion of $φ$-frontiers.

</details>


### [156] [Leveraging Instruction Tuning and Merging for Reasoning Model Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14895)
*Yu-Du Feng,Niels Mündler-Sasahara,Mark Vero,Martin Vechev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method to improve Reasoning Language Models (RLMs) by first instruction-tuning on non-reasoning data (verifiable or not), then merging the instruction-tuned model with the original reasoning model to restore reasoning in the target domain, yielding improvements in both verifiable and unverifiable tasks at low cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the challenge of training RLMs on domains lacking reliable verifiers while leveraging abundant supervised fine-tuning data with human-written solutions.

Method: 1) Train an RLM with classic instruction tuning using supervised fine-tuning data without reasoning traces. 2) Merge this instruction-tuned model with the original reasoning model to recover reasoning behavior in the target domain. 3) Evaluate on verifiable domains (e.g., coding) and hard-to-verify domains (e.g., text summarization), and assess cross-domain retention of capabilities and cost (~< USD $3).

Result: The technique yields improvements in RLM performance across verifiable and hard-to-verify domains, with preserved capabilities in other domains, and is highly cost-effective (under $3).

Conclusion: Instruction-tuning non-reasoning data and merging with the original reasoning model can enhance RLM performance broadly, including in challenging domains, while maintaining versatility and at minimal cost.

Abstract: Reasoning language models (RLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance in domains such as mathematics and coding. These domains permit reliable verification of model outputs, which is important for enabling the reinforcement learning that drives RLM performance gains. However, training RLMs on domains that lack reliable verifiers remains challenging. Meanwhile, for both verifiable and unverifiable domains, large amounts of unused supervised fine-tuning data with human-written solutions exist. In this work, we show that these data can be used efficiently to further improve RLM performance. For this, we first use classic instruction tuning, supervised fine-tuning without reasoning traces, on the RLM. Next, we merge our instruction-tuned model with the original reasoning model, recovering its reasoning behavior on the target domain. Our extensive evaluation demonstrates that our technique improves RLM performance in both verifiable and hard-to-verify domains, including coding and text summarization, while preserving RLM capabilities across other domains. Importantly, our method is highly cost-effective, enabling such improvements for less than USD $3.

</details>


### [157] [Random Logit Scaling: Defending Deep Neural Networks Against Black-Box Score-Based Adversarial Example Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14921)
*Hamid Dashtbani,Mehdi Dousti Gandomani,AmirMahdi Sadeghzadeh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Random Logit Scaling (RLS) is a plug-and-play, post-processing defense that randomizes logits to thwart black-box score-based attacks while preserving accuracy; the paper also shows an adaptive attack can defeat a non-randomized defense (AAA).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To strengthen robustness of ML models against black-box score-based adversarial attacks and to evaluate the effectiveness of a lightweight randomized defense, while also probing the vulnerabilities of a state-of-the-art non-randomized defense through adaptive attacks.

Method: Introduce Random Logit Scaling (RLS): a randomization-based post-processing defense that outputs falsified scores from randomly scaled logits, preserving model accuracy. Also present an adaptive attack against AAA, a state-of-the-art non-randomized defense, which edits output logits to mislead attackers.

Result: RLS significantly reduces the success rate of state-of-the-art black-box score-based attacks while maintaining accuracy and reducing confidence-score distortion compared with existing randomized defenses. The adaptive attack demonstrates the vulnerability of AAA to attacks that adaptively modify logits, highlighting a weakness in non-randomized defenses.

Conclusion: RLS provides a practical, plug-and-play defense with favorable accuracy and robustness characteristics against black-box score-based attacks; however, adaptive threats can still compromise non-randomized defenses like AAA, suggesting a need for defenses that maintain robustness under adaptive strategies.

Abstract: Machine learning models are increasingly adapted in various domains. However, adversarial examples pose a significant threat to the reliable deployment of these models. In recent years, some powerful adversarial example attacks have been proposed for the fast and query-efficient generation of adversarial examples, even in black-box scenarios, highlighting the need for scalable, low-cost, and powerful defenses. In this work, we present two contributions to the domain of black-box adversarial example attacks and defenses. First, we propose Random Logit Scaling (RLS), a randomization-based defense against black-box score-based adversarial example attacks. RLS is a plug-and-play, post-processing defense that can be implemented on top of any existing ML model with minimal effort. The idea behind RLS is to confuse an attacker by outputting falsified scores resulting from randomly scaled logits while maintaining the model accuracy. We show that RLS significantly reduces the success rate of state-of-the-art black-box score-based attacks while preserving the accuracy and minimizing confidence score distortion compared to state-of-the-art randomization-based defenses. Second, we introduce a novel adaptive attack against AAA, a SOTA non-randomized black-box defense against black-box score-based attacks that also modifies output logits to confuse attackers, demonstrating its vulnerability against adaptive attacks.

</details>


### [158] [A Minimal Interpretable Architecture for Zero-Shot Reconstruction of Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14937)
*Christoph Jürgen Hemmer,Florian Plaswig,Daniel Durstewitz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A minimal two-parameter model (DynaBase) explains zero-shot DS reconstruction in foundation models and connects to broader literature, achieving competitive performance with analytical tractability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover the minimal mechanisms enabling DS zero-shot reconstruction, enabling interpretability and principled design of DS-focused FMs; strip down DynaMix to bare essentials.

Method: Iterative model reduction from DynaMix to DynaBase; derive linear blend of current latent state and nearest in-context neighbor and its successor; explore a 1-parameter family of maps; analyze training strategies; recover context-parroting at one extreme and chaotic behavior at the other.

Result: DynaBase achieves competitive zero-shot DS reconstruction across chaotic and cyclic systems with very few parameters; allows closed-form one-step MSE; leads to 1-parameter map family; training regimes trade off short-term prediction vs DS reconstruction; small footprint; explains divergent literature observations.

Conclusion: Identifies minimal mechanisms for zero-shot DS reconstruction; reconciles apparently conflicting results; demonstrates interpretability and potential for optimized DS reconstruction; positions simple models as informative building blocks for DS-focused FMs.

Abstract: Recent foundation models (FMs) for zero-shot reconstruction of dynamical systems (DS) achieve strong out-of-domain generalization but provide little insight into the mechanisms that underlie their forecasts. Such an understanding could help to strip down overladen FM architectures to their bare essence and expose the minimal requirements for in-context learning in the DS domain. Toward this goal, here we iteratively reduce a recent powerful SOTA model for DS reconstruction, DynaMix (Hemmer & Durstewitz, 2025), to a minimal interpretable two-parameter form, which we call DynaBase. DynaBase produces forecasts through a linear blend of the current latent state and the nearest in-context neighbor and its temporal successor. Surprisingly, despite its extreme simplicity, DynaBase produces highly competitive zero-shot DS reconstructions across chaotic and cyclic systems, with a negligible parameter load, many orders of magnitude below that of other FMs. Even more, this extreme simplicity permits direct model optimization on DS reconstruction measures, as well as closed-form one-step analytical solutions on prediction MSE. Theoretical and empirical analysis of DynaBase further leads to a 1-parameter family of maps, with the context-parroting algorithm of (Zhang & Gilpin, 2026) recovered at one end, and chaotic (divergent but bounded) behavior at the other. We further show how different training strategies lead to models either optimal for short-term prediction or for DS reconstruction. Thus, DynaBase not only exposes the minimal mechanisms required for producing zero-shot DS reconstruction, but also reconciles within an accessible mathematical frame divergent observations in the literature.

</details>


### [159] [Causal Inference for Sequential Settings under Interference and Latent Confounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14940)
*Phevos Paschalidis,Constantinos Daskalakis,Devavrat Shah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MPLE-based learning for sequential, outcome-interference Ising-like models with latent low-rank confounding; provides non-asymptotic consistency and faithful causal estimates from a single sample; validated on synthetic data and a vaccine-COVID real-world case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address causal inference under interference in high-dimensional, time-dependent binary outcomes with latent confounding; enable scalable estimation from limited data (single sample).

Method: Model outcomes as time-ordered Ising system with external fields capturing treatment and latent confounding; latent factors with low-rank structure; estimate parameters via Maximum Pseudo-Likelihood Estimation; assess non-asymptotic consistency; generate causal estimates by sampling from learned model.

Result: Non-asymptotic consistency established for parameter estimation; faithful recovery of causal quantities after model-based sampling; empirical validation through simulations and a real-world analysis of vaccine rates affecting COVID-19 death rates across US counties.

Conclusion: The MPLE-based framework effectively handles outcome interference and latent confounding in sequential observational data, enabling reliable causal inference and practical application to epidemiological questions.

Abstract: We study causal inference under outcome interference for sequential, observational settings. Specifically, we consider settings where the binary outcomes over N units are Markovian across T time steps. At each time step, the outcomes of N units have dependencies captured through an Ising model; each outcome is also impacted through an external field capturing the effects of its treatment as well as latent confounders. Similar to panel data literature, these latent confounders are modeled to have a low-rank factor structure. Our data is a single sample from this high-dimensional distribution. To estimate causal quantities of interest, we provide a computationally efficient method based on Maximum Pseudo-Likelihood Estimation (MPLE) for learning the model parameters. Under mild assumptions, we establish non-asymptotic consistency for parameter estimation and show this translates to faithful estimation of causal quantities of interest after sampling from the learned model. We demonstrate the efficacy of the method through synthetic experiments as well as a real-world case-study investigating causal effects of vaccine rates on COVID-19 death rates within US counties nationwide.

</details>


### [160] [LongStraw: Long-Context RL Beyond 2M Tokens under a Fixed GPU Budget](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14952)
*Changhai Zhou,Kieran Liu,Yuhua Zhou,Qian Qiao,Jun Gao,Harry Zhang,Irvine Lu,Nolan Ho,Lucian Li,Andrew Lei,Cleon Cheng,Steven Chiang,Yihang Zeng,Di Zhang,Rio Yang,Kaijie Chen,Andrew Chen,Pony Ma,Weizhong Zhang,Cheng Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LongStraw is an architecture-aware execution stack enabling million-token RL post-training within fixed GPU budgets by avoiding autograd on the shared prompt, retaining minimal model state, and replaying short response branches; validated on Qwen3.6-27B and GLM-5.2 under multi-GPU setups, achieving multi-million-token capacity while acknowledging it demonstrates execution capacity, not full training correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between long inference contexts (millions of tokens) and RL post-training workloads (hundreds of thousands of tokens) to support AI agents with long observation histories, tool outputs, and decisions under practical hardware budgets.

Method: Introduce LongStraw: an architecture-aware execution stack that evaluates the shared prompt without autograd, retains only model-specific state for later tokens, and replays short response branches one at a time to reduce the live training graph. Implemented for hybrid recurrent/full-attention Qwen3.6-27B and compressed-attention MoE GLM-5.2. Evaluated on eight H20 GPUs with grouped Qwen scoring/backward passes and a 2–8 group setting, observing memory overhead of ~0.21 GB when increasing group size. Stress test reaches 4.46M positions. On 32 GPUs, end-to-end validation for a 2.1M-token prompt across 78 GLM-5.2 layers.

Result: Demonstrates execution capacity for million-token RL post-training under fixed GPU budgets. Achieves 2.1M positions per group on eight GPUs with minimal additional memory per group; validates end-to-end path on GLM-5.2 up to 2.1M tokens across 78 layers. However, it does not verify full training correctness since the prompt state is detached and some distributed forward/gradient paths remain incomplete.

Conclusion: LongStraw plausibly enables scalable million-token RL post-training under practical hardware constraints by rethinking execution and gradient workflow, but further work is needed to integrate complete training correctness and broader model support.

Abstract: A growing gap separates inference context lengths from RL post-training: inference systems are approaching million-token contexts, while post-training workloads often remain at 256K tokens or below and rely on length generalization at deployment. The gap is especially important for AI agents, whose observations, tool outputs, documents, and prior decisions accumulate over long trajectories. LongStraw is an architecture-aware execution stack for million-token RL post-training under a fixed GPU budget, instantiated with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). It evaluates the shared prompt without autograd, retains only model-specific state needed by later tokens, and replays short response branches one at a time, reducing the live training graph at the cost of additional replay time. We implement it for the hybrid recurrent and full-attention Qwen3.6-27B and the compressed-attention mixture-of-experts GLM-5.2. On eight H20 GPUs, LongStraw completes grouped Qwen scoring and response backward at 2.1M positions for groups of 2 and 8; increasing the group size adds only 0.21 GB of peak allocated memory, while a separate stress test reaches 4.46M positions. On 32 H20 GPUs, we validate the end-to-end LongStraw execution path for a 2.1M-token prompt across all 78 layers of GLM-5.2. These experiments establish execution capacity rather than complete training correctness because the captured prompt state is detached and some distributed forward and gradient composition paths remain incomplete.

</details>


### [161] [Multi-Axis Max@K Reinforcement Learning for Representative Diversity in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14962)
*Ku Onoda,Paavo Parmas,Hiroki Furuta,Soichiro Nishimori,Yuta Oshima,Shohei Taniguchi,Yutaka Matsuo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A group-based reinforcement learning objective (multi-axis max@K) improves target-mode coverage in diffusion-based text-to-image models, increasing fairness across prompts while preserving image quality and alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard T2I models often generate limited visually distinct modes for the same prompt, leading to biased or skewed representations especially for person-centric prompts. The paper formalizes this as target-mode coverage and aims to broaden mode coverage to reduce demographic skew.

Method: Introduce multi-axis max@K: for a group of samples and one score per target category, take per-category maxima across samples and sum them. This yields a credit assignment where a sample gets positive weight for a category only if it increases that category's group-wide maximum. Validate on a synthetic mixture and SD3.5-M with deterministic color rewards; extend to perceived-appearance fairness. Evaluation uses three automatic evaluators on held-out prompts.

Result: The approach yields improvements in fairness across evaluators: Fairness Score increases by 0.23–0.36 relative to the base model, while preserving image quality and text alignment.

Conclusion: Group-based multi-axis max@K effectively expands target-mode coverage and mitigates demographic skew in T2I outputs without sacrificing prompt fidelity or image quality.

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) models can synthesize realistic, prompt-aligned images, yet samples generated for the same prompt often cover only a small subset of visually distinct modes. This limits the diversity of images, and for person-centric prompts, can reflect or amplify demographic skew. We formalize this problem as coverage of a predefined set of semantically specified modes, which we call target-mode coverage. We then propose multi-axis max@K, a group-based reinforcement learning objective for improving such coverage in diffusion-based T2I models. Given a group of samples and one score per target category, multi-axis max@K first takes the maximum score across samples for each category and then sums these category-wise maxima. The resulting credit assignment gives a sample positive weight on a category only when it increases that category's group-wise maximum, allowing different samples to contribute to different categories. We first validate the credit-assignment mechanism on a synthetic mixture and on SD3.5-M using deterministic pixel-based color rewards. We then evaluate the same objective on perceived-appearance fairness. Across three automatic evaluators on held-out prompts, multi-axis max@K improves the Fairness Score by 0.23-0.36 relative to the base model, while maintaining image quality and text alignment.

</details>


### [162] [Multimodal Semantic-Aware Contrastive Learning For False Negative Mitigation in 3D Medical Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14995)
*Sara Ketabi,Matthias W. Wagner,Cynthia Hawkins,Uri Tabori,Birgit Betina Ertl-Wagner,Farzad Khalvati*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A semantic-aware multimodal contrastive learning framework (MseaCL) is proposed to reduce false negatives in medical multimodal data by leveraging semantic similarity between radiology reports, improving downstream tasks after pretraining on pediatric MRI and reports (e.g., +22.6% AUC for brain tumor molecular classification).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard instance-based CL treats all non-paired samples as negatives, which is problematic in medical data where samples share high-level semantic attributes; reducing false negatives is crucial for robust multimodal representations.

Method: MseaCL trains on a pediatric cohort of 3D brain MRI scans and radiology reports, using semantic similarity between reports as a guiding signal during contrastive learning to mitigate the impact of semantically similar false negatives.

Result: When used as a pretraining stage, the method yields substantial downstream gains, notably at least a 22.6% absolute increase in AUC for pediatric brain tumor molecular classification.

Conclusion: Semantic-aware multimodal learning can produce more robust and clinically meaningful representations, with pretraining on semantically aligned cross-modal data improving downstream diagnostic tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal Contrastive Learning (CL) has shown significant performance in aligning representations across various data modalities and improving downstream tasks, especially in healthcare. It works by minimizing the distance between matched (positive) data modalities, while maximizing the distance between mismatched (negative) samples. Traditional CL frameworks typically assume instance-based correspondence within data batches, treating all non-paired samples as negatives. However, this assumption often fails in medical settings, where samples may share high-level semantic attributes, leading to false negatives that degrade representation quality. In this paper, we propose Multimodal Semantic-Aware Contrastive Learning (MseaCL), a CL framework trained on a pediatric cohort of 3D brain magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) scans and radiology reports. The goal of this framework is to mitigate the impact of semantically similar false negative samples by incorporating semantic similarity between radiology reports, as a guiding signal during the learning process. Our results indicate that applying this framework as a pretraining stage can achieve notable improvements in downstream tasks, e.g., at least a 22.6\% increase in the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of pediatric brain tumor molecular classification, demonstrating its potential for more robust and semantically aligned multimodal representations in clinical applications.

</details>


### [163] [Kernel weighted importance sampling for off-policy evaluation in contextual bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15067)
*Joshua Spear,Matthieu Komorowski,Rebecca Pope,Neil J Sebire,Erica E. M. Moodie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Kernel-WIS: a kernelized off-policy evaluator for contextual bandits using offline data; provably asymptotically consistent and empirically superior to strong baselines (including vanilla WIS), even under behavior policy misspecification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Off-policy evaluation (OPE) in contextual bandits using only offline data faces high variance and robustness issues, especially with policy misspecification. There is a need for an estimator that is both consistent and more stable than standard weighted importance sampling approaches.

Method: Introduce Kernel-WIS, a kernel-based weighting estimator that combines the boundedness characteristic of vanilla WIS with the linearity of vanilla importance sampling to produce a robust, bounded, and tractable OPE estimator for offline data in contextual bandits.

Result: The estimator is shown to be asymptotically consistent and empirically outperforms strong baselines, including vanilla WIS, particularly in complex settings with misspecified behavior policies.

Conclusion: Kernel-WIS provides a robust and consistent offline OPE method for contextual bandits, delivering improved empirical performance by leveraging the boundedness and linearity properties of established IS approaches.

Abstract: This article presents a novel estimator for performing off-policy evaluation using only offline data for contextual bandits. The proposed estimator, Kernel-WIS is demonstrated to be asymptotically consistent and to empirically outperform strong baselines (including vanilla weighted importance sampling), particularly under complex conditions including behaviour policy miss-specification. The benefit of Kernel-WIS is derived from combining the bounded property of vanilla weighted importance sampling with the linearity of vanilla importance sampling.

</details>


### [164] [An Introduction to Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics for Engineering Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15077)
*Yao Cheng Li,Ana Larrañaga,Steven L. Brunton,Urban Fasel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SINDy provides a data-efficient, interpretable framework for discovering governing equations from small engineering datasets, with extensions for noise robustness and parameterization, demonstrated through UAV system identification and a chaotic thermosyphon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Engineering problems often involve poorly characterized dynamics and limited data; surrogate models like neural nets require large datasets and lack interpretability. SINDy offers a sparse, physics-inspired approach to identify concise, interpretable governing equations from limited data.

Method: Present the standard SINDy algorithm and progressively extend it with noise-robust weak-form and ensemble variants, plus constrained and parametrizable formulations. Structure the discussion as a three-part tutorial: (1) standard SINDy, (2) extensions with practical engineering details, and (3) concrete case studies (UAV identification and chaotic thermosyphon).

Result: The tutorial demonstrates that SINDy is simple to implement yet flexible for engineering applications, providing actionable identification tools and robust variants; case studies illustrate practical feasibility and interpretability of the learned models.

Conclusion: SINDy is a practical, interpretable, and adaptable tool for engineering system identification, with extensions that improve robustness and expressiveness. The tutorial format and real-world case studies aim to broaden adoption among engineers.

Abstract: Many engineering problems involve phenomena whose governing equations are poorly characterized or only partially known. Surrogate modeling techniques such as neural networks can capture the behavior of these systems, but they typically demand large training datasets that are difficult to obtain in engineering contexts and yield models with limited physical interpretability. The Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics (SINDy) method addresses both limitations by performing sparse regression over libraries of candidate nonlinear terms, recovering interpretable governing equations from comparatively small datasets. Although SINDy has been demonstrated extensively on canonical benchmark systems, its application to practical engineering problems is less widely documented. This tutorial introduces the SINDy method and progressively builds toward its main extensions, from noise-robust weak-form and ensembling-based variants to constrained and parametrizable formulations. The paper and the accompanying tutorial (available at https://github.com/paullililili/SINDy4Engineers) is organized in three parts: the first introduces the standard SINDy algorithm and progressively extends it, inviting readers without prior knowledge to follow each step and adapt the methods to their own problems; the remaining two parts present detailed case studies on (1) the system identification of an unmanned aerial vehicle and (2) a chaotic thermosyphon heat exchanger. Through these examples, we aim to demonstrate that SINDy is simple to implement yet flexible enough to serve as a valuable identification tool for advanced engineering applications.

</details>


### [165] [Evaluating covariate balance for long time horizon Markov decision processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15080)
*Joshua Spear,Rebecca Pope,Neil J Sebire*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Covariate balance diagnostics are inconclusive for offline RL–based treatment recommendations: either high risk of bias in existing studies or insufficiency of current metrics to detect issues; overall robustness is not established, with future methodological directions proposed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether covariate balance metrics can detect hidden confounding and model misspecification in offline RL used for treatment recommendations, and to evaluate the robustness of current studies.

Method: Apply covariate balance diagnostics to studies employing offline RL for treatment recommendations; assess whether diagnostics reveal bias or misspecification; synthesize implications for robustness.

Result: Findings indicate either a high risk of bias in existing offline RL treatment‑recommendation studies or that covariate balance metrics are insufficient to diagnose such issues; there is no evidence of statistical robustness for these studies.

Conclusion: Call for future research directions to develop more methodologically robust offline RL applications in treatment recommendation, including improved diagnostics for confounding and model misspecification.

Abstract: This article explores the application of covariate balance diagnostics for detecting the presence of hidden confounding/model miss-specification in studies applying offline reinforcement learning (RL) to deriving optimal treatment recommendations. The results demonstrate that, either there is a high risk of bias within existing offline RL studies for treatment recommendations or, existing covariate balance metrics are not sufficient to assess such studies. Regardless, existing offline RL studies cannot be concluded as being statistically robust. The conclusions propose future research directions for obtaining more methodologically robust applications of offline RL to treatment recommendation problems.

</details>


### [166] [Learning in Infinitesimal Non-Compositional Sketches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15107)
*Sridhar Mahadevan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A categorical framework (LINCS) reframes ML as learning via universal factorization in sketches, uses tangent lifts to study infinitesimal compositionality, and defines an INC endofunctor to build a tower of factorization problems. It proves the existence of a final INC coalgebra under a realizability condition; experimental evaluation is planned in DL, LLMs, and RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repair non-compositionality in ML by treating failures to factor through universal constructions as fundamental defects. Provide a principled, category-theoretic approach to compositionality and robustness under infinitesimal perturbations in learning systems.

Method: Define learning sketches with graphs and constraints (D, L, K). Base defect Obs(Fact_S(D)) measures obstruction to universal factorization. Apply tangent functor to obtain TD. Define Tangent Learning Sketches with Cockett-Cruttwell tangent structure. Define INC endofunctor that iterates tangent lift to generate a tower D, TD, T^2D, ... Formulate ML as finding a coalgebraic fixed point where successive tangent unfoldings stabilize (νT_INC). Use Aczel–Mendler theorem to prove existence of a final INC coalgebra under set-based class realization creating its final carrier.

Result: Theoretical: existence of a final INC coalgebra under the stated realizability conditions; the framework provides a principled method to analyze non-compositionality via universal factorization and its infinitesimal perturbations; empirical validation is planned but not reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: LINCS offers a rigorous tangent-category-based paradigm for studying and repairing non-compositionality in ML. It recasts learning as a coalgebraic fixed-point problem over a tower of tangent lifts, with future work centered on concrete experiments in deep learning, LLMs, and reinforcement learning, as described in companion papers.

Abstract: This paper develops a categorical framework -- Learning in Infinitesimal Non-Compositional Sketches (LINCS) -- as the repair of non-compositionality: failures of diagrams to factor through quotient sketches lifted to the tangent category setting. Machine learning problems are specified as sketches: graphs with commutativity conditions $\mathcal D$, limit cones $\mathcal L$, and colimit cocones $\mathcal K$, generalizing the usual scalarization of loss functions or vector space assumptions. Non-compositionality is defined purely as failure of a universal factorization problem, not as arithmetic error between the desired and actual predictions. Given a learning sketch $\mathbb S=(S,\mathcal D,\mathcal L,\mathcal K)$, whose underlying graph is $S$, and a model $D:J \rightarrow C$, the base defect is the obstruction to factorization $\mbox{Obs}(\mbox{Fact}_{\mathbb S}(D))$. The tangent lift applies the tangent functor $T$ to obtain $TD:J \rightarrow C$, and LINCS is defined as the obstruction $\mbox{Obs}(\mbox{Fact}_{\mathbb S}(TD))$ -- asking whether infinitesimal perturbations preserve the compositionality constraints.The paper also introduces Tangent Learning Sketches, which are sketches equipped with Cockett-Cruttwell tangent structure. The paper defines the INC endofunctor, which iterates the tangent lift, producing a tower $D,TD,T^2D, \cdots$ of factorization problems. ML is thereby formulated as the search for a coalgebraic fixed point where successive tangent unfoldings stabilize ($νT_{\mbox{INC}}$). Using the Aczel--Mendler theorem, we prove existence of a final INC coalgebra whenever $T_{\mbox{INC}}$ admits a set-based class realization that creates its final carrier. A detailed experimental evaluation of LINCS is underway in a number of concrete ML settings, including deep learning, large language models, and reinforcement learning, and is described in companion papers.

</details>


### [167] [On-Policy Delta Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15161)
*Byeongho Heo,Jaehui Hwang,Sangdoo Yun,Dongyoon Han*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduce a delta signal for on-policy distillation—OPD^2. The delta is the difference between a teacher and its base model before instruction tuning for reasoning. This signal improves distillation effectiveness across math, science, and code reasoning benchmarks, enabling strong reasoning LLMs with short post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard on-policy distillation uses the teacher's output distribution, which may not capture the changes induced by instruction tuning for reasoning. A signal that isolates the reasoning-shaping changes (the delta between teacher and base) could transfer reasoning capabilities more directly and efficiently.

Method: Define the delta signal as teacher minus base model outputs prior to instruction tuning. Use this signal in on-policy distillation to train a student model (OPD^2). Evaluate on mathematics, science, and code-reasoning benchmarks with ablation and cross-domain tests; emphasize post-training efficiency.

Result: OPD^2 consistently outperforms conventional on-policy distillation across the tested domains. The delta signal provides a stronger supervisory signal for reasoning capabilities, achieving strong performance with a short post-training period.

Conclusion: Delta signaling is an effective objective for on-policy distillation, enabling efficient transfer of reasoning capabilities in LLMs. OPD^2 offers a practical post-training method with broad applicability; code release planned.

Abstract: On-policy distillation is an alternative post-training method in reinforcement learning that alleviates the constraints imposed by reward models by providing token-level supervision from a teacher model. Although on-policy distillation has been studied and applied across various settings, its fundamental design remains underexplored. In this paper, we introduce a new distillation reward, termed the delta signal, instead of directly imitating the teacher's output distribution. The delta signal is defined as the difference between the teacher model and its base model prior to instruction tuning for reasoning capability. It therefore captures the changes induced by reasoning tuning and provides a more direct signal for transferring reasoning capabilities. Using extensive empirical evidence, we show that the delta signal substantially improves on-policy distillation and refer to the new distillation method as On-Policy Delta Distillation (OPD$^2$). Experiments across mathematics, science, and code-reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that OPD$^2$ consistently outperforms conventional on-policy distillation, enabling reasoning LLMs to achieve strong performance with only a short post-training period. Code will be available at https://github.com/naver-ai/opd2

</details>


### [168] [RTS Smoother-Guided Learning of Physics-Based Neural Differential Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15180)
*Ahmet Demirkaya,Georgios Stratis,Tales Imbiriba,Zachary D. Danziger,Deniz Erdogmus*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hybrid neural–physics framework for recovering unknown ODE components with partial observations; alternates RTS smoothing for latent state estimation and backpropagation to train neural surrogates, yielding interpretable models and improved long-horizon predictions across linear, nonlinear, and stiff dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unknown dynamics in ODE models and limited observability hinder accurate state estimation and prediction. A hybrid approach preserves known mechanistic terms while learning missing components with neural networks.

Method: Two-stage iterative procedure: (1) assume parameters known and use Rauch–Tung–Striebel smoothing to infer latent states from measurements; (2) fix the smoothed trajectories and train neural network components by backpropagation. The process alternates until convergence under a stopping criterion.

Result: The method learns the missing ODE components from incomplete measurements across benchmark systems, maintaining interpretable structure, and improving latent-state reconstruction and long-horizon prediction.

Conclusion: A principled framework that combines physics-based structure with data-driven components under partial observability. It handles linear, nonlinear, and stiff dynamics and shows promise for recovering unknown dynamics while preserving interpretability.

Abstract: Ordinary differential equations (ODEs) are widely used to model dynamical systems in physics, biology, neuroscience, and physiology, but in many applications some equations of the dynamics are unknown and only a subset of the state variables are measured. We propose a hybrid neural--physics framework in which the known components of the ODE are kept explicit and the missing components are represented by a neural network. The proposed method consists of two stages where we alternate between state and parameter estimation and iterate until a predetermined criterion is met. Specifically, in the first step, we treat the model parameters as being known and we infer the latent states from the available measurements using a Rauch--Tung--Striebel (RTS) smoother. In the second stage, we treat the smoothed trajectories as being known and use them to estimate the neural networks' parameters through backpropagation. We evaluate the method on benchmark systems spanning linear, nonlinear, and stiff dynamics under partial state observation. Across these settings, the proposed method learns missing ODE components from incomplete measurements while exploiting and retaining interpretable mechanistic structure and improving latent-state reconstruction and long-horizon prediction.

</details>


### [169] [Data Driven Block Replacement Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15229)
*Aniruddhan Ganesaraman,VIdyadhar Kulkarni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Data-driven learning of the optimal block replacement interval for N iid machines under unknown lifetimes, via bandit and nonparametric renewal estimation; with regret guarantees, policy consistency, and threshold-structure insights for related MDP formulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce maintenance costs by learning an optimal replacement interval from censored operational data when lifetimes are unknown.

Method: Treat k in {1,..,K} as arms in a stochastic multi-armed bandit with mixed complete and right-censored lifetimes; develop Hoeffding- and Bernstein-based LCB algorithms achieving O(K log T) regret (Lai–Robbins bound). Exploit a nested observation property for correlated variants achieving O((K−k*) log T) regret, requiring only O(1) pulls of suboptimal arms. Use a Kaplan–Meier renewal estimator to nonparametrically learn the lifetime distribution from censored data, yielding almost-sure policy consistency and near-zero incremental regret in long horizons. Analyze two average-cost MDPs: a time-elapsed formulation showing block replacement is optimal within its policy class for any lifetime distribution, and an age-vector formulation proving a monotone threshold structure under increasing failure rate distributions and providing a benchmark. Numerical experiments support the theoretical ordering and identify cost gaps between block- and age-dependent replacement.

Result: Proposes practical learning algorithms with provable regret bounds (O(K log T); improved O((K−k*) log T) for nested observations), plus a Kaplan–Meier renewal method with strong consistency. Establishes optimality within a policy class (time-elapsed MDP) and a monotone threshold structure (IFR) for age-vector MDP, with empirical validation of ordering and gaps between block and age-dependent policies.

Conclusion: Block replacement is optimal within its class for arbitrary lifetimes; data-driven methods (bandits plus nonparametric renewal estimation) effectively learn and approach the cost of the optimal policy, with structural insights guiding age-dependent alternatives and confirming practical relevance via experiments.

Abstract: We develop data-driven algorithms for maintaining $N$ independent identical machines under a \textit{block replacement policy}, in which each machine is replaced upon failure and all machines are jointly replaced at regular intervals of length $k$. The goal is to learn the cost-minimizing interval $k^*$ from operational data when the lifetime distribution is unknown. At each decision epoch, the operator selects $k \in \{1, 2, \ldots, K\}$, observes the resulting failure history (a mixture of complete and right-censored lifetimes) and incurs a per-unit-time cost governed by the renewal function. We formulate this as a stochastic multi-armed bandit and propose Hoeffding- and Bernstein-based lower-confidence-bound algorithms achieving $O(K \log T)$ regret, matching the Lai--Robbins lower bound. Exploiting a nested observation property unique to block replacement, correlated variants attain $O((K-k^*)\log T)$ regret and require only $O(1)$ direct pulls of suboptimal arms $k < k^*$. A complementary Kaplan--Meier renewal algorithm estimates the lifetime distribution nonparametrically from censored data, achieving almost-sure policy consistency and empirically near-zero incremental regret at long horizons. We additionally analyze two average-cost MDPs: a time-elapsed formulation establishing that block replacement is optimal within its policy class for any lifetime distribution, and an age-vector formulation proving a monotone threshold structure under increasing failure rate distributions and providing a gold-standard cost benchmark. Numerical experiments confirm the theoretical ordering and reveal structural cost gaps between optimal block and age-dependent replacement.

</details>


### [170] [Mutable Low-Rank Sketches for Retrain-Free Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15242)
*Hector J. Garcia,Nick Clayton*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces mutable sketches using a KP-tree to mitigate embedding staleness in two-stage recommenders by updating user embeddings on the fly with new ratings; provides a monotone error-tightening guarantee (Theorem 1) and shows strong empirical gains (faster updates, lower RMSE, rapid cold-start recommendations) with density-aware sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embedding staleness in two-stage recommender systems during batch retraining; need real-time adaptation and efficient data usage, especially in sparse data regimes.

Method: Store per-user preferences in a KP-tree (sparse segment tree with sum aggregation); fit a low-rank projection once; recompute embeddings online as ratings arrive; prove monotone tightening of the prediction error envelope (Theorem 1); compare sampling strategies, showing norm-proportional sampling improves item coverage in sparse settings.

Result: KuaiRec: RMSE 0.810 with 1.8% data read versus ALS 0.822 with 100% data read; 8x faster per-batch updates; new user gets personalized recommendations in <1 ms after first rating with no retraining; norm-proportional sampling yields 40-130% better item coverage on sparse data (<1% density).

Conclusion: Mutable sketches offer accurate, low-latency updates without retraining, addressing embedding staleness more effectively than FunkSVD/ALS in both accuracy and efficiency, particularly in sparse regimes; theoretical guarantee complements empirical gains.

Abstract: A common bottleneck in two-stage recommendation is embedding staleness: when a user rates a new item, their embedding remains fixed until the next retrain cycle. We propose mutable sketches, which store each user's preferences in a KP-tree (a sparse segment tree with sum aggregation), fit a low-rank projection once, and recompute embeddings on-the-fly as ratings arrive. We prove that each new observation monotonically tightens the prediction error envelope (Theorem 1), a guarantee that FunkSVD and eALS lack. On KuaiRec, the mutable sketch achieves 0.810 RMSE at 1.8% data read vs. ALS 0.822 at 100%, with 8x faster per-batch updates. A new user receives personalized recommendations in <1 ms after their first rating, with no model retraining required. A comparison of sampling strategies across density regimes shows that the KP-tree's norm-proportional sampling provides 40-130% better item coverage on sparse data (<1% density), while uniform sampling suffices on dense matrices.

</details>


### [171] [Decoding Market Emotion from Blockchain Activity: A Data-Driven Sentiment Classifier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15258)
*Arthur G. Bubolz,Abreu Quevedo,Giancarlo Lucca,Rafael A. Berri,Eduardo Borges,Bruno L. Dalmazo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Combines on-chain, price history, and Twitter sentiment to explain Bitcoin market sentiment using ML; XGBoost achieves ~0.84 F1 in sentiment classification; SHAP reveals on-chain feature contributions; supports data-driven crypto analysis and potential for deep learning improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand drivers of Bitcoin market sentiment in a data-rich, multi-source setting, beyond price prediction, to improve transparency and actionable insights.

Method: Integrates on-chain metrics, historical BTC prices, and daily Twitter sentiment classifications into a normalized dataset; tests multiple ML models with cross-validation; XGBoost is top performer for sentiment classification; SHAP used for feature attribution to enhance interpretability.

Result: XGBoost achieves average F1-score ~0.84 for sentiment classification; SHAP quantifies the contribution of on-chain features to predictions; the data combination yields meaningful predictive signals for market analysis.

Conclusion: Multi-source data integration enhances sentiment explainability in cryptocurrency markets; supports data-driven analysis and lays groundwork for future deep learning enhancements.

Abstract: The growing use of Bitcoin as a decentralized digital asset and investment tool has sparked strong interest in understanding its market behavior. This study presents a new approach to analyze Bitcoin market sentiment by combining on-chain and financial data with social media posts. Unlike models that aim to predict prices, this work focuses on explaining market sentiment using blockchain transactions, historical price data of Bitcoin, and daily Twitter sentiment classifications. The method merges sentiment trends with on-chain and financial metrics, normalized into a dataset for detailed market analysis. Multiple machine learning models were tested using cross-validation, with Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) emerging as the most reliable model for classifying sentiment, achieving an average F1-score of about 0.84. SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations), a game theory-based method for model interpretability, was used to quantify the contribution of on-chain features to the model's predictions, improving transparency. The results indicate that this data combination yields meaningful predictive signals and insights, supporting data-driven cryptocurrency analysis and future improvements with deep learning.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [172] [Adaptive Control of Motor-Position-Controlled Flexible Joint Robots with Uncertain Joint Stiffness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14177)
*Annika Kirner,Grazia Zambella,Igor Kovacevic,Hannes Höppner,Jee-Hwan Ryu,Christian Ott*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Adaptive control for position-controlled flexible-joint robots with uncertain nonlinear stiffness using an input-dependent regressor and an implicit control law, validated experimentally.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate stiffness models are often unavailable and joint stiffness varies with operation, wear, and aging, limiting model-based control performance.

Method: Adaptive law that updates estimates of the nonlinear torque–deflection relation for each joint; operates with an implicit control law and a control-input-dependent regressor matrix to account for uncertain joint stiffness; includes robustness analysis against motor position controller errors.

Result: Experimental validation on a flexible joint with nonlinear stiffness demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed adaptive approach.

Conclusion: An adaptive, input-dependent regressor with implicit control improves robustness to stiffness uncertainties for position-controlled flexible-joint robots and shows promise in experimental settings.

Abstract: Model-based control of flexible joint robots with position-controlled actuators relies on accurate knowledge of the joint compliance. In practice, precise stiffness models are often unavailable as the properties of physical elastic elements vary with operating conditions and slowly change over time due to wear and aging. To improve model-based control of these systems, we propose an adaptive control approach in this work, which updates an estimate of the uncertain, nonlinear torque-deflection relation of each joint. As opposed to classical adaptive control approaches for non-elastic robots, we rely on an implicit control law and a control-input-dependent regressor matrix to account for the uncertain joint stiffness. We analyze robustness of the approach against errors induced by the motor position controller. Experimental results on a flexible joint with nonlinear stiffness characteristics demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [173] [Semantic Audio-driven Understanding for Dynamic Humanoid Whole Body Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14182)
*J. M. A. Marcelo,M. Brienza,E. Bugli,L. Comito,D. Nardi,D. D. Bloisi,V. Suriani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A multi-modal audio-driven framework that autonomously maps music and speech to humanoid motion policies via a unified RL-based scheduler, demonstrated on simulation and a Unitree G1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current humanoid robotics performances are largely scripted or externally triggered, limiting autonomy and responsiveness in dynamic environments; there is a need for autonomous, real-time interpretation of continuous audio streams to drive motion.

Method: A two-branch audio processing system: music branch uses audio fingerprinting and semantic embeddings to identify tracks and align them with motion policies; speech branch maps utterances to a discrete library of imitation-learned skills. Both branches feed a unified interface that schedules skill execution over a reinforcement learning control pipeline.

Result: Validation in both simulation and on a Unitree G1 humanoid showing robust sim-to-real transfer and consistent audio-conditioned policy selection.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the feasibility of autonomous, real-time audio-conditioned humanoid control by coupling semantic audio understanding with a library of skills and RL control, enabling responsive, context-aware motion in dynamic environments.

Abstract: Recent advances in humanoid robotics and reinforcement learning have enabled the acquisition of highly expressive whole-body motion policies. However, most robotic performances remain based on pre-scripted sequences or externally triggered behaviors, limiting autonomy and responsiveness to dynamic environments. In this work, we introduce a novel multi-modal orchestration framework for semantic audio-driven humanoid control, enabling robots to autonomously select and execute appropriate motion skills in real time. The system processes continuous audio streams and routes them into music or speech branches. Music input is handled via audio fingerprinting and semantic embeddings to retrieve track identity and temporal alignment, allowing dynamic mapping between musical segments and motion policies. Speech input is grounded into a discrete library of imitation-learned skills, enabling direct human-robot interaction. Both modalities share a unified interface that schedules skill execution over a reinforcement learning control pipeline.
