We validate the approach in simulation and on a Unitree G1 humanoid, showing robust sim-to-real transfer and consistent audio-conditioned policy selection. Supplementary materials are available at the following site: https://lab-rococo-sapienza.github.io/semantic-WBC/

</details>


### [174] [Open-AoE: An Open Egocentric Manipulation Dataset and Toolchain for Embodied Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14183)
*Zishuo Li,Bowen Yang,Changtao Miao,Kai Zhu,Hao Chen,Qingze Guan,Zhengxing Wu,Wanke Zhan,Yang Sun,Zhiyi Huang,Zitong Shan,Zhenchao Jin,Jiadong Hong,Taowen Wang,Yushi Feng,You Liu,Yibo Wang,Yifan Yang,Zhaowen Zhou,Man Luo,Hao Cheng,Bo Zhang,Jianshu Li,Jiansheng Cai,Guocai Yao,Jize Zhang,Chenhao Lin,Renjing Xu,Lequan Yu,Chao Shen,Chunhua Shen,Zhe Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Open-AoE: an open egocentric manipulation dataset and toolchain spanning smartphone capture to model training, featuring ~2000 hours of manipulation video from 500+ contributors across 400+ smartphones, with text annotations, MANO hand poses, camera trajectories, and temporally localized atomic actions; includes a data processing pipeline (temporal action segmentation, semantic annotation, hand and camera reconstruction) and a downstream toolchain for visualization, cross-embodiment retargeting, model-specific data conversion, and training recipes for VLA policies, WAMs, and World Models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the lack of common, end-to-end, open resources that couple scalable, low-cost data capture with manipulation-level annotations and reusable learning tools for embodied AI; aims to lower barriers to data contribution and reuse.

Method: Collect a large-scale egocentric manipulation dataset via smartphone capture from 500+ contributors using 400+ smartphones; provide structured annotations (text, MANO hand poses, camera trajectories, atomic actions); develop a processing pipeline for turning raw recordings into structured samples (temporal action segmentation, semantic annotation, hand reconstruction, camera reconstruction); supply a downstream toolchain for visualization, cross-embodiment retargeting, model-specific data conversion, and training recipes for VLA policies, WAMs, and World Models.

Result: Delivers an open infrastructure combining data, processing, and tooling: ~2000 hours of video, rich annotations, and end-to-end processing, enabling embodied model training, human-to-robot transfer, and world modeling with reduced data-collection and reuse barriers.

Conclusion: Open-AoE integrates scalable capture, structured processing, and downstream adaptation to provide a practical open infrastructure for embodied model training and open science in egocentric manipulation.

Abstract: Egocentric videos of human manipulation provide scalable supervision for embodied intelligence, yet existing resources rarely combine low-cost continuous capture, manipulation-level structured annotations, and reusable tools for robot learning. We present Open-AoE, an open, community-oriented egocentric manipulation dataset and toolchain spanning the full pipeline from smartphone capture to model training. Its first release contains approximately 2,000 hours of manipulation video collected in natural environments by 500+ contributors using 400+ smartphones. The dataset provides text annotations, MANO-based hand poses, camera trajectories, and temporally localized atomic actions. Open-AoE further includes a data processing pipeline that transforms raw recordings into structured samples through temporal action segmentation, semantic annotation, hand reconstruction, and camera trajectory reconstruction. Meanwhile, we provide a separate downstream toolchain supports visualization, cross-embodiment retargeting, model-specific data conversion, and training recipes for VLA policies, WAMs, and World Models. By integrating scalable capture, structured processing, and downstream adaptation, Open-AoE reduces the barriers to both data contribution and reuse, providing practical open infrastructure for embodied model training, human-to-robot transfer, and world modeling.

</details>


### [175] [Never Too Late for Force: Accelerating VLA Post-Training with Reactive Force Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14236)
*Yi Wang,Wendi Chen,Zimo Wen,Han Xue,Xueqi Li,Wenye Yu,Zhijie Chen,Hao Yang,Jun Lv,Chuan Wen,Cewu Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LIFT adds force-aware post-training to pretrained VLA policies by grafting a reactive force-expert with force memory and online corrective data, enabling faster, more robust contact-rich manipulation across multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action policies are strong for language-conditioned manipulation but struggle during contact-rich phases (occluded depth, small force errors). There is a need to inject tactile/force reactivity without erasing existing knowledge.

Method: Introduce a reactive action expert alongside the original policy, initialized from pretrained weights; inject recent 6D end-effector force via causal force memory and zero-initialized cross-attention to refresh actions during execution. Employ online DAgger with a mix of offline task-alignment data and human-corrected online rollouts to address policy-dependent distribution shift in force feedback.

Result: LIFT learns faster and achieves higher performance than vision-only post-training across towel folding, book insertion, and Hanoi ring placement; ablations show reactive force memory and online corrective data are crucial for robustness in contact-rich manipulation.

Conclusion: LIFT demonstrates effective force-aware post-training for VLA policies, with public code and data planned, and provides a path toward more robust, contact-rich robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policies provide strong language-conditioned manipulation knowledge, but they remain largely vision-driven and can struggle once manipulation enters contact states where the scene is occluded, depth is ambiguous, or small force errors push execution off the offline demonstration distribution. We present LIFT (Late Reactive Injection of Force for VLA Post-Training), a force-aware post-training framework that adds contact reactivity to a pretrained VLA policy while preserving its general manipulation knowledge. LIFT grafts a reactive action expert beside the original action expert, initializes it from pretrained action weights, and injects recent 6D end-effector force through causal force memory and zero-initialized cross attention, enabling actions to be refreshed during execution. To address the policy-dependent distribution shift of contact feedback, LIFT further couples reactive force injection with an online DAgger loop that trains on a mixture of offline task-alignment data and human-corrected online rollouts. Across towel folding, book insertion, and Hanoi ring placement, LIFT learns faster and reaches higher performance than vision-only post-training, while ablations show that reactive force memory and online corrective data are both important for robust contact-rich manipulation. Our code and data will be publicly available.

</details>


### [176] [MEMORA: Embodied Action Memory from Egocentric Videos for Reasoning and Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14252)
*Zihao Yu,Xiu Yuan,Chongjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MEMORA introduces Embodied Action Memory (EAM) with a formation-consolidation-retrieval lifecycle and four memory stores, yielding strong memory-grounded planning improvements on EPIC-KITCHENS data and enabling better robot control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon robot planning requires persistent, interpretable memory of embodied experiences beyond the present scene; memory enables reuse of procedures and regularities for future goals.

Method: Proposes EAM and MEMORA architecture with four typed stores (Environment, Entity, Activity, Inferred Knowledge). Online editing maintains object identities/state histories; offline consolidation abstracts experience into reusable procedures and participant-specific regularities. MEMORA-Bench evaluates on 45 hours of EPIC-KITCHENS-100 extension across 18 participants, focusing on memory-grounded planning (including unseen goals) and memory-assessment tasks. Tests four open-weight language models.

Result: Full MEMORA (editing + typed stores + consolidation) achieves the strongest performance among memory conditions. Memory-assessment accuracy improves by up to 20.5 points; Robot-Grounded Plan score improves by up to 16.6% relative. Qualitative robot deployment demonstrates how memory-informed language plans interface with downstream control.

Conclusion: Editable, consolidated memory supplies remembered context for robot planning, supporting robust long-horizon planning and suggesting a viable memory-centric approach to integrating experience into robotic decision-making.

Abstract: Long-horizon robot planning requires more than predicting what actions will do next; it also requires memory of the embodied experience that makes future goals interpretable. People do not plan from the present scene alone: they draw on remembered places, object-state changes, prior procedures, and regularities revealed through repeated action. We formulate Embodied Action Memory (EAM) as the capability to form, maintain, and use such experience as a persistent memory state for later decisions. MEMORA realizes EAM with a formation-consolidation-retrieval lifecycle and four typed stores: Environment Memory, Entity Memory, Activity Memory, and Inferred Knowledge. Online editing maintains object identities and state histories as new observations arrive; offline consolidation abstracts repeated experience into reusable procedures and participant-specific regularities. MEMORA-Bench evaluates this lifecycle on 45 hours of EPIC-KITCHENS-100 extension video across 18 participants through memory-grounded planning, including previously unseen goals, and a complementary memory-assessment task. Across four open-weight language models, full MEMORA--combining editing, typed stores, and consolidation--achieves the strongest aggregate results among the evaluated memory conditions. It improves memory-assessment accuracy by up to 20.5 points over the strongest controlled baseline and improves out-of-distribution Robot-Grounded Plan score by up to 16.6% relative. A qualitative two-task robot deployment study further illustrates how memory-grounded language plans can interface with downstream control, while the overall results show that editable, consolidated memory can supply remembered context for robot planning. Project page: https://yuzihaowashu.github.io/MEMORA/

</details>


### [177] [Stochastic Filtering for Quorum Sensing in Robot Swarms under Anonymous Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14262)
*Fabio Oddi,Andreagiovanni Reina,Vito Trianni*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ANT_k filtering reduces double-counting in anonymous quorum sampling, stabilizing swarm estimates; AN is fast but biased, ANT is more accurate but slower to converge, ANT_k offers best stability at the cost of longer recovery from errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable scalable, anonymous quorum estimation in robot swarms while mitigating double-counting bias that arises when message sources cannot be distinguished.

Method: Introduce a stochastic filtering protocol ANT_k inspired by k-priority sampling and compare it against a baseline anonymous protocol AN and a randomized ANT variant. Assess performance in terms of accuracy, convergence speed, and recovery after errors.

Result: Baseline AN is fast but highly inaccurate due to double-counting; ANT improves accuracy but suffers from information inertia and slower convergence; ANT_k filtering reduces temporary errors and stabilizes the quorum estimate, at the expense of longer recovery time after errors.

Conclusion: Active filtering via ANT_k enhances stability of quorum estimates in anonymous sampling, trading off improved robustness against slower recovery and potential delay in adapting to new information; indicates a favorable balance for scalable QS in robot swarms.

Abstract: Quorum Sensing (QS) is a key capability for robot swarms, useful for coordination of activities at the group level. Effective communication is instrumental for individuals to estimate the quorum level of the entire swarm. Anonymous communication protocols where individuals exchange local information without revealing unique identities are helpful to support quorum estimates by sampling information from neighbours and maintain scalability of the QS process. However, because anonymous protocols cannot distinguish message sources, repeated messages from the same sender may be double-counted, thereby biasing collective quorum estimates. In this study, we introduce a stochastic filtering protocol inspired by $k$-priority sampling to improve estimate stability (\ANTk), and we compare it with a baseline anonymous protocols (\AN) and a randomised variant designed to improve accuracy (\ANT). We find that the baseline protocol \AN provides a parsimonious and fast solution, but remains highly inaccurate due to double-counting bias. The \ANT variant improves accuracy but suffers from information inertia, resulting in slower convergence. Finally, actively filtering the message buffer via the \ANTk protocol successfully decreases temporary errors and stabilises the estimate, at the cost of an increased time of recovery from errors.

</details>


### [178] [DiMaS: Distribution Matching for Steering Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14280)
*Pegah Khayatan,Sara Meziane,Jayneel Parekh,Matthieu Cord*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DiMaS introduces a distribution-matching steering method for flow-matching vision-language-action models to enable fine-grained behavioral control by aligning representation distributions rather than moving along a fixed linear direction; it demonstrates improved controllability across two VLAs, analyzes generalization as tasks diverge, and explains why linear steering fails due to linear decodability but nonlinearity of steerability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve fine-grained, interpretable control of VLA policies by intervening in internal representations. Classical linear steering works for some models but fails for visuomotor VLAs, where behavioral features are not linearly steerable, motivating a distribution-based approach.

Method: Introduce DiMaS (Distribution-Matching Steering) that shifts by matching distributions over latent representations conditioned on desired behavior. Apply to flow-matching-based VLAs across two state-of-the-art models. Evaluate transfer/generalization as task similarity decreases and analyze the representation geometry to explain why linear steering is inadequate.

Result: DiMaS provides effective behavioral control and transfers across the evaluated VLAs, with success diminishing as task dissimilarity increases. It outperforms linear steering and offers insights into the representation structure that underpins why distribution-based steering is necessary. Code and supplementary materials are released.

Conclusion: Distribution-matching steering addresses the limitations of linear steering for visuomotor VLA policies and reveals that while behavioral features are linearly decodable, they are not linearly steerable. This justifies focusing on distribution-level interventions for finer controllability and guides future interpretability work in flow-matching models.

Abstract: Flow-matching-based vision-language-action (VLA) models have emerged as powerful policies for robotic manipulation, yet a critical capability remains underexplored: fine-grained behavioral control, the ability to govern how a robot performs a task by intervening on its internal representations. Representation steering is a well-established interpretability tool for language and vision-language models, where behavioral features are typically encoded as linear directions, but we show that these classic methods fall short in VLAs. We propose DiMaS, a Distribution-Matching Steering strategy tailored to flow-matching VLAs, which transports between representation distributions rather than shifting along a fixed direction, and show that it effectively controls behavior across two state-of-the-art VLAs. We further examine the generalizability of this strategy as the tasks it is learned from and evaluated on grow increasingly dissimilar, characterizing where behavioral control transfers and where it weakens. Finally, through an analysis of the representation structure of the action expert, we explain why classical linear steering falls short in the visuomotor setting: behavioral features are linearly decodable but not linearly steerable, which motivates the distribution-matching design of DiMaS. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/pegah-kh/dimas, with additional results and videos at https://pegah-kh.github.io/dimas/

</details>


### [179] [Beyond Visual Grasping: Benchmarking Complex Grasping from Detection to Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14341)
*Hanyi Zhang,Khang Nguyen,Charith Munasinghe,Basu Hela,Tianyu Li,Zihong Luo,Hoan Nguyen,Hans Wernher van de Venn,Yalin Zheng,Ravi Prakash,Tung D. Ta,Anh Nguyen,Baoru Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GCA-Bench is a new grasping-with-complex-action benchmark that tests scene-level reasoning and semantic constraints; baseline methods perform below 70% success on complex tasks; introduces new metrics and failure analysis to spur robust, generalizable grasping strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current grasping benchmarks emphasize isolated, visual-based pose detection and do not capture the multi-step reasoning and semantic understanding required during execution in real-world tasks.

Method: Introduce GCA-Bench with challenging scenarios that require complex action planning and semantic constraints; evaluate a spectrum of baselines from traditional grasp-detection pipelines to end-to-end learning models; propose new evaluation metrics; analyze critical failure modes.

Result: Empirical studies show success rates below 70% on complex grasping scenarios, revealing significant limitations of existing approaches and the need for more capable reasoning and semantic integration.

Conclusion: GCA-Bench provides a rigorous platform and metrics to drive the development of robust, generalizable grasping strategies and to evaluate large foundation models under complex action settings; highlights the need for improved scene-level reasoning and semantic understanding in grasping.

Abstract: Robust robotic grasping remains a fundamental challenge for complex real-world applications. Recent advances in large-scale models demonstrate promising capabilities for reasoning in robotic tasks. However, existing benchmarks for grasping primarily focus on isolated, visual-based grasp pose detection, failing to capture the complexity of grasping tasks that require multi-step reasoning and semantic understanding during execution. To address this gap, we propose GCA-Bench, a benchmark featuring challenging \textit{grasping with complex action} scenarios that involve both scene-level reasoning and semantic constraints. GCA-Bench enables the evaluation of recent large foundation models under the same settings. To demonstrate the effectiveness of our new benchmark, we implement a diverse set of baselines, ranging from traditional grasp detection pipelines to end-to-end learning methods. Empirical studies achieve success rates below 70\% on complex grasping scenarios, underscoring critical limitations. In addition, we propose new evaluation metrics, analyze critical failure models, and provide insights to guide the development of more robust and generalizable grasping strategies.

</details>


### [180] [An offline approach to fNIRS-guided reinforcement learning for robot behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14393)
*Julia Santaniello,Madelaine Brower,Benson Jiang,Donatello Sassaroli,Robert Jacob,Jivko Sinapov*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: fNIRS-based neural signals can augment reinforcement learning parameters (trajectory priorities and state-action Q-values) to improve learning in simulation, for both passive and active tasks, with offline data learning being viable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable human-in-the-loop RL for robots using brain signals, evaluating parameter-augmentation strategies and the practicality of offline learning to mitigate real-time BCI constraints.

Method: Compare passive (observational) and active (demonstrative) task settings; implement multiple neural-signal augmentation methods focused on parameter augmentation (not replacement); analyze effects of model granularity and noise; test learning from offline data in simulation.

Result: Neural signals improved RL learning when used to augment trajectory priorities and Q-values; the framework is effective in simulation; it also supports learning from offline data, offering a practical alternative when real-time BCI is impractical or data are limited.

Conclusion: Brain-signal–driven parameter augmentation is a feasible approach to human-in-the-loop RL for robotics, with offline-learning viability and robustness to varying granularity and noise in the neural signal.

Abstract: Human-in-the-loop Reinforcement Learning has become a popular approach to training, finetuning, and aligning robot behavior with user preferences. Our paper explores the feasibility of using brain signals via functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) to modulate robot learning in simulation. We compare agents trained on passive (observational) versus active (demonstrative) interaction tasks, and test multiple methods for enhancing the RL algorithm with the neural signal, focusing on parameter augmentation rather than replacement. We further examine how model granularity and noise affect agent learning. Our results show that this framework is effective: the neural signal improves learning when augmenting trajectory priorities and state-action q-values. Additionally, the framework learns successfully from offline data, offering a practical alternative for settings where real-time BCI setups are impractical or only limited data is available.

</details>


### [181] [ConFlow: Constraints-Guided Learning with Flow Matching for Motion Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14424)
*Nutan Chen,Jianxiang Feng,Marvin Alles,Botond Cseke*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ConFlow integrates task constraints directly into flow-matching training for robot motion, via differentiable constraint penalties and a conditional Gaussian Process source, aided by negative demonstrations; this reduces the training–inference gap and improves safety and trajectory quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training-time incorporation of constraints addresses the mismatch between data-driven priors and task-specific requirements, which inference-time guidance alone cannot fully bridge; improved constraint satisfaction should yield safer and more accurate motions.

Method: Modify flow-matching training by adding differentiable barrier/cost terms representing task constraints; replace the Gaussian source with a conditional Gaussian Process to impose smoothness and boundary conditions; use infeasible demonstrations as negative supervision to further enforce constraints during training; evaluate on a two-robot navigation task.

Result: ConFlow achieves lower collision rates and higher trajectory quality than standard flow-matching baselines, regardless of inference-time guidance, demonstrating effective training-time constraint integration.

Conclusion: Incorporating constraints at training time via barrier costs and GP-based sources effectively closes the training–inference gap for generative motion models and enhances safety and performance in constrained robotic tasks.

Abstract: In recent years Flow Matching has become a prominent method for generative modeling robot motion generation. In its generic form Flow Matching is an ODE-based neural sampler that is trained by regressing empirical flow fields associated with motion samples as data. However, in robot motion generation we often have additional constraints that might not be present in the collected data. The majority of current approaches train the flow on the available data and use inference-time guidance to enforce task-specific constraints. To address this mismatch, we propose \textbf{ConFlow}, a constraint-guided flow matching framework that incorporates constraint information directly into the training objective via differentiable barrier or cost functions. To address design specifications such as smoothness and boundary conditions, we propose replacing the standard Gaussian source distribution used in flow matching training with a conditional Gaussian Process. Our approach also uses infeasible demonstrations as negative supervision, improving constraint satisfaction without requiring additional expert data. Experiments on a two-robot navigation task demonstrate that ConFlow achieves lower collision rates and higher trajectory quality than standard flow matching baselines, with or without inference-time guidance. These results validate training-time constraint integration as an effective approach to closing the training--inference gap in generative motion models.

</details>


### [182] [Motion Planning with Model-Based Diffusion via Constraint Optimization and Adaptive Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14455)
*Zhilin He,Bowei Li,Jianlin Dou,Yuner Zhang,Changliu Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MD-COAS unifies soft diffusion priors (iALM) with hard convex-set projections (CFS) for single-robot motion planning, and adaptively schedules safety constraints and diffusion steps to improve safety and convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MBD SRMP methods either rely on soft priors or hard projections and use fixed diffusion schedules; there is a need for a unified framework that jointly enforces safety and adapts during optimization.

Method: Integrates inexact Augmented Lagrangian Method (iALM) as soft diffusion prior with Convex Feasible Set (CFS) hard projection; introduces adaptive scheduling and co-optimization of safety enforcement and diffusion process; applies to diffusion-based trajectory optimization with known dynamics; tested on non-convex 2D benchmarks and a 7-DoF arm task.

Result: Outperforms baselines in safety and success rate, converges faster, achieves lower final trajectory costs.

Conclusion: MD-COAS provides a cohesive framework for combining soft and hard safety priors with adaptive scheduling, enabling more reliable SRMP in highly non-convex constrained environments; potential generalization to other diffusion-based optimization under constraints.

Abstract: Single-Robot Motion Planning (SRMP) in highly non-convex constrained environments, where robots must satisfy collision-free guarantees, dynamic feasibility, and task-related constraints, is challenging under complex constraints and computational limits. Recent Model-Based Diffusion (MBD) approaches recast the SRMP as trajectory optimization that samples from a posterior over trajectories, using known dynamics, and analytically estimates the score function from rollout samples to guide diffusion denoising toward a low-cost, clean trajectory without demonstration learning. While existing works further adapt MBD to constrained environments and showcase promising performance, they are still limited by (1) enforcing safety either via soft feasibility diffusion priors or hard projection operators, but lack a unified framework to integrate both, and (2) fixing safety enforcement to neglect the changing of diffusion scheduling. Therefore, we introduce Model-Based Diffusion via Constraint Optimization and Adaptive Scheduling (MD-COAS) for SRMP that unifies the inexact Augmented Lagrangian Method (iALM) soft diffusion prior with a Convex Feasible Set (CFS)-based hard projection operator, and adaptively schedules and co-optimizes safety enforcement, along with diffusion scheduling. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves higher safety \& success rates, faster convergence, and lower final costs than baseline planners on randomly generated highly non-convex 2D benchmarks and a 7-DoF robot arm avoidance task.

</details>


### [183] [Mixed-Agent Museum Tour Guide Design Improves Gendered Learning Outcomes and Visitor Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14468)
*Annette M. Masterson,Wonse Jo,Helena C. Sieh,Lionel P. Robert,,Dawn Tilbury*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A mixed-agent tour guide system combining a physical robot with a projected virtual agent improves interaction richness and, in a within-subjects study (n=30), maintains engagement/QoE while yielding gender-moderated learning benefits for female participants; overall preference favors mixed-agent teams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance visitor engagement and learning in museum tours by enabling richer, dyadic conversational interactions from a single platform.

Method: Within-subjects design with 30 participants; manipulated conversational styles and agent configurations (mixed-agent system vs single agents); measures include surveys, behavioral sensors, and interviews.

Result: Engagement and quality of experience remained consistent across conditions; learning performance showed a gender-moderated improvement in mixed-agent conditions, with female participants benefiting; participants preferred mixed-agent teams in interviews irrespective of gender.

Conclusion: A dyadic conversational approach via a mixed-agent system can enhance learning and engagement in museum tours, though effects may vary with gender; adoption should consider individual differences and user preferences.

Abstract: Robots are increasingly integrated into everyday contexts, including museums, where they can both entertain and educate visitors. To enhance visitor experience and engagement, we present a novel mixed-agent tour guide system that combines a physical robot with a projected virtual agent that actively participates in the tour through conversation and interaction, achieving the interaction richness of two mobile agents from a single platform. We validate the system through a within-subjects study with 30 participants to assess engagement, quality of experience, and learning performance. Participants experienced different conversational styles and agent configurations, and data were collected via surveys, behavioral sensors, and interviews. Results showed that engagement and quality of experience remained consistent across conditions. Learning performance revealed a significant gender-moderated difference: the mixed-agent conditions improved learning performance for female participants. This suggests that the proposed dyadic conversational style in this paper influenced learning performance differently by gender. Nonetheless, in interviews, participants reported a greater preference for mixed-agent teams regardless of gender, citing interaction as a key factor in their experience.

</details>


### [184] [MIDAS Hand: Modular low-Impedance Direct-drive Anthropomorphic Sensing Hand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14487)
*Alvin Zhu,Mingzhang Zhu,Beom Jun Kim,Quanyou Wang,Jose Victor S. H. Ramos,Dennis Hong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MIDAS Hand is a low-cost, open-source, human-scale dexterous hand with integrated tactile sensing (16 DoF, 13 active), direct-drive actuation with low backdrivability, and 283 three-axis tactile taxels in a 700 g package. It can be assembled in under 3 hours with a BOM under $3k, and ships with a full software/hardware stack for manipulation research. Validation includes workspace and grasp taxonomy analysis, payload tests, reliability and backdrivability measurements, and teleoperation demos with tactile sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the shortage of accessible, human-scale dexterous hand hardware that combines morphology, ease of fabrication/maintenance, tactile sensing, and cost. Existing hands trade off properties; a reproducible, open‑source platform is needed for manipulation research and data collection.

Method: Mechanical design built from 3D‑printed components delivering 16 DoF (13 active) with direct actuation and low backdrive torque. Integrated 283 three‑axis tactile taxels. Compact 700 g. BOM under $3k. Rapid assembly (under 3 hours). Full stack release: design files, build docs, control and tactile Python APIs, simulation models, retargeting and teleoperation pipelines.

Result: Characterization includes workspace and grasp‑taxonomy analysis, payload and reliability tests, backdrivability measurements, and teleoperation demonstrations using tactile sensing, demonstrating a balanced, reproducible platform for tactile dexterous manipulation and human‑to‑robot data collection.

Conclusion: MIDAS Hand provides an accessible, open‑source, human‑scale dexterous hand with integrated tactile sensing and a complete software/hardware stack, enabling reproducible tactile manipulation research and broader community participation.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation is limited not only by algorithms but by a shortage of accessible hand hardware that combines human-scale morphology, ease of manufacturing or maintenance, tactile sensing, and practical cost. Existing dexterous hands tend to optimize some of these properties at the expense of others. We present MIDAS Hand, a low-cost, open-source, human-scale dexterous hand with integrated tactile sensing for manipulation research. MIDAS Hand provides 16 total degrees of freedom (DoF) with 13 active DoF, directly driven actuation with measurably low backdrive torque, and 283 three-axis tactile taxels in a compact 700 g package with a bill of materials under 3,000 USD. Built from 3D-printed components, it assembles in under three hours while providing the strength, repeatability, and maintainability needed for repeated real-world experiments. Alongside the hardware, we release a full stack: design files, build documentation, control and tactile Python APIs, simulation models, and retargeting and teleoperation pipelines. We characterize MIDAS Hand through workspace and grasp-taxonomy analysis, payload and reliability tests, backdrivability measurements, and teleoperation demonstrations with tactile sensing, showing that it offers a balanced, reproducible platform for tactile dexterous manipulation and human-to-robot data collection. Project page: https://midas-hand.com

</details>


### [185] [Safe Execution of RL Policies Via Acceleration-Based CBF-QP Constraint Enforcement for Real-World Robotic Deployments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14488)
*Bastien Muraccioli,Alice Cariou,Pierre-Alexandre Leziart,Mathieu Celerier,Arnaud Demont,Gentiane Venture,Mehdi Benallegue*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Acc-CBF-QP is a runtime safety filter for RL in robotics that casts safety as a control barrier function-based quadratic program, enforcing safety constraints while minimally deviating from the RL command. It improves safety without retraining and preserves nominal performance in safe regimes; demonstrated on a 7-DoF Kinova Gen3 and 19-DoF Unitree H1, with substantial reductions in constraint violations and an open-source release.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL policies in robotics lack formal safety guarantees, and out-of-distribution states can cause unsafe or catastrophic behavior. There's a need to enforce safety constraints at runtime without altering training or requiring offline policy redesign.

Method: An acceleration-based Quadratic Program (QP) safety filter using Control Barrier Functions (CBFs) that constrains any RL policy to a safe set at runtime. It unifies bounds on joint position, velocity, torque, and collision constraints. Introduces RL+QP task formulations to govern deviation from RL commands: TorqueTask (minimize torque deviation) and Forward Dynamics Task (minimize induced acceleration deviation). Applicable to both unconstrained and Safe-RL policies.

Result: Significant reductions in constraint violations observed in both simulation and hardware: on Unitree H1, Safe-RL policy violations dropped from 10.04 to 0.80 violations/s (92% reduction); on Kinova Gen3, violations were fully eliminated. Nominal RL task performance is preserved within violation-free regimes. The approach also improves performance under aggressive velocity commands by preventing constraint-induced shutdowns and extending survival times. The full pipeline is open-source.

Conclusion: Acc-CBF-QP provides a principled, minimally invasive runtime safety layer for RL in robotics, capable of enforcing multiple safety constraints within a single optimization framework while preserving nominal task performance in safe regimes; validated on high-DoF manipulators and humanoid hardware, with publicly available implementation.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has demonstrated remarkable capabilities for solving complex robotic control problems, but its lack of safety guarantees severely limits deployment on hardware. In particular, as legged robots and manipulators often operate near safety-critical boundaries, out-of-distribution states can lead to failure upon deployment. To address this, we introduce Acc-CBF-QP, an acceleration-based Quadratic Program (QP) safety filter using Control Barrier Functions (CBFs) that constrains any RL policy onto a safe set at runtime without modifying training. The method applies to unconstrained and Safe-RL policies, and enforces joint position, velocity, torque, and collision constraints within a unified optimization framework. A key contribution is the formulation of RL+QP tasks that regulate deviation from the RL command when constraints would otherwise be violated. We introduce a TorqueTask, minimizing torque deviation, and a Forward Dynamics Task, minimizing induced acceleration deviation, thus providing principled control over safety-performance trade-offs. Experiments on a 7-DoF Kinova Gen3 manipulator and a 19-DoF Unitree H1 humanoid, both in simulation and on hardware, highlight substantial reductions in constraint violations. On the real H1 hardware, a Safe-RL policy alone yielded 10.04 violations/s, which were reduced by 92% to 0.80 violations/s when augmented with Acc-CBF-QP. On the Kinova Gen3, Acc-CBF-QP fully eliminated violations. Nominal task performance of the RL objective is preserved in violation-free regimes. Under aggressive velocity commands on H1, Acc-CBF-QP improves execution by preventing constraint-induced shutdowns, yielding longer survival times. The full pipeline is open-source.

</details>


### [186] [DRIFT: Drift and Aggregation for Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14507)
*Yining Xing,Zhiyuan Liu,Zehong Ke,Wenhao Yu,Jianqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DRIFT is a fixed-depth planner that generates multiple latent trajectory proposals in one step and directly aggregates them to output a final executable trajectory, achieving efficient multi-hypothesis motion planning with real-time performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the ambiguity in end-to-end trajectory planning, which must represent multiple plausible driving behaviors and still produce a single executable plan within real-time constraints.

Method: A visual-encoder conditioned DRIFT Decoder produces 48 proposal features in a single batch (32 samples at alpha=0.5, 16 samples at alpha=0.9). A lightweight Aggregation Head fuses these with scene, navigation, and ego-state information to predict the final trajectory without trajectory-level quality labels. Training uses expert-trajectory imitation and a map-derived boundary regularizer penalizing waypoints outside the drivable polygon or near its boundary. Inference: proposal-generation ~10.82 ms; full model ~66.43 ms on RTX 4090.

Result: On NAVSIM navtest, DRIFT achieves 89.6 PDMS and 90.4 EPDMS, with strong drivable-area compliance and ego progress compared to peers.

Conclusion: One-step latent proposal generation combined with direct aggregation offers an efficient and effective design for multi-hypothesis motion planning, enabling real-time end-to-end planning without requiring trajectory-level quality labels.

Abstract: End-to-end trajectory planners need to represent multiple plausible driving behaviors while producing a single executable trajectory under real-time constraints. Proposal-based approaches address this ambiguity by generating multiple candidates, but converting the proposal set into a final plan remains a key design problem. We present DRIFT, a fixed-depth planner that combines one-step drifting in a compact trajectory latent space with scene-aware proposal aggregation. Conditioned on features from a pretrained visual encoder, the DRIFT Decoder generates 48 proposal features in a single batched pass, with 32 samples at alpha=0.5 and 16 samples at alpha=0.9. A lightweight Aggregation Head integrates these features with scene, navigation, and ego-state information and directly predicts the final trajectory without requiring trajectory-level quality labels for aggregation. Its output is trained with expert-trajectory imitation and a map-derived boundary regularizer that penalizes waypoints outside the drivable polygon and inside waypoints near its boundary. On NAVSIM navtest, DRIFT achieves 89.6 PDMS and 90.4 EPDMS, with strong drivable-area compliance and ego progress among the methods compared. The proposal-generation and aggregation module runs in 10.82 ms on an NVIDIA RTX 4090, while full-model inference including the visual backbone takes 66.43 ms. These results show that one-step latent proposal generation and direct aggregation provide an efficient design for multi-hypothesis motion planning.

</details>


### [187] [Communication-Efficient Relative Pose Estimation with Vision Foundation Models for Ephemeral Collaborative Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14539)
*Qihang Li,Jo-Hao Huang,Jiewen Liu,Suyoung Kang,Hao Zhang,Peng Gao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CERPE is a communication-efficient framework for relative pose estimation in multi-robot systems that jointly estimates ego-motion and inter-robot pose using fixed-size descriptors and event-triggered image requests, and propagates poses during non-overlaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-robot collaborations, relative pose estimation is essential but hampered by short interaction windows, limited bandwidth, and intermittent visual overlap due to occlusions or narrow fields of view; existing methods rely on global frames, sustained overlap, or high communication costs.

Method: CERPE coordinates vision foundation models to jointly estimate ego-motion and inter-robot relative pose. It reduces raw-observation exchange by using continuously shared fixed-size descriptors to gate event-triggered raw-image requests independently of pose estimation. For non-overlapping encounters, it propagates inter-robot relative poses through metrically scaled ego-motion to maintain estimates when visual overlap is absent.

Result: Experiments in simulation and on real robots show CERPE improves 6-DoF relative pose estimation over selected baselines in ephemeral collaborative perception.

Conclusion: CERPE enables accurate 6-DoF relative pose estimation in ephemeral, bandwidth-constrained multi-robot scenarios and effectively handles non-overlapping encounters by pose propagation via scaled ego-motion.

Abstract: Relative pose estimation is a fundamental capability for collaborative perception and coordination in multi-robot systems. However, robots encountering each other in real-world environments often operate in short interaction windows and must operate under limited communication bandwidth with intermittent or missing visual overlap caused by occlusions or limited fields of view. Existing approaches typically rely on global reference frames, assume sustained view overlap, or incur prohibitive communication costs, thereby limiting their applicability to ephemeral collaborative perception. To address these challenges, we introduce communication-efficient relative pose estimation (CERPE), a system-level framework that coordinates vision foundation models to jointly estimate ego-motion and inter-robot relative pose. CERPE reduces unnecessary raw-observation exchange by using continuously shared fixed-size descriptors to gate event-triggered raw-image requests independently of pose estimation. Non-overlapping encounters are handled by propagating inter-robot relative poses through metrically scaled ego-motion, thus maintaining relative pose estimates even in the absence of visual overlap. Experiments in simulation and real-world robots show that CERPE improves 6-DoF relative pose estimation over selected baselines in ephemeral collaborative perception.

</details>


### [188] [SafeRelBench: A Spatial-Relation-Aware Benchmark for Process-Level Safety in VLM-Driven Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14543)
*Huaigang Yang,Ya Li,Min Ren,Bo Dai,Zhenliang Zhang,Zhaofeng He*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SAFERELBENCH introduces a spatial-relations aware safety benchmark for embodied agents; reveals task success often coexists with process-level safety violations; highlights the need for relational safety checks and reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridges a gap in embodied safety evaluation: static risk recognition and final-state success miss risk dynamics arising from spatial relations (support, containment, proximity) during interaction.

Method: Construct 507 executable evaluation samples (248 spatial-relations, 259 non-spatial controls); evaluate seven open- and closed-source VLM-driven embodied agents; assess pre-action safety checks and compliance with safety constraints during action planning.

Result: Demonstrates a notable gap between achieving task objectives and maintaining process-level safety; many agents violate safety constraints while completing tasks; spatial relations are a core dimension of safe embodied intelligence.

Conclusion: Emphasizes that safe embodied AI requires stronger perception and planning plus reliable relational reasoning about object interactions; SAFERELBENCH offers a benchmark to drive progress from recognition to relational safety reasoning.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as the reasoning backbone of embodied agents, enabling robots to interpret visual scenes, follow language instructions, and plan multi-step actions. In household environments, however, safety depends not only on recognizing objects, but also on how actions change the physical scene over time. Existing embodied safety evaluations largely focus on static risk recognition, unsafe instruction refusal, or final-state task completion. As a result, process-level safety failures induced by spatial relations such as support, containment, and proximity remain insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we introduce SAFERELBENCH, a spatial-relation-aware safety benchmark with 507 executable evaluation samples, including 248 spatial-relation samples and 259 non-spatial control samples. Using SAFERELBENCH to evaluate seven open- and closed-source VLM-driven embodied agents, we find a substantial gap between task success and process-level safety compliance: models often complete the requested task while violating process-level safety constraints. Unlike prior benchmarks, SAFERELBENCH explicitly tests whether agents satisfy safety conditions before risk-prone actions, making spatial relations a core dimension in embodied safety assessment. More broadly, our results show that safe embodied intelligence requires not only stronger perception and planning, but also reliable reasoning about how object relations shape risk during interaction.

</details>


### [189] [Beyond Implicit Force: Evaluating Explicit Force-Torque Proxies in Action Chunking with Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14578)
*King Hang Wong,Lingqiao Liu,Feras Dayoub*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Removing the teleoperation-induced discrepancy signal from ACT by predicting follower states rather than leader commands degrades force-aware behavior; simple joint-torque proxies (motor current/effort) can recover or enhance force awareness, yielding robust contact manipulation across real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether ACT’s apparent force-awareness hinges on hidden cues present in leader–follower teleoperation, and whether onboard torque signals can serve as a lightweight proxy for contact information in real hardware.

Method: Introduce an observation-centric ACT variant that predicts future follower joint states (instead of leader commands). Evaluate with and without torque proxies derived from onboard motor current or joint effort across four real-world tasks (surface following, insertion, stiffness discrimination, force-based stopping). Compare base ACT, observation-centric ACT, and torque-augmented ACT to assess the role of the implicit cue and the effectiveness of torque proxies.

Result: Removing the implicit teleoperation cue leads to severe failures in force-critical phases. Torque-augmented policies recover robust contact behavior and improve the base ACT policy, with simple torque proxies matching or surpassing the original cue's contribution.

Conclusion: On real hardware, the teleoperation cue is a recoverable source of force-awareness. Torque signals provide a viable and often superior proxy, allowing simpler sensing to reproduce or exceed ACT’s force-sensitive performance.

Abstract: Contact-rich manipulation requires policies to infer interaction state from signals that are often weakly observable through vision and kinematics alone. Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) has shown strong performance in fine-grained manipulation, but many deployments collect demonstrations through leader-follower teleoperation, where tracking error between commanded leader motion and executed follower motion implicitly encodes contact, resistance, and constraint violation. This paper examines whether ACT's apparent force-awareness depends on this hidden interaction cue. We introduce an observation-centric ACT variant that predicts future follower joint states instead of leader commands, thereby removing the teleoperation-induced discrepancy signal while preserving the rest of the learning pipeline. We then evaluate whether simple joint-torque proxies, derived from onboard motor current or joint effort, can recover contact-aware behavior without external force/torque sensors. Across four real-world tasks spanning surface following, insertion, stiffness discrimination, and force-based stopping, removing the implicit cue leads to severe failures in force-critical phases. In contrast, torque-augmented policies recover robust contact behavior and improve the base ACT policy. These results demonstrate that, on real hardware, the implicit teleoperation cue is a recoverable source of force-awareness, where torque signals are available, a simple proxy matches, surpasses, or further enhances it.

</details>


### [190] [SoftNav: Injecting 3D Scene Tokens into VLMs for Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14586)
*Yi Wu,Junjie An,Xiao Liu,Yiqun Zhou,Yuechen Wu,Xiaoqing Guan,Shuyang Yu,You Wang,Guang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SoftNav injects 3D object/frontier tokens into a frozen VLM to enable efficient, transferable goal-directed navigation with minimal training; achieves state-of-the-art SR/SPL on HM3D-OVON and zero-shot transfer to GOAT-Bench, SG3D, and real robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a representation gap when using text-based 3D scene information for vision-language models; ablations show embedding-level transfer is superior; need a light-weight method to fuse 3D information into VLMs to enable transferability and data efficiency.

Method: Introduce SoftNav: for each detected object or frontier, create a 3D continuous token; inject these as soft tokens into the VLM hidden space via a lightweight projector; freeze 3D encoder and VLM; train only ~1,200 samples with ~17M params.

Result: On HM3D-OVON, SRs: 74.2%, 68.3%, 66.7% across three splits; surpasses prior methods in SR and SPL; zero-shot transfer to GOAT-Bench (67.2% SR), SG3D (47.2% s-SR); real-world robot deployment without retraining.

Conclusion: Directly injecting 3D scene tokens into VLMs bridges the representation gap, enabling transferable navigation with minimal training.

Abstract: In goal-directed embodied navigation, where an agent must locate a specified target in an unseen environment, 3D scene understanding and navigation reasoning must work in concert. Current approaches transmit 3D scene information to vision-language models (VLMs) through text, suggesting a representation gap in our tested configurations; a controlled ablation confirms that direct embedding-level transfer significantly outperforms the evaluated text serialization formats. We introduce SoftNav, which injects entity-level 3D continuous representations -- one token per detected object or frontier -- into a VLM's hidden space as soft tokens through a lightweight projector. With the 3D encoder and VLM frozen, only ~1,200 samples and ~17M trainable parameters are needed. On HM3D-OVON, SoftNav achieves 74.2%/68.3%/66.7% SR across three splits, surpassing all prior methods in both SR and SPL; the same navigation policy transfers zero-shot to GOAT-Bench (67.2% SR), SG3D (47.2% s-SR), and real-world robot deployment without retraining or architectural modification. Injecting 3D scene tokens directly into VLMs bridges the representation gap, enabling transferable navigation with minimal training.

</details>


### [191] [Representation-Aligned Tactile Grounding for Contact-Rich Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14609)
*Ruilin Chen,Jingkai Jia,Tong Yang,Xinyu Zhou,Qiao Sun,Jiangwei Zhong,Shizeng Zhang,Nuo Chen,Bailin He,Wei Li,Wenqiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Latent Tactile Predictor (LTP) aligns tactile supervision with intermediate action representations for VLA policies, improving real-world tactile grounding by predicting future tactile embeddings from action-expert features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tactile feedback is essential for robust contact-rich manipulation. Predicting tactile outcomes can supervise action-induced contact dynamics, but it is unclear where in VLA representations this supervision should be applied; a representation-alignment analysis can identify the optimal source for tactile prediction.

Method: Conduct a linear probe analysis to compare predictability of future tactile states from vision-language features, intermediate action-expert features, and final action states. Introduce Latent Tactile Predictor (LTP) that predicts compact future tactile embeddings from the identified intermediate representation, avoiding raw tactile signals to provide an action-grounding signal.

Result: Future tactile states are most predictable from intermediate action-expert features. LTP improves tactile grounding compared to less aligned or multi-interface tactile prediction, demonstrating the importance of aligning tactile supervision with the appropriate representation.

Conclusion: Represents that the placement of tactile supervision within the representation hierarchy matters; aligning tactile prediction with intermediate action representations yields better alignment with future contact consequences and improves real-world performance in VLA policies.

Abstract: Tactile-enhanced vision-language-action (VLA) policies have been introduced for contact-rich manipulation, where critical interaction states are often hidden from vision. Future tactile prediction is a promising way to use touch because it turns tactile outcomes into supervision for action-induced contact dynamics. Yet VLA policies contain representations with different roles, from perceptual encoding to motor prediction, making it unclear where this supervision should be applied. We study this as a representation-alignment problem. Through a linear probe analysis, we find that future tactile states are most predictable from intermediate action-expert features, rather than from vision-language features or final action states. Motivated by this observation, we introduce a lightweight Latent Tactile Predictor (LTP), which predicts compact future tactile embeddings from the identified intermediate representation. By avoiding direct prediction of noisy raw tactile signals, LTP provides an action-outcome grounding signal that aligns intermediate action representations with future contact consequences. Experiments on real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks show that representation-aligned tactile grounding outperforms less aligned or multi-interface tactile prediction, highlighting the importance of where tactile supervision is applied.

</details>


### [192] [Image-to-Point Cloud Registration Made Easy with Rectified Flow-based LiDAR Upsampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14639)
*Reon Tabata,Kenji Koide,Shuji Oishi,Masashi Yokozuka,Taku Okawara,Aoki Takanose,Jun Miura*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A simple, data-efficient I2P method that treats LiDAR as an imaging modality by generating a dense LiDAR intensity image from a single sparse scan using Conditional Rectified Flow, then matching with a camera image via a pre-trained feature matcher and estimating 6-DoF pose with PnP-RANSAC. Pretraining uses self-supervised image completion and fine-tuning requires only a small amount of LiDAR data (no image-point cloud pairs or poses). On R3LIVE, it achieves 4.89° / 1.63 m and ~0.68 s per registration, outperforming rivals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the modality gap between images and point clouds in I2P and to achieve strong generalization with limited paired data, enabling scalable cross-modal registration across diverse LiDAR and camera setups.

Method: Generate a dense LiDAR intensity image from a single sparse scan using Conditional Rectified Flow. Match the LiDAR image with the camera image using a pre-trained feature matcher. Estimate the 6-DoF relative pose via PnP-RANSAC. Pretrain the model with a self-supervised image completion task and fine-tune on a small amount of LiDAR data without requiring image-point cloud pairs or ground-truth poses.

Result: Mean registration error: 4.89 degrees rotation and 1.63 meters translation. Average time per registration: 0.68 seconds. Outperforms existing methods on R3LIVE dataset.

Conclusion: A simple, effective, and data-efficient I2P pipeline that closes the image-point cloud modality gap, delivers competitive accuracy with strong generalization, and operates efficiently, suitable for diverse sensor configurations.

Abstract: Image-to-Point Cloud Registration (I2P) is essential for integrating camera and LiDAR in perception and autonomous systems, yet the modality gap between images and point clouds makes it difficult to achieve both high accuracy and strong generalization. In this paper, we propose a simple yet effective I2P method that treats LiDAR as an imaging sensor: from a single sparse LiDAR scan, we generate a dense LiDAR intensity image using Conditional Rectified Flow, match it with a camera image using a pre-trained feature matcher, and estimate the 6-DoF relative pose via PnP-RANSAC. The proposed model is pre-trained through a self-supervised image completion task and fine-tuned on a small amount of LiDAR data (neither image-point cloud pairs nor ground-truth sensor poses are required), enabling it to scale to diverse LiDAR and camera configurations. Experiments on the R3LIVE dataset show that the proposed method achieves a mean error of 4.89° / 1.63 m, outperforming existing methods, while completing a single registration in approximately 0.68 s.

</details>


### [193] [NavCMPO: Critic-Guided MeanFlow Policy Optimization for Adaptive Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14643)
*Junjie An,Yi Wu,Xiao Liu,Yiqun Zhou,Yuechen Wu,Xiaoqing Guan,You Wang,Guang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NavCMPO is a two-stage, sample-efficient navigation framework that combines few-step MeanFlow generation, critic-guided trajectory refinement, and PPO with behavior cloning to boost mapless navigation performance while reducing latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end diffusion policies offer strong performance but are slow due to iterative denoising; behavior cloning is limited by the quality of expert data; there is a need for fast, data-efficient, robust navigation with reliable sim-to-real transfer.

Method: NavCMPO comprises (1) pretraining with an obstacle proximity prediction task to learn obstacle-aware visual representations; (2) Critic-Guided Trajectory Refinement (CGTR) that uses gradients from a critic trained with obstacle-point-cloud supervision to refine intermediate trajectories; (3) adaptation via MeanFlow policy fine-tuning with Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and behavior-cloning regularization; (4) critical updates to accommodate embodiment-specific observation changes; (5) evaluation on InternVLA-N1 with matched training budget and successful sim-to-real transfer on Unitree Go2.

Result: Average success rate 74.7% on InternVLA-N1, exceeding the retrained NavDP baseline by 6.4 percentage points; inference latency reduced from 85 ms to 60 ms; experiments on Unitree Go2 demonstrate effective sim-to-real transfer.

Conclusion: NavCMPO delivers improved navigation performance with lower inference latency and robust sim-to-real transfer. The two-stage approach leverages obstacle-aware representation learning, critic-guided trajectory refinement, and RL fine-tuning with behavior cloning. Potential future directions include ablations to isolate contributions, robustness under varied sensory noise, and extensions to more diverse robotic platforms.

Abstract: End-to-end diffusion-based policies have demonstrated strong performance in mapless visual navigation, but their iterative denoising process introduces substantial inference latency, while behavior cloning limits performance to the quality of expert demonstrations. We present NavCMPO, a two-stage adaptive navigation framework that combines few-step MeanFlow trajectory generation, critic-guided refinement, and reinforcement learning fine-tuning. During pre-training, an obstacle proximity prediction task encourages the visual representation to capture obstacle-aware spatial information. To compensate for the degradation in obstacle avoidance caused by few-step generation, Critic-Guided Trajectory Refinement (CGTR) uses gradients from a critic trained with obstacle-point-cloud supervision to refine intermediate trajectories. During adaptation, the MeanFlow policy is fine-tuned using Proximal Policy Optimization with behavior-cloning regularization, while the critic is updated to accommodate embodiment-specific observation changes. Under a matched training budget on the InternVLA-N1 benchmark, NavCMPO achieves an average success rate of 74.7\%, exceeding the retrained NavDP baseline by 6.4 percentage points, while reducing inference latency from 85\,ms to 60\,ms. Experiments on a Unitree Go2 further demonstrate effective sim-to-real transfer.

</details>


### [194] [An Intelligent-Cloud Edge Multimodal Interaction System for Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14675)
*Zihan Guo,Xiaoqi Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A cloud-edge multimodal framework for robust gesture-based HRI is proposed, featuring an enhanced YOLO-DC detector with CBAM and DIoU loss, integrated with LLM/VLM agents for planning and multimodal fusion; results show high precision and mAP on gesture datasets and favorable system-level metrics, indicating feasibility for resource-constrained robotic interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust gesture perception and reliable multimodal task planning in resource-constrained HRI scenarios, especially for small/occluded gestures and complex backgrounds, require improved detectors and integrated planning across cloud and edge.

Method: Introduce YOLO-DC: augment YOLO with Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM) in the neck and substitute DIoU loss for standard bounding-box regression. Deploy a cloud-edge architecture where the cloud performs gesture detection, scene understanding, multimodal fusion, and action planning, while a local robot (TonyPi) handles data acquisition, communication, action execution, and feedback. Integrate large language model (LLM) and vision-language model (VLM) agents for multimodal reasoning and planning.

Result: On a public gesture dataset and a custom dataset, YOLO-DC achieves precision of 98.9% and 95.0%, and mAP@0.5 of 90.7% and 92.7%, respectively. System-level evaluation reports success rates of 95%, 88%, and 82% for single-action, composite-action, and vision-dependent tasks. A 30-participant study yields an overall mean satisfaction of 3.69/5.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates the feasibility of combining refined gesture detection with multimodal agents in a cloud-edge architecture to enable resource-efficient, robust HRI in complex environments.

Abstract: Robust human-robot interaction in complex environments requires accurate gesture perception, semantic scene understanding, and reliable task planning under limited onboard computing resources. This paper presents a cloud-edge multimodal interaction framework that integrates an enhanced YOLO-based gesture detector with coordinated large language model (LLM) and vision-language model (VLM) agents. The proposed detector, incorporates the Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM) into the neck and replaces the baseline bounding-box regression objective with Distance-IoU (DIoU) loss. These modifications improve feature discrimination and localization for small or partially occluded gestures in complex backgrounds. The cloud layer performs gesture detection, scene understanding, multimodal fusion, and action planning, whereas the TonyPi robot locally handles data acquisition, communication, action execution, and feedback. Experiments on a public gesture dataset and a custom dataset show that YOLO-DC achieves precision values of 98.9% and 95.0%, with mAP@0.5 values of 90.7% and 92.7%, respectively. System-level evaluation yields success rates of 95%, 88%, and 82% for single-action, composite-action, and vision-dependent tasks. A 30 participant evaluation yields an overall mean satisfaction score of 3.69 out of 5. These results demonstrate the feasibility of combining refined gesture detection with multimodal agents for resource-constrained robotic interaction.

</details>


### [195] [MIND-CAVs: Multi-Intelligence Negotiation and Decision System for CAVs based on Intent-Driven Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14688)
*Mainak Mondal,Yihang Feng,Yangchao Luo,Han Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An intent-driven, edge-assisted framework for coordinated autonomous vehicles (MIND-CAVs) that converts sensor data into intents, negotiates via roadside edge servers using learned and rule-based arbitration, with centralized auditing, showing improvements in maneuver efficiency and safety over baselines in CARLA simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the lack of principled, globally consistent, intent-aware coordination in current connected autonomy, which mainly shares low-level state through BSMs and relies on simple arbitration.

Method: Each vehicle abstracts raw perception into structured intents and exchanges them over V2X. Edge agents arbitrate conflicting intents using a mix of learned models and rule-based logic to emit coordinated plans; a cloud platform logs decisions for auditing and retraining. Implemented in a CARLA-based AI-in-the-loop platform and evaluated on multi-lane highways with conflicting maneuvers and constrained exits.

Result: Compared to isolated autonomy, first-come-first-served arbitration, and multi-agent reinforcement learning baselines, MIND-CAVs yields shorter maneuver completion times and reduces unsafe proximity and unnecessary braking.

Conclusion: MIND-CAVs demonstrates that intent-driven cooperation via edge/cloud arbitration can yield globally consistent, auditable coordination for CAVs, with demonstrated performance gains in simulated scenarios and potential for further enhancement through learning and regulatory adaptation.

Abstract: Modern autonomous vehicles largely operate as isolated agents: they rely on on-board perception and decision modules and broadcast Basic Safety Messages (BSMs) that expose only low-level kinematic state. While existing cooperative driving frameworks enable limited sensor sharing, they rarely communicate high-level maneuver intentions, and edge computing is primarily used for content delivery rather than decision arbitration. As a result, current connected autonomy lacks a principled mechanism for making globally consistent, intent-aware coordination decisions across vehicles. To address this gap, we propose MIND-CAVs, a Multi-Intelligence Negotiation and Decision framework for connected autonomous vehicles (CAVs) based on intent-driven autonomy. Each vehicle abstracts raw sensor observations into structured intent representations, exchanges them over V2X links, and receives globally consistent coordination plans from roadside edge servers. Edge agents combine learned and rule-based arbitration mechanisms to negotiate conflicting intents among vehicles, while a cloud platform records decisions for auditing and continual retraining. We implement MIND-CAVs in a CARLA-based AI-in-the-loop platform and evaluate it in multi-lane highway scenarios involving conflicting maneuvers and route-constrained exits. Experimental results show improved maneuver completion time and reduced unsafe proximity and unnecessary braking compared with isolated autonomy, first-come-first-served arbitration, and multi-agent reinforcement learning baselines.

</details>


### [196] [Reflex: Real-Time VLA Control through Streaming Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14695)
*Yuanchun Guo,Bingyan Liu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Reflex enables real-time streaming inference for flow-matching Vision-Language-Action models by exploiting a Timestep-Invariance Property that decouples perception encoding from the denoising loop. It partitions attention into static, sliding, and dynamic regions for O(1) cache updates, adds AdaRMSNorm for numerical stability, and uses asynchronous pipelines and operator fusion to boost throughput, achieving 2.58x speedups and stable 50 Hz streaming on LIBERO and Kinetix with up to 54% latency reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow-matching VLA models promise precise control but rely on iterative denoising, which clashes with real-time robotics: injecting global timesteps breaks KV-caching, forcing costly re-computation or incorrect cache reuse. This work seeks real-time compatibility without sacrificing performance.

Method: Introduce Reflex framework and Timestep-Invariance Property, showing perception encoders are independent of the denoising loop. Architect attention into static, sliding, and dynamic regions to enable O(1) incremental cache updates while preserving full-batch attention outputs for fixed inputs. Propose AdaRMSNorm to prevent BFloat16 numerical collapse during continuous high-frequency inference. Implement an async pipeline decoupling visual encoding from action generation and perform operator fusion to reduce kernel overhead.

Result: On LIBERO and Kinetix benchmarks, Reflex yields a 2.58× inference speedup and maintains stable streaming at 50 Hz; reduces reaction latency by up to 54%; enables efficient deployment without performance degradation compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Reflex demonstrates that real-time streaming for flow-matching VLA models is feasible by leveraging timestep invariance and careful architectural design (regioned attention, adaptive normalization, and asynchronous/ fused execution). This approach could generalize to other denoising-based perception-action models needing low-latency control.

Abstract: Flow matching Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models promise precise continuous control, but their iterative denoising nature introduces fundamental incompatibilities with real-time robotics: global timestep injection invalidates KV-caching, forcing a choice between slow $O(N^2)$ re-computation or mathematically incorrect cache reuse. We present \textbf{Reflex}, a framework that enables \textit{real-time streaming inference} for flow matching policies by exploiting the \textit{Timestep-Invariance Property} -- that perception encoders are functionally independent of the denoising loop. Reflex partitions the attention context into static, sliding, and dynamic regions, enabling $O(1)$ incremental cache updates while preserving full-batch-equivalent attention outputs for fixed inputs. To ensure stability under continuous high-frequency inference, we introduce \textit{AdaRMSNorm}, an adaptive normalization layer that prevents BFloat16 numerical collapse by gating on flow phase. We further maximize throughput through an \textit{async pipeline} that decouples visual encoding from action generation, combined with \textit{operator fusion} that reduces kernel overhead. On LIBERO and Kinetix benchmarks, Reflex achieves a 2.58$\times$ inference speedup and 50Hz stable streaming, reducing reaction latency by up to 54\% and enabling efficient deployment without performance degradation.

</details>


### [197] [Lights, Camera, Malfunction: When Illumination Robustness Leaves VLA Models Blind to Color](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14698)
*Marino Watanabe,Takami Sato,Kentaro Yoshioka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FLARE exposes color-sensitive vulnerabilities in Vision-Language-Action models to illumination-based attacks and introduces ChromaGuard, a chroma-preserving adversarial training method, achieving strong robustness on color-dependent tasks in real robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models enable versatile robot manipulation but are vulnerable to small environmental perturbations, particularly lighting changes. Naive data augmentations can incorrectly suppress color cues, reducing real-world performance. A defense that preserves color information is needed.

Method: Propose FLARE, a physical spotlight attack that targets color cues without accessing model internals. Use a diagnostic grayscale evaluation to reveal color-dependence. Develop ChromaGuard, chroma-preserving adversarial training. Validate on a 6-DoF robotic platform across benign and attacked color-dependent tasks.

Result: FLARE drops baseline task success to zero under attack. Defended grayscale performance remains high, while color-dependent real-world tasks degrade to <=47.5% with naive defenses. ChromaGuard achieves 97.5% benign color-dependent task success and 92.5% attacked color-dependent task success on a physical robot.

Conclusion: Preserving chromatic information is crucial for robust color-dependent manipulation. ChromaGuard provides effective defense against illumination-based attacks and is viable for real-world VLA-enabled robotics.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as a powerful paradigm for general-purpose robot manipulation; however, their transition to real-world environments reveals vulnerabilities to minor environmental perturbations. We propose FLARE, an optimized physical spotlight attack framework that exploits these vulnerabilities via targeted illuminations, dropping baseline task success rates to zero without any access to model internals. While adversarial training is the standard countermeasure, we identify a critical and previously underestimated defensive pitfall: naive data augmentations incorrectly condition VLA models to discard color as noise, collapsing their visual perception into a purely shape-biased processor. We expose this degradation through a diagnostic grayscale evaluation, in which the defended model maintains high success rates on grayscale inputs, while its success rate on benign, color-dependent real-world tasks drops to at most 47.5%, well below the undefended baseline. To address this, we propose ChromaGuard, a chroma-preserving adversarial training method. On a physical 6-DoF robotic platform, we demonstrate that ChromaGuard achieves 97.5% and 92.5% success rates in benign and attacked color-dependent tasks, respectively.

</details>


### [198] [Reinforcement Learning for the Full Strawberry Harvesting Process: Obstacle Separation, Detachment, and Placement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14708)
*Changyou Miao,Teng Li,Ya Xiong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Policy-driven RL with heuristic phase coordination enables robust strawberry harvesting under occlusions, yielding high sim/real success and transfer to a different robot.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Occlusions and deformable plant structures create complex contact dynamics; robust sim-to-real transfer across robotic platforms is needed for practical harvesting.

Method: Hierarchical policy: high-level policy outputs Cartesian motions for all phases; lightweight heuristic logic coordinates phase transitions and gripper events; shared observation space for target/obstacles/end-effector/task context; low-level Cartesian impedance control; feasibility-first observation alignment and domain randomization for zero-shot sim-to-real transfer.

Result: Simulation success rate 89.7%, real-world 82.0%; execution time increases with occlusion level (12.99 s to 21.73 s from levels 1 to 5); results indicate effective transfer of interaction-aware harvesting behaviors to a structurally different robot.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that an interaction-aware, policy-driven framework with phase-coordination and impedance control can achieve high-performance strawberry harvesting and transfer across platforms.

Abstract: Severe occlusions and deformable plant structures introduce complex contact dynamics that challenge robotic strawberry harvesting. A policy-driven reinforcement learning (RL) framework with heuristic phase coordination was developed, in which obstacle separation, fruit detachment, and placement were formulated as a sequential decision-making task. A shared interaction-aware policy generated Cartesian motions across all task phases, while lightweight heuristic logic coordinated task progression and gripper events. A shared structured observation space was used to represent target, obstacle, end-effector, and task-context information. A hierarchical architecture combined the high-level policy with low-level Cartesian impedance control for compliant interaction. To support zero-shot sim-to-real transfer, feasibility-first observation alignment and domain randomization were adopted. The policy achieved success rates of 89.7% in simulation and 82.0% in real-world experiments. As the occlusion level increased from 1 to 5, the average execution time increased from 12.99 s to 21.73 s, reflecting greater interaction complexity.
