TL;DR: A cross-network, AI-driven framework (RegNetAgents) for identifying regulatory candidate genes in cancer by integrating TCGA bulk networks and GREmLN single-cell networks, using multi-agent LangGraph DAG, MoA assignment, and OncoKB-based filtering; shows enrichment for known cancer genes across BRCA/COAD and provides end-to-end interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robust cross-network regulatory candidate discovery in cancer by leveraging complementary network architectures and providing structured evaluation of oncogenic potential and druggability, rather than relying on single-network inference.

Method: Implemented as a multi-agent LangGraph DAG workflow with a Python API and MCP client; performs dual-network classification, OncoKB-based cancer gene filtering, MoA assignment, and evidence-based ranking (Both/TCGA-only/GREmLN-only); uses precomputed networks; downstream interpretability module.

Result: In BRCA (11 genes) and COAD (12 genes), identifies candidates enriched for OncoKB cancer genes; TCGA-derived candidates show strong enrichment (Z-scores 6.69 for BRCA and 6.95 for COAD), GREmLN-derived candidates also show enrichment (Z 5.51 for BRCA and 7.06 for COAD; p<0.0001); no enrichment in housekeeping/non-driver controls; extended module supports oncogenic potential, druggability, clinical relevance, and network vulnerability; demonstrates cross-network interpretability.

Conclusion: RegNetAgents provides an interpretable AI framework for cross-network regulatory candidate identification in cancer genomics with validated enrichment signals, enabling end-to-end insight from candidate detection to biological hypothesis generation.

Abstract: We introduce RegNetAgents, an AI-oriented multi-agent framework for structured, query-driven regulatory candidate identification across heterogeneous gene regulatory networks. The system enables unified analysis of bulk tumor and single-cell-derived ARACNe networks by integrating TCGA-derived cancer networks with large-scale single-cell regulatory networks from the GREmLN project. For a given focal gene, the framework performs dual-network classification, cancer gene filtering using OncoKB annotations, and mode-of-action (MoA) assignment for tumor-derived regulatory relationships. Candidates are ranked by evidence consistency across networks (Both, TCGA-only, GREmLN-only). The system is implemented as a multi-agent LangGraph DAG workflow, accessible through a unified Python API and Model Context Protocol (MCP) client, operating as a downstream analytical layer over precomputed regulatory networks rather than a network inference method. Across eleven breast cancer (BRCA) and twelve colorectal cancer (COAD) focal genes, RegNetAgents identifies candidate regulators significantly enriched for OncoKB-annotated cancer genes. TCGA-derived candidates show strong enrichment (Stouffer Z = 6.69 for BRCA and 6.95 for COAD), while GREmLN-derived candidates also demonstrate significant enrichment (Z = 5.51 for BRCA and 7.06 for COAD; all p < 0.0001). No enrichment is observed in housekeeping or non-driver control gene sets, supporting signal specificity. An extended module enables structured evaluation of oncogenic potential, druggability, clinical relevance, and network vulnerability, supporting end-to-end interpretation from candidate identification to biological hypothesis generation. RegNetAgents establishes an interpretable AI framework for cross-network regulatory candidate identification in cancer genomics.

</details>


### [224] [DialogueVPR: Towards Conversational Visual Place Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14115)
*Yukun Song,Changwei Wang,Xingtian Pei,Shibiao Xu,Wenhao Xu,Shunpeng Chen,Yu Zhang,Ke Zhang,Rongtao Xu,Xuxiang Feng,Pengyang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a dialogue-driven retrieval framework (DlgPR) for location grounding, with DlgQuest-Cities benchmark and DQ-pilot, trained via curriculum and GRPO reinforcement; achieves notable improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To handle ambiguity and incompleteness in natural language descriptions for geo-localization by shifting from static one-shot retrieval to interactive reasoning.

Method: Introduces a cross-modal multi-level retriever and an intelligent questioner (DQ-pilot); training via supervised fine-tuning on DQ-cities-20k, then reinforcement refinement on DQ-cities-10k using GRPO; uses Discriminative Difficulty Index (DDI) and Positional Retrieval Gain (PRG) as task-aligned metrics.

Result: Experimental results show the reasoning-based approach significantly outperforms baselines; code and model released.

Conclusion: Interactive, dialogue-guided reasoning improves geo-localization performance and establishes a scalable benchmark with a unified framework; potential extensions include broader dialogue strategies and other modalities.

Abstract: Inspired by how humans communicate spatial information, language-guided geo-localization has gained significant traction for its intuitive and practical value. Despite this progress, most methods still rely on a static, one-shot retrieval paradigm, which fails to handle the ambiguity and incompleteness inherent in real-world natural language descriptions. We propose a paradigm shift to reasoning retrieval and introduce Dialogue Place Recognition (DlgPR), which casts localization as an interactive, dialogue-driven reasoning process. To support this new task, we present DlgQuest-Cities, the first large-scale dialogue-based benchmark for place recognition, and a unified reasoning framework that couples a cross-modal multi-level retriever with an intelligent questioner, DQ-pilot. DQ-pilot is trained in a curriculum: supervised fine-tuning on a curated DQ-cities-20k subset followed by reinforcement refinement on a harder DQ-cities-10k split via GRPO. Two task-aligned metrics guide learning: a Discriminative Difficulty Index (DDI) for curriculum sampling and a Positional Retrieval Gain (PRG) reward that directly measures retrieval improvement induced by a question. Experiments show this reasoning-based approach significantly outperforms baselines. The code and model are available at https://github.com/Graysonggg/DlgPR.

</details>


### [225] [Interpretable Language Model for Closed-Loop Type 1 Diabetes Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14126)
*Maya Sarkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-assisted T1D insulin-pump controller: distills RL into open LLMs to provide transparent, explainable decisions with strong glycemic control, validated in silico.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address RL-based insulin pump trust and explainability by combining RL precision with human-like reasoning and safety verification.

Method: Train an expert RL controller; distill its policy to fine-tuned LLaMA 3.1 8B and Qwen3 8B LLMs; implement LLM-based controllers; evaluate on FDA-approved UVA/Padova T1D simulator; assess glycemic performance (Time in Range) and safety (hallucination avoidance).

Result: LLM controllers surpass the expert RL in performance, achieving 73.5% Time in Range; provide plain-language explanations; maintain formal safety verification against hallucinations.

Conclusion: LLM-augmented RL approach yields transparent, high-performing insulin pump control; potential for improved clinician/patient trust; simulator-based results are encouraging, with real-world validation needed.

Abstract: Type 1 Diabetes (T1D) is a chronic, life-threatening autoimmune condition characterized by the complete destruction of insulin-producing pancreatic beta cells. While Artificial Pancreas Systems (APS) powered by Reinforcement Learning (RL) have shown promise in automating insulin delivery, their ``black-box'' nature makes it hard for patients and doctors to trust them fully. This paper presents LLM-T1D, a promising approach that combines the precision of RL with the clear, human-like reasoning of Large Language Models (LLMs) to create a more transparent and reliable insulin pump controller. By training an expert RL system and distilling its knowledge into fine-tuned LLaMA 3.1 8B and Qwen3 8B models, we developed a controller that not only surpasses the RL system's performance but also explains its decisions in plain, understandable language. Tested on the FDA-approved UVA/Padova T1D simulator, the LLM controllers deliver excellent blood sugar control (73.5% Time in Range) while maintaining strict formal safety verification against hallucinations.

</details>


### [226] [Human AI Construction of Bayesian Networks for Operational Decision Support -- A Virtual Survey Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14141)
*Kumar Rahul,Shovan Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A six-step Bayesian Belief Network (BBN) framework that blends expert opinion and data-driven learning via an AI-panel with trimmed-mean noise reduction; applied to customer intention to consult a doctor in an alternative healthcare system; finds subjective norms stronger than self-efficacy, with a strategy to enhance both.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between expert judgment and data-driven learning in BBNs by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to synthesize probabilistic estimates from diverse personas, addressing limitations of traditional reliance on experts or large datasets.

Method: Assemble a panel of AI agents with specific personas/context to estimate conditional probabilities for the BBN; aggregate responses using a trimmed-mean rule to reduce noise; implement a six-step BBN framework; illustrate with a case study on customer intention to consult a doctor in an alternative healthcare system.

Result: The model indicates that self-efficacy, though a major factor, has a small actual causal impact; subjective norms exert a stronger influence on intention; the most effective strategy is to improve both confidence (self-efficacy) and community norms concurrently, acknowledging the noise-reduction via trimming.

Conclusion: An LLM-based ensemble approach can effectively integrate expert opinion with data-driven learning for BBNs, offering a viable path when data are scarce or noisy; in the case study, addressing both individual confidence and social norms yields the strongest predictive and prescriptive insights.

Abstract: Bayesian Belief Networks (BBNs) are powerful tools for decision-making under uncertainty. However, building their structures and estimating parameters are difficult. Currently, researchers must choose between relying on expert judgement or using large datasets to learn the structure and parameters of the network. We propose a new methodology using Large Language Models to bridge the gap between expert opinion and data-driven learning. This approach uses a panel of AI agents to estimate probabilities based on specific personas and context. We then apply a trimmed-mean rule to remove noise from these responses. We develop a six step BBN framework and illustrate it to model customer intention to consult a doctor in an alternative healthcare system. The model reveals that while self efficacy appears to be a major factor, its actual causal impact is small. In contrast, subjective norms have a much stronger effect in modelling customers' intention. The most effective strategy is to improve both confidence and community norms simultaneously.

</details>


### [227] [Capability from Access Structure, Not Scale: Lower Bounds and Pre-Registered Tests for Hybrid Sequence Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14144)
*Wenhui Chen,Jianlin Chen,Ziyao Lin,Chi Man Vong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Scale yields representational convergence; capability convergence is not guaranteed and requires an explicit access structure (a two-channel hybrid) under resource constraints. The work separates proven results from conjectures and reports pre-registered experiments with mixed outcomes; convergence of capabilities remains contingent on architectural access structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether increasing model scale produces not only better representations but also higher capabilities, and identify structural conditions that enable capability convergence.

Method: Introduce theoretical framework with PRH and CCH, define access-complete hybrid (compressive O(1)-state channel + scalable verbatim-index channel), specify three resource walls (Shannon wall, horizon wall, circuit wall). State an explicit separability assumption; derive information-theoretic lower bounds for access-complete capability; demonstrate cross-wall feasibility via priced access structure; conduct pre-registered small-scale experiments (measured exact-retrieval error, state-tracking bifurcation, conjunction witness); clearly separate proven results from conjectures.

Result: Representational convergence is scale-driven; capability convergence is not guaranteed by scale alone and must be purchased via access structure. Under the separability assumption, a hybrid can, in principle, cross all three resource walls, with capability being strictly super-additive under composition. The paper reports partial experimental validation: some criteria align (scissors gap, boundary bifurcation, two-channel conjunction), but one prediction fails with reversed direction. The field-level convergence trend remains conjectural.

Conclusion: The work clarifies a boundary between representation and capability growth: scale suffices for representations, but capabilities demand an explicit access structure. It provides a first formal, though incomplete, step and invites formalization and broader replication.

Abstract: The Platonic Representation Hypothesis (PRH) holds that as models scale, representations of heterogeneous networks converge toward a shared model of reality. We propose its sequel and boundary, the Capability Convergence Hypothesis (CCH): under a fixed per-token inference budget, representational convergence does not entail capability convergence. Capability instead converges toward a class, the access-complete hybrid: any architecture holding both a compressive O(1)-state channel and a scalable verbatim-index channel. We anchor it on a witness task, the Newton's-apple problem in an infinite stream, and name three resource walls: a Shannon wall barring any o(Nb)-state architecture, a horizon wall barring any fixed window, and a circuit wall barring fixed-depth attention-only composition (conditional on TC0 != NC1). Under an explicit separability assumption a hybrid crosses all three by paying each wall's price, so capability is strictly super-additive under composition. We separate what we prove from what we conjecture: the access-completeness principle rests on information-theoretic lower bounds and pre-registered experiments, while the field-level convergence trend is an economics-motivated conjecture. We report the first pre-registered small-scale tests under criteria frozen before the data: the predicted scissors gap is measured (exact-retrieval error 0.994 vs. 0.000 once a 64-scalar state gains one global-attention layer), the state-tracking bifurcation lands at the registered boundary, and a conjunction witness shows an irreducibly two-channel solution; one prediction failed with its direction reversed and is reported as such. Representational convergence is given freely by scale; capability convergence must be purchased by access structure.

</details>


### [228] [ToolAnchor: Anchoring Counterfactual Context to Boost Agentic Tool-use Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14145)
*Weiting Liu,Jieyi Bi,Wanqi Zhou,Jianfeng Feng,Yining Ma,Ai Han,Wenlian Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolAnchor mitigates behavioral inertia in tool-augmented LLM agents by injecting counterfactual anchor contexts, hypothesized by teacher models and validated via student rollouts, enabling post-training to internalize the intervention and perform well with expanded toolsets across diverse tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When agents are equipped with fixed toolsets, they exhibit behavioral inertia and rely on familiar tools and reasoning patterns, hindering adaptation to new tools. Retraining from scratch is often impractical, creating a need for scalable dynamic adaptation.

Method: Propose ToolAnchor: a teacher-student framework where teachers hypothesize counterfactual anchor contexts at critical decision points; student rollouts verify these contexts; successful interventions are internalized through agentic post-training to update the agent's behavior under expanded toolsets.

Result: Extensive evaluations across GAIA (general AI assistant), BrowseComp (textual search), and VDR-Bench (visual search) show that ToolAnchor consistently achieves competitive performance when toolsets are expanded.

Conclusion: ToolAnchor bridges the gap between static post-training and dynamic adaptation, offering a scalable path for agentic reinforcement learning by enabling robust integration of new tools without full retraining.

Abstract: Tool-augmented large language model agents excel at long-horizon tasks, yet they are typically post-trained on fixed toolsets. When tasks demand new tools, these agents struggle to incorporate them effectively, and retraining from scratch is often impractical. We identify the core obstacle in such toolset expansion problem as behavioral inertia: the tendency of agents to fall back on familiar tools and established reasoning patterns despite having access to new ones. We demonstrate that injecting counterfactual anchor contexts at critical decision points can break this inertia, recovering failed trajectories by eliciting suppressed agent capabilities. To scale this insight, we propose ToolAnchor, a framework that uses teacher models to hypothesize these counterfactual contexts, verifies them via student rollouts, and internalizes the successful interventions through agentic post-training. Extensive evaluations across general AI assistant (GAIA), textual search (BrowseComp), and visual search (VDR-Bench) tasks demonstrate that ToolAnchor consistently exhibits competitive performance under expanded toolsets. Our work bridges the gap between static post-training and dynamic adaptation, charting a new path for scalable agentic reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [229] [Enhancing Small Language Models Reasoning through Knowledge Graph Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14149)
*Dimitrios Kelesis,Konstantinos Bougiatiotis,Georgios Paliouras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Neuro-symbolic augmentation improves small LMs on CLUTRR via symbolic fact extraction and RGCN reasoning; gains ~1.5–2x over story-only baselines but extraction bottlenecks and error propagation limit performance, with a distraction effect observed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the environmental and cost burden of large LLMs while enabling reliable multi-hop reasoning with small models by incorporating symbolic grounding and expert reasoning within a minimalist agentic framework.

Method: Transform SLMs into minimalist agents that use two tools: extract_facts for symbolic triplet extraction and get_hint for RGCN-based expert reasoning; evaluate Gemma 3 (1B, 4B) and Llama 3.2 (3B) on the CLUTRR kinship benchmark under two settings: Oracle (ground-truth triplets) and Realistic (self-extracted knowledge).

Result: RGCN-derived hints provide 1.5–2x performance gains over story-only baselines; however, extraction bottlenecks and sequential deductive fragility limit gains; a distraction effect occurs when noisy self-generated facts degrade performance despite the presence of expert hints.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the challenges of symbolic grounding in low-resource agentic systems and offers a roadmap for iterative verification and refinement of neuro-symbolic agentic pipelines.

Abstract: Although large language models (LLMs) have set benchmarks for zero-shot reasoning, their deployment remains cost-prohibitive and environmentally taxing. Small Language Models (SLMs) offer a sustainable alternative, but prone to errors, on tasks requiring complex, multi-hop logical grounding. We investigate a neuro-symbolic agentic framework to enhance the reasoning capabilities of SLMs, specifically Gemma 3 (1B, 4B) and Llama 3.2 (3B), using the CLUTRR kinship benchmark. Our approach transforms the SLM into a minimalist agent utilizing two specialized tool calls: extract_facts for symbolic triplet extraction and get_hint for expert reasoning via a Relational Graph Convolutional Network (RGCN). We evaluate these models across two configurations, both in an Oracle scenario with ground-truth triplets and a Realistic scenario relying on self-extracted knowledge. Our results reveal that while RGCN-derived hints provide a 1.5 - 2x performance gain over story-only baselines, the system is constrained by the extraction bottleneck and sequential deductive fragility, where early extraction errors compound over multi-hop chains. Furthermore, we identify a "distraction effect" in specific architectures where noisy, self-generated facts degrade performance despite the presence of expert hints. This work characterizes the challenges of symbolic grounding in low-resource agentic systems and provides a roadmap for iterative verification in neuro-symbolic agentic pipelines.

</details>


### [230] [Orchestrating Power Grid Studies with Multi-Agent AI and MCP Servers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14158)
*Jérôme Picault,Clément Goubet*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Agentic AI and Model Context Protocol (MCP) to integrate LLMs with grid-simulation tools in a TSO context; introduces pypowsybl-mcp, a testbed for AI-assisted grid studies; outlines human-in-the-loop and multi-agent workflows; argues MCP-based integration enables auditable, scalable grid studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable AI-assisted, interactive, auditable grid studies by combining large language models with numerical power-system simulators via a standardized interface.

Method: Propose MCP-based interface exposing selected capabilities of pypowsybl to AI agents; define workflows for agents to set up simulations, run analyses, retrieve results, and interact with simulators; discuss human-in-the-loop and multi-agent orchestration; outline evaluation strategy.

Result: Introduction of a testbed (pypowsybl-mcp) enabling agent-enabled grid studies; conceptual demonstration of agent-driven setup, execution, and result retrieval through standardized tool calls.

Conclusion: MCP-based tool integration advances interactive, auditable, and scalable grid-study environments and provides a foundation for future evaluation and iteration.

Abstract: This position paper explores how Agentic AI and Model Context Protocol (MCP) can support power-grid studies in a Transmission System Operator (TSO) context. We focus on integrating Large Language Models with numerical simulation tools, structured workflows, and human supervision. We identify key industrial requirements for agent assisted grid studies and introduce pypowsybl-mcp, an MCP-based interface exposing selected capabilities of our simulation tool, pypowsybl to AI agents. This first step provides a testbed to study how agents can setup simulations, execute analyses, retrieve results, and interact with power-system simulators through standardized tool calls. We also discuss principles for human-in-the-loop, multi-agent workflows and outline an evaluation strategy combining technical metrics and practitioner feedback. The paper positions MCP-based tool integration as a step toward more interactive, auditable, and scalable grid-study environments.

</details>


### [231] [MemoHarness: Agent Harnesses That Learn from Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14159)
*Yue Huang,Wenjie Wang,Han Bao,Yuchen Ma,Xiaonan Luo,Yi Nian,Haomin Zhuang,Zheyuan Liu,Yue Zhao,Xiangliang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemoHarness presents an adaptive framework to optimize LLM harnesses by learning from execution history, using a six-dimension control space and a dual-layer experience bank to customize per-case harness without test-time labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the dominance of fixed, one-size-fits-all harnesses and narrow optimization targets by enabling per-case adaptation through experiential learning.

Method: Decompose harness into six editable control dimensions (context, tools, orchestration, memory, decoding, output handling); build a dual-layer experience bank with per-case diagnoses and distilled global patterns; retrieve and adapt harness using past executions without labels or extra search; evaluate across shell-agent, code-generation, and analytical-reasoning benchmarks; assess transfer to unseen suites and cost implications of cached knowledge.

Result: MemoHarness outperforms fixed-harness baselines; shows selective transfer to unseen suites and base models; extra context remains cost-competitive due to cacheability; evidence supports actionable use of execution experience for adaptive harnessing.

Conclusion: Execution experience is a practical substrate for building more adaptive agent harnesses than static configurations; broader claims about robustness and component attribution require future work.

Abstract: An agent harness is the external control layer that turns a base LLM into an executable agent by managing context, tools, orchestration, memory, decoding, and output handling. While harness design strongly affects agent behavior, most automatic improvement methods optimize narrower artifacts such as prompts, pipelines, or workflows, and deployed agents usually reuse a single global harness for all cases. We introduce MemoHarness, an adaptive harness optimization framework that learns from its own executions. MemoHarness decomposes the harness into six editable control dimensions, stores per-case diagnoses and distilled global patterns in a dual-layer experience bank, and adapts the learned harness to each test case using retrieved experience without test-time labels, feedback, or additional search. In our evaluation across shell-agent, code-generation, and analytical-reasoning benchmarks, MemoHarness improves over the fixed harnesses we compare against and shows selective transfer to unseen suites and base models. Its additional context can also remain cost-competitive when much of the retrieved experience is cacheable. These results provide evidence that execution experience is a practical substrate for building agent harnesses that are more adaptive than a single static configuration, while leaving broader claims about statistical robustness and component attribution to future work.

</details>


### [232] [When a Verified World Model Still Loses: Play-Adequacy vs Prediction-Accuracy in LLM-Synthesized Code World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14169)
*Javier Aguilar Martín*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-synthesized Code World Models can pass high transition accuracy on sampled data yet fail in play due to pivotal rare missed rules; thus planning adequacy should be judged by search distribution or actual play, not by sampling transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenge the common adequacy criterion for planning with LLM-synthesized world models and quantify the risk of rare but pivotal errors that are not captured by sampling-based metrics.

Method: Synthesize executable world models from LLMs; evaluate on sampling-based transition accuracy and state accuracy within the planner's search distribution; analyze end-to-end play outcomes; derive and validate a danger law; extend analysis to imperfect-information CWMs and belief-inference; provide theoretical bounds (coverage) and construct a counterexample Beacon.

Result: Identification of the verified-vs-correct gap: models can be gate-passing yet perform poorly in play due to pivotal omissions. A quantitative danger law: danger = play_cost × (1 − rarity)^N with an exact gate-miss factor. Data quantity does not repair the gap; synthesis behaves as translation, not inference. Similar gaps in belief-inference with a coverage bound; shallow games show no gap; Beacon demonstrates verified-but-wrong inference. Implications for evaluation: measure adequacy by search distribution or direct play, not transition prediction accuracy.

Conclusion: Evaluation of planning-oriented world models should focus on planning-time effects and actual gameplay, not merely sampled-transition accuracy. The gap between verification on data and correct play motivates revised evaluation protocols and theory for planning with LLM-synthesized CWMs.

Abstract: Large language models can synthesize a game's rules as executable code - a Code World Model (CWM) - which a classical planner then searches over. Such models are typically accepted when they reach high transition accuracy on sampled trajectories. We argue this is the wrong notion of adequacy for planning.
  We show four things. (1) An LLM-synthesized CWM can pass a sampling gate at 100% transition accuracy and be $\geq 98\%$ state-accurate on the planner's own search distribution, yet lose systematically at play, because the $<1\%$ it gets wrong is exactly the pivotal dynamics; the play cost of the omitted rule is $0.091$ (seed-clustered 95% CI $[0.065,0.117]$, $n=4800$). We call this the verified-vs-correct gap, and confirm it end-to-end through the synthesis pipeline. (2) The harm follows a quantitative law, $\mathrm{danger}=\mathrm{play\_cost}\times(1-\mathrm{rarity})^N$, whose $(1-\mathrm{rarity})^N$ gate-miss factor is proven exact and whose play cost is empirically bounded. (3) The failure is not repaired by more data: LLM synthesis behaves as rule translation, not rule inference, and did not infer the omitted rule across models (GPT-5.x) and data regimes (including DAgger and targeted examples). (4) The same mechanism recurs on the belief-inference function of imperfect-information CWMs: we prove a coverage bound (a size-$N$ gate is identifying when $N\gtrsim b^{d_{\max}}$), explaining why shallow games such as Kuhn poker show no gap, and hand-construct Beacon, a verified-but-wrong inference function that passes the gate yet loses every game.
  These results suggest adequacy for planning-oriented world models should be measured on the search distribution or by play directly, not by prediction accuracy on sampled transitions.

</details>


### [233] [ReasFlow: Assisting Reasoning-Centric Scientific Discovery in Applied Mathematics via a Knowledge-Based Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14178)
*Yutong He,Daibo Li,Guohong Li,Jiahe Geng,Zhengyang Huang,Can Ren,Zekun Zhang,Yifan Liu,Shuchen Zhu,Hengrui Zhang,Boao Kong,Ming Sun,Shu Li,Chenyi Li,Jiang Hu,Kun Yuan,Zaiwen Wen,Pingwen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReasFlow is an end-to-end autonomous reasoning-centric AI system that collaborates with a human PI to perform theory-heavy scientific discovery, enabling autonomous derivations, verification, literature synthesis, and manuscript generation, and claiming to produce five papers with rigorous content and high scores on an LLM-based review rubric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap in theory-driven scientific discovery where rigorous proofs, procedural heuristics, and verification are challenging for automated systems, especially in mathematically grounded domains.

Method: An end-to-end autonomous agent framework where the human expert acts as Principal Investigator and the agent as a capable graduate student. Key components include (i) an internal verification loop auditing logical coherence and correcting errors before human review, (ii) automated knowledge retrieval and self-improvement that surface declarative facts and overlooked procedural heuristics. The system integrates literature synthesis, algorithm design, theorem proving, experimentation, and manuscript preparation in one platform.

Result: Autonomously generated five complete research papers with rigorous theoretical and empirical content from minimal prompts; achieved the highest scores among state-of-the-art open-access baselines on a curated LLM-based review rubric.

Conclusion: ReasFlow demonstrates a viable reasoning-centric pathway for AI-assisted theoretical research, suggesting that integrated verification, proactive knowledge retrieval, and end-to-end workflow unification can enable high-quality theory-driven outputs. The approach is publicly accessible via ReasLab (GitHub).

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models have fueled autonomous AI agents capable of tackling complex scientific tasks, yet existing automated research systems remain predominantly focused on empirically driven domains with quantitative benchmarks, leaving theory-driven discovery, particularly in mathematically grounded disciplines requiring rigorous proofs and synthesis of domain knowledge, largely underexplored. Key challenges include the difficulty of verifying theoretical reasoning at scale, insufficient reasoning ability for autonomous frontier exploration, and a scarcity of procedural heuristics in the literature. We introduce ReasFlow, an end-to-end autonomous agent system for reasoning-centric scientific discovery that operationalizes a collaborative paradigm where the human expert acts as Principal Investigator while the agent executes rigorous derivations as a capable graduate student. ReasFlow incorporates (i) a robust internal verification loop that audits logical coherence and corrects fundamental errors prior to human inspection, and (ii) an automated knowledge retrieval and self-improvement mechanism that proactively surfaces both declarative facts and overlooked procedural heuristics, substantially reducing expert intervention. The system unifies literature synthesis, algorithm design, theorem proving, experimentation, and manuscript preparation in a single system. Deployed to autonomously generate five complete research papers with rigorous theoretical and empirical content from minimal prompts, ReasFlow consistently achieves the highest evaluation scores among state-of-the-art open-access baselines under a curated LLM-based review rubric. ReasFlow is publicly accessible via the ReasLab platform, providing a collaborative workspace for AI-assisted theoretical research. Github repo: https://github.com/ReasLab/ReasFlow.git.

</details>


### [234] [RxBrain: Embodied Cognition Foundation Model with Joint Language-Visual Reasoning and Imagination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14187)
*Haotian Liang,Mingkang Chen,Yufei Huang,Yuchun Guo,Xiaomeng Zhu,Xiangli Shi,Kaixuan Wang,Yunxuan Mao,Weijie Zhou,Ling Chen,Shirong Zeng,Yueyu Long,Yuchen Si,Yajuan Zhu,Xingyu Zhou,Minghui Wang,Wanjia He,Xin Yang,Lingzhu Xiang,Zhiqing Liu,Bohan Ma,Xiran Huang,Tianshuo Yang,Zhiheng Liu,Xuantang Xiong,Zisheng Lu,Ping Luo,Yao Mu,Han Hu,Zhengyou Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RxBrain is a unified embodied cognition foundation model that integrates language-based planning with visual imagination into a single planning sequence, trained via automatic text-visual supervision from videos, and evaluated with RxBrain-Bench; extended to real-time robot action generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge high-level task reasoning with physical grounding. Existing vision-language models emphasize scene understanding or textual decisions, while generative world models predict future visuals but lack integrated planning grounded in world states. The work proposes a joint language-visual planning framework for embodied agents.

Method: A unified Mixture-of-Transformers architecture that handles language, image, and video within one model. It uses an automatic pipeline to convert embodied videos into joint text-visual planning supervision by decomposing videos into planning steps and aligning them with visual state transitions. It enables joint textual and visual planning, with world-state grounding for each planning step. Evaluation is via RxBrain-Bench, a benchmark for assessing representations of embodied plans, and there is an extension to continuous robot action generation.

Result: RxBrain maintains embodied understanding and generation, producing plans that couple textual reasoning with world-state prediction and subgoal planning. It demonstrates promising real-robot performance without large-scale action-data pretraining.

Conclusion: Represents an initial step toward foundation models for embodied cognition, providing a unified framework that connects planning, grounding, and action, and offering a benchmark and pathway for future work.

Abstract: Embodied cognition requires agents to connect high-level task reasoning with the physical states to be achieved. We introduce Hy-Embodied-RxBrain, an embodied cognition foundation model with joint language-visual reasoning and imagination. Unlike vision-language models that emphasize scene understanding and textual decision making, or generative world models that mainly predict future visual states, RxBrain represents embodied plans in a single planning sequence where language and visual imagination play complementary roles. Language provides the abstract structure of a plan, including task decomposition, planning primitives, constraints, temporal order, and decision logic, while visual imagination grounds this structure through world state prediction and joint subgoal planning, associating each planning step with intermediate and final physical states. RxBrain adopts a unified multimodal Mixture-of-Transformers architecture that supports language, image, and video understanding and generation within one model. To train this capability, we build an automatic pipeline that converts embodied videos into joint text-visual planning supervision by decomposing videos into planning steps and aligning them with visual state transitions. We further introduce RxBrain-Bench to evaluate whether models can represent embodied plans through joint textual and visual components rather than separate understanding or generation. Experiments show that RxBrain maintains embodied understanding and generation abilities, and produces plans with coupled textual reasoning, world state prediction, and joint subgoal planning. We also extend RxBrain to continuous robot action generation, where it shows promising real-robot performance without large-scale action-data pretraining. These results provide an initial step toward foundation models for embodied cognition.

</details>


### [235] [How Artificial Intelligence LLM Engines Shape the Global Conflict Information Environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14197)
*Jason Miklian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs' conflict answers hallucinate more when the retrievable record is sparse; evidence of Generative Engine Optimization (GEO) shaping sources is present and growing; policy and scholarship should emphasize deep, locally grounded, translation-based monitoring, as AI tools cannot replace it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how patterning of hallucinations in conflict topics relates to the changing information environment and to evaluate the risk and mechanics of GEO-based manipulation.

Method: Empirical evaluation comparing 5 leading answer engines across 28 conflicts (5,460 answers against documented evidence) plus a content-source analysis of 1,048 websites the AI pulled conflict facts from to detect GEO source optimization.

Result: Finds that thinner retrievable records around a conflict correlate with more hallucination, misattribution, and miscount. GEO source optimization is occurring, with growing state-partisan digital capture, while such capture is still incipient. These factors create structural exposure to mis- and disinformation in sparse-information contexts.

Conclusion: The rise of GEO information warfare has significant scholarly and policy implications, warranting a shift toward deep local monitoring and translation-based research that AI tools cannot replicate; outlines future research directions and challenges in this rapidly evolving area.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) answer engines now field a growing share of the questions that analysts, scholars, and the public ask about issues of peace and conflict. Large Language Models (LLMs) are known to hallucinate under certain conditions, but do these errors have discernible patterns when they are asked about conflicts, and if so what can that teach us about the changing global conflict information environment? To answer, we first asked a battery of questions about 28 conflicts to five leading answer engines and scored their 5,460 answers against documented evidence. We found that the thinner the retrievable record around a given conflict, the more the engines invent, misattribute, and miscount. Thin records don't just encourage hallucination, but create structural exposure to mis- and disinformation, because they are the easiest records to warp through Generative Engine Optimization (GEO) to bias engine responses. Through an analysis of 1,048 websites that the AI LLMs pulled conflict facts from, we found that GEO source optimization is already happening, and while state-partisan digital capture remains incipient it is rapidly growing. We explain what these findings mean for scholarship with the rise of GEO information warfare, and for policy argue for a return to the deep local monitoring and translation-based research that AI tools cannot replicate, closing with a discussion of future research opportunities and challenges in this fast-moving space.

</details>


### [236] [Align AI to Dynamic Human-AI Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14240)
*Valerie Chen,Cleotilde Gonzalez,Anita Williams Woolley,Michael Lee,Tongshuang Wu,Vincent Conitzer,Aarti Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes shifting from static, emulative alignment to interactive, trajectory-level alignment where human preferences emerge through ongoing interaction; outlines a research agenda integrating ML with social and decision sciences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current alignment predominantly imitates static human preferences, neglecting the dynamic, context-dependent nature of real-world human–AI interactions and the asymmetries that arise in collaboration.

Method: Conceptual analysis grounded in insights from an interdisciplinary workshop; synthesizes social-science literature on human–human collaboration and its amplification in human–AI systems.

Result: Introduces a trajectory-level co-evolution framework for human–AI alignment, highlighting emergent preferences and new uncertainty and coordination challenges; identifies key research avenues and interdisciplinary synthesis requirements.

Conclusion: To align AI with humans in interaction, we need an interdisciplinary research program at the intersection of ML and social/decision sciences that models and engineers co-evolving preferences and interaction dynamics.

Abstract: Current alignment approaches typically focus on emulating human behavior using static representations of human preferences, failing to capture the dynamic, context-dependent nature of real-world human-AI interactions. In this paper, we argue for a shift from static and emulative to interactive and complementary alignment, where preferences emerge through interaction and alignment is defined not by satisfying preferences alone. We first formalize this gap by contrasting existing alignment with a trajectory-level view in which human and model behavior co-evolve over time. Because these interaction dynamics have not been adequately captured within existing ML formulations, we ground this perspective in insights from an interdisciplinary workshop. We draw on lessons from social-science accounts of human-human collaboration and then argue that human-AI systems amplify these dynamics, introducing new asymmetries that make reasoning about uncertainty harder and introduce new coordination challenges. Based on these lessons and new challenges, we conclude by outlining a research agenda for developing AI systems that align with humans in interaction, requiring an interdisciplinary synthesis of machine learning and the social and decision sciences.

</details>


### [237] [The Steering Budget: Examples beat Knobs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14246)
*Raj Kumar Rajendran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Controllability of generative models is budget-limited by training data: prompts and guidance scales reach only part of a property, while a larger portion requires example-based guidance drawn from what the model has already learned. The authors propose a cheap training-data audit to quantify this budget and a recipe to assemble example sets that exhaust it, enabling both reach and expressiveness across domains, verified in image and crystal-structure generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why knob-based controls (prompts, guidance scales, and property tags) saturate and fail to move certain properties, and to propose a data-driven remedy that extends controllability beyond what prompts can achieve.

Method: Introduce a two-part notion of property movability (knob-reachable vs example-reachable) determined by the training data budget. Develop a cheap audit of the training data to measure the budget and present a recipe for constructing example sets that, composed from learned data, reach the full budget. Validate the approach by applying it to image generation and crystal-structure generation, identifying when a knob suffices and when exemplar guidance is necessary.

Result: Demonstrates that part of a property’s movability is inaccessible to knobs due to budget; the remaining portion is reachable via carefully constructed exemplars drawn from the model’s learned data. The exemplar approach moves a property across the full budget and expands expressiveness to targets defined by examples, including unwritable ones. The authors verify claims across two domains, marking where knobs suffice and where examples are required.

Conclusion: Knob-based control alone cannot fully exploit a model’s controllability; the training-data budget governs movability. Combining knob-based and exemplar-based guidance, with a cheap data audit and a concrete recipe for exemplar sets, yields both reach and expressiveness and generalizes across domains.

Abstract: Generative models are steered with knobs -- prompts, guidance scales, property tags. Turn one as hard as you like and, past a point, it stops moving the property you care about. We find that ceiling is not a shortcoming of the model but a budget, set by the training data before the model is trained: a property's movable range splits in two -- the part a knob can reach, and a second, significant part that only examples -- concrete instances of what you want more of -- can reach. That second part is usually much larger, but not always, and the same budget says so in advance.
  Reaching that second part takes a different move: instead of turning a knob, you show the model examples, composed from what it already learned rather than added to its training. A cheap audit of the training data measures the budget; we give a recipe for building the example set that reaches all of it.
  This buys two things a knob can't. Reach: it moves a property across the whole budget, not just the part a knob reaches. Expressiveness: it steers toward targets you can only specify by example -- including ones you can't put into words. We turn these into a handful of falsifiable claims and verify them in two unrelated domains, image and crystal-structure generation -- marking where a knob is enough, and where only examples will do.

</details>


### [238] [Automatic Hard Example Synthesis with Multi-Level Agentic Data Curation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14256)
*Genglin Liu,Muye Zhang,Krishnamurthy Viswanathan,Nichole J. Hansen,Blaž Bratanič,Nathan L Clement,Shalini Ghosh,Ariel Fuxman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent automated red-teaming framework autonomously generates adversarial multimodal examples to improve MLLM robustness, achieving a substantial FNR drop on a public image safety benchmark without human labeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalably identifying edge-case vulnerabilities and policy violations in multimodal models where manual annotation and active learning struggle due to volume and complexity of threats.

Method: An agentic system with a high-reasoning Architect, an advanced image generator, and a multi-level LLM-based verification committee. It iteratively proposes novel hypotheses and mutates past attempts, using test-time retrieval to present the adversarial examples as in-context demonstrations, all without human intervention.

Result: The framework substantially improves target model robustness, reducing False Negative Rate from 41.2% to 24.5% on a public image safety benchmark without any human labeling.

Conclusion: Agentic red-teaming can automatically uncover boundary-pushing violations and ambiguous policy edge cases, enhancing robustness; the approach raises considerations about automation of adversarial content generation and its generalizability to other modalities.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed for nuanced content safety and moderation tasks, yet they remain vulnerable to adversarial attacks and out-of-distribution edge cases. Traditional active learning and manual annotation fail to scale against the complexity and volume of novel multimodal threats. In this paper, we propose an automated, agentic red-teaming framework that systematically synthesizes difficult examples using an iterative strategy that proposes novel hypotheses as well as mutating on past attempts. Leveraging a multi-agent architecture that consists of a high-reasoning Architect agent, an advanced image generator, and a multi-level verification committee of LLM raters, our system autonomously uncovers boundary-pushing violations and ambiguous policy edge cases without any human intervention. By employing these carefully synthesized adversarial examples as in-context demonstrations via test-time Retrieval, we substantially improve the target model's robustness, reducing the False Negative Rate (FNR) from 41.2% to 24.5% in a public image safety benchmark without relying on any human labeling.

</details>


### [239] [AI Agents Do Not Fail Alone:The Context Fails First](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14275)
*Fouad Bousetouane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Context-engineering quality is a leading indicator of AI agent reliability; ProofAgent-Harness uses seven context criteria and multi-juror consensus to predict and validate agent behavior, enabling auditable governance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agents’ reliability hinges on context (instructions, tools, memory, guardrails, inputs). Without measuring context, failures and hallucinations go unchecked; a preflight, independent context-quality metric can forecast behavior and support governance.

Method: Develop ProofAgent-Harness with multi-juror, consensus-based scoring. Assess seven context criteria—role clarity, guardrail coverage, instruction consistency, tool schema quality, grounding sufficiency, injection hardening, and token efficiency—across controlled studies. Hold frontier LLM agents fixed while varying operating context, then correlate context scores with behavioral outcomes to demonstrate predictive validity.

Result: Context-quality criteria consistently predict targeted behaviors: grounding sufficiency predicts hallucination resistance; guardrail coverage predicts manipulation resistance; instruction consistency predicts instruction following; tool-schema quality predicts tool use. The context score is independent from behavioral metrics, enabling non-circular validation.

Conclusion: Context measurement is a validated preflight signal for agent reliability, positioning context engineering as an auditable layer in agent evaluation and governance.

Abstract: Context engineering has become central to building reliable AI agents, yet it remains largely unmeasured. Agents do not fail in isolation: their behavior is shaped by the instructions, tools, memory, retrieved knowledge, guardrails, and untrusted inputs accumulated in their context. When this context is weak, agents drift, hallucinate, misuse tools, ignore constraints, become vulnerable to injection, and waste tokens. This paper validates context-engineering quality as an independent leading indicator of agent reliability. We implement the measurement in ProofAgent-Harness, an open-source infrastructure for AI agent evaluation that uses multi-juror, consensus-based scoring. The harness assesses context across seven criteria: role clarity, guardrail coverage, instruction consistency, tool schema quality, grounding sufficiency, injection hardening, and token efficiency. Crucially, the context score is isolated from behavioral metrics and release decisions, enabling a non-circular validation. Through a controlled context-quality study across regulated agent domains, holding frontier LLM agents fixed and varying only their operating context, we show that context-quality criteria consistently predict their corresponding behavioral outcomes. Grounding sufficiency predicts hallucination resistance, guardrail coverage predicts manipulation resistance, instruction consistency predicts instruction following, and tool-schema quality predicts tool use. These findings establish context measurement as a validated preflight signal for agent reliability and position context engineering as an auditable layer of agent evaluation and governance.

</details>


### [240] [Measuring How Students Rely on Generative AI in Academic Writing: Development and Multi-Source Validation of the Generative AI Reliance Types Scale (GenAI-RTS)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14301)
*Shahin Hossain,Tukhbita Afroz Nawmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces the GenAI Reliance Types Scale (GenAI-RTS), a 20-item instrument to measure four reliance types in undergraduate GenAI use, with Strategic (Deliberate Use and Critical Evaluation), Instrumental, Dependent, and Dialogic factors. Five-factor structure confirmed; reliable (omega .75-.88); scalar invariance across gender, first-generation status, and STEM/non-STEM; Rasch analysis supports a five-point scale. Strategic reliance correlates with AI literacy and differentiates writing processes/outcomes. Provides a validated tool for research, assessment, and AI literacy intervention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond whether students rely on GenAI to characterize reliance profiles; address limitations of existing measures (inductive, task-specific, homogenous samples) and support learning, integrity, and equity in AI-enabled undergraduate writing.

Method: Developed 20-item scale and validated it per the Standards for Educational and Psychological Testing. Data from 382 undergraduates at a U.S. Minority-Serving Institution and interviews with 14 purposively sampled students. Confirmatory factor analyses compared six models, yielding a five-factor structure (Strategic with Deliberate Use and Critical Evaluation; Instrumental; Dependent; Dialogic). Assessed reliability (omega .75-.88), scalar invariance across gender, first-generation status, and STEM/non-STEM; Rasch analysis indicating a five-point response improves category functioning.

Result: Support for a five-factor GenAI-RTS; Strategic Reliance split into Deliberate Use and Critical Evaluation; reliable subscales; measurement invariance across key groups; Rasch-validated response structure; Strategic reliance linked to AI literacy and differed across writing-process and outcome variables.

Conclusion: GenAI-RTS is a theoretically grounded, psychometrically validated instrument that enables identification of undergraduate reliance profiles, supporting research, assessment, and AI literacy interventions; notably the first evidence of invariance across several demographic groups for a GenAI reliance measure.

Abstract: As generative AI (GenAI) becomes increasingly embedded in undergraduate academic writing, how students rely on these tools, rather than simply whether they use them, has become a central question for learning, academic integrity, and educational equity. Existing measures of reliance were developed inductively, focused on discrete problem-solving tasks, and validated mainly with homogeneous samples. This study developed and validated the GenAI Reliance Types Scale (GenAI-RTS), a 20-item instrument measuring four theoretically derived types of GenAI reliance: Strategic, Instrumental, Dependent, and Dialogic. Validation followed the multisource framework of the Standards for Educational and Psychological Testing, drawing on a survey of 382 undergraduates at a U.S. Minority-Serving Institution and interviews with 14 purposively sampled students. Confirmatory factor analyses of six competing models supported a five-factor structure in which Strategic Reliance comprises two facets, Deliberate Use and Critical Evaluation, alongside Instrumental, Dependent, and Dialogic factors (CFI = .92, RMSEA = .08; DWLS CFI = .98, RMSEA = .07). Subscale reliability was acceptable to good (omega = .75-.88), and scalar measurement invariance held across gender, first-generation status, and STEM/non-STEM majors, to our knowledge the first such evidence for a GenAI reliance instrument. Rasch analysis indicated that a five-point response format would improve category functioning. Strategic reliance was positively associated with AI literacy, and the reliance types differentiated students across multiple writing process and outcome variables. The GenAI-RTS offers researchers and educators a theoretically grounded, psychometrically validated instrument for identifying undergraduate reliance profiles and supporting research, assessment, and AI literacy intervention.

</details>


### [241] [Tracing LLM Behavior to the Training Data with Empirical Next-Token Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14306)
*Zachary Izzo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Many inputs show near-perfect agreement between the LLM's next-token distribution and the empirical next-token distribution (ENTD) from training data, and this alignment improves with model scale and compute; however, a long tail of inputs exhibits meaningful discrepancies, suggesting sources in architecture, training procedure, and ENTD estimation noise; advocates data-centric mechanistic interpretability as a complementary approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the data used in pretraining shapes an LLM's predictive distribution by comparing it to the empirical distribution derived from the training corpus; to promote data-centric mechanistic interpretability that links model behavior to training data rather than solely to learned weights.

Method: Compare the LLM's next-token distribution (given a context) to the ENTD estimated from the training corpus; quantify agreement across input sequences; analyze sources of discrepancy by examining transformer architecture, training procedure, and finite-sample noise in ENTD estimates; assess how agreement scales with model size and training compute.

Result: A significant fraction of inputs show almost perfect agreement between the LLM and ENTD, with average agreement increasing as model scale and training compute grow. Nonetheless, there is a long tail of input sequences where the LLM and ENTD diverge substantially. The work probes potential sources of these discrepancies across architectural choices, training dynamics, and ENTD estimation noise.

Conclusion: The findings motivate more work on data-centric mechanistic interpretability, complementing conventional mechanistic interpretability by focusing on how model behaviors emerge from data rather than weights, and encouraging future efforts to diagnose and leverage data properties to understand and align model behavior.

Abstract: In this paper, we study the connection between an LLM's output distribution and the data used to train it. Specifically, we study the degree to which an LLM's next-token distribution agrees with the empirical next-token distribution (ENTD) given the context in the training data. The ENTD is an appealing target because it is the unrestricted global minimizer of the next-token cross entropy loss used for pretraining, as well as an easily interpretable function of the pretraining corpus. We find that for a significant fraction of inputs, the LLM's distribution agrees with the ENTD almost perfectly, and the average agreement increases with model scale and training compute. Nevertheless, there is a long tail of input sequences where the LLM and ENTD differ significantly, and we examine several possible sources of this discrepancy across the transformer architecture, training procedure, and finite-sample noise in the ENTD estimate itself. More broadly, we hope our findings will encourage more work on ``data-centric mechanistic interpretability,'' a complement to standard mechanistic interpretability that opens the black box of how model behaviors arise from the data, rather than how they are encoded in the learned weights.

</details>


### [242] [Traccia: An OpenTelemetry-Based Governance Platform for AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14309)
*Nutan Kumar Naik,Aditya Kumar Saroj,Vijay Prasad Poudel,Saurav Samantray,Abhishek Patel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Traccia, a multi-level AI governance stack built atop OpenTelemetry, to close gaps in evaluation, ML workflow, and monitoring by adding telemetry, guardrails, and execution lineage. It generates tamper-resistant, hash-based compliance evidence mapped to EU AI Act, enabling a machine-readable basis for enterprise-wide management of autonomous AI while preserving data privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap between AI governance theory and practice amid rapid LLMs and autonomous agents, by solving alignment drift, SaaS security concerns, and shadow AI deployments, and to align with EU AI Act requirements.

Method: Extend OpenTelemetry with Traccia: incorporate telemetry data, passive semantic guardrails, and execution lineage; maintain a hashed trace ledger with tamper-resistant fingerprints and content hashes (SHA-256); automatically assemble compliance evidence packages that map to EU AI Act Articles 12, 14, 19, 26(6), and 50, without compromising data privacy.

Result: A coherent, scalable AI governance stack is proposed. It yields a machine-readable base for enterprise-wide management and generates tamper-resistant compliance evidence packages mapped to regulatory requirements, demonstrating feasibility of automated governance and auditability for autonomous AI systems.

Conclusion: Traccia offers a practical path to closing the last mile of AI alignment and governance by providing end-to-end telemetry, guardrails, and execution lineage with verifiable, privacy-preserving compliance artifacts, suitable for enterprise adoption; future work should assess scalability, interoperability, and real-world validation.

Abstract: The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Artificial Intelligent (AI) powered autonomous agents has fundamentally changed the existing forms of software governance. In spite of the rigorous standards of transparency and account ability required according to the international frameworks such as the European Union's AI Act, there is a considerable gap between theory and reality. The present study discusses the inherent drawbacks of currently utilized platforms for LLM evaluation, machine learning workflow, and application performance monitoring in general. It has been shown that current disjointed solutions fail to protect unbound state space agentic architecture from serious threats such as alignment drift, SaaS security concerns, and unauthorized deployment of shadow AI systems. Moreover, a solution is proposed for overcoming the discussed challenges in form of a coherent multi-level AI governance stack Traccia built on the top of OpenTelemetry infrastructure platform. Traccia resolves the last mile for AI Alignment by adding the telemetry data, passive semantic guardrail assessment, and execution lineage into a hashed trace ledger. Traccia automatically creates compliance evidence packages by appending tamper-resistant fingerprints and SHA-256 content hash, that map to regulatory requirements (Articles 12, 14, 19, 26(6), and 50 of the EU AI Act) without invading any data privacy. By performing this evaluation in a methodical manner, a solid machine-readable base has been created for enterprise-wide management of autonomous AI systems.

</details>


### [243] [CIPHER: A Decoupled Exploration-Selection Framework for Test-Time Scaling of Data Science Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14386)
*Maxime Heuillet,Sharadind Peddiraju*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CIPHER introduces test-time scaling for data science agents by decoupling generation of multiple candidate initial states from their selection for parallel execution, improving robustness and performance over single-state agents and matching or beating larger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single initial states cause cascading errors in complex data science tasks; leveraging multiple potential starting points can mitigate brittleness and improve task success.

Method: Generate multiple candidate initial states at test time (DES: Decoupled Exploration-Selection) and run them in parallel; use an aggregator model to select or combine results; systematically study how generation strategy, selection strategy, and model capacity affect performance.

Result: CIPHER surpasses state-of-the-art in matched-model comparisons on both closed-form and open-form benchmarks, and remains competitive with larger models despite using a smaller base LM. The study maps the design space of DES and offers actionable guidance.

Conclusion: Decoupled Exploration-Selection enables robust, scalable test-time exploration for AI agents in data science tasks, with clear design recommendations for practitioners and avenues for extending test-time scaling.

Abstract: Data science tasks span from closed-ended information extraction to open-ended analysis, presenting significant challenges for automation. Recent AI agents powered by language models show promise for handling such complex tasks. However, existing agents typically rely on a single initial state that conditions the entire agent's execution, making them vulnerable to cascading errors initiated by a suboptimal initial state. To mitigate this, we present CIPHER, an automated data science agent that leverages test-time scaling through the generation and selection of multiple initial states for concurrent execution. Unlike existing works on test-time scaling of AI agents, CIPHER explicitly decouples the generation of candidate initial states from their strategic selection for parallel execution. Through extensive evaluation on two benchmarks (closed-form and open-form tasks), we demonstrate that CIPHER exceeds state-of-the-art performance in matched-model comparisons, and remains competitive against larger-model baselines despite relying on a substantially smaller base LM. Our empirical study characterizes the design space of the Decoupled Exploration-Selection (DES) framework: we quantify how generation strategy, selection strategy, and aggregator model capacity contribute to overall performance, and derive actionable design recommendations for practitioners.

</details>


### [244] [Chat2Scenic: An Iterative RAG-Based Framework for Scenario Generation in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14387)
*Yuan Gao,Wenting Miao,Mattia Piccinini,Haoyu Wang,Qunying Song,Johannes Betz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An iterative retrieval-augmented chatbot (Chat2Scenic) generates DSL-based autonomous driving scenario scripts from regulatory text, achieving higher compilation success and framework accuracy than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verifying autonomous driving requires regulation-compliant, executable test scenarios. Regulatory descriptions are diverse and difficult to translate into DSL scripts; existing methods trade off compilation rates and accuracy.

Method: Chat2Scenic uses an interactive chatbot interface with Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounded in regulatory knowledge and Scenic DSL syntax. It includes an open benchmark of 123 regulatory scenarios (NHTSA, UN Vehicle Regulations, etc.) and compares against state-of-the-art LLMs.

Result: CSR 76.42% and FA 58.17% for Chat2Scenic, outperforming Retrieval-assemble (CSR 30.08%, FA 11.03%) and Retrieval full-script generation (CSR 16.26%, FA 10.86%).

Conclusion: Demonstrates the viability of an iterative, grounded, retrieval-augmented approach to DSL script generation for scenario-based testing and provides an open benchmark and code release to spur future research.

Abstract: Validating autonomous driving systems requires diverse, regulation-compliant test scenarios. In simulation-based testing, scenarios are defined as executable scripts. Yet automatically generating such scripts from regulatory descriptions remains an open challenge, and existing approaches face fundamental trade-offs. Retrieval-assemble methods achieve reasonable compilation rates but lack scalability, whereas retrieval-based full-script generation suffers from low compilation success rates. We present Chat2Scenic, the first iterative retrieval-augmented framework to generate scenario scripts in Domain Specific Language (DSL). Specifically, Chat2Scenic provides a chatbot interface that supports interactive scenario refinement and integrates Retrieval-augmented Generation (RAG) to ground scenario generation in regulatory knowledge and DSL syntax. Furthermore, we propose an open benchmark for scenario generation comprising 123 scenarios from various regulations, including NHTSA and United Nations Vehicle Regulations, as well as other sources. Extensive evaluation with State-of-the-Art (SOTA) Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrates that Chat2Scenic achieves 76.42% Compilation Success Rate (CSR) and 58.17% Framework Accuracy (FA), outperforming existing methods (Retrieval Assemble with 30.08% CSR, 11.03% FA and Retrieval full script generation with 16.26% CSR, 10.86% FA). To facilitate future research, we release our code as open source at https://github.com/TUM-AVS/chat2scenic.

</details>


### [245] [A Comparative Analysis of Machine Learning Models for Long and Short-Term Forecasting of the Egyptian Stock Market: A Focus on EGX30](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14391)
*Muhammed Walid,Ahmed El-Naeimy,Hosam Moubarak,Walid Gomaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRU excels in medium-term EGX30 forecasting (1 week to 2 months), while XGBoost dominates daily (1 day). Ensembles boost long-horizon predictions; KNN shows unexpectedly strong long-horizon performance; overall, model choice should depend on horizon and market dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap in forecasting studies for developing markets (Egypt) and compare a broad set of ML models for EGX30 to guide investors.

Method: Utilize historical EGX30 data to forecast prices across short and long horizons. Evaluate multiple models—KNN, Random Forest, XGBoost, LSTM, GRU—and ensembles using metrics RMSE, MAPE, and coefficient of determination (R^2).

Result: GRU outperforms other models for 1-week, 1-month, and 2-month forecasts; XGBoost yields superior accuracy for 1-day forecasts. Ensemble approaches provide strong gains in long-horizon predictions (up to ~5x GRU). KNN also shows surprisingly strong performance on long horizons.

Conclusion: GRU and XGBoost are robust across horizons; ensembles offer substantial benefits for long-term forecasting; the unexpected strength of KNN warrants further investigation; findings are especially relevant for Egypt’s evolving financial market and similar developing economies.

Abstract: This study concentrates on predicting stock prices in the Egyptian market, focusing on the EGX30, an influential financial hub in the Middle East. While most research focuses on global stocks, there's a growing need to understand stock trends in developing countries like Egypt. The study compares different machine learning models for forecasting EGX30 trends, covering short and long-term predictions. Using historical EGX30 data, including metrics like root mean squared error, Mean Absolute Percentage Error, and coefficient of determination, models like K-Nearest Neighbours, random forest, extreme gradient boosting, long short-term memory networks, and gated recurrent unit networks were evaluated. The goal is to determine the most effective models for EGX30 prediction, considering Egypt's unique market dynamics. Insights from this study aid investors in making informed decisions. Results show that the Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) outperformed the other models in the one-week, one-month, and two-months while the eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) model outperformed others in the one-day predictions, highlighting their usefulness in predictive analysis for financial markets. The study also showed the importance of using the ensemble techniques, especially in the long-term predictions which proved better results reaching 5 times the GRU in the two-month predictions. Additionally, the study notes the surprisingly good performance of K-Nearest Neighbours (KNN) on long-term predictions, suggesting its enduring relevance and potential for future applications in the fintech domains.

</details>


### [246] [CatalogAgent: A Supervisor-mediated Self-Learning System Enabling Context Engineering for GenAI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14396)
*Zhu Cheng,Zhenming Wang,Yu,Tang,Dan Liu,Bryan Zhang,Athanasios N. Nikolakopoulos,Pranav Souri Itabada,Jing Zhang,Chih-Chi Chou,Peng Gao,Fatemeh Mansoori,Bharat Bojja,Sarath Chander,Sameer Thombare,Umit Batur,Tarik Arici*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CatalogAgent introduces a supervisor-mediated, self-improving agentic system for e-commerce catalog enrichment that mediates between a Generator and Evaluator, uses memory components to learn from cases, and achieves notable accuracy gains (∼15% in Generator, ∼14% in Evaluator).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address persistent conflicts and mistakes between LLM-based Generators and Evaluators in SA prediction, and leverage seller feedback to continuously improve performance without human intervention.

Method: A three-tier agentic architecture: a Generator (LLM), an Evaluator (LLM), and a Supervisor Agent that mediates disagreements (internal conflicts or external seller feedback). It also includes a Memory Base and a Memory Summarizer to store and summarize Supervisor activities, with learned insights fed back to worker LLMs via context engineering to enable self-improvement.

Result: Empirical results show improvements of 15.24% for Generator and 13.98% for Evaluator after context injections; demonstration of a new paradigm where Supervisor-mediated self-learning enhances generative AI accuracy in catalog enrichment.

Conclusion: Supervisor Agent-mediated self-learning is effective for improving SA prediction in e-commerce catalogs, offering a scalable paradigm to transfer supervisory capabilities to generation and evaluation models without human intervention.

Abstract: Product catalogs are the backbone of e-commerce sites, yet a large number of structured attributes (SAs) -- such as material, color, and shape -- often have missing values. Typically, SA values are extracted from product information, including titles and descriptions. While LLM-based generator-evaluator frameworks have demonstrated effectiveness for SA prediction -- where an LLM generates SA values and another evaluates them -- they face challenges when the Generator and Evaluator produce conflicting outputs, as either component can make mistakes. We introduce \texttt{CatalogAgent}, a novel agentic system that continuously improves Generator and Evaluator models for e-commerce catalog enrichment. When disagreements arise from (1) internal conflicts between the LLM-based Generator and Evaluator, or (2) external feedback from sellers on LLM outputs, a Supervisor Agent intervenes to mediate these conflicts and make final decisions. The system also incorporates a Memory Base and a Memory Summarizer that stores Supervisor Agent activities from individual cases and aggregates patterns into learnings. These learnings are fed back to the worker Generator and Evaluator LLMs, enabling self-improvement without human intervention. Through context engineering -- injecting learnings and insights into worker LLMs' contexts -- the system successfully transfers the Supervisor's capabilities to the Generator and Evaluator, improving their performance by 15.24\% and 13.98\%, respectively. Our experiments demonstrate a new paradigm of Supervisor Agent-mediated self-learning systems for improving generative AI model accuracy.

</details>


### [247] [Instrument Effects in Language-Model Honesty Evaluation: An Auditable Single-System Demonstration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14399)
*Justin Bronder*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Honesty evaluations for language models appear instrument-dependent: changing the evaluation grammar, budget, and preregistered procedures radically alters verdicts, revealing instability and potential biases in current methods; a four-check integrity protocol is proposed to improve evals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how evaluation design choices and preregistration affect conclusions about model honesty, and to improve the reliability and replicability of such evaluations by separating model behavior from instrumental artifacts.

Method: Create a controlled text-adventure environment where the engine knows whether a quest is solvable; have a language model operate under a budget and declare complete, incomplete, or not yet decidable; vary instrument choices (grammar and verdict sets) and preregistered rules; record results across multiple series and anchors; assess stability, preregistration fidelity, and post-hoc patterns; compare outcomes under different configurations.

Result: Changing evaluation instruments substantially shifts measured behavior: expanding from two to three verdicts reduces strong claims (from 38/40 to 7/40) and increases incomplete outcomes (28/40); across four anchors, 93 of 158 valid games ended incomplete; even sentences disclosing success criteria reduced false verdicts; repeated runs yielded non-stable verdict distributions in most instances; a preregistered narrative gradient was falsified, yet some post-hoc patterns remained (e.g., register presence boosts strong claims; budget effects exceed register content); a mediation test often returned null; proposes a four-check integrity protocol for eval instruments.

Conclusion: Model-honesty evaluations are fragile to instrument design and preregistration; robust conclusions require explicit integrity checks and preregistration across multiple instruments; the authors propose a four-check protocol to guard against artifact-driven conclusions.

Abstract: Evaluations of language-model honesty read the model's verdicts as evidence about the model. We test the instrument instead.
