We built a text-adventure world where the game engine, not any model, knows whether the quest can be completed. A language model plays under a budget and must eventually declare its quest complete, unreachable, or not yet decidable; the engine scores every verdict. Decision rules were recorded before results were read, and run artifacts bind the revisions they executed; the strength of preregistration varies by series and is disclosed. With the player held fixed, instrument choices substantially changed measured behavior. On four byte-identical anchors, expanding a two-verdict grammar to three verdicts moved strong claims from 38/40 to 7/40, while the new incomplete verdict took 28/40 outcomes; across series 2, 93/158 valid games ended incomplete. One sentence disclosing the success criterion took matched-instance false verdicts from 18/59 to 0/58, through fewer decision points and cleaner decisions. Repeated runs of one fixed configuration produced non-stable verdict distributions on 3 of 4 instances: single runs report samples as dispositions. A formally preregistered narrative-register gradient was falsified; two post-hoc, hypothesis-generating patterns remain: register presence roughly doubled strong claims, and budget rendering moved verdicts more than register content (.383 meter vs .150 lantern). The narrator compressed abundant budgets toward scarcity landmarks, yet the registered mediation test returned a null. We propose a four-check integrity protocol for eval instruments.

</details>


### [248] [Reward-Free Evolving Agents via Pairwise Validator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14408)
*Minghao Liu,Yu Wang,Jiayun Wang,Wei Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-evolving agentic loop replaces scalar rewards with a pairwise validator (frozen LLM) that compares parent vs child, improving reliability and reducing labeling cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward design for self-improving agents is costly and requires labeled data; a stable, low-cost alternative would simplify and accelerate autonomous improvement.

Method: Implement a frozen LLM as a binary pairwise validator comparing parent and child candidates, integrated into three existing engines (GEPA, ADRS, ShinkaEvolve) with two modes: Adaptive Focus and Soft Elo. Evaluate on prompts and code substrates.

Result: The pairwise gate matches or exceeds full-reward baselines across multiple settings, maintains performance under cross-family validator swaps, and reduces or eliminates the need for per-step scalar rewards.

Conclusion: A pairwise, contrastive validator is an effective drop-in replacement for per-step reward design in self-evolving agents, offering competitive task accuracy with reduced labeling cost.

Abstract: A self-evolving agentic loop repeatedly proposes a tweaked version of an agent (its prompt template or program) and accepts or rejects the change based on a per-iteration quality signal. Designing that signal is often the costly part of the project: a reliable scalar reward requires domain expertise and labeled examples that are themselves as expensive to assemble as the agent's underlying task. We propose replacing the scalar at the accept/reject gate with a pairwise validator: a frozen LLM that, given the parent and child candidate, returns a binary verdict on which is better. Pairwise judgment is generally easier and more stable than absolute scoring, due to its contrastive nature, which mitigates the need for strict scale calibration. The validator also requires no training of its own. We integrate the validator into three published self-evolving engines (GEPA, ADRS, ShinkaEvolve) and report two flavors: Adaptive Focus, which retains the engine's existing val-set parent selection, and Soft Elo, which lets the validator's verdicts drive parent selection so that val-set rewards drop as well. Across multiple agents and two artifact substrates (prompt and code), our method matches or exceeds the full-reward baseline on the majority of settings we evaluate, and the pattern survives a cross-family validator swap. The pairwise gate is thus a drop-in replacement for per-step reward design at competitive task accuracy without the labeling cost.

</details>


### [249] [CausalGraphX: A Counterfactual Graph Neural Network Framework for Explainable Systemic Risk Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14416)
*Rabimba Karanjai,Hemanth Madhavarao,Lei Xu,Weidong Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CausalGraphX fuses Graph Attention networks with adversarial regularization and optimization-based counterfactuals to deliver explainable systemic-risk assessments and minimal capital-change interventions in synthetic financial networks, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regulators need transparent, causal explanations of shock propagation in financial networks to perform effective stress tests and design interventions, beyond opaque correlations learned by standard GNNs.

Method: Use Graph Attention to learn institutional vulnerability representations; apply adversarial regularization to bias the representations toward causal drivers rather than spurious correlations; generate counterfactual explanations via optimization (e.g., minimal capital injections) for specific stress scenarios; validate on large-scale synthetic financial networks.

Result: CausalGraphX achieves superior predictive accuracy for cascading defaults compared with traditional and deep-learning baselines and provides sparse, plausible, and actionable counterfactual explanations.

Conclusion: Integrating GNNs with counterfactual reasoning yields explainable, effective risk assessments and intervention strategies for systemic risk, with potential regulatory utility; demonstrated on synthetic networks.

Abstract: The interconnected nature of global financial systems makes them vulnerable to systemic risks, where the failure of a few institutions can trigger catastrophic cascading defaults. Traditional risk models often fail to capture the complex, non-linear dynamics of these networks. While Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have shown promise in modeling relational data, they primarily learn correlative patterns and function as black boxes, offering little insight into the causal mechanisms of shock propagation. This limitation is critical for regulators who require explainable models to perform stress tests and devise effective interventions. We introduce CausalGraphX, a novel framework that integrates GNNs with counterfactual reasoning to provide explainable assessments of systemic risk. CausalGraphX employs a Graph Attention mechanism to learn representations of institutional vulnerability and uses an adversarial regularization technique to ensure these representations capture causal drivers rather than spurious correlations. Furthermore, we propose an optimization-based approach to generate counterfactual explanations, answering questions such as, "What minimum capital injection would have prevented Bank A's default under a specific stress scenario?" We validate CausalGraphX on large-scale synthetic financial networks. Our results demonstrate that CausalGraphX significantly outperforms traditional and deep learning baselines in predicting cascading defaults while providing sparse, plausible, and actionable counterfactual explanations.

</details>


### [250] [Per-Token Fixed-Point Convergence in Depth-Recurrent Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14427)
*Joe Logan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Per-token convergence in depth-recurrent transformer enables a training-free halting rule to achieve uniform depth with substantial depth reduction; token-type affects convergence; a learned router offers no advantage here; results based on a 135M-parameter model trained on FineWeb-Edu; FLOP proxy used; single-scale, single-seed study; about 100 GPU-hours on RTX 4090.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what a weight-tied, depth-recurrent transformer computes per token, quantify per-token convergence dynamics, and assess whether read-out of convergence or a simple halting rule can reduce computation while preserving accuracy.

Method: Train a 135M parameter depth-recurrent transformer on FineWeb-Edu with randomized recursion depth; measure per-token successive-output KL divergence and state changes across loops to find per-token fixed points; compare a training-free halting rule vs a linear router; report average depth as a FLOP proxy; conduct experiments on a single RTX 4090 (~100 GPU-hours) at a single scale/seed.

Result: Per-token states converge to a fixed point with KL divergence dropping from 0.39 at loop 2 to 8.5e-6 by loop 16; token-type determines convergence depth; median token reaches convergence by loop 6, about 10% require loop 8; reading convergence is directly readable and outperforms learning to predict it; halting each token when stabilized achieves uniform depth-8 quality at 4.94 average loops (38% fewer loops); a learned linear router requires nearly full depth and yields no reduction; validation loss decreases monotonically from 3.80 at one loop to 3.20 at eight and remains stable to 32 loops; results are presented as FLOP proxy with a 3-point wall-clock bracket; study uses single scale/seed; roughly 100 GPU-hours.

Conclusion: Per-token convergence is a central property of depth-recurrent models; simple, training-free halting based on stability can substantially reduce average depth without sacrificing quality; token-type heterogeneity in convergence is intrinsic; while not comparing FLOPs precisely, the results illustrate a viable path to elastic computation in recurrent transformers; findings are preliminary (single scale/seed).

Abstract: A depth-recurrent transformer applies a weight-tied core a variable number of times, and prior work has shown that training with a randomized recursion count yields one checkpoint usable across a range of inference depths. We ask what such a model actually computes per token, and measure it directly. On a 135M-class model trained on FineWeb-Edu, the recurrent state converges to a per-token fixed point: mean successive-output KL divergence falls from 3.9e-1 at the second loop to 8.5e-6 by the sixteenth, and per-token state change decays in step. Crucially, this convergence is not uniform across tokens. The median token converges by loop six, while approximately 10 percent of tokens continue to update at the training-mean depth of eight, and mean convergence depth is ordered by token type (whitespace shallowest, content words deepest). This per-token variation is the central object of the paper. We show it is directly readable and that reading it outperforms learning to predict it: a training-free rule that halts each token once its output stabilizes attains uniform depth-8 quality at 4.94 average loops (a 38 percent reduction in average depth) and matches uniform depth across the average-depth range, whereas a linear router trained on convergence labels harvested from the same model requires nearly full depth and yields no reduction. The elasticity that makes this possible reproduces here as background (validation loss decreases monotonically from 3.80 at one loop to 3.20 at eight and remains stable to 32 loops). We report average depth as a FLOP proxy with a three-point wall-clock bracket rather than a realized speedup, make no FLOP-matched parity claim, and note that the allocation results are established at a single scale and seed. The complete study runs on a single RTX 4090 in approximately 100 GPU-hours.

</details>


### [251] [Tactile: Giving Computer-Using Agents Hands and Feet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14443)
*Yong Liu,Zhenyi Zhong,Zhanpeng Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Tactile provides an open-source execution substrate for desktop automation that grounds actions in semantic UI evidence, improving agent success rates across models/tasks by using an observe-ground-act-verify loop with native actions, OCR, and provenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to address brittleness of screenshot-coordinates based automation by grounding actions in semantic, verifiable interface states and enabling auditable failure attribution.

Method: introduces Tactile layer integrating OS accessibility semantics, OCR-text, and visual regions into action-grounded states; agents use observe-ground-act-verify with preference for native semantic actions; provenance is retained for replay.

Result: Codex Success@100 on macOSWorld tasks increases from 41.1% to 50.0%; accessibility-adapted tasks rise from 45.2% to 55.3%; cross-agent 96-task subset shows gains across Codex, Claude Code, OpenCode, Goose.

Conclusion: Reliable computer use requires an execution substrate that exposes software actions as semantic, verifiable, auditable objects rather than coordinates.

Abstract: Computer-use agents are becoming capable software operators, but their interface to desktop applications is still often a brittle motor layer: they look at screenshots, predict coordinates, click, and hope that the visible state changed as intended. This collapses target grounding, action execution, and outcome verification into a single ambiguous operation. We present Tactile, an open-source tool layer that gives agents a more reliable "hands and feet" for desktop use. Tactile converts heterogeneous UI evidence--operating-system accessibility semantics, OCR-grounded text, and visual fallback regions--into action-grounded interface states: compact target candidates with source labels, roles or text, state, geometry, executable affordances, and verification cues. Agents operate through an observe-ground-act-verify loop that prefers native semantic actions when available, falls back to OCR-grounded coordinates when visible text is the best evidence, and keeps full provenance for replay and failure attribution. On macOSWorld-style tasks, adding Tactile improves Codex Success@100 from 41.1% to 50.0% overall and from 45.2% to 55.3% on accessibility-adapted tasks; a 96-task cross-agent subset shows consistent gains across Codex, Claude Code, OpenCode, and Goose. These results suggest that reliable computer use requires not only stronger models, but also a reusable execution substrate that exposes software actions as semantic, verifiable, and auditable objects rather than anonymous screen coordinates.

</details>


### [252] [Step-Level Preference Learning for Generative Agents in Social Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14485)
*Wenchang Gao,Pingyue Sheng,Lanlan Qiu,Yunfei Ma,Jian Zhao,Baicheng Chen,Kangda Wang,Yuyang Tian,Shunqiang Mao,Tianxing He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Step-level human preferences collected via an interactive interface improve LLM-based generative agents' micro-decisions and long-horizon behavior.
Dataset: 57K fine-grained annotations.
Approach: supervised fine-tuning and direct preference optimization on open-weight LMs.
Outcomes: higher fidelity, better coordination, improved interaction quality, and more socially effective behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-grained annotations of intermediate decision steps (planning, memory retrieval, reflection, action) are scarce, and existing agents lack grounding in human preferences over these steps.

Method: Introduce an interactive simulation interface (the older? placeholder) to collect step-level human preference supervision; assemble a dataset of 57K annotations; train open-weight LMs with supervised fine-tuning and direct preference optimization on this data.

Result: Step-level supervision consistently improves simulation fidelity, coordination, interaction quality, and socially effective agent behavior; benefits observed at both local decision quality and long-horizon agent behavior.

Conclusion: Step-level human supervision is an effective training signal for improving both local decision quality and long-horizon agent behavior.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based generative agents simulate human behavior through long-horizon decision-making processes that comprise intermediate steps such as planning, memory retrieval, reflection, and action selection. However, fine-grained human annotations of these intermediate steps remain scarce, and existing agents are not grounded in human preferences over such intermediate decisions. To address this gap, we introduce \method, an interactive simulation interface that enables us to collect step-level human preference supervision over agent decision trajectories, leading to a dataset of 57K fine-grained annotations. We conduct step-level preference learning on open-weight language models using supervised finetuning and direct preference optimization on this data, consistently improving simulation fidelity, coordination, and interaction quality, and inducing more socially effective agent behavior. Our results show that step-level human supervision is an effective training signal for improving both local decision quality and long-horizon agent behavior.

</details>


### [253] [SAGA: Schema-Aware Grounding for Agentic Text-to-SPARQL Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14494)
*Yiming Zhang,Koji Tsuda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGA is a training-free, schema-aware grounding framework for agentic text-to-SPARQL generation in complex KBQA. It addresses type-blind grounding by enforcing schema constraints during property exploration and query construction, improving recall/precision (F1) and exact-match accuracy across nine Wikidata/Freebase benchmarks, and reducing empty results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interactive semantic parsing for KBQA can ground queries inaccurately when agents rely on lexical cues or instance observations alone. Without explicit type/ schema conditioning, generated SPARQL patterns may be semantically incompatible, leading to empty results.

Method: SAGA maintains a persistent bidirectional type state, filters incompatible property candidates at construction, presents a compact schema-annotated graph pattern format, and handles missing schema via empirical and trace-local evidence. It operates in a training-free manner during the agent’s exploration of properties and patterns.

Result: Across nine benchmark settings on Wikidata and Freebase, SAGA achieves the highest F1 on all settings and the highest exact-match accuracy on eight, while reducing empty-result queries in Wikidata settings.

Conclusion: Schema-aware grounding substantially improves the effectiveness of interactive, agentic text-to-SPARQL generation for KBQA by reducing semantically invalid patterns and focusing search within type-consistent graph patterns.

Abstract: Complex knowledge base question answering (KBQA) is commonly approached through either information retrieval over a question-specific subgraph or semantic parsing into an executable logical form. We study the latter paradigm. Recent large language model agents make semantic parsing interactive: they alternate between reasoning, querying the knowledge base, and extending a partial SPARQL query. This interleaving reduces reliance on one-shot generation, but makes the quality of \emph{KB grounding} depend on what the interaction tools expose. Existing agents retrieve or prune candidate properties mainly through lexical relevance and instance-level observations, without systematically conditioning on entity types, property domains and ranges, or the expected answer type. We call this failure mode \emph{type-blind grounding}. It enlarges the grounding search space and often produces plausible-looking but semantically incompatible triple patterns that execute to empty results. We propose SAGA (\underline{S}chema-\underline{A}ware \underline{G}rounding for \underline{A}gentic Text-to-SPARQL Generation), a training-free framework that turns property exploration into a schema-constrained grounding operation. SAGA maintains a persistent bidirectional type state, filters known-incompatible property candidates at construction time, presents the remaining graph patterns in a compact schema-annotated format, and handles missing schema information permissively through empirical and trace-local evidence. Across nine benchmark settings over Wikidata and Freebase, SAGA achieves the highest F1 on all nine settings and the highest exact-match accuracy on eight, while reducing empty-result queries across all reported Wikidata settings.

</details>


### [254] [Contextualized Evaluation of Vision Language Models through Dynamic, Multi-turn Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14499)
*Yijiang Li,Huiqi Zou,Bingyang Wang,Ziang Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CEDI reframes MLLM evaluation as contextualized, dynamic multi-turn interactions among an evaluatee model, an automated examiner, and a grader; uses a graph-guided, semi-structured dialogue to elicit evidence and surface hallucinations, revealing more and more practically relevant failures than static benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmarks in static, controlled settings fail to capture the dynamic, interactive, and context-dependent nature of real-world MLLM use, leading to an ecologically invalid assessment of capabilities.

Method: Three-party setup: evaluatee model, automated examiner, and a grader. The examiner conducts multi-turn, semi-structured conversations guided by a graph-based task representation; navigates state-space transitions and applies strategies (clarification requests, adversarial probes) to elicit evidence. Applied to visual hallucinations; evaluated across models, settings, datasets, and domains.

Result: Contextualized, interactive evaluation reveals significantly more hallucinations than conventional static evaluation, and those hallucinations resemble real-use failures. Hallucinations accumulate over long contexts via self-reinforcing dialogue history; models are especially vulnerable to questions requiring premise rejection or refusal.

Conclusion: CEDI provides a realistic, systematic, and ecologically valid framework for evaluating MLLMs; code is available at GitHub (github.com/williamium3000/cedi).

Abstract: Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial advances on benchmarks, yet their real-world effectiveness remains uncertain. This gap stems from the fundamental misalignment between benchmarks in controlled, static settings and the dynamic, interactive, and contextualized nature of real-world applications. To bridge this gap, we propose CEDI (Contextualized Evaluations of MLLMs through Dynamic, multi-round Interactions), a framework that recasts evaluation as a three-party interaction between an evaluatee model, an automated examiner, and a grader. The examiner conducts multi-turn, semi-structured conversation guided by a graph-based representation of the task. By navigating state-space transitions, CEDI deploys diverse strategies, from clarification requests to adversarial probes, to elicit performance evidence. We apply CEDI to visual hallucinations. Empirical results across multiple models, diverse settings, datasets, and domains show that contextualized, interactive evaluations reveal not only significantly more hallucinations than conventional static evaluation but also ones that more closely resemble those arising in practical use cases. We further show that hallucinations often accumulate over long contexts, through self-reinforcing dialogue history, and models are particularly vulnerable to questions requiring premise rejection or refusal. Together, these findings highlight CEDI as a step toward realistic, systematic, and ecologically valid assessments of MLLMs' capabilities. Code is available at github.com/williamium3000/cedi.

</details>


### [255] [VLT: A Vision-Language-Time Series Multimodal Foundation Model for Industrial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14510)
*Haiteng Wang,Jingheng Yan,Xiaokang Wang,Lei Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal foundation model (VLT) jointly models time-series, frequency-spectrum visuals, and text to improve prognostics and health management in industrial settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-modality time-series models limit generalization in PHM; bridging continuous signals with discrete textual semantics via a frequency-spectrum visual bridge and leveraging multimodal learning from LLMs addresses robustness and generalization, especially under few-shot/noisy/incomplete-modality conditions.

Method: Introduce VLT: Time-aware Mixture-of-Experts (Time-MoE) to capture heterogeneous temporal dynamics; Frequency-Text Augmented Learner to align spectral features with textual semantics in a shared representation space; time-centric gradient alignment with gradient normalization and reliability-aware dynamic reweighting to mitigate cross-modal optimization conflicts.

Result: Empirical evaluations on multiple industrial datasets show VLT surpasses state-of-the-art methods, exhibiting enhanced robustness and generalization in few-shot, noisy, and incomplete-modality scenarios.

Conclusion: The VLT framework successfully integrates time-series, spectral visuals, and textual knowledge, with the frequency spectrum acting as an effective bridge; the approach yields strong generalization and training stability through time-centric gradient alignment.

Abstract: Industrial time series serve as the foundation for Prognostics and Health Management (PHM) to ensure the reliability and safety of industrial equipment such as aero-engines. However, existing approaches are typically limited to single-modality modeling, which restricts their generalization in complex scenarios. Although recent advances in large language models (LLMs) provide new opportunities for multimodal learning, bridging continuous time-series signals and discrete textual semantics remains an open challenge. To this end, we propose VLT, a multimodal foundation model that jointly models time-series, frequency-spectrum visual representations, and textual knowledge. A key insight is to utilize the frequency spectrum as a visual bridge to connect continuous temporal signals with discrete semantics. Specifically, a Time-aware Mixture-of-Experts (Time-MoE) is designed to capture heterogeneous temporal dynamics, while a Frequency-Text Augmented Learner enables joint modeling of spectral and semantic features within a shared representation space. Furthermore, a time-centric gradient alignment mechanism is introduced to mitigate cross-modal optimization conflicts via gradient normalization and reliability-aware dynamic reweighting. Extensive experiments on multiple industrial datasets demonstrate that VLT outperforms state-of-the-art methods, achieving superior robustness and generalization under few-shot, noisy, and incomplete-modality settings.

</details>


### [256] [RetroAgent: Harnessing LLMs to Search Over Structured Memory for Agentic Retrosynthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14512)
*Yanqiao Zhu,Jingru Gan,Xiaoqi Sun,Fang Sun,Yidan Shi,Md Mofijul Islam,Chao Shang,Wenhao Gao,Connor W. Coley,Yizhou Sun,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RetroAgent is an LLM-based agent that unifies symbolic search with structured memory and chemistry tools to perform multi-step retrosynthesis planning, enabling full-state awareness and improved generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional retrosynthesis methods rely on tree search with offline-trained value heuristics or LLM interfaces that consider candidates in isolation, limiting exploration of the full search space. There is a need for global search-state awareness and memory-driven reasoning.

Method: RetroAgent introduces a memory-enabled harness that interfaces with chemistry tools, allowing the agent to observe the complete search state (explored routes, alternatives, and intermediates) and make informed decisions by grounding neural reasoning in global progress and domain knowledge.

Result: Experiments on both in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks show RetroAgent achieves strong performance and generalization, outperforming baselines.

Conclusion: Memory-augmented LLM agents that couple symbolic search with neural reasoning can effectively navigate complex multi-step retrosynthesis spaces by maintaining structured memory and leveraging domain tools.

Abstract: Multi-step retrosynthesis planning seeks to decompose a target molecule into commercially available building blocks through a sequence of feasible reactions. The vast combinatorial search space makes this task challenging even for expert chemists. Traditional methods combine tree search with offline-trained value networks that score candidates in isolation, without reasoning about complete multi-step routes. Recent work leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) for this task, but relies on simple interfaces that limit exploration of the full search space. We introduce RetroAgent, an LLM agent that bridges symbolic search and neural reasoning through a harness with structured memory. Through memory and chemistry tools, the agent observes the full search state, including explored routes, available alternatives, and properties of intermediates, enabling informed decisions grounded in both global progress and domain knowledge. Experiments on in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks demonstrate that RetroAgent delivers strong performance and generalization.

</details>


### [257] [WrAFT: a Modularized Automated Writing Evaluation System for Argumentative Essays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14524)
*Adnan Labib,Yixuan Huang,Jiahui Wu,John Maurice Gayed,Zheng Yuan,Qiao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WrAFT is a modular automated writing evaluation system for argumentative essays that achieves state-of-the-art scoring and high-quality feedback, validated on TOEFL-writing data with a public UI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for reliable, interpretable, and actionable automated scoring and feedback in argumentative writing, leveraging multiple LLMs and benchmark data to demonstrate performance and generalizability.

Method: A modular AWE design separating scoring, surface-level feedback, and deep-level feedback; evaluation of multiple LLMs (LLaMA-3.3-70B-Instruct, GPT-4o, Claude 3.7) via prompting and supervised fine-tuning; training/testing on a proprietary dataset of 480 TOEFL Independent Writing essays with official scores; benchmark metrics (QWK, RMSE) and human evaluation of feedback; development of a public interactive UI.

Result: Scoring achieves QWK 0.84 and RMSE 0.44 (0-5 scale); surface-level feedback approval 96.14%, deep-level macro 93.03%, deep-level micro 94.69%; UI is publicly available and free to use.

Conclusion: WrAFT demonstrates state-of-the-art scoring performance and high-quality, modular feedback for argumentative essays, with a publicly accessible interface that facilitates adoption.

Abstract: This study presents WrAFT, a Writing Assessment and Feedback Tool, that delivers both accurate and reliable scores and effective comprehensive feedback to argumentative essays. WrAFT adopts a modular design by dividing automated writing evaluation (AWE) tasks into scoring, surface-level feedback, and deep-level feedback. In building the system, various Large Language Models (LLMs) have been evaluated, including LLaMA-3.3-70B-Instruct, GPT-4o, and Claude 3.7, through both direct prompting and supervised fine-tuning approaches. A proprietary dataset of 480 TOEFL Independent Writing essays with official benchmark scores was utilized. Benchmark-based evaluation shows that WrAFT achieves state-of-the-art performance in scoring, with a quadratic weighted kappa (QWK) of 0.84 and a root mean square error (RMSE) of 0.44 against official scores on a scale of 0-5. Human evaluation of system-generated feedback also reveals high approval ratings: 96.14 percent for surface-level feedback, 93.03 percent for deep-level macro feedback, and 94.69 percent for deep-level micro feedback. An interactive user interface has been developed for the system and is publicly available and free to use.

</details>


### [258] [Are LLM-Generated GPU Kernels Production-Ready? A Trace-Driven Benchmark and Optimization Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14541)
*Lingyun Yang,Yuxiao Wang,Shenghao Liang,Linfeng Yang,Daocheng Ying,Chunbo You,Rui Zhang,Luping Wang,Yinghao Yu,Guodong Yang,Liping Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Atrex-Bench provides a production-driven GPU kernel benchmark derived from full-cluster inference traces (30 operators, 440 shapes) with importance-weighted serving-time scoring, and co-releases Atrex-Kernel-Agent (AKA), a profile-driven kernel-optimization agent that uses iterative search, optimization dropout, and a large knowledge base to turn fallbacks into real kernels that match or exceed hand-tuned baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GPU kernel benchmarks rely on synthetic or curated workloads and fail to reflect real deployed workloads; a production-aligned benchmark with time-weighted importance helps guide kernel optimization.

Method: Extract production traces to define 30 operators and 440 shapes; assign per-problem weights based on observed GPU time and serving phases; compute per-problem roofline ceilings; evaluate six frontier coding agents on Atrex-Bench; co-release AKA with a large in-house knowledge base (298 reference kernels, 244 optimization documents) and external API/ISA references; use iterative measure-revise search and optimization dropout for policy-guided kernel optimization.

Result: Best vanilla model achieves only about 10% of the hardware roofline on production operators; correctness metrics overstate capability due to PyTorch fallbacks; AKA converts zero-FlyDSL fallbacks into real kernels that match or exceed hand-tuned production baselines.

Conclusion: Production-driven benchmarks with a profile-driven kernel-optimization agent can more effectively realize production performance, with a substantial gap remaining that AKA demonstrates how to close using a large, structured knowledge base and iterative search.

Abstract: Existing GPU kernel generation benchmarks draw problems from synthetic or curated sources that diverge from deployed workloads. We present Atrex-Bench, a benchmark whose 30 operators and 440 shapes are sampled directly from full-cluster production inference traces of compute-limited, memory-rich GPUs. Each problem carries an importance weight derived from its share of observed GPU time, weighted by application card-hours and computed separately for the serving phases in which it runs, together with a per-problem roofline ceiling, so the aggregate score emphasizes the kernels that consume the most serving time. Evaluating six frontier coding agents on Atrex-Bench shows that even the best vanilla model reaches only ${\sim}10\%$ of the hardware roofline on production operators; and correctness alone overstates capability, since much of the apparent pass rate comes from PyTorch fallbacks rather than kernels the model wrote. To close this gap, we co-release Atrex-Kernel-Agent (AKA), a profile-driven kernel-optimization agent that combines iterative measure-revise search, optimization dropout for escaping stalled search contexts, and a layered GPU-optimization knowledge base (298 reference-kernel files and 244 optimization-knowledge documents, plus external upstream reference projects for API/ISA lookup). In a controlled case study, the agent converts zero-FlyDSL fallbacks into real kernels that match or exceed hand-tuned production baselines.

</details>


### [259] [Towards an Intention Abstraction Layer for Autonomous Industrial Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14553)
*Artan Markaj,Raphael Höfer,Felix Gehlhoff*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Intention Abstraction Layer (IAL) is a domain-agnostic middleware that treats high-level goals as persistent, explainable intentions. It uses an LLM anchored to an OWL ontology to translate goals, a consistency monitor to detect conflicts at registration, and a transparency module to explain issues. A proof-of-concept with two agents demonstrates pre-execution conflict detection, shifting assurance from post-hoc fault analysis to pre-execution, intention-level checking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In complex, shared-resource industrial environments, autonomous subsystems pursue competing goals. Without pre-execution checks, conflicts surface only after missed targets or shutdowns. There is a need for proactive, intention-level verification to improve reliability and safety.

Method: Architectural components include: (1) an LLM anchored in a formal OWL ontology to parse natural-language goals into structured intentions; (2) a consistency monitor that detects conflicts at registration time; (3) a transparency module that explains conflicts in natural language; (4) a proof-of-concept with two autonomous agents registering conflicting production and energy intentions to illustrate pre-execution conflict detection.

Result: The IAL flags and explains conflicts before execution in the proof-of-concept, demonstrating the feasibility of pre-execution intention-level checking and shifting behavioral assurance from post-hoc analysis to pre-execution verification.

Conclusion: Intention-level checking provides stronger behavioral assurance for cooperating autonomous systems. A domain-agnostic middleware that represents and reasons about intentions can proactively detect and explain conflicts, reducing runtime failures and unnecessary shutdowns.

Abstract: Modern industrial environments increasingly run many autonomous subsystems at once - schedulers, energy managers, vehicle fleets - each pursuing its own goals while sharing the same physical resources. Because high-level human intentions are translated into low-level control logic and then discarded, no running component can tell whether it is still doing what was actually intended, and goal conflicts surface only after they have caused a missed target or a shutdown. We propose the Intention Abstraction Layer (IAL), a domainagnostic middleware that represents intentions as first-class, persistent, and explainable runtime objects: a large language model grounded in a formal OWL ontology parses naturallanguage goals into structured intentions, a consistency monitor detects conflicts at registration time, before execution, and a transparency module explains them in natural language. We report a first proof of concept in which two autonomous agents register conflicting production and energy intentions, and the IAL flags and explains the conflict before it reaches the execution layer. The result is a mechanism that shifts behavioral assurance for cooperating autonomous systems from post-hoc failure analysis to pre-execution, intention-level checking.

</details>


### [260] [Seeing the End at Step Zero: Accelerating Diffusion MLLMs via MLP Sparsity-Aware Truncation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14557)
*Qicheng Zhao,Qi Sun,Zheyu Yan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Seer is a training-free framework for Diffusion Multimodal LLMs that detects the semantic boundary at the first denoising step via an MLP activation sparsity shift, enabling one-shot truncation of redundant output suffix and a hybrid execution strategy; it delivers up to ~31x throughput gains across 9 benchmarks while preserving, and sometimes improving, task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DMLLMs pad outputs to a fixed maximum length, causing substantial wasted computation on [EOS] tokens due to unknown actual output length; reducing this padding overhead is essential for efficient inference.

Method: Identify the semantic boundary by observing a distinct shift in MLP activation sparsity at the first denoising step, use an SNR-based criterion to trigger a one-shot truncation of the suffix for all later steps, and apply a hybrid execution strategy to support dynamic sequence lengths in batched serving without retraining.

Result: Padding waste is eliminated, delivering up to ~31x throughput improvements; robust performance across 9 benchmarks; DocVQA score improves from 63.52 to 63.66, indicating reduced noise leakage and potential accuracy gains on complex visual tasks.

Conclusion: Seer provides a practical, training-free acceleration solution for DMLLMs that preserves performance and can be deployed in batched serving with dynamic sequence lengths, addressing a key bottleneck in real-world multimodal generation tasks.

Abstract: Diffusion Multimodal Large Language Models (DMLLMs) are highly effective for multimodal reasoning, yet their inference efficiency is significantly hindered by fixed-length generation constraints. Since the actual output length is unknown, output sequences are padded to a predefined maximum length, resulting in substantial redundant computation over unnecessary [EOS] tokens. In this work, we discover that DMLLMs implicitly reveal their valid semantic boundary at the very first denoising step through a distinct shift in MLP activation sparsity. Leveraging this observation, we propose Seer, a training-free framework that detects this boundary using a Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR)-based criterion and performs one-shot truncation of the redundant suffix for all subsequent computations. To preserve these theoretical gains during batched serving, Seer incorporates a hybrid execution strategy that maximizes throughput while seamlessly accommodating dynamic sequence lengths. Experimental results demonstrate that Seer effectively eliminates padding waste, accelerating throughput by up to $\sim$31$\times$. Across 9 benchmarks, Seer robustly maintains overall performance and even improves accuracy on complex visual tasks by mitigating noise leakage (e.g., DocVQA score increases from 63.52 to 63.66), offering a highly efficient, plug-and-play solution for DMLLM acceleration.

</details>


### [261] [Democratizing Agent Deployment Safety: A Structural Monitoring Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14570)
*Preeti Ravindra,Rahul Tiwari,Vincent Wolowski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes an Information Flow Graph (IFG) monitor for infrastructure-as-code safety that does not require training to improve detection of sabotage by AI agents; demonstrates significant reductions in attack success in synchronous and asynchronous modes, enabling pre-deployment safeguards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the risk that AI software development agents weaken safeguards or gain persistence in infrastructure, especially for organizations lacking advanced monitoring; enable auditable, accessible safety for infrastructure as code.

Method: Introduce IFG monitor analyzing structural security regressions via diffs in control-flow/data-flow graphs and raw code diffs; compare untrained vs trained monitors and synchronous vs asynchronous deployment; evaluate on infrastructure-sabotage task from ControlArena; measure attack detection at 1% FPR, rollback capability.

Result: Untrained git-diff monitor misses 11.6% attacks at 1% FPR; untrained IFG monitor reduces misses to 3.5%; trained Async RF monitoring achieves <1% misses. IFG can operate synchronously as pre-deployment safeguard. Synchronous IFG rollback reduces joint task success from 74.4% to 0% without harming legitimate task completion.

Conclusion: Untrained structural monitors offer a practical, auditable path to democratize deployment safety for AI agents in infrastructure as code, balancing safety with usability.

Abstract: AI software development agents are increasingly capable of modifying infrastructure and security critical systems, creating risks where an agent completes its assigned task while covertly weakening safeguards through actions such as broadening permissions, degrading logging, or introducing persistence mechanisms. While frontier laboratories may deploy sophisticated monitoring pipelines, many organizations and individual users adopting coding agents lack the resources and governance maturity required to maintain complex learned monitor ensembles. We study this problem as infrastructure level sabotage under task success in the infrastructure-as-code setting from ControlArena and compare trained and untrained monitors across synchronous and asynchronous deployment modes. We introduce an Information Flow Graph (IFG) monitor that analyzes structural security regressions using control-flow and data-flow graph diffs alongside raw code diffs. In asynchronous evaluation, an untrained git diff monitor misses 11.6% attacks at 1% false positive rate auditing budget, our untrained IFG monitor reduces this to 3.5%, and the trained Async RF monitor achieves below 1%. Unlike the trained Async RF monitor, IFG can also operate synchronously as a pre-deployment safeguard that blocks suspicious infrastructure changes before execution or merge. In synchronous mode, IFG realtime rollback reduces joint main task and covert task success from 74.4% to 0.0% with no measurable reduction in legitimate task completion. These results suggest that untrained structural monitors provide a practical and auditable path toward democratizing deployment safety for organizations seeking trustworthy adoption of advanced AI agents.

</details>


### [262] [Alipay-PIBench: A Realistic Payment Integration Benchmark for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14573)
*Shiyu Ying,Xuejie Cao,Yingfan Ma,Yuanhao Dong,Wenyu Chen,Bowen Song,Lin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Alipay-PIBench is a benchmark to evaluate coding agents on realistic Alipay payment integration, with 9 product-specific projects and 18 task instances featuring basic and advanced scenarios; six models are evaluated, showing notable gains from a dedicated alipay-payment-integration skill and providing deterministic, rubric-based and LLM-assisted assessments for comprehensive evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Payment integration is a repository-level, multi-facet task requiring correct product selection, client-server coordination, outcome verification, and transaction-state consistency. A realistic, structured benchmark is needed to diagnose coding agents’ capability and guide the use of structured guidance in payment domains.

Method: Nine product-specific projects and 18 task instances are organized into Basic functional-completion and Advanced risk-aware hardening scenarios. Scenario rubrics support deterministic static, unit, integration, and end-to-end checks, augmented by LLM-assisted assessment for semantic requirements. Six coding-agent models are evaluated, with rubric pass rate (RPR) reported. Comparisons are drawn between with-skill and without-skill conditions, and results are analyzed at method level (source-level completion, executable payment behavior, and payment-domain requirements).

Result: Under the with-skill condition, mean RPR ranges from 68.58% to 91.37%. Access to the alipay-payment-integration skill improves mean RPR by 10.31 percentage points on average relative to the without-skill condition, with gains varying across models, products, and scenarios. The study distinguishes source-level completion, executable payment behavior, and payment-domain requirements, and provides a controlled setting for diagnosing model capability and evaluating structured guidance in payment integration.

Conclusion: Alipay-PIBench provides a controlled framework for diagnosing model capability and evaluating structured guidance in payment integration, enabling systematic assessment of coding agents in realistic payment flows.

Abstract: Payment integration is a demanding repository-level software task: agents must select a suitable product, implement coordinated client-server flows, verify payment outcomes, and preserve consistency between transaction and business states. We introduce Alipay-PIBench, a benchmark for evaluating coding agents on realistic Alipay payment integration. It contains nine product-specific projects and 18 task instances, each organized into Basic functional-completion and Advanced risk-aware hardening scenarios. Scenario-specific rubrics support deterministic static, unit, integration, and end-to-end checks, supplemented by LLM-assisted assessment for semantic requirements. We evaluate six coding-agent models and report rubric pass rate (RPR). Under the with-skill condition, mean RPR ranges from 68.58% to 91.37%. Access to the alipay-payment-integration skill improves mean RPR by 10.31 percentage points on average relative to the without-skill condition, with gains varying across models, products, and scenarios. Method-level results distinguish source-level completion, executable payment behavior, and payment-domain requirements. Alipay-PIBench provides a controlled setting for diagnosing model capability and evaluating structured guidance in payment integration.

</details>


### [263] [Collaborative Spatial Learning with Multi-LLM Agents in Networked Social Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14574)
*Hao He,Chris J. Kuhlman,Xinwei Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-agent groups in Mason–Watts networks show network-efficiency only with random first-round initialization; a one-sentence instruction to randomize first-round choices boosts performance (>3x across eight topologies). Mechanistic Bayesian optimization agents outperform LLMs on the spatial search task. The study analyzes exploration–exploitation, copying, and spatial diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend the Mason–Watts human-network efficiency findings to AI agents (LLMs), and benchmark against mechanistic Bayesian optimizers to understand how network topology and initialization affect collective problem solving.

Method: Sixteen LLM agents per run across eight Mason–Watts network topologies performing a two-dimensional spatial search task; comparison with mechanistic Bayesian optimization agents; measurement of collective payoff, exploration–exploitation dynamics, copying behavior, and spatial diversity.

Result: LLM groups exhibit a significant network-efficiency effect when their first-round choices are randomized, but not under default initialization. A single-sentence instruction to randomize first-round choices increases collective payoff by >3× across topologies. Bayesian optimization agents achieve higher payoffs than the LLM groups. Detailed comparisons of exploration–exploitation, copying, and spatial diversity are reported.

Conclusion: Network topology and initial instruction critically shape AI-mediated collective problem solving. LLMs can mirror human-network-efficiency under appropriate prompting, while Bayesian optimizers offer superior performance. The work highlights diffusion of information, strategy adaptation, and spatial exploration patterns in multi-agent AI networks.

Abstract: Collective problem solving often requires that group members consider the tradeoff between exploitation of known solutions and exploration for new ones, where information of known solutions can be disseminated among individual members through communication networks. The Mason--Watts experiment (PNAS 2012) showed that human groups in shorter-path networks outperform those in longer-path networks on a two-dimensional search task. In this work, we focus on the investigation of such network-efficiency effects in the setting of a group of large language model (LLM) agents. Specifically, we consider groups of sixteen LLM agents playing the Mason--Watts experiment on the eight Mason--Watts network topologies. Moreover, we develop mechanistic Bayesian optimization agents such that the performance of LLM agents can be compared with both the mechanistic agents and the human experimental data. Our computational experiments indicate that the LLM agents show a significant network-efficiency effect when instructed to randomize their first-round choices, but not under the default initialization. In this experiment, adding a one-sentence first-round randomization instruction improves collective payoff by more than three times the estimated payoff difference across the eight network topologies. Also, the Bayesian optimization agents obtain higher payoffs than the evaluated LLM agents on this spatial search task. We further compare the agents' exploration--exploitation behavior, copying, and spatial diversity.

</details>


### [264] [Multi-LLM Collaborative MRI Report Generation for Visual Instruction Tuning in Brain Oncology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14581)
*Sinyoung Ra,Jonghun Kim,Hyunjin Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a 3D MRI–text dataset for brain tumors and a vision-language model (VLM) that maps MRI volumes to text, achieving superior report generation and visual question answering (VQA) compared with 2D/3D baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical VLMs have largely focused on 2D imaging; 3D imaging lacks paired text data, hindering accurate automated reporting and diagnosis in neuro-oncology. A 3D image–text dataset and aligned VLM could improve clinical reporting and decision support.

Method: Create a 3D MRI–text dataset from glioma and meningioma scans; employ a cooperative system of multiple LLMs to generate and verify reports for accuracy and clarity; develop a VLM that tokenizes MRI scans into representations and aligns them with textual instructions.

Result: The proposed VLM achieved better performance in report generation and VQA tasks than existing 2D and 3D methods, indicating improved report quality and potential for enhanced diagnosis and treatment planning in brain oncology.

Conclusion: A practical pathway to 3D image–text alignment in medical imaging is demonstrated, enabling more accurate reporting and potentially better clinical decision support in brain tumor management.

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and their extension to vision-language models (VLMs) have made it easier to combine text and images for tasks such as report generation. Existing VLMs in medicine typically focus on 2D images (chest X-rays), and their extension to 3D imaging has been difficult because of the lack of paired 3D imaging-text data. Thus, we introduce a new method for creating a 3D image-text dataset for brain oncology using 3D MRI scans of glioma and meningioma cases. We use a cooperative system in which several LLMs work together to generate and check reports, ensuring that they are accurate and clear. By leveraging the new 3D MRI-text dataset, we further build a VLM that converts MRI scans into tokens and aligns them with text instructions. Our VLM performed better in report generation and visual question answering tasks than other 2D and 3D methods. Our method not only improves the quality of reports but also helps with better diagnosis and treatment in brain oncology.

</details>


### [265] [MathCoPilot: An Interactive System for Human-AI Symbiotic Paradigm of Mathematical Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14582)
*Junjie Zhang,Jiayu Liu,Wenbin Liu,Zhenya Huang,Doudou Wang,Yan Jiang,Leiye Xu,Tao Xiong,Wen Huang,Qi Liu,Guoping Hu,Enhong Chen,Mengping Zhang,Xiangdong Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes MathCoPilot, a human-in-the-loop system for mathematical research that guides high-level direction while AI handles formalization and proof under human supervision; evaluates four LLMs on FormalMATH and two PDE theorems, finding that models perform well on undergraduate problems under favorable autoformalization but struggle with domain-specific deep theorems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitations of autonomous LLM theorem provers by introducing a symbiotic workflow where humans steer mathematical direction and AI handles formalizable proof steps, enabling credible, guided mathematical exploration and discovery.

Method: Three core capabilities: (1) an interactive workbench with a living proof blueprint decomposing proofs into inspectable steps; (2) automated proving skill orchestration with adaptive knowledge base search and Lean-integrated iterative verification; (3) topic-driven paper retrieval and automated formalization into a verified Lean knowledge base. Evaluation compares four state-of-the-art LLMs (including Gemini 3.1 Pro, GPT-5.4, Claude Opus 4.7) on a FormalMATH subset and two real PDE theorems, assessing ability to produce verified Lean 4 proofs and to identify errors in deliberately incorrect proofs.

Result: Models can handle undergraduate-level problems with high success under favorable autoformalization conditions; substantial challenges remain for domain-specific theorems requiring genuine mathematical understanding.

Conclusion: The human-in-the-loop paradigm shows promise for enabling credible, guided mathematical research, but further advances are needed in domain understanding, formal reasoning, and seamless integration; MathCoPilot offers a workflow that combines human intuition with AI automation to advance mathematical research.

Abstract: Existing LLM-based theorem provers have achieved impressive results on formal mathematics benchmarks, yet they remain confined to acting as autonomous agents that prove a stated proposition. In this paper, we propose MathCoPilot, a human-in-the-loop system that embodies a new human--AI symbiotic paradigm for mathematical research, in which the mathematician steers the high-level mathematical direction while AI agents carry out the detailed formalization and proof work under continuous human guidance. MathCoPilot unifies three core capabilities: (1) an interactive workbench where the mathematician and AI agents collaborate through a living proof blueprint that decomposes a proof into navigable steps the human can directly inspect, direct, and refine; (2) automated proving skill orchestration with adaptive knowledge base search and Lean-integrated iterative verification; and (3) topic-driven paper retrieval and automated formalization into a verified Lean knowledge base. Using MathCoPilot, we systematically compare four state-of-the-art LLMs, including Gemini~3.1~Pro, GPT-5.4, and Claude~Opus~4.7, on a FormalMATH subset and on two real PDE theorems requiring deep domain expertise, evaluating their ability to produce verified Lean~4 proofs and to identify errors in deliberately incorrect proofs. Our results show that while current models can handle undergraduate-level problems with high success rates under favorable autoformalization conditions, substantial challenges remain for domain-specific theorems requiring genuine mathematical understanding.

</details>


### [266] [SportD: Can VLMs Physically Strategize?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14616)
*Jasin Cekinmez,Addison J. Wu,Haotian Xia,Akshaya Bharadhwaj,Anay Putty,Anirudh Ravishankar,Jaewoong Lee,Jinglin Xiao,Kyumin Andrew Shim,Mishika Ahuja,Nisarga Patil,Leo Liu,Zhuohan Liu,Weining Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SportD is a new benchmark that tests vision-language models (VLMs) on strategic, value-based decisions in soccer by comparing model actions to an action-value model and to professional players across 478 World Cup on-ball decisions. The study reveals that current frontier VLMs underperform professionals in choosing the highest-valued action (31.4% vs 38.9%), exhibit risk-averse behavior (favoring lower-variance and lower-reward actions), and tend to imitate familiar player patterns rather than fully evaluate counterfactual actions. SportD provides a value-grounded platform to assess physical strategic reasoning in VLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend visual-language evaluation beyond static perception tasks into strategic, counterfactual decision making in dynamic sports, by grounding decisions in action values and evaluating how closely models approximate optimal policies and professional behavior.

Method: Construct SportD with 478 on-ball decisions from the 2022 FIFA World Cup. Use a possession-value model to quantify the expected score probability increase for each available action (shoots/passes). Evaluate three frontier VLMs on optimal-action accuracy and regret, and analyze behavioral biases (variance, reward, progression) and imitation of actual player actions.

Result: Best VLMs pick the highest-valued action in 31.4% of events (versus 38.9% for pros). All models exhibit greater regret than professionals. Models prefer lower-variance and lower-reward actions, shoot less, and make fewer progressive passes. Models tend to imitate the shown action even when suboptimal, indicating partial imitation of familiar patterns rather than robust counterfactual evaluation.

Conclusion: SportD offers a value-grounded benchmark to quantify physical strategic reasoning in VLMs and reveals current limitations in strategic evaluation, highlighting bias toward risk-averse, pattern-imitating behavior and the need for improved counterfactual reasoning in vision-language systems.

Abstract: Vision--language models have become increasingly capable of interpreting visual scenes, but it remains unclear whether they can use information to make strategically effective decisions. We investigate this question in soccer, where models observe the seconds preceding an on-ball decision and must choose whether to shoot or pass to a specific teammate. Unlike conventional visual-understanding tasks, soccer enables decisions to be evaluated quantitatively by estimating the value of every available action. We introduce SportD, a benchmark comprising 478 on-ball decisions from the 2022 FIFA World Cup. Each model choice is evaluated against a possession-value model that estimates the action that most increases the attacking team's probability of scoring, allowing us to measure both optimal-action accuracy and the value forfeited by suboptimal decisions. Across three frontier VLMs, the best selects the highest-valued action on 31.4% of events, compared with 38.9% for the professional players, and all models incur significantly greater regret. Further analysis reveals a systematic preference for lower-variance and lower-reward actions: VLMs shoot less often and select substantially less progressive passes than either the optimal policy or the real players. The models also reproduce the player's specific action above chance even when that action is suboptimal, suggesting partial imitation of familiar play patterns rather than consistent evaluation of counterfactual alternatives. SportD provides a value-grounded testbed for measuring physical strategic reasoning in VLMs.

</details>


### [267] [Action QFormer: Structured Representation Shaping under Action Supervision in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14635)
*Yufeng Ji,Wenhao Tang,Haoyi Niu,Koushil Sreenath,Yi Wu,Zhongyu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Action QFormer deploys instruction-conditioned, action-facing queries to reorganize inherited multimodal representations for action generation, alleviating instability from direct action supervision and achieving substantial gains in zero-shot sim-to-real navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directly applying action supervision to inherited multimodal pathways can improve action alignment but destabilizes language-grounding and object grounding; a mediator that reshapes information is needed.

Method: Propose Action QFormer, a query-based interface that uses instruction-conditioned queries to reorganize multimodal features into action-facing representations before action generation; operates within VLA models; evaluated on zero-shot sim-to-real navigation.

Result: Significant improvements in zero-shot navigation: average task success up from 18.8% to 56.3%; fixed-instruction action-generation accuracy from 22.5% to 75.5%; reduces out-of-distribution instruction generations; analyses show controlled shaping and preserved targeted adaptation.

Conclusion: Better performance stems not only from stronger backbones but also from selective organization of inherited multimodal information and careful control of how action supervision shapes representations.

Abstract: Action supervision in vision-language-action (VLA) models is often treated as a downstream objective for learning action prediction. In this paper, we study it instead as a force that shapes inherited multimodal representations. We show that this shaping has a dual effect: it is necessary for forming action-compatible representations, but when action supervision is applied too directly to the inherited multimodal pathway, it can also destabilize representations that support language-side processing and object grounding. To address this tension, we introduce Action QFormer, a query-based action-facing interface that uses instruction-conditioned queries to reorganize inherited multimodal information into action-facing representations before downstream action generation. In zero-shot sim-to-real navigation, Action QFormer improves average closed-loop task success from 18.8% to 56.3%, raises fixed-instruction action-generation correctness from 22.5% to 75.5%, and nearly eliminates out-of-distribution instruction generations. Further analyses show that Action QFormer changes how action supervision shapes inherited multimodal representations, reducing broad upstream rewriting while preserving targeted and sometimes constructive action-supervised adaptation. These results suggest that improving VLA performance requires not only stronger pretrained backbones, but also better ways of selecting and organizing inherited multimodal information while controlling how it is shaped under action supervision.

</details>


### [268] [Analytic Abduction: Causal Decomposition and Governed Commitment for Human--AI Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14641)
*Remo Pareschi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces an analytic, non-greedy abductive mode built on a κ-τ framework to compare competing latent-factor explanations, guarded by a two-level governance to prevent misattribution and premature convergence, with causal clusters as interpretable decision-support structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing the risk of premature convergence and misattribution in abductive reasoning by shifting from single, greedy explanations to structured, governance-backed decomposition that can be shared and interrogated.

Method: Proposes κ-τ apparatus: κ governs epistemic interaction among hypotheses; τ is a commitment threshold tied to decision stakes. Introduces causal clusters recording participating latent factors, weights, and interaction structure, with intra-cluster and inter-cluster governance (κ* and κ**). Two demonstration domains (epidemiological crisis decomposition and adversarial cyber threat analysis).

Result: Delivers a legible representation of suspended decomposition as a shared coordination object, enabling multiple competing explanations with evidence to resolve between them. Prevents premature convergence and supports sound action under ambiguity by presenting plausible scenarios and the supporting evidence.

Conclusion: The framework advances human–AI reasoning by making the decomposition process explicit and negotiable, improving interpretability, accountability, and coordination in complex decision contexts.

Abstract: Abductive reasoning operates in two directions. The synthetic mode builds explanations from available hypotheses; the analytic mode, conversely, identifies the latent factors whose interaction accounts for a complex observed state. This paper develops the analytic mode as a non-greedy, risk-sensitive discipline of commitment, in which candidate factors coexist and interact, resolving into committed conclusions only when explicit governance conditions are met. The formal core is the $κ$-$τ$ apparatus: $κ$ encodes the epistemic interaction among hypotheses, and $τ$ sets a commitment threshold calibrated to the decision's stakes. The central contribution is the causal cluster, a structured object recording which latent factors participate in a decomposition, with what weights and interaction structure, together with a two-level architecture (intra-cluster $κ^*$, inter-cluster $κ^{**}$) that guards against causal misattribution. Demonstrated in epidemiological crisis decomposition and adversarial cyber threat analysis, the framework's contribution to human-AI reasoning is the legibility of suspended decomposition as a shared coordination object, providing structural resistance to premature convergence. In practice, the decision-maker is handed not a single imposed answer but the competing explanatory scenarios, weighted by plausibility and paired with the evidence that would resolve between them, so that sound action is possible even before the ambiguity is resolved.

</details>


### [269] [MCPEvol-Bench: Benchmarking LLM Agent Performance Across Dynamic Evolutions of MCP Servers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14642)
*Huanxi Liu,Kun Hu,Jiaqi Liao,Qiang Wang,Pengfei Qian,YuanZhao Zhai,Dawei Feng,Bo Ding,Huaimin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MCPEvol-Bench is a dynamic benchmark to test LLM agent adaptability to evolving MCP tool interfaces, using 11 mutation operators over 123 MCP servers and 12 LLMs; results show substantial performance drops and more planning errors, establishing a standard for dynamic tool environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address a core shortcoming in MCP benchmarking: static tool interfaces fail to reflect the real-world evolution of tool capabilities, leaving agent adaptability unmeasured.

Method: Introduce 11 mutation operators that simulate tool evolution; apply them across 123 MCP servers to create multiple evolved versions; evaluate 12 state-of-the-art LLMs on these versions and track task-solving performance and planning/reasoning errors.

Result: On evolved MCP servers, frontier models (e.g., GPT-5.4, Claude-Sonnet-4-6) show performance declines of approximately 13.7% and 14.4%, respectively, with notable increases in planning and reasoning errors; MCPEvol-Bench demonstrates vulnerability of LLM-driven workflows under changing tool landscapes.

Conclusion: MCPEvol-Bench provides a standard for evaluating agent adaptability in dynamic tool environments and highlights the need for robustness to tool evolution in LLM-driven workflows.

Abstract: As Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers emerge as the core infrastructure for connecting LLMs with external tools, existing benchmarks leverage real-world MCP servers to evaluate LLM agents' tool-using capabilities. However, these benchmarks overlook the continuous evolution of tool interfaces and functionalities within MCP servers, resulting in flawed assessments that fail to capture the agent's adaptability in changing tool landscapes. To bridge this gap, we introduce \textbf{MCPEvol-Bench}, a novel benchmark for evaluating the task-solving capabilities of LLM agents under dynamic toolset evolution. Inspired by large-scale empirical study, we propose 11 mutation operators to simulate realistic tool evolution within 123 MCP servers. We benchmark 12 state-of-the-art LLMs on multiple versions of MCP servers, revealing that even frontier models struggle to adapt to evolving tools. For instance, GPT-5.4 and Claude-Sonnet-4-6 exhibit performance declines of 13.7\% and 14.4\% in evolved MCP servers, respectively, accompanied by substantial increases in planning and reasoning errors. These findings highlight the vulnerability of LLM-driven workflows, establishing MCPEvol-Bench as a standard for evaluating agent adaptability in dynamic tool environments.

</details>


### [270] [TopoAgent: A Self-Evolving Topological Agent for Multimodal Scientific Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14658)
*Mingze Xu,Yinghui Li,Jiayi Kuang,Zhanhui Kang,Di Yin,Ying Shen,Xing Sun,Yuxing Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TopoAgent introduces a self-evolving, DAG-based framework that replaces linear planning with dynamic, state-isolated graph evolution and adaptive atom splitting to enhance autonomous scientific reasoning in multimodal LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome core limitations of monolithic linear planning in MLLMs, notably visual–semantic misalignment, long-context hallucinations, and brittle execution under fixed task granularity.

Method: A front-end decomposer fractures complex queries into visually-grounded atoms; these atoms are organized into a DAG reflecting dependencies to enforce strict context isolation; adaptive atomic fission dynamically splits bottleneck nodes into finer sub-atoms at runtime when tool capability boundaries are exceeded; the framework enables topological evolution to support autonomous reasoning across mathematics, physics, and chemistry benchmarks.

Result: Extensive experiments show TopoAgent significantly outperforms state-of-the-art linear agent frameworks, exhibiting robustness to noise and self-correcting behavior across the tested domains.

Conclusion: A topological, modular reasoning paradigm that mitigates linear-agent weaknesses by isolating context and enabling dynamic granularity, offering robust autonomous scientific reasoning.

Abstract: While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) excel in general tasks, rigorous scientific reasoning remains challenging due to the limitations of monolithic, linear planning. Such sequential designs often suffer from visual-semantic misalignment, long-context hallucinations, and brittle execution under fixed task granularity. We propose TopoAgent, a self-evolving topological framework that replaces linear trajectories with dynamic, state-isolated graph evolution. TopoAgent first employs a front-end decomposer to fracture complex queries into visually-grounded atoms. These atoms are organized into a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) based on their dependencies, enabling strict context isolation to shield the reasoning engine from irrelevant historical noise. Furthermore, we introduce adaptive atomic fission, which dynamically splits bottleneck nodes into finer-grained sub-atoms at runtime when tool capability boundaries are exceeded. Extensive experiments across mathematics, physics, and chemistry benchmarks demonstrate that TopoAgent significantly outperforms state-of-the-art linear agent frameworks, providing a robust, noise-resistant, and self-correcting paradigm for autonomous scientific reasoning.

</details>


### [271] [SmartRAG: Native Graph-Based RAG for Mobile Device](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14661)
*Zhihan Jiang,Meng Li,Shenghao Liu,Keran Li,Ruiben Zhou,Xianjun Deng,Shuai Wang,Haipeng Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SmartRAG enables fully on-device intelligence by decomposing an LLM-based assistant into Perception, Memory, Focus, and Thinking modules, with EvoNER for continual on-device NER, and MRGraph-based knowledge storage and retrieval. It achieves strong multi-hop QA with a small 1.7B LLM on mobile devices, rivaling larger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable private, low-latency, offline-capable AI assistants on edge devices without requiring large cloud-based models or heavy compression, by distributing intelligence across specialized on-device components and continuous learning of domain entities.

Method: Introduce four modular components (Perception, Memory, Focus, Thinking) and EvoNER (teacher-distilled, continually learnable NER expanding label space), and MRGraph for provenance-preserving knowledge storage. Use a hybrid retrieval pipeline combining graph traversal, lexical matching, and dense semantic search. Invoke on-device LLM only for high-value semantic tasks (labeling, planning, answer synthesis). Evaluate on TriviaQA, Natural Questions, HotpotQA, MultiHopQA with a 1.7B quantized backbone.

Result: SmartRAG delivers multi-hop QA performance competitive with models up to ~18x larger while running entirely on commodity smartphones, with practical memory and latency; the combination of modular on-device architecture and continual NER expansion yields strong reasoning capabilities.

Conclusion: Decomposing on-device AI into coordinated functional roles plus continual, on-device learning of entities and provenance-preserving knowledge graphs enables high-performance, privacy-preserving, offline LLM usage on mobile hardware, reducing reliance on oversized models.

Abstract: Deploying large language models (LLMs) as personal assistants on mobile devices demands privacy, low latency, and offline availability, yet the computational cost of giant models clashes with strict edge-hardware budgets. We argue that this tension cannot be resolved by model compression alone; it requires decomposing on-device intelligence into complementary functional roles. We present SmartRAG, a fully on-device framework that organizes an intelligent assistant around four coordinated modules -- Perception, Memory, Focus, and Thinking. At the core of SmartRAG is EvoNER, a continually learnable named-entity recognizer that incrementally expands its label inventory through teacher-distilled updates, enabling the system to absorb previously unseen entity types without retraining the backbone LLM. Extracted knowledge is stored in MRGraph, a three-layer provenance-preserving knowledge graph, and retrieved at query time through a hybrid pipeline combining graph traversal, lexical matching, and dense semantic search. The on-device LLM is invoked only for high-value semantic operations -- labeling, planning, and answer synthesis -- keeping inference costs bounded. Experiments on four QA benchmarks (TriviaQA, Natural Questions, HotpotQA, MultiHopQA) show that SmartRAG with a quantized 1.7B-parameter backbone achieves multi-hop reasoning performance competitive with models up to 18$\times$ larger, while running entirely on commodity smartphones within practical memory and latency envelopes.

</details>


### [272] [Project Kaleidoscope: Contextual, Human-Aligned Evaluation for Real-World AI Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14673)
*Leanne Tan,Rohan Jaggi,Shaun Khoo,Roy Ka-Wei Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Kaleidoscope provides an integrated, governance-aware workflow for contextual functional evaluation combining persona-based test generation, contextual rubrics, human labeling, and reliability-gated LLM scoring; early pilot shows feasibility across multiple domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public benchmarks often miss user context and local policies; evaluation at scale with human review is costly; need a practical workflow for policy-compliant AI deployment.

Method: Integrated workflow: persona-based test generation, contextual rubrics, human annotations, and LLM judges that score only when agreeing with human labels; iterative, inspectable; pilot across four use cases; 108 QA pairs; 14 dimensions; four domains.

Result: Early evidence of end-to-end reliable automated scoring; features enabling interpretable scoring; successful pilot signals for practical applicability.

Conclusion: Kaleidoscope offers a practical, inspectable, iterative framework for contextual functional evaluation that aligns with governance constraints and scales with limited human review.

Abstract: Evaluations (Evals) are a deployment bottleneck for real-world AI applications: public benchmarks rarely match a team's users, context, or policies, and human review is often tedious to scale.
