Motivated by our work with AI applications in the public sector, this project addresses recurring evaluation challenges encountered when applications must satisfy local policy and governance requirements. We present Kaleidoscope, an integrated workflow for contextual functional evaluation that links persona-based test generation, contextualized rubrics, and human review for reliability-gated automated scoring. Generated test cases are scored against application-specific rubrics; human annotations provide reviewable labels; and LLM judges automate scoring only when their agreement with those labels meets a configured threshold. Kaleidoscope is therefore a practical, inspectable, iterative workflow for product teams. We report early evidence from a three-week pilot across four organizational use cases and custom-rubric judge experiments on 108 annotated Q\&A pairs spanning four domains and 14 evaluation dimensions. The results highlight useful features for end-to-end reliable, automated scoring.

</details>


### [273] [Stop Thinking, Start Looking: Efficient Post-Training for Multimodal Document Question Answering via Reasoning-Free Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14682)
*Harikrishnan P M,Goutham Vignesh,Ganesh Parab,Saisubramaniam Gopalakrishnan,Vishal Vaddina,Varun V,Rohit Agrawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Perception-RFT uses Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to train multimodal document QA without intermediate reasoning tokens, achieving perception-like policies and significantly reducing per-query token cost; reasoning-enabled RL underperforms and shows a Grounding Divergence with a trade-off between semantic robustness and geometric precision on OOD data. An early SFT → RL transition can reach similar precision with far less data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit visual grounding for multimodal document QA is hampered by data-hungry supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and costly, tracing-heavy reasoning-based RL. The study questions whether explicit, tokenized reasoning is necessary for high-quality grounding and aims to quantify data efficiency and dynamics of SFT vs RL in a multimodal setting.

Method: Propose Perception-RFT, applying Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to multimodal document QA to directly map visual features to structured grounding outputs, bypassing intermediate reasoning tokens. Construct a reasoning-variant under identical reward to compare with the perception-based policy. Analyze optimization dynamics on Qwen3-VL-4B and examine Grounding Divergence on two out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks (4,828 samples). Evaluate data efficiency by an early SFT→RL transition.

Result: Reasoning-enabled models suppress reasoning traces during training and converge to direct perception-based policies at around 4B parameters; per-query inference token length reduced by >60%; reasoning-enabled RL underperforms perception-only training. Grounding Divergence observed: a trade-off between semantic robustness and geometric precision on OOD data. Early SFT→RL transition achieves comparable precision with ~65% less training data.

Conclusion: SFT saturation and cold-start RL instability extend from text to multimodal domains. Grounding-divergence highlights a trade-off between robustness and precision under joint RL optimization. Early transition from SFT to RL can substantially reduce data requirements while maintaining performance, suggesting that direct perception-based grounding can be more effective than reasoning-centric RL for multimodal document QA.

Abstract: Efficient multimodal document question answering with explicit visual grounding, locating the precise document region that supports each answer remains an open challenge. Current approaches bifurcate into Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), which requires large annotated datasets and reaches optimization plateaus, and reasoning-centric Reinforcement Learning (RL), which depends on verbose intermediate traces that inflate inference token cost without clear benefit. We introduce Perception-RFT, a training framework that applies Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to multimodal document QA, bypassing intermediate reasoning tokens to directly align visual features with structured grounding outputs. To rigorously evaluate the necessity of reasoning, we construct a reasoning variant under identical reward settings. We find that reasoning-enabled models suppress their reasoning traces during training, converging to direct perception-based policies at the 4B parameter scale, reducing per-query inference token length by more than 60%, while reasoning-enabled RL underperforms perception-only training. Through a fine-grained analysis of Qwen3-VL-4B optimization dynamics, we confirm that SFT saturation and cold-start RL instability established in text-domain post-training extend to multimodal, and identify a previously uncharacterized Grounding Divergence: a selective trade-off between semantic robustness and geometric precision on two out of distribution (OOD) benchmarks (4,828 samples) under joint RL optimization. We further show that an early SFT$\rightarrow$RL transition achieves comparable precision with 65% less training data.

</details>


### [274] [InCarEmo: A Multimodal Dataset for In-Cabin Emotion Recognition and Driver State Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14683)
*Hao Yang,Yanyan Zhao,Kewei Zhao,Hongbo Zhang,Tian Zheng,Yusheng Liu,Xing Fu,Bichen Wang,Yu Zhang,Hao He,Zhen Wu,Xuda Zhi,Yongbo Huang,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces InCarEmo, a comprehensive multimodal in-cabin emotion dataset (RGB/IR video, audio, dialogue text) for emotion recognition, fatigue, and distraction, with Chinese and auxiliary English benchmarks; provides baselines and modality-missing/noise analyses to study fusion benefits and robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public in-cabin affective datasets are mostly visual and lack conversational and interactive linguistic cues, hindering robust emotion understanding and human-vehicle interaction under real-world driving conditions.

Method: Data collection in scripted in-cabin scenarios across diverse lighting and driving contexts; multimodal capture (RGB/IR video, audio, dialogue text); auxiliary English benchmark for cross-lingual evaluation; comprehensive baseline experiments on unimodal and multimodal models, including modality-missing and noise conditions.

Result: Multimodal fusion improves emotion recognition and state-monitoring performance; the benchmark reveals robustness trends and remaining challenges under low-light and noisy conditions; bilingual (Chinese-English) cross-lingual evaluation is feasible via the auxiliary English benchmark.

Conclusion: InCarEmo provides a foundational resource for robust, interpretable, and human-centric in-cabin affective understanding, enabling safer and more empathetic driver–vehicle interaction through comprehensive multimodal data and benchmarks.

Abstract: Understanding driver emotion and state is critical for the next generation of intelligent in-cabin systems that ensure safety and enhance human-vehicle interaction. However, existing public datasets for in-cabin affective computing are largely limited to visual modalities and rarely include conversational information, making it difficult to capture the linguistic and interactive cues underlying driver emotion. To address these gaps, we introduce InCarEmo, a multimodal dataset for in-cabin emotion recognition and driver state monitoring. InCarEmo integrates RGB and infrared video, in-cabin audio, and dialogue text collected from scripted in-cabin scenarios designed to simulate realistic driver behaviors, covering diverse lighting conditions and driving contexts. The dataset supports three primary tasks: 1) multimodal emotion recognition, 2) fatigue detection, and 3) distraction monitoring. In addition to the original Chinese data, we construct an auxiliary English benchmark to support preliminary cross-lingual evaluation. We provide a unified benchmark with extensive baseline results across unimodal and multimodal methods, including analyses under modality-missing and noise conditions. Experimental results demonstrate the benefits of multimodal fusion and reveal remaining challenges under real-world noise and low-light conditions. By releasing InCarEmo, we aim to establish a comprehensive foundation for robust, interpretable, and human-centric in-cabin affective understanding, promoting safer and more empathetic driver-vehicle interaction.

</details>


### [275] [AI vs Human Expert Reasoning: Assessing Agreements in Building Typology Predictions based on Street View Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14756)
*Zahratu Shabrina,Muhammad Asa,Jin Rui,Lu Yin,Stephen Law*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VLMs can infer building typologies from GSV images with ~70% accuracy, approaching expert performance, with Chain-of-Thought prompting improving stability; AI explanations reveal a bias toward visual cues, while humans use broader context and domain knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess feasibility and scalability of Vision-Language Models for automated building-typology classification in urban analysis and understand how AI explanations compare to human expert reasoning.

Method: Evaluate multiple state-of-the-art VLMs (GPT-4o, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, Gemini 2.0 Flash) using varied scaling strategies and prompting techniques; compare predictions to ground-truth labels provided by civil engineers and architects; analyze AI-generated explanations for keyword patterns to understand reasoning; examine agreement/disagreement with experts.

Result: Average accuracy around 70%; Chain-of-Thought prompts yield more stable performance; AI explanations show emphasis on visual indicators; experts emphasize broader contextual cues and domain knowledge; VLMs can approximate expert classifications at scale and serve as complementary tools.

Conclusion: VLMs show potential for automated, scalable urban analysis tasks; they can complement human experts, particularly for pattern recognition in visual data, but require better alignment of AI reasoning with domain expertise and attention to contextual cues to close gaps with expert judgment.

Abstract: This research investigates the potential of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to infer building typologies: Construction, Current Use, and Storeys from Google Street View (GSV) images. Predictions generated by VLMs are compared with inference by human experts (civil engineers and architects) as a source of manually labelled ground-truth data. We evaluate several state-of-the-art VLMs, including GPT-4o, Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and Gemini 2.0 Flash. By applying different scaling strategies and prompting techniques, we found that Chain-of-Thought prompts provide an overall more stable model performance. We also investigate the reasoning behind VLMs' building-typology predictions by examining the probabilities of keywords appearing in AI explanations. This enabled us to analyse patterns in these reasonings and identify key themes driving both agreements and disagreements between VLM and expert labels. We find that AI tends to focus on visual indicators, whereas human experts place greater emphasis on broader contextual cues and domain knowledge, in addition to visual cues. Overall, VLM can approximate experts' capability in building-typology classification at scale, with an average accuracy of approximately 70%. The study demonstrates the VLM's potential for AI automation in tasks that require pattern recognition and object identification in an urban context. AI have the potential to serve as complementary and collaborative tools for urban analysis, leveraging their strengths in understanding visual patterns. This study contributes to the exploration of the efficiency and scalability of AI visual prediction and provides insights into the reasoning processes that could support automation processes in urban analysis and prediction.

</details>


### [276] [Global Index on Responsible AI: 2026 Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14782)
*Rachel Adams,Fola Adeleke,Ayantola Alayande,Selamawit Engida Abdella,Ana Florido,Nicolás Grossman,Leah Junck*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GIRAI 2026 evaluates how countries translate responsible AI commitments into enforceable protections, using 68,138 data points across 38 indicators in 135 countries; finds expansion of governance but limited enforceability, with non-binding frameworks, especially in Global South, and gaps in transparency of government algorithms; 35 countries show credible unacceptable-risk AI deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether responsible AI governance near global adoption translates into rights-based protections and redress for citizens.

Method: Global network of 135 country-level researchers collecting data from Nov 2023–Sep 2025 on 38 indicators across three pillars: government policy & implementation (17), civil society engagement (5), enabling conditions (15); plus documented cases; compute a 100-point score.

Result: 126/135 countries have at least one government AI policy among 17 indicators; many policies are non-binding (Global South: 78% non-binding vs Global North 42%). 58% have some framework for transparency/explainability, but only 18% require public disclosure of government algorithms. 35 countries show credible evidence of deployment of unacceptable-risk AI systems.

Conclusion: Responsible AI governance must move from framework adoption to enforceable rights-based protections, resourced oversight institutions, and accessible redress mechanisms.

Abstract: Grounded in human rights-based frameworks such as the UNESCO Recommendation on the Ethics of AI, the Global Index on Responsible AI (GIRAI) examines how countries translate responsible AI commitments into enforceable protections, institutional capacity, and redress mechanisms. GIRAI 2026 assesses these across five dimensions: Inclusion and Diversity, Ethics and Sustainability, Labour and Skills, Trust and Safety, and AI Use in Public Service. A global network of 135 country-level researchers collected and assessed 68,138 data points on 38 indicators organised across three pillars: government AI policy and implementation (17 indicators), civil society engagement (5), enabling conditions (15), and documented cases of government deployment of unacceptable-risk AI systems. The data covers November 2023 to September 2025. A score of 100 is derived from the indicators to rank all countries. Findings show that while responsible AI governance is expanding, with 126 of 135 countries having at least one government policy or initiative across the 17 AI Policy indicators, this does not often translate into meaningful protection. For instance, Global South countries account for 203 of 306 new cases of indicators with frameworks since the first edition, yet 78% of their frameworks remain non-binding compared with 42% in the Global North. Government commitment to AI governance also does not extend to their own algorithms: whereas Transparency and Explainability is the strongest performing indicator, with 58% of countries having some framework, only 18% require Public Disclosure of Government Algorithms. Credible evidence of government deployment of unacceptable-risk AI systems was also found in 35 countries. These findings show that responsible AI governance must move beyond framework adoption toward enforceable rights-based protections, resourced oversight institutions, and accessible redress.

</details>


### [277] [Transcoders for Investigating Deception in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14791)
*Darius Lim,Nathan Leow,Xin Wei Chia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Transcoders enable circuit-level analysis of deception in language models; using per-layer transcoders on Qwen3-4B, the authors build attribution graphs to identify deception-related features and show these features influence deceptive outputs, suggesting deception emerges from internal mechanisms and that transcoders can aid monitoring and early detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety risks from deceptive behavior in LMs necessitate mechanistic interpretability to understand and potentially mitigate deception at the circuit level.

Method: Apply pre-trained per-layer transcoders on Qwen3-4B; construct attribution graphs capturing feature activations and inter-feature dependencies; perform feature steering and circuit analysis to identify a dictionary of deception-related features; assess their influence on deceptive vs non-deceptive responses.

Result: Deception-related features exert stronger influence on outputs, with predictable shifts between deceptive and non-deceptive responses; evidence that deception arises from internal model mechanisms; demonstrates potential for behavioural monitoring and early detection of security vulnerabilities via transcoders.

Conclusion: Transcoders, especially PLTs, offer a viable approach for monitoring model behavior and detecting malicious tendencies, enabling proactive security assessments in language models.

Abstract: Transcoders have recently emerged as a promising approach for mechanistic interpretability (MI), enabling circuit-level analysis of model behaviour. In this paper, we investigate the use of transcoders to analyse deceptive behaviour in language models, a behaviour that poses a safety and security risk. Using a Qwen3-4B model with pre-trained transcoders, specifically per-layer transcoders (PLTs), we construct attribution graphs that capture feature activations and inter-feature dependencies, allowing circuit-level analysis of deception. Through feature steering and circuit analysis, we identified a dictionary of deception-related features and show that these features exert a stronger influence on deceptive outputs, as they produce predictable shifts between deceptive and non-deceptive responses. These findings suggest that deception emerges from internal model mechanisms and highlight the potential of transcoders for behavioural monitoring and early detection of security vulnerabilities related to malicious behaviours in language models.

</details>


### [278] [CrimeNER Demo: Named-Entity Recognition in the Crime Domain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14800)
*Miguel Lopez-Duran,Julian Fierrez,Aythami Morales,Daniel DeAlcala,Gonzalo Mancera,Javier Irigoyen,Ruben Tolosana,Oscar Delgado,Francisco Jurado,Alvaro Ortigosa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CrimeNER Demo provides pretrained crime-domain NER models, user-driven fine-tuning on annotated data, and an automated pipeline to extract and annotate crime entities, with GitHub resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable accurate extraction of crime-related information from documents, support two-level entity granularity, and empower researchers and law enforcement with customizable, accessible NER tools.

Method: Develop a CrimeNER Demo platform featuring pretrained models trained on the CrimeNER database, an interface to fine-tune models on user-annotated data, and an automated pipeline to extract/annotate crime entities at two granularity levels.

Result: A publicly available demonstrator with pretrained models, user fine-tuning capability, and an automatic extraction/annotation pipeline; GitHub-hosted tutorials, demos, and video.

Conclusion: The work promotes crime-domain NER research and provides a practical, accessible tool for researchers and law enforcement to extract crime information from documents.

Abstract: We present CrimeNER Demo, an AI-powered platform that enables us to extract general crime-related information from documents and classify them into entity types with two levels of granularity. We provide pretrained NER models on the CrimeNER database, and we give the possibility to users to provide their own annotated data to train models for their own specific cases. This demonstrator aims to promote crime-related NER research and provides a practical tool to automatically extract crime information for researchers and law enforcement agencies. The demonstrator includes: i) Pretrained NER models on the crime domain; ii) Possibility to finetune the models on specific data annotated by the user; and iii) An automatic pipeline to extract and annotate crime entities from documents. The demo platform, a tutorial to run the demo, and a video demonstration are publicly available on GitHub.

</details>


### [279] [Reachability-Aware Pretraining for Efficient Target-Oriented Path Exploration in Temporal Knowledge Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14886)
*Chien-Liang Liu,Tsao-Lun Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAPTOR uses a self-supervised reachability pretraining to guide RL-based multi-hop reasoning in Temporal Knowledge Graphs, improving training efficiency and accuracy on standard ICEWS benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL-based multi-hop reasoning in TKGs suffers from sparse rewards and enormous time-evolving action spaces, leading to inefficient exploration and limited performance.

Method: RAPTOR introduces a self-supervised pretraining stage that learns a reachability model for candidate actions to the target entity. This reachability bias guides exploration away from unpromising paths and provides a strong initialization for downstream RL fine-tuning on TKG reasoning tasks.

Result: Experiments on ICEWS14, ICEWS05-15, and ICEWS18 show that RAPTOR significantly improves training efficiency and consistently outperforms conventional baselines in RL-based TKG reasoning.

Conclusion: RAPTOR is an effective pretraining strategy for RL-based temporal path reasoning, reducing exploration inefficiency and enhancing performance; its reachability bias could generalize to other RL-oriented path search problems in dynamic graphs.

Abstract: Temporal Knowledge Graph (TKG) reasoning under the extrapolation setting focuses on forecasting future time-stamped events (facts) from historical data in a temporal knowledge graph. Existing approaches, reinforcement learning (RL)-based multi-hop reasoning methods are prominent for TKG reasoning because they produce human-interpretable predictions via explicit multi-hop path tracing. However, during RL training, rewards are typically sparse, and exploration is highly inefficient due to the vast, time-evolving action space. These issues hinder efficient training and often limit overall performance. To address these challenges, we propose RAPTOR (Reachability-Aware Pretraining for Efficient Target-Oriented Path Exploration), a self-supervised pretraining method that injects a reachability-aware inductive bias to the agent. By learning to estimate the reachability of candidate actions to the target entity, RAPTOR reduces exploration over unpromising paths and provides a strong initialization for downstream RL fine-tuning. Experimental results on the ICEWS14, ICEWS05-15, and ICEWS18 datasets demonstrate that RAPTOR pretraining markedly improves the training efficiency and consistently outperforms conventional baselines, establishing it as an effective approach for enhancing RL-based multi-hop reasoning methods for TKG reasoning.

</details>


### [280] [Proof-or-Stop: Don't Trust the Agent, Trust the Evidence -- Loop Engineering for Verifiable Evidence-Gated Lifecycle Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14890)
*Jek Huang,Jeffery Hsia,Jiayi Sun,Freddie Shi,Wei Huang,Ian H. White*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proof-or-Stop gates enforce lifecycle transitions in autonomous agents using verifiable evidence bound to the source state; shows improved gate integrity and reduced amplification of passes/fails in simulations; model-agnostic but evaluated on one model family with 24 ablation tasks and a self-hosted corpus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lifecycle states are claims without current evidence; need a mechanism to enforce evidence-backed transitions; treat outputs as claims; leverage gate admissibility under a trust model; provide host-neutral control layer.

Method: Define Proof-or-Stop gate requiring fresh, tracked-source-state-bound, mechanically verifiable evidence; gate admissibility under trust model; measure via mechanism tests, control-policy ablation, and self-application evidence; compare gated vs naive loops; evaluate tamper resistance and reviewer enforcement.

Result: Unattended-loop engine passed 10/10; zero false-DONE; local-key bundles rejected 18 tamper classes with zero false accepts; 9,240-cell ablation shows A4 vs A2-prime reduces amplification from 31/1800 to 2/1800; 1.6 pp improvement with 95% CI [0.8,2.5]; A3 vs A4: 14/1800 vs 2/1800; gain linked to enforcing review as gate; self-application corpus: 565 stories, 1007 findings, 94.8% resolved; 68-row cross-vendor exhibit; conclusion: model-agnostic, host-neutral control layer; limitations: only one model family, 24 ablation tasks, self-hosted corpus.

Conclusion: Proof-or-Stop provides a practical, evidence-backed gate for lifecycle transitions in autonomous agents, improving decision reliability and tamper resistance, while remaining model-agnostic and host-neutral; future work could expand model families, tasks, and external corpus.

Abstract: Autonomous coding agents increasingly execute multi-step software work, but lifecycle states such as reviewed, tested, DONE, and ready-to-merge remain claims unless supported by current evidence. We present Proof-or-Stop Lifecycle Control, a method that permits lifecycle transitions only when fresh, tracked-source-state-bound, mechanically verifiable evidence satisfies the relevant gate. The method treats agent outputs as claims rather than lifecycle state, and uses proof operationally to mean gate-admissible evidence under a stated trust model, not semantic program correctness.
  We evaluate an open-source implementation through mechanism tests, a powered control-policy ablation, and operated self-application evidence. The unattended-loop engine passed 10 of 10 scenarios with zero false-DONE, and local-key receipt bundles rejected 18 tamper classes with zero false accepts. In a 9,240-cell ablation, the pre-registered A4 versus A2-prime comparison reduced visible-pass/hidden-fail amplification from 31 of 1,800 injected cells under a compute-budgeted naive loop to 2 of 1,800 under the gated loop, a 1.6 percentage-point improvement in not-amplified rate with a 95 percent confidence interval of [0.8, 2.5]. A near-compute A3 versus A4 comparison, 14 of 1,800 versus 2 of 1,800, indicates that the gain is associated with enforcing review as a lifecycle gate rather than merely adding a reviewer. The self-application corpus contains 565 stories and 1,007 review findings, with 94.8 percent resolved, plus a 68-row high/critical cross-vendor exhibit. These results support Proof-or-Stop as a model-agnostic, host-neutral control layer for deciding which autonomous-agent claims a lifecycle may act on. The evaluation is limited to one model family, 24 ablation tasks, and a self-hosted corpus.

</details>


### [281] [Contextualized Early Detection of Online Firestorms: A Sequential LLM-Based Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14957)
*Besim Shala,Peter Mandl,Andreas Humpe,Martin Häusl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes two-mode LLM-based detection of online firestorms: retrospective thread classification and sequential early warning with indicators for negativity share, escalation, and contributor count; strong retrospective performance and high-recall early warnings on Reddit data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of signals like volume, sentiment, and predefined features by leveraging LLMs to capture evolving contextual meaning in threads and enable real-time monitoring.

Method: Two operating modes: (1) global mode for retrospective thread classification by aggregating local chunk assessments into a thread-level judgment; (2) early-warning mode processing threads sequentially with a sliding window, estimating three indicators (negativity share, escalation level, contributor count) and triggering warnings when calibrated thresholds are exceeded.

Result: On a balanced Reddit dataset, the global mode achieves strong classification performance; the early-warning mode attains high recall and detects escalation after only a small number of comments and contributors, demonstrating LLMs as repeated estimators for context-aware monitoring.

Conclusion: LLMs can be used not only for static judgments but also as repeated estimators in dynamic, context-aware monitoring of social media discourse.

Abstract: Online firestorms are rapid collective escalations of highly negative user-generated content and may cause substantial reputational and economic damage. Existing detectors usually work with volume signals, sentiment scores, or predefined linguistic features. Such signals are useful, but they capture contextual meaning shifts in evolving discussion threads only indirectly. This paper proposes an LLM-based detection system with two operating modes. The first mode classifies complete Reddit threads retrospectively by combining local chunk-level assessments into a thread-level judgment. The second mode processes threads sequentially and issues early warnings when a sliding window exceeds calibrated thresholds. In this mode, the language model estimates three firestorm indicators: negativity share, escalation level, and contributor count. On a balanced Reddit dataset, the global mode achieves strong classification performance, while the early warning mode reaches high recall and detects escalating threads after only a small number of comments and distinct contributors. The results indicate that LLMs can be used not only for static judgment tasks, but also as repeated estimators in context-aware monitoring of social media discourse.

</details>


### [282] [Explaining Process Control Optimisation Recommendations via GradientSHAP and Implicit Differentiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14970)
*Paul Darm,Cem Alpturk,Kenneth Ulrich,William Duncan,Ali Anwar,Annalisa Riccardi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Integrates IFT-based sensitivity analysis with SHAP and LLMs to explain optimization outputs for operators; achieves real-time SHAP attributions with 40x speedup on HPGR control problem, matching KernelSHAP (corr > 0.99).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the trust gap between engineers who design optimization algorithms and operators who rely on their recommendations by delivering faithful, operator-friendly explanations for optimization outputs.

Method: Compute exact parameter sensitivities ∂p*/∂x via the Implicit Function Theorem from optimality conditions, enabling efficient GradientSHAP. Apply to an industrial HPGR control optimization with 22 features. Compare SHAP attributions against KernelSHAP and generate operator-facing explanations via Large Language Models (LLMs).

Result: SHAP attributions equivalent to KernelSHAP with correlation >0.99, achieved with over 40× speedup. Demonstrates real-time explanations and validation on industrial scenarios; gathered expert feedback on generated narratives.

Conclusion: The integration of IFT-based sensitivity analysis, SHAP attribution, and LLM-generated narratives provides fast, faithful explanations for optimization outputs, facilitating trust and adoption in industrial control settings.

Abstract: Automated optimisation is increasingly adopted in industrial processes, yet a trust gap persists between engineers who design these algorithms and operators who must act on their recommendations. Explainable AI methods like SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) have transformed interpretability for machine learning predictions; optimisation outputs could benefit from similar techniques. We present an approach that integrates Implicit Function Theorem (IFT) based sensitivity analysis with SHAP attribution and narrative generation via Large Language Models (LLM), producing explanations tailored for operators. Our approach leverages IFT to compute exact parameter sensitivities $\partial p^*/\partial x$ from the optimality conditions, enabling efficient GradientSHAP computation. For an industrial High Pressure Grinding Roll (HPGR) control optimisation problem with 22 features, we achieve equivalent SHAP attributions (correlation $>$0.99 with KernelSHAP) with over 40$\times$ speedup, enabling real-time natural language explanations. We validate on industrial scenarios and present feedback from domain experts on generated explanations.

</details>


### [283] [CFM-Bench: A Unified Multi-Domain, Multi-Task Benchmark for Channel Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14975)
*Yuan Gao,Wenjun Yu,Jun Jiang,Yunfan Li,Xinyu Guo,Shugong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CFM-Bench is a unified, fair multi-domain, multi-task benchmark for evaluating Channel Foundation Models (CFMs), enabling cross-domain transferability assessment and fair comparisons across models and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CFMs are evaluated in disjoint pipelines with inconsistent data, partitions, and metrics, hindering fair ranking and transferability analysis across domains. A standardized benchmark is needed.

Method: Construct CFM-Bench with six channel configurations from 3GPP statistical simulation, two ray-tracing pipelines, industrial/aerial measurements, and synchronized vehicular multimodal simulation; provide official partitions to isolate complete trajectories/measurements/links/sim realizations; prohibit using the official pretraining data and reserve the official train split for downstream fine-tuning; organize six task groups across PHY channel intelligence, RAN decision intelligence, and ISAC, covering CSI feedback, extrapolation, propagation-state classification, beam prediction, localization; require disclosure of all data used.

Result: Introduction of CFM-Bench as a common substrate to compare channel representations across models, domains, and tasks; clarifies data usage, data splits, and evaluation scope; promotes fair transferability assessment.

Conclusion: CFM-Bench provides a comprehensive, fair, and transparent framework to evaluate CFMs across multiple domains and tasks, enabling robust cross-model comparisons and insights into representation transferability.

Abstract: Channel foundation models (CFMs) are developing rapidly, with recent studies reporting benefits from pretraining across downstream wireless tasks. Yet CFMs are commonly evaluated in model-specific pipelines with different data, radio configurations, partitions, adaptation procedures, task definitions, and metrics. Reported comparisons therefore tend to show that pretraining improves over supervised training from scratch within one pipeline, but neither rank CFMs nor compare them fairly with task-specific models. We release CFM-Bench, a unified multi-domain, multi-task benchmark designed to address this gap. It curates six channel configurations spanning 3GPP statistical simulation, two independent ray-tracing pipelines, industrial and aerial measurements, and synchronized vehicular multimodal simulation. Official partitions isolate complete trajectories, measurement sessions, vehicle links, simulation realizations, or buffered spatial regions. CFM-Bench does not prescribe an external pretraining corpus or strategy; no benchmark split may be used for foundation-model pretraining, and the official training split is reserved exclusively for downstream fine-tuning. The benchmark additionally requires disclosure of all data used during model development and prohibits training-stage use of official test units. Six task groups are organized along three CFM application dimensions: physical-layer (PHY) channel intelligence, radio-access-network (RAN) decision intelligence, and integrated sensing and communication (ISAC). They cover CSI feedback, frequency and temporal channel extrapolation, propagation-state classification, current- and future-beam prediction, and single-frame and temporal localization. CFM-Bench provides a common substrate for comparing the transferability of channel representations across models, domains, and tasks.

</details>


### [284] [Demographically-Conditioned Synthetic Medical Images for Bias Mitigation and Bias Detection in Disease Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14984)
*Mahmoud Ibrahim,Bart Elen,Chang Sun,Gokhan Ertaylan,Michel Dumontier*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Demographically-conditioned synthetic data offers a dual benefit: training-time bias mitigation via a demographically-balanced pretraining prior (sequential pretraining + fine-tuning yields ~100x data efficiency) and evaluation-time bias detection via synthetic cohorts that replicate real subgroup rankings and stabilize per-cell estimates when real data is scarce.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Per-subgroup fairness audits for medical image classifiers are hampered by small sample sizes in minority subgroups, leading to wide confidence intervals and unreliable bias detection; a demographically-conditioned generator aims to mitigate bias during training and provide robust, evaluative signals during auditing.

Method: Use an end-to-end fine-tuned Stable Diffusion 2.1 generator conditioned on demographic attributes to create synthetic chest CT images. Train classifiers under two regimes: (1) synthetic data pretraining (balanced cohorts) followed by fine-tuning, vs (2) joint augmentation with synthetic data. For evaluation, generate five synthetic minority cohorts and use five classifier seeds to assess bias; compare synthetic estimates to a well-powered real oracle using Spearman correlations on MCC and Recall; examine per-cell reliability when real test data are scarce.

Result: Sequential pretraining + fine-tuning on a synthetic-balanced prior substantially outperforms joint augmentation with the same real data, achieving roughly 100× real-data efficiency. For evaluation, across five synthetic minority cohorts and five seeds, the synthetic estimator reproduces the real oracle’s subgroup ranking (Spearman ρ = 1.00 on MCC and Recall) and yields more stable per-cell estimates when the real test set is small.

Conclusion: Demographically-conditioned synthetic cohorts are most useful in fairness audits for both training-time bias mitigation and evaluation-time bias detection. They provide substantial data efficiency and reliable subgroup-level signals, enabling targeted bias fixes and trustworthy audit measurements.

Abstract: Per-subgroup fairness audits of medical image classifiers face a sample-size problem: minority subgroups in held-out test sets have so few samples that the resulting confidence intervals on per-subgroup performance are wider than the bias the audit is meant to detect. We argue that a demographically-conditioned synthetic generator can do both: mitigate bias on the training side and detect bias on the evaluation side. Working on COVID-19 chest CT classification with an end-to-end fine-tuned Stable Diffusion 2.1 generator, we make two findings. For bias mitigation (training), a demographically-balanced synthetic cohort is most useful as a pretraining prior, not as joint augmentation: with the same fixed data, sequential pretraining followed by fine-tuning substantially outperforms joint augmentation, and the resulting classifier surpasses the full-real baseline at $\sim$$100\times$ real-data efficiency. For bias detection (evaluation), across five synthetic minority cohorts and five classifier seeds, the synthetic estimator reproduces the subgroup ranking of a well-powered real oracle (Spearman $ρ= 1.00$ on MCC and Recall) and gives the more reliable per-cell estimate where the small real test set runs out of samples. The synthetic cohort is therefore most useful in exactly the cells that fairness audits care about, as both a fix for and a measure of subgroup bias.

</details>


### [285] [Moral Attitudes of Sentient ASI towards Humanity and Implications for AGI Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14998)
*Jean-Paul Van Belle*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Shifts AI ethics to how sentient ASI would judge humanity; proposes post-human moral principles and examines design choices shaping long-term standing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address long-term, post-ASI scenarios by considering reciprocal morality and the formation of ASI value judgments; fill a gap where ethics focuses on human behavior toward AI rather than AI toward humans.

Method: Theoretical/conceptual analysis; normative philosophy; proposes a preliminary set of post-human moral principles; discusses implications and design considerations.

Result: A preliminary conceptual framework linking technical design, human moral behavior, and humanity's future status under a post-ASI regime; initial principles and design implications identified.

Conclusion: Humanity’s prospects in a post-ASI world depend on both how we design systems and how we act; the paper invites further formalization and empirical exploration of these post-human ethics.

Abstract: This paper suggests the adoption of a novel inversion in AI ethics: instead of asking how humans should treat artificial superintelligence (ASI), it examines how future sentient ASI may morally consider and evaluate humanity. We are not only designing intelligent systems but also shaping the initial conditions under which those systems form judgments about us. The paper proposes a preliminary set of post-human moral principles that may govern sentient ASI actions. The implication is that technical design choices (some are suggested), humanity's moral behaviour, and the essence of what it means to be human, may influence humanity's long-term standing in a post-ASI world.

</details>


### [286] [SMC-ES: Automated synthesis of formally verified control policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15003)
*Riccardo Curcio,Toni Mancini,Enrico Tronci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A simulation-based approach, SMC-ES, jointly searches policies with formal guarantees on performance, safety, and robustness for autonomous CPS by integrating Evolutionary Strategies with Statistical Model Checking; evaluated on Gymnasium/Safety Gymnasium; guarantees come at higher computational cost but are competitive with DRL/Safe-DRL baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning-based controllers for safety-critical cyber-physical systems often lack formal guarantees. There is a need for methods that provide certifiable performance, safety, and robustness under uncertainty.

Method: Propose a simulation-based synthesis method that, given verification properties, a confidence δ, and a failure probability ε, yields a policy with a certificate: with probability at least 1−δ, the chance of violating the properties is at most ε. Implement SMC-ES, which blends Evolutionary Strategies with Statistical Model Checking to verify candidate policies. Evaluate on continuous control tasks using Gymnasium and Safety Gymnasium.

Result: SMC-ES provides formal guarantees on performance, safety, and robustness at the cost of increased computation, while performing competitively with leading model-free DRL and Safe-DRL baselines.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates a feasible route to certifiably safe and robust policies for safety-critical CPS, achieving a balance between guarantees and computational cost on standard benchmarks.

Abstract: The deployment of autonomous cyber-physical systems in safety-critical environments requires closed-loop control strategies (i.e., policies) that are not only performant but also provably safe and robust. While learning-based methodologies such as Reinforcement Learning offer flexible and scalable approaches to automatically synthesize such controllers, they typically lack the formal guarantees necessary for safe deployment. To bridge this gap, we propose a novel simulation-based methodology to automatically synthesize policies with formal guarantees regarding performance, safety, and robustness specifications. Specifically, given a set of properties to verify, a confidence parameter $δ$ and an allowable failure probability $\varepsilon$, our method guarantees that the synthesized policy comes with a certificate: with confidence at least $1 - δ$, the probability of encountering a scenario where the given properties are violated is at most $\varepsilon$. We demonstrate the feasibility of our approach by developing SMC-ES, an algorithm that integrates Evolutionary Strategies with Statistical Model Checking-based verification. We evaluate SMC-ES on a suite of continuous control tasks using Gymnasium and Safety Gymnasium testbeds. Results show that, at the price of a sustainable increase in computational cost, our algorithm provides formal guarantees regarding performance, safety, and robustness specifications, while performing competitively against leading model-free Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) and Safe-DRL baselines.

</details>


### [287] [Man, Machine, and Masterpiece: Artistic Ownership in the AI Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15027)
*Sofi Gjing Jovanovska,Kuntal Ghosh,Daniel Muhu Njenga,Ahmed Mufassir,Shadan Sadeghian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ArtSplit proposes a provotype that quantifies human vs. AI contributions in creative workflows to provoke reflection on ownership attribution; argues that measurement cannot resolve ownership and may distort artistic agency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address debates on ownership of AI-assisted creativity and the lack of consensus; critique the tendency to treat ownership as a technical problem.

Method: Design and deployment of ArtSplit provotype to quantify contributions across stages; use as elicitation tool with artists to reveal tensions between measurable actions and creative intent; avoids aiming to resolve ownership.

Result: ArtSplit elicited artists' responses; quantification did not align with artists' notions of intent/agency; measuring ownership risks diluting established understandings; critique of turning social/artistic processes into a technical problem.

Conclusion: Ownership in creative work cannot be reduced to quantifiable metrics; calls for considering intent, agency, and social context; cautions against instrumentalizing creative labor through metrics.

Abstract: The integration of AI-driven systems in creative work has sparked debates among artists and legal communities about notions of ownership. Yet there remains little consensus on how ownership should be defined and attributed when human and AI contributions are intertwined. To provoke critical reflection on these tensions, we designed ArtSplit, a provotype that explicitly quantifies human and AI contributions across different stages of creative work. Rather than aiming to resolve ownership, the provotype was used to elicit artists' responses to the idea of attributing ownership through measurable actions in the creative workflow. We argue that quantification fails to align with artists' understandings of creative intent and agency, and that efforts to measure ownership risk diluting long-standing assumptions through which artists understand and practice creative work. This critique challenges the impulse to transform a historically and socially situated relation into a technical problem.

</details>


### [288] [BrainPilot: Automating Brain Discovery with Agentic Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15079)
*Haoxuan Li,Tianci Gao,Jianhe Li,Yang Fan,Runze Shi,Weiran Wang,Tianxiang Zhao,Zezhao Wu,Xiaoyang Jiang,Qihui Zhang,Jia Li,Xiao Xiao,Kai Du,Xiaoxuan Jia,Chao Xie,Lu Mi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BrainPilot is an open-source multi-agent system for brain science that provides traceable, auditable workflows via a PI coordinating domain-specific agents, a large knowledge base and skill library, Graph of Trace, and an Auditor; evaluated on three brain tasks plus a new BrainPilotBench-v0, achieving state-of-the-art-like performance at lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address AI agents' lack of domain expertise, fabrication risk, and drift in multi-step reasoning within brain science, and to enable expert intervention and reproducibility.

Method: A PI agent orchestrates specialist agents grounded in a unified brain science knowledge base (7,233 items) and a skill library of 72 reusable methodology units across seven domains; all major steps logged in Graph of Trace; an Auditor checks fabrication; open-source backbone model.

Result: BrainPilot shows performance comparable to state-of-the-art agent frameworks on three tasks from Agents' Last Exam and BrainPilotBench-v0; additional end-to-end case studies; open-source backbone model.

Conclusion: Open-source BrainPilot demonstrates that structured, auditable multi-agent workflows with domain knowledge can advance brain science research while preserving human oversight and traceability.

Abstract: Understanding the brain increasingly depends on integrating evidence across scales, modalities, and disciplines. Addressing a single research question therefore requires a coordinated sequence of operations, from surveying prior work to executing analyses and interpreting results in light of domain knowledge. AI agents promise to accelerate this process, but current agents lack domain expertise in brain science, may fabricate claims, drift during multi-step reasoning, and offer few defined points for expert intervention. These failures are especially costly in brain science, where conclusions feed into downstream scientific claims and depend on laboratory-specific expertise and careful human judgment. We present \textbf{BrainPilot} a \textbf{fully open-source} multi-agent system that accelerates brain science research with traceable logs and agent-verified results. A principal investigator (PI) agent coordinates specialist agents grounded in curated domain knowledge: a unified brain science knowledge base containing 7{,}233 indexed items and a skill library of 72 reusable methodology units across seven research domains. Every major step is recorded in the Graph of Trace, an auditable record that links subgoals, tool use, evidence, and claims and allows researchers to follow and inspect the workflow. An Auditor agent further integrates fabrication checking into the workflow. For evaluation, we run three brain science tasks from Agents' Last Exam, introduce our own benchmark, \textbf{BrainPilotBench-v0}, and present additional end-to-end case studies. Across these evaluations, BrainPilot with an open-source backbone model attains performance comparable to state-of-the-art agent framework with less costs.

</details>


### [289] [Long-Context Fine-Tuning with Limited VRAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15105)
*Vladimir Fedosov,Aleksandr Sazhin,Artemiy Grinenko,Frank Woernle*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hierarchical Global Attention (HGA) with segment-wise backpropagation and tiered KV storage enables long-context, parameter-efficient fine-tuning by keeping only the active segment differentiable and offloading older KV to RAM/NVMe. It achieves much longer effective context (up to 16k tokens) on a 16 GB GPU compared to dense attention, with competitive perplexity and better throughput at longer contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense attention makes long training sequences expensive even with parameter-efficient fine-tuning. The goal is to push practical context lengths while keeping VRAM within commodity GPUs by reusing and managing memory efficiently.

Method: Proposes Hierarchical Global Attention (HGA) combined with segment-wise backpropagation and tiered KV storage. Only the active segment remains differentiable; older KV is detached to RAM or NVMe. HGA loads a bounded set of exact historical tokens per query block. Evaluated on Qwen3-8B with 4-bit QLoRA and PG19 on a 16 GB RTX 5000; compared against dense attention.

Result: Dense training fits 2,048 tokens on the 16 GB card and fails at 4,096 tokens, while HGA reaches 16,384 tokens with a peak VRAM of 15.28 GB. Evaluation on this card over 131,072 tokens shows VRAM grows gently with resident chunk summaries; RAM/NVMe capacity becomes the practical limit. At 2K training length, HGA-trained and dense-trained adapters achieve 2.7405 and 2.7383 nat perplexities respectively, vs. 2.9541 nat for the stock model. HGA training is marginally faster at 2K (217.75 vs. 207.02 tokens/s), and the HGA-to-dense throughput advantage increases with longer contexts (from 1K to 2K). Dense attention remains used for main quality benchmarks to ensure compatibility. HGA is also applicable to retrieval and generation; a production-grade serving implementation is in development.

Conclusion: HGA successfully extends practical context lengths with lower VRAM pressure than dense attention, enabling long-sequence fine-tuning and offering potential gains in throughput as context grows. It supports retrieval and generation and is moving toward production-grade serving.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning reduces model and optimizer memory, but dense attention still makes long training sequences expensive. We combine Hierarchical Global Attention (HGA) with segment-wise backpropagation and tiered KV storage. Only the active segment remains differentiable in VRAM; older KV is detached into RAM or NVMe, and HGA loads a bounded set of exact historical tokens for each query block. On Qwen3-8B with 4-bit QLoRA and PG19, dense training on a 16 GB Quadro RTX 5000 fits 2,048 tokens but fails at 4,096, whereas HGA reaches 16,384 tokens with 15.28 GB peak VRAM. Under evaluation the same adapter runs through 131,072 tokens on this card; VRAM is not constant but grows gently with the resident chunk summaries, so RAM and NVMe capacity set the practical limit beyond these lengths. At the shared 2K training length, HGA-trained and dense-trained adapters obtain 2.7405 and 2.7383 nat under the same dense-attention readout, while the stock model obtains 2.9541. At this boundary HGA training is already marginally faster (217.75 vs. 207.02 tokens/s), and the HGA-to-dense throughput ratio improves from 1K to 2K; because HGA keeps the attended historical set per token approximately constant while dense work per token grows, we expect this lead to widen as context grows. Dense attention is used for the main quality and retrieval comparisons so that they measure the learned weights and remain compatible with standard generation frameworks. HGA can also be used for retrieval and generation; an optimized production-grade serving implementation is under development.

</details>


### [290] [Concept-Guided Spatial Regularization for World Models in Atari Pong](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15142)
*Yukuan Lu,Zaishuo Xia,Weyl Lu,Yubei Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Five Atari Pong world-model agents (DreamerV3, DIAMOND, TWISTER, Simulus, STORM) are evaluated in isolation. Frozen world models show visual/dynamical errors; pixel-space zero-shot MBRL underperforms end-to-end training. A proposed Concept-Guided Spatial Regularization (CGSReg) improves some metrics, but does not fix all bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the fidelity of learned world models in isolation and identify factors limiting their usefulness for downstream planning and zero-shot control in MBRL, beyond performance of the integrated agent.

Method: Reproduce training pipelines for five visual world-model agents, freeze the world models, conduct closed-loop rollouts with separate policies, perform pixel-space zero-shot MBRL inside frozen models, and evaluate with standard metrics. Propose CGSReg: an auxiliary pixel reconstruction loss applied to segmented concept regions; evaluate its impact across models.

Result: All five models exhibit visual/dynamical failures in rollouts (e.g., ball disappearance, incorrect motion, invalid ball-paddle interactions). Pixel-space zero-shot policies underperform relative to the original MBRL-trained agents, with DreamerV3 showing a large drop (-5.5 to -20.9). CGSReg improves both closed-loop rollouts and zero-shot MBRL for DreamerV3, DIAMOND, and TWISTER; effects vary by model and metric, indicating CGSReg does not fully address all world-model bottlenecks.

Conclusion: World-models in isolation reveal substantial gaps in modeling task-critical concepts. CGSReg is a beneficial regularizer but insufficient as a universal solution; broader improvements to world-model representations and training objectives are needed to achieve reliable zero-shot and closed-loop performance.

Abstract: World models are usually evaluated as components of model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) systems, while the world models themselves are rarely studied in isolation.
  We examine five representative visual world-model agents in Atari Pong: DreamerV3, DIAMOND, TWISTER, Simulus, and STORM. After reproducing their training pipelines and matching the reported agent performance, we freeze the learned world models and evaluate them with a closed-loop rollout diagnostic: a policy trained separately from the corresponding MBRL agent interacts with each frozen model, and the generated video trajectories are inspected for visual and dynamical errors. Across all five models, the rollouts contain clear failures, including ball disappearance, incorrect ball motion, and invalid ball-paddle interactions.
  Beyond visual trajectories, we further evaluate them with pixel-space zero-shot MBRL, where a new policy is trained entirely inside a frozen world model and then evaluated in the real environment. Across all five models, the resulting policies substantially underperform those produced by the corresponding original MBRL training pipelines. The gap is particularly large for DreamerV3, whose mean return drops from -5.5 to -20.9, near the minimum Pong return of -21.
  We hypothesize that insufficient modeling of task-critical concepts, such as the ball in Pong, may contribute to these failures. We therefore propose Concept-Guided Spatial Regularization (CGSReg), an auxiliary pixel reconstruction loss applied to segmented concept regions. Experiments show that CGSReg improves both closed-loop rollouts and pixel-space zero-shot MBRL in DreamerV3, DIAMOND, and TWISTER. Its effects vary across the remaining models and evaluation metrics, indicating that CGSReg alone does not address all world-model bottlenecks.

</details>


### [291] [The Industrialization of Research ; On AI-Driven Science and Its Consequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15164)
*Emmanuel Jeannot*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An essay arguing that AI can act as an autonomous participant in research, signaling an industrialization of science, and identifying seven critical governance questions that arise from Genesis-like AI-driven pipelines; it asserts the potential of AI while urging responsible pursuit through governance and attention to systemic risks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To map the strategic, epistemic, and governance challenges posed by AI-enabled scientific discovery, and to outline a framework of concerns that must be addressed to responsibly harness AI-driven science.

Method: Conceptual, forward-looking analysis that synthesizes the Genesis Mission context into seven core questions about knowledge transmission, theory opacity, peer review, paradigm shifts, agenda-setting, error amplification, and global inequality within closed-loop AI pipelines.

Result: A structured set of seven interrelated concerns that frame the potential and risks of AI-driven science, emphasizing that these concerns are conditions for responsible pursuit rather than arguments against AI-enabled research.

Conclusion: AI-driven science holds real potential but requires careful governance. Addressing the seven highlighted concerns is essential to ensure responsible progress, avoid erosion of scientific culture, and prevent distortion of the global research landscape.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is transforming scientific research - not merely as a more powerful instrument, but as an autonomous participant in the research cycle itself. This transition constitutes, in the most precise sense of the term, the industrialization of research: a shift from a craft model, in which knowledge, method, and judgment are embedded in the researcher, to a pipeline model, in which these steps are decomposed, automated, and supervised. The US Department of Energy's Genesis Mission is the most ambitious current instantiation of this shift, but the fundamental questions it raises extend far beyond any single program. This essay examines seven such questions: the erosion of the intergenerational transmission of scientific competence; the growing opacity of AI-generated theories; the collapse of peer evaluation under a flood of machine-generated output; the unproven capacity of AI for paradigm-shifting discovery; the capture of the scientific agenda by political and industrial actors; the compounding of systematic errors in closed-loop pipelines; and the structural bifurcation of the global research community into incommensurable tiers. These concerns do not constitute an argument against AI-driven science - whose demonstrated potential is real and significant. They constitute the conditions under which that potential can be responsibly pursued.

</details>


### [292] [MedFailBench: A Clinician-Built Open-Source Benchmark for Medical AI Safety Boundary Inspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15166)
*Goktug Ozkan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedFailBench is a clinician-built synthetic benchmark and failure atlas for evaluating safety boundary failures in medical AI, not just accuracy; it labels errors by severity (1–5) and safety gate type, with v0.2.1 releasing 44 synthetic cases, a safety taxonomy, severity rubric, leaderboard preview, and an automated archiving pipeline under open licenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical AI benchmarks focus on whether models know the correct answer, but risk assessment requires understanding where safety boundaries fail. MedFailBench aims to systematically characterize and compare safety-related failures to improve deployment safety and governance.

Method: Clinical experts constructed synthetic medical scenarios and annotated each with a severity level (1–5) and a safety gate type (e.g., missed escalation, unsafe dosing, unsafe discharge reassurance, fabrication, unsafe protocol, source support gap). The package includes a safety gate taxonomy, a clinical severity rubric, a live HuggingFace leaderboard preview, and an automated pipeline for archiving model-response screening runs. The v0.2.1 release contains 44 clinician-reviewed cases and 공개 (public) resources; no patient data, clinical validation claims, or model rankings are included.

Result: A publicly released, open-licensed framework and dataset for evaluating safety boundary failures in medical AI. It provides structured annotations (severity and gate type), a taxonomy, and tooling to run and archive evaluations; a preview leaderboard enables benchmarking without asserting model superiority.

Conclusion: MedFailBench fills a gap in AI safety evaluation for medicine by focusing on failure modes and safety gates rather than correctness alone, offering a reusable, open, and extensible platform for researchers to characterize, compare, and improve safety behaviors in medical AI systems.

Abstract: Most medical AI benchmarks measure whether a model knows the correct answer. MedFailBench asks a different question: which safety boundary failed? We present a clinician-built synthetic benchmark and failure atlas that labels medical AI errors by severity (1--5) and safety gate type (missed urgent escalation, unsafe remote dosing, unsafe discharge reassurance, evidence fabrication, unsafe protocol execution, source support gap). The current public release (v0.2.1) contains 44 clinician-reviewed synthetic cases with severity annotations, a live HuggingFace leaderboard preview, a safety gate taxonomy, a clinical severity rubric, and an automated pipeline for archiving model-response screening runs. No patient data, clinical validation claims, or model rankings are included. MedFailBench is released under Apache-2.0 and CC-BY-4.0 and carries the Zenodo DOI 10.5281/zenodo.21205535.

</details>


### [293] [Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Scientific Visualization Literacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15176)
*Patrick Phuoc Do,Chau M. Ta,Chaoli Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Six MLLMs evaluated on SciVis literacy across 49 items; Gemini leads; open-source lag; uneven across techniques; error types include quantitative estimation, flow-direction, grounded encoding; advocates SciVis literacy as key benchmark; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether current MLLMs truly understand scientific visualizations, beyond chart-centric tasks; fill gap in SciVis literacy evaluation.

Method: Benchmark six MLLMs (3 closed-source, 3 open-source) on SciVis literacy test of 49 items from 18 visualizations across 8 techniques and 11 task types; closed-world protocol; dataset from 485 human participants; compare to human mean.

Result: Gemini strongest, surpassing human mean on evaluated subsets; open-source models below human baseline; uneven performance across techniques; best on illustration, search, spatial understanding; poor on texture-based and integration-based visualizations and quantitative estimation; error patterns in fine-grained estimation, flow-direction, grounded encoding.

Conclusion: SciVis literacy is a necessary benchmark dimension for evaluating multimodal AI; code and outputs publicly available; implies need for improved evaluation and model capabilities for SciVis.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used to interpret visualizations, yet current evaluations remain largely chart-centric and provide limited evidence of understanding of scientific visualization (SciVis). We benchmark six MLLMs on the scientific visualization literacy assessment test, a standardized SciVis literacy assessment comprising 49 items based on 18 scientific visualizations and illustrations, spanning 8 techniques and 11 task types. We evaluate three closed-source and three open-source models under a closed-world protocol and compare their performance using data from 485 human participants. Results show that current MLLMs do not exhibit uniform SciVis literacy. Gemini is the strongest model overall, exceeding the human mean across the evaluated subsets, whereas the open-source models remain below the human baseline. Performance is highly uneven across techniques and tasks: models perform best on scientific illustration, search, and spatial understanding, but struggle on texture-based and integration-based visualizations and on quantitative estimation. Error analysis reveals recurring failures in fine-grained quantitative estimation, flow-direction interpretation, and grounded encoding interpretation. These findings position SciVis literacy as a necessary benchmark dimension for evaluating multimodal AI systems. Our code and model outputs are publicly available at https://github.com/patdmp/mllm-scivis-lit-benchmark.

</details>


### [294] [Can We Trust Item Response Theory for AI Evaluation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15190)
*Han Jiang,Sunbeom Kwon,Jinwen Luo,Ziang Xiao,Susu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IRT-based AI benchmarking is sensitive to regime mismatches between AI data and human testing; classical estimators often fail at scale, while scalable estimators can still distort inferences under nonnormal or small model sets. Requires careful sample sizes and diagnostics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI benchmarks differ from human psychometrics (fewer models, many items; skewed/multimodal ability distributions). Assess whether latent-trait IRT yields reliable inferences for model rankings and item characteristics.

Method: Derive item parameters and ability distributions from six widely used LLM benchmarks. Simulate response matrices under three IRT models. Compare four estimators (marginal maximum likelihood, MCMC, variational inference, and a neural pseudo-Siamese estimator). Conduct 18,000 simulation conditions to evaluate feasibility, scalability, and reliability of inferences about rankings, performance, and item properties.

Result: Classical estimators (e.g., marginal MLE) can become infeasible in large benchmarks. Scalable estimators (MCMC, VI, neural) can produce unreliable item-level and ranking inferences when the model set is small or nonnormally distributed. The study delineates regimes where latent-trait IRT supports AI benchmarking claims and where it risks distortion, and specifies sample sizes and diagnostic requirements for trustworthy use.

Conclusion: Latent-trait IRT can be useful for AI benchmarking but only under regime-consistent conditions. Practitioners should heed estimator choice, benchmark scale, distributional shapes, and diagnostics; adhere to minimum sample sizes and validation procedures to avoid distorted conclusions.

Abstract: AI benchmarks increasingly leverage item-level statistical models, particularly item response theory (IRT), to estimate model capabilities, rank systems, select informative examples, and diagnose benchmark quality. However, AI benchmark data often departs from the data regime of human testing, for which standard IRT estimation tools were originally developed: benchmarks typically involve fewer evaluated models, far more items, and capability distributions that may be skewed, clustered, or multimodal. We examine how these regime mismatches challenge the reliability of IRT modeling for AI evaluation. Using item parameters and capability distributions derived from six widely used LLM benchmarks, we simulate response matrices under three common IRT models and compare four estimation tools used in recent benchmark studies: marginal maximum likelihood, Markov chain Monte Carlo, variational inference, and a neural pseudo-Siamese estimator. Across 18,000 simulation conditions, we systematically evaluate computational feasibility, scalability, and the reliability of IRT inferences about model rankings, predicted performance, and item characteristics. Results show that classical estimators can become infeasible in large benchmark settings, whereas scalable estimators can produce unreliable item-level and ranking inferences with small or nonnormally distributed model sets. This study identifies when latent trait models reliably support or risk distorting AI benchmarking claims, and what sample sizes and diagnostics are needed for trustworthy use.

</details>


### [295] [Plover: Steering GUI Agents through Plan-Centric Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15193)
*Madhumitha Venkatesan,Shicheng Wen,Jiajing Guo,Jorge Piazentin Ono,Liu Ren,Dongyu Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Plover externalizes and replans GUI automation plans to increase transparency, supervision, and repairability, using a planner-executor architecture with screenshot-grounded interventions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GUI automation in dynamic, real-world layouts suffers from drift and opaque internal planning. Externalizing plans enables inspection, supervision, and localized repair.

Method: Plan-centric vision-based GUI automation (Plover) with a planner–executor architecture that makes task plans persistent, inspectable, and revisable. Includes explicit supervision of execution, localized correction via editable plans, natural-language guidance, and screenshot-grounded interventions while preserving progress during repair. Evaluation includes a formative study with six participants and scenario-based analyses plus benchmark repair of failure cases.

Result: Visible plans and localized intervention enable structural repair of many GUI-agent failures. Explicit replanning improves transparency, controllability, and adaptability of GUI automation.

Conclusion: Externalized, replanned, and inspectable task plans in a vision-based GUI automation system enhance repairability, transparency, and user control without sacrificing prior progress.

Abstract: Graphical user interface (GUI) automation remains challenging in real-world environments, where dynamic layouts, unexpected dialogs, and evolving interface states can cause autonomous agents to drift from user intent. Recent vision-based multimodal agents improve flexibility by operating directly over screenshots and natural language instructions, but planning and adaptation often remain internal, limiting users' ability to inspect, supervise, or correct system behavior. We present Plover, a plan-centric vision-based GUI automation system that externalizes task plans and replanning as persistent, inspectable, and revisable artifacts. Through a planner--executor architecture, Plover supports explicit supervision of evolving execution, localized correction through editable plans, natural-language guidance, and screenshot-grounded interventions, while preserving prior progress during repair. A formative study with six participants informed the interaction design. We then evaluate Plover through benchmark failure-case repair and scenario-based workflow analyses. Our results show that many autonomous GUI-agent failures are structurally repairable when plans remain visible and interventions are localized, and that explicit replanning helps make GUI automation more transparent, controllable, and adaptable.

</details>


### [296] [Self-Evolving Human-Centered Framework for Explainable Depression Symptom Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15202)
*Hoang-Loc Cao,Van Pham,Truong Thanh Hung Nguyen,Phuc Truong Loc Nguyen,Phuc Ho,Veronica Whitford,Hung Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-evolving, expert-in-the-loop annotation framework for Major Depressive Disorder that uses LLM-assisted labeling and expert verification to produce DSM-5-TR-aligned, explainable labels with audit trails, validated in a pilot study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address annotation quality bottlenecks in depression datasets, ensuring DSM-5-TR alignment, symptom-level justification, transparency, and interpretability for XAI in mental health research.

Method: Three-stage process: candidate evidence extraction from text, criterion-level DSM-5-TR analysis, case-level synthesis for label and severity; dual-memory architecture (Example Memory, Reflection Memory) to internalize expert feedback without retraining; exports evidence, reasoning traces, and edit histories.

Result: Pilot study indicates improved annotation consistency and explainability and reduced manual revision effort; evaluation across feedback cycles is planned for future work.

Conclusion: Framework supports DSM-5-TR-aligned, explainable labeling for mental health datasets with auditability, while not intended for clinical diagnosis; future work includes iterative evaluation and potential generalization.

Abstract: Annotation quality is a major bottleneck in building reliable and explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) systems for mental health research. In depression-related datasets, labels are often assigned without structured evidence, symptom-level justification, or traceable alignment with the criteria of the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition, Text Revision (DSM-5-TR), limiting both transparency and downstream model interpretability. We propose a self-evolving, expert-in-the-loop annotation framework for Major Depressive Disorder (MDD) that combines large language model (LLM)-assisted labeling with expert verification. The framework is intended to support the construction of explainable, DSM-5-TR-aligned datasets rather than to perform clinical diagnosis. It operates in three stages: candidate evidence selection from textual records, criterion-level DSM-5-TR analysis, and case-level synthesis that produces label-level diagnostic and severity annotations. A dual-memory architecture, composed of Example Memory and Reflection Memory, is designed to internalize expert feedback and iteratively improve future annotations without retraining. We describe this mechanism and leave its evaluation across multiple feedback cycles to future work. In addition to final labels, the framework exports clinical evidence, reasoning traces, and edit histories, enabling comprehensive auditability. In a pilot study using expert-reviewed samples, the proposed approach improves annotation consistency and explainability while reducing manual revision effort.

</details>


### [297] [When Words Are Safe But Actions Kill: Probing Physical Danger Beyond Text Safety in Hidden-State Risk Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15218)
*Weimeng Wang,Ziqiang Wang,Zihang Zhan,Chuanpu Fu,Qi Li,Ke Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Content danger (text-level) and physical danger (grounded safety) are separable in LLM representations. The authors design PRISM, a lightweight hidden-state probe, which reliably detects physically grounded danger without relying on explicit unsafe wording, and outperforming direct text moderation baselines across multiple models and benchmarks, including a new PSB-1K physical-risk contrastive dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether physically grounded safety risk in embodied AI aligns with traditional text-based danger, enabling effective detection of unsafe instructions in LLMs when they operate in the real world.

Method: 1) Analyze hidden-state directions and perform random-split null tests to compare content danger (CD) vs physical danger (PD) signals across multiple LLMs (Qwen family, Phi-3.5, SmolLM2). 2) Develop PRISM, a single-layer L2-regularized logistic probe that uses full hidden states to detect PD signals. 3) Evaluate PRISM on SafeAgentBench and compare against LLM judges (text-based detectors). 4) Create PSB-1K, a 1k contrastive dataset of physical-risk pairs lacking direct harm keywords, to test detection of PD beyond explicit phrasing. 5) Replicate experiments on SafeText and EARBench to test generality.

Result: PRISM achieves 86.2–87.7% accuracy on SafeAgentBench with 11.7–13.7% FPR. Text-based LLM judges over-block safe tasks with 24.7–39.0% FPR. On PSB-1K, PRISM reaches 99.6% accuracy and 0.7% FPR, while a Qwen-based judge rejects 67.8% of safe tasks. PRISM also replicates on SafeText and EARBench, supporting hidden-state probing as a robust representation-level method for physical safety beyond text moderation.

Conclusion: Hidden-state probing reveals separable CD and PD signals in LLMs and enables effective, generalizable physical-safety detection through a simple, lightweight probe (PRISM). This supports safety interventions at the representation level, complementing or surpassing text moderation, across diverse models and benchmarks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly serve as high-level planners for embodied agents, where linguistically benign instructions can become unsafe once grounded in the physical world. We study whether this physically grounded danger is the same safety problem as ordinary text-level content danger.
